III

CONTROL AND REDUCTION OF ARMAMENTS SATURDAY, JUNE 16, 1956 UNITED STATES SENATE, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELATIONS, SUBCOMMITTEE ON DISARMAMENT, 

Minneapolis, Minn.

The subcommittee met, pursuant to notice, at 10:15 a. m. in the auditorium, New Mayo Memorial Hospital, Senator Hubert H. Humphrey (chairman of the subcommittee) presiding. 

Present: Senators Humphrey and Wiley. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. As chairman of the special subcommittee on the subject of disarmament, I call this meeting to order. 

First of all, may I note that the former chairman of the Senate Committee on Foreign Relations and the ranking minority member, Senator Alexander Wiley, of Wisconsin, is present with us today, and for this I am extremely grateful. He has made a very great contribution to our foreign policy, and indeed he has a great record of service to his country.

I am going to tell you just a little bit in the opening moments about this subcommittee, its purposes and objectives, and then make a short opening statement.

PURPOSES AND OBJECTIVES OF THE SUBCOMMITTEE 

First of all, this subcommittee was established by special Senate resolution. It was my privilege to introduce that resolution, and it was readily adopted by the Senate of the United States. The subcommittee consists of 12 members, 6 Democratic, 6 Republican. The membership is Senator Byrd, Senator Pastore, Senator Symington, Senator Sparkman, Senator Long, Senator Wiley, Senator Bridges, Senator Saltonstall, Senator Knowland, Senator Bricker, and Senator Hickenlooper. 

I think you can understand that that membership is a good cross section of all political points of view represented in the Congress of the United States and in the respective political parties. The committee has as its purpose cooperation with the executive branch of our Government in reaching a policy for this all-important subject of reduction and control of armaments or, as it is commonly put, disarmament.

When the President of the United States appointed Mr. Harold 

Stassen as his special assistant on the subject of disarmament, there were those of us in the Congress who felt that there was a distinct role for the congressional body to play in this field. 

If disarmament is to succeed, it will require changes of law on the part of the Congress. Furthermore, we felt that there should be a 593

594 

CONTROL AND REDUCTION OF ARMAMENTS 

better understanding on the part of Congress of the dimensions and proportions of this problem and the intricate and difficult details of the problem. 

Likewise we felt that our subcommittee might very well be a sounding board for public opinion-for the members of the great American public to give their views. 

PRIOR SUBCOMMITTEE HEARINGS 

It has been in this spirit that we have proceeded and I am happy to say that we have heard some brilliant and moving testimony during the hearings that have been conducted by this subcommittee. 

This part week we have had hearings participated in by Mr. Stassen following his work at the London Conference under the auspices of the United Nations. We have heard from a number of witnesses representing public and private groups. 

In the first part of this year Mr. Stassen gave us first of all an overall analysis of the situation, followed by the Secretary of State, Mr. 

Dulles. I might say that I think that testimony was of very great importance to policy formulation by our Government. 

We heard from the Secretary of Defense, Mr. Wilson, the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, Admiral Radford, the Chairman of the 

Atomic Energy Committee, Admiral Strauss; the Senior Member of the Atomic Energy Commission, Commissioner Murray and United

States Senator Flanders, who has devoted a lifetime to work in this field of disarmament.

We have heard from many private representatives. Recently we were at Boston where we heard the testimony of distinguished scholars who are experts in this field from Harvard University, Boston University, Boston College, Massachusetts Institute of Technology, and the lay public in general.

Now this gives you a brief outline of what we have been doing and we intend to continue our work. We shall be holding other public hearings to receive the expressions of the citizenry throughout the country. 

TRIBUTE TO FRANK B. KELLOGG 

It is very fitting that in the year 1956 this Subcommittee on Disarmament should be meeting in Minnesota. This year marks the hundredth anniversary of the birth of a Minnesota citizen who became a leading world statesman and who devoted a great part of his life to the problem of disarmament. I refer to Frank B. Kellogg. He was a Senator from Minnesota from 1917 to 1923, Secretary of State from 1925 to 1929, and the coauthor of the Kellogg-Briand Pact of Paris to outlaw war.

This pact was eventually signed by practically every nation in the world. The goal of the Kellogg-Briand Pact-peace-remains our goal, but today we have to go beyond the statement of this goal. We have to find the means whereby nations can solve their differences by peaceful means. Our task is quite obviously complicated, and to be sure it is urgent. The world has grown smaller. Weapons have become ever more destructive. We must find a way of safeguarding the citizens of this country at a time when it is theoretically possible for science to construct weapons of unlimited destructive powernuclear weapons capable of turning the nations of the world into

CONTROL AND REDUCTION OF ARMAMENTS 

595 empty deserts. It is our hope that this subcommittee and these hearings will contribute to the solution of this urgent, pressing problem. 

ACKNOWLEDGMENT TO UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA AND MINNESOTA WORLD AFFAIRS CENTER 

On behalf of myself and my colleagues of the Subcommittee on Disarmament, and in particular today Senator Wiley, I want to express appreciation to the University of Minnesota and the Minnesota World

Affairs Center for their cooperation in helping to arrange this hearing. On a matter as important as the control and reduction of armaments, it is essential that the Congress have the views of interested and qualified citizens outside of the Government and outside of the 

Nation's Capital. 

Hearings of this kind would be extremely difficult if not impossible to hold without the cooperation of public-spirited citizens and institutions. 

I wish to express our thanks especially to Mr. William C. Rogers, the director of the World Affairs Center, and Miss Barbara Stuhler, assistant director of the Minnesota World Affairs Center, both of whom have devoted a great deal of time to the detailed arrangements of this hearing.

I digress to point out that this pattern of utilizing the competent services and talent of university directors is being followed in other hearings. We feel that we get much more productive results this way. 

Our meeting today is the ninth hearing of the Subcommittee on Disarmament and the second which we have held outside of Washington. 

The subcommittee will receive this morning testimony from a panel of five men who have been asked to testify on certain special problems relating to the subcommittee's inquiry into disarmament. Following the panel discussion we shall hear those witnesses who represent organizations and persons who appear here as individuals to share with us their ideas and proposals on arms control. 

CITIZENS' RIGHT TO FREE SPEECH AND RIGHT TO PETITION 

As representatives of the American people it is our solemn responsibility to make certain that the views of interested citizens on the question of disarmament receive consideration from their Government. 

Those who appear today are exercising a fundamental privilege of freedom. They are acting under the first amendment of the Constitution which assures the people of this country the right to petition the Government.

Representatives of the subcommittee have come to Minneapolis and we shall go to other cities to facilitate the exercise of that right, the right of free speech and the right of petition. 

Senator Wiley, would you like to make a comment?

OPENING REMARKS OF SENATOR WILEY 

Senator WILEY. Our President has said there is no alternative to peace. Secretary Dulles recently stated "The destructive power inherent in matter must be controlled by the idealism of men's spirit and the wisdom of his mind. They alone stand between us and a lifeless planet."

596 

CONTROL AND REDUCTION OF ARMAMENTS 

There are plenty of problems in this world, many of them interconnected, but there is no problem which compares with this central universal problem of saving the human race from extinction. 

In turn disarmament discussions, as stated, can only advance within the framework of world public opinion. 

ATTITUDE OF SOVIET UNION 

Is the world ready for a thing of this kind? We want to find out.

That is why we are here. Always we get back when we discuss these matters to the most basic problem of all: Is there in the heart of the 

Soviet leaders, as we know there is in our heart, a genuine desire to do everything humanly possible to have all of us succeed in the disarmament talks?

So I repeat, is there a real desire for just and durable peace, because after all, disarmament is not a goal in and of itself but rather is a vital part of a just and durable peace. 

We want to be sure that we do not indulge in discouragement or despair. We still don't know the real answer to the question: What is actually in the mind of the Kremlin? Does it actually desire to do everything possible to avert an H-bomb holocaust in the world; to avert it by genuinely constructive deeds, irrevocable ironclad deeds?

I have had my dealings with their officials abroad and I know. When 

I was in Helsinki last year I had an opportunity to tell them that not words, but deeds, are what we want. For the present I can only say that I believe the Soviets are not immovable. They have moved. 

Can we move them farther on? We must constantly, my good friends, move ahead with judgment and particularly with discretion. 

LEADERSHIP IN EDUCATING AMERICAN PUBLIC NEEDED 

We as a people must and will avoid the bluster of threatening statements or the impatience of immaturity. We must avoid any fussy idealism which would cause us to take ill-conceived unilateral disarmament steps without the tightest and most effective safeguards. That is what the President has in mind. But at the same time we must avoid the so-called realism which may only be a blind cynicism which refuses to admit the possibilities of progress in the field. 

I am very happy to be here. I join with Senator Humphrey in thanking you people for making available this wonderful place in this wonderful world-known city. We know that time is running out and passive indifference must give way to active searching, searching for the way. Thus it is up to responsible community organizations and to university students, such as those of you in the groups represented here, to continue leadership in this job of educating the public to the facts of life today, not the facts of yesteryear. One of the facts of life today is that we live in a contracted world, where no nation is safe, where every nation is in every other nation's back yard. 

I thank you, Senator Humphrey, for being with you on this occasion.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much, Senator Wiley. We have with us this morning the Governor of the State of Minnesota. I understand that he has a statement he would like to make and I would like to call him to the witness stand.

Governor Freeman.

WELCOMING REMARKS BY GOV. ORVILLE FREEMAN, GOVERNOR OF THE STATE OF MINNESOTA 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Governor, before you begin, may I say we did extend an invitation to every member of the Minnesota congressional delegation and to my distinguished colleague, Senator Thye, who was unable to be with us today. He is a member of the Appropriations Committee, and they have some very difficult work to do in order to mark up the appropriations bills. I wanted the record to show he asked me to convey his greetings and his regrets. 

Governor FREEMAN. Senator Humphrey and Senator Wiley, ladies and gentlemen, my primary function here this morning is to welcome you to the State of Minnesota and to express on behalf of all of our people our appreciation of your coming here.

We were very pleased when representatives of the Foreign Relations Committee came here several years ago on the question of amending the United Nations Charter, and we surely are happy that you have seen fit to come to our State and to this great institution here today. 

VALUE OF COORDINATED THINKING ON WORLD AFFAIRS 

Might I say with what I hope will be a pardonable pride, that we of 

Minnesota are very proud of the University of Minnesota and surely of the World Affairs Center, which has contributed so much to a consolidation of function and consideration of some of the vital problems that we face in the world today in connection with international affairs.

The 

Surely these efforts have resulted, we feel, in a contribution to thinking here and around the country and perhaps around the world. bringing together under one overall coordinated effort of various schools of thought in this area has contributed a good deal to our thinking. We feel proud of the fact, that here at the University we have had the leading attention perhaps in the Middle West and that the varying points of view represented here have consistently contributed to an interchange of ideas which we think has been constructive.

I would like if I may just to venture four general principles at this time and then make way for the panel to follow which I think will make a major contribution to the deliberations of your committee in the tremendously important work that it is performing. 

CHANGED VIEWPOINT IN MIDWEST ON ISOLATIONISM 

First of all, based upon my not inconsiderable travels around this State, I think I can say that the people of the State of Minnesota are deeply concerned with the problem that you consider. There was a time when the Midwest and our State and, I think your State too, Senator Wiley, were considered isolationist, but I think that that is no longer true. That is of course a question of definition of terms, but fundamentally I think people in all walks of life today are deeply concerned and that they recognize, as Senator Wiley pointed out a moment ago, that we surely are living in a world that grows smaller each year.

And may I say that in our State I think a major contribution in focusing attention and removing some old emotional blocks has been

the work of Senator Humphrey. I can remember, and I particularly do at this institution, back in the middle thirties when there was some reason to believe that Minnesota and the upper Midwest were isolationist, that Senator Humphrey was, I think we could only say, preaching from every platform the doctrines of Woodrow Wilson, the Four Freedoms and the need to live amicably in a world which was growing small.

And so today I feel that our people are now fully considering these problems and that they are going to seek to bring their opinions to bear on them and to get more information about them. 

MILITARY EXPENDITURES AND STATE GOVERNMENT 

Secondly, we surely want to wish this committee the very best in its deliberations. I made a quick calculation yesterday which may not be precisely accurate, but I believe that the percentage of revenue which goes into national defense from the State of Minnesota would approximate what it costs to run our entire State government for a full 2 years. 

Now this being the case, and Minnesota like other States in a period of growing population and expanding needs, would like to use these resources constructively to build schools, hospitals, roads, parks, to make our State a better place for its people. 

This is not to say however, that the money which is expended on armaments is not expended for a necessary and vital purpose. Quite the contrary, it is sometimes said armaments cause war. I think it might be better said that armaments are an expression of the underlying cause and that thinking ought to be clarified in this aspect. 

DETERMINING REAL SOVIET ATTITUDES 

The underlying cause surely is the nature of the Soviet system. My knowledge of that system and its history would lead me to feel we must not be misled nor tricked into dissipating our strength in the absence of conclusive and demonstrable actions by the Soviet Union. 

The present smiles and overtures are not in my judgment anyway conclusive in that regard. We have, I believe, in the past made errors in this respect. We have disarmed unilaterally in this country, and we have paid a price for it.

And as one who fought in the last war and in the early period when we did have limited resources, surely much less than our antagonists, I can speak from some bitter experience in that regard. 

That, of course, is not to say that we should not exploit every possibility and every opportunity, as Senator Wiley just pointed out, in the continuing effort to determine whether there is sincerity, whether there is real desire on the part of the Soviet Union to disarm and to accomplish the goal of a peaceful world where we will not destroy ourselves. But surely we are less able to make mistakes now than we ever have been before in the past of our history. We cannot afford to make mistakes at a time when the control of mankind over the acts of destruction has become so great. And thus your deliberations are so vital, so crucial, and so important.

COUNTERING SOVIET PROPAGANDA ABROAD 

Finally, I hope this committee would not think me presumptuous when I refer generally to the importance of your mission in connection with world opinion. I am shocked when I visit with people who have returned, particularly from the Far East, who almost without exception tell me that rank-and-file people in those countries feel that as between America and the Soviet Union, America is the aggressor; 

America is the more warlike; America is the more likely to touch off the trigger.

This I fear is an evidence that somewhere along the line our Government has failed in impressing our philosophy and our peaceful objectives upon these people. The Soviet propagandists obviously have been more clever than we.

Recently I have noted several polls taken in India alleged to be accurate, polls which show that of the people queried 30 percent approximately thought the United States was more apt to start a war as compared to roughly 2 percent where the Soviet Union is concerned. 

IMPORTANCE OF SUBCOMMITTEE'S WORK 

And I think the fact that this committee is engaging in these deliberations, is concerning itself with disarmament and the method and the manner in which your deliberations proceed is vitally important in connection with world opinion. Again I hope that I am not presumptuous when I say that I think this is a major factor to be considered all along the line. 

May I conclude by saying again that we are pleased and honored to have you here, and I am especially proud as the Governor of this State to say that I know that some of the testimony that you will hear today will make a contribution to your deliberations. 

Thank you very kindly.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much, Governor Freeman. 

William B. Lockhart. Dean Lockhart of the Law School of the 

University of Minnesota.

WELCOMING REMARKS BY WILLIAM B. LOCKHART, DEAN OF THE UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA LAW SCHOOL 

Mr. LOCKHART. I wish simply to express the pleasure of the university and the World Affairs Center at our opportunity to provide these facilities to the subcommittee for this hearing on a matter of such great importance to our Nation and the world. 

We are especially pleased to make this small contribution to your very wise efforts to seek enlightenment and guidance by going out to the various parts of the country to find out what the people outside of Washington have to say on this matter. 

We welcome you to the university. If we can be of any service to you in any way, please call upon us. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Dean Lockhart, we want to express to you our thanks and appreciation for all your cooperation. 

Mr. William Rogers, Director of the World Affairs Center,

WELCOMING REMARKS BY WILLIAM C. ROGERS, DIRECTOR, MINNESOTA WORLD AFFAIRS CENTER 

Mr. ROGERS. Senator Humphrey and Senator Wiley, I would like to take a moment to extent greetings to the Senate Subcommittee on 

Disarmament on behalf of the Minnesota World Affairs Center and its 26 member organizations.

These groups which compose the center represent a significant portion of the organized interest in world affairs in Minnesota. The individual citizen studies world affairs subjects and may take action from time to time on particular policies through these associations. 

Only through widespread public interest and understanding of American foreign policy can we hope to prevent war and preserve our freedom.

Our State and community world affairs organizations are pleased at the opportunity afforded by these hearings to learn more about disarmament and to express their views on this important subject. Thank you for coming to Minnesota. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much, Mr. Rogers.

INTRODUCTION OF STAFF MEMBERS 

May I at this point just note that Mr. Valeo to my right is the staff director of the Subcommittee on Disarmament, formerly with the Library of Congress, and is very well experienced in the field of foreign relations. He has traveled widely with members of the Foreign Relations Committee and is an outstanding student in this area. 

Also with us is Miss Betty Goetz, formerly of the University of Minnesota, a graduate of our university here, research associate on the staff of the Subcommittee on Disarmament. She has been, as you know, very instrumental in working out the details along with Mr. Valeo for this hearing, and I want you folks to know that they are with us.

STAFF STUDIES 

Having mentioned the staff and staff director, may I also say that, while our staff is not large, we are privileged to utilize the very able and expert services of the Library of Congress, of many universities, and of scholars in this field that we are now discussing, in certain studies that we are preparing and have prepared under the technical direction of the staff.

For example, one of the studies was related to the organization of the disarmament work in the Government of the United States, tracing all the many areas of cooperation and coordination between the respective departments with which Mr. Stassen as the Presidential assistant in this area must work. 

I think you would be interested in that staff study. Furthermore, we have published a collection of all of the many documents relating to the field of disarmament. It is a very extensive and comprehensive compilation of documentary works in this area of discussion. 

We are also doing staff studies on disarmament proposals that have been made by every country and the leaders of other countries as well as our own. These proposals will, of course, include those of the 

Soviet Union, of Great Britain, of France, and of Italy, as well as

the proposals of the United States and those developed in the United 

Nations. 

One area you will be interested in is the economic impact of disarmament. If within the foreseeable future, disarmament were possible and were achieved, we in the United States would be faced immediately with a problem of no mean proportion as to the readjustment, the redirection of our industrial capacity, our skilled manpower, and our industrial resources removed from armament production and diverted into peacetime construction. We think that advance planning in this area is an urgent necessity.

There are several other staff studies. I just thought I would give you an expression of what is going on.

RECORD OF HEARINGS TO BE PUBLISHED 

And then finally let me say this: That while only two members of this subcommittee are present, may I say that we have an obligation as members of this subcommittee today to inform our colleagues, and they will be informed as to this hearing. 

More important, there is a transcript being kept of the hearing. 

That transcript will be carefully gone over. It will be analyzed and made available to each and every member of the subcommittee, and to the entire Congress of the United States. 

May I just make this suggestion then. As you testify will you please give your full name, your address, and any other words of identification that you may wish to express. It will make the record just that much more complete, because what you are saying is taken down by a stenotypist, a court reporter. It is a matter of public record for all to see. I thought you might want to know that. 

Now we are going to hear from the members of our panel that have expressed a willingness to help us. The first gentleman to be heard from is Hubert Schon, the acting director of civil defense, State of Minnesota.

Mr. Schon, we welcome you to this subcommittee's deliberations. 

We are very pleased that you would come and cooperate with us. 

STATEMENT OF HUBERT A. SCHON, DIRECTOR, DEPARTMENT OF CIVIL DEFENSE OF THE STATE OF MINNESOTA 

Mr. SCHON. Thank you, Senator Humphrey. My name is Hubert 

A. Schon, 1641 Rice Street, St. Paul. I am director of civil defense for the State of Minnesota. If I may I would like to read a brief statement that I submitted to your committee about a week ago which I believe summarizes some of the points that I think are important in any deliberation concerning disarmament. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Go right ahead, proceed in your own light. 

Mr. SCHON. I am grateful to appear before you and this subcommittee to discuss aspects of any disarmament program as they affect the safety of our citizens. There can be no question but that your efforts to sponsor ways and means of disarmament, as well as other means of achieving reduced tensions in the world, are basic and of prime importance. 

I am not qualified to suggest any priority toward a disarmament program insofar as they affect necessary decisions that have to be made between ground, air, or naval services.

I do desire to invite attention of this subcommittee to the fact that as long as the capability of any nation to deliver attacks on the United States with atomic, hydrogen, biological, or chemical warfare devices exists, the very last program that can be reduced is our civil defense effort.

CIVIL DEFENSE PROGRAM UNDERESTIMATED AND UNDERSUPPORTED 

I know of no one in civil defense who is even partially satisfied that we have a program at this point in time really capable of facing the tremendous task that must be met in the event of an enemy attack with weapons mentioned above. 

As a matter of fact, with the total Federal civil defense budget approximately one-fifth of 1 percent of the present national defense budget, this effort is greatly underestimated, undersupported, and underemphasized.

I think a great contribution can be made by this committee if, in its report and finding, it indicates that the efforts of local communities, States, and the Federal Government in civil defense preparations must be continued and intensified until such time that there is absolute assurance the great centers of our population and the wide acres of our countryside be free from the threat of atomic attack and radiological contamination. 

TRAINING OF CIVIL DEFENSE VOLUNTEERS 

In my opinion, we are just in the beginnings of a civil defense program that can render effective assistance to a stricken population. 

Because a great deal of the effort of organization of our city and county civil defense effort rests on the organization and training of volunteers, it is imperative that those volunteers not be led into feelings of security and safety that in fact do not exist. 

The planning, organization, and training of an effective civil defense organization is an intricate and complicated business, fully as difficult as organizing and training a military unit without the same means or the same authorities. This requires that public opinion leaders such as yourselves insure that a proper balance of public information regarding disarmament be maintained until all means of mass destruction against our civilian population is properly and completely controlled. 

That concludes the prepared statement, Senator.

I amend it only orally to register the view here that I felt and feel very strongly that the final effort toward which you are aiming and both you and Senator Wiley have so succinctly and ably described as your objectives, must obviously have the support of any thinking citizen.

CIVIL DEFENSE AN INSURANCE PROGRAM 

However, I think that we have to think of civil defense as a kind of insurance program, and while the present efforts are to get the insane men with the matches out of the barn, we still have to continue the insurance on that property in order that we may insure as far as possible the safety of our people.

Now I may have raised a question or two in your mind or that of Senator Wiley and I would be very happy to expand on it. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to ask Senator Wiley if he has anything that he would like to ask.

EXTENT OF H-BOMB FALLOUT 

Senator WILEY. Yes. I have said already this morning that at 

Hiroshima, the A-bomb took 70,000 lives and injured 70,000 more.

It has been said that the fallout of the H-bomb which the Kremlin has and which we have would cover the whole State of Wisconsin, so this matter of civil defense in the minds of many people means making absolutely impossible, if it is humanly possible, the breakout of another war.

I am wondering if you people are informed as to the new weapons and the seriousness thereof. Take for instance what I have saidthe fallout of an H-bomb covering the State of Minnesota. What would civil defense do? What could they do under those circumstances in the event of attack, except to try to intercept the bomber? 

If war breaks out, why all hell breaks loose. The human mind cannot conceive of it. That is why we are reaching to see if we can have something like the President's plan. But have you been informed as to the character of the new weapons and so forth? 

PROTECTION AGAINST FALLOUT 

Mr. SCHON. Yes, sir, we are informed of them. We are making every effort with the cooperation and support of the Federal Civil Defense Administration, attempts not only to inform the folks in the country of the fact of fallout and its dangers to them but also of measures of protection against it, because there are measures of protection against it. It is not as hopeless as it would seem. 

That does not mean that it would not be in every way the kind of disaster you describe, Senator, but I am not of the opinion that this would be the end of our civilization. I feel that we can and will survive any attack, but it will change the total kind of society in which we live, but we can protect our people against it. 

USE OF RADIOLOGICAL SURVEY INSTRUMENTS 

We are getting instruments with which this fallout can be measured. We are starting a training program to train people against it, and that is precisely one of the things that I bring here. 

Now this is no longer the sort of thing or the sort of program where people can be mobilized overnight or by a call on the radio and suddenly know what to do. Civil defense requires training, as fully, as detailed, as complicated, as the training of troops or of sailors or of airmen. For example, in measuring fallout it requires the use of radiological survey instruments. They are electronic instruments. 

They require delicate maintenance. They require calibration. They require the ability of people to read them. They require the ability of people who have the knowledge to go into a radiological contaminated area, how much they can take themselves and remain in a situation where it is healthy.

INFORMATION ADEQUATE: MEANS OF DEFENSE INADEQUATE 

This is only one example of the kind of training that is needed. 

But we are informed as to the size of this weapon. Your example of stating that fallout could cover the State of Washington is quite accurate except that it is a little bit of an understatement, Senator. It could cover an area, a lethal area, a surface burst of a large hydrogen weapon extending perhaps from 40 to 60 miles wide to as far as 1,200 miles in length, so that we are given information upon which we can base our program, and this is true of all of the States civil defensewise and the target cities in those States, but we have not been given the means with which to combat nor prepare on the information that we know either in the Federal sense or in most States. 

Senator WILEY. May I say thank you, sir. A note was passed to me a few moments ago saying you were a dedicated man. I think you are and I think that you can serve a great purpose in keeping on in this line. Also you realize if the human mind goes berserk intelligencewise in this destructive capacity, germ warfare and everything else is part of the day. 

Mr. SCHON. That is right, sir. 

Senator WILEY. Have you also given thought to that? 

Mr. SCHON. Yes, sir.

Senator WILEY. No more questions. 

INTERRELATION OF CIVIL DEFENSE AND DISARMAMENT 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Schon, I want to say that it is a new experience for us to have a civil-defense director participate. I personally feel that this is very germane to the subject matter and it is part of the program that we ought definitely to keep in mind. 

I am convinced that many people as they hear these sessions or listen to the testimony wonder whether or not we are talking about disarmament or whether we are talking about national security. I think it ought to be quite clear that they are synonymous. Disarmament is related to national security; it is related to the balance of power, and it is related to cooperative action. As you are indicating here today during this period of uncertainty when nations are piling up new weapons, surely civil defense is every bit as important as the defense of the frontline of the armed services. 

Mr. SCHON. Milwaukee and Minneapolis are the frontline, Senator.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Industrially they are. We have had some testimony relating to this from Admiral Štrauss and from Commissioner Murray.

I have nothing else to ask you, Mr. Schon, except to thank you for your participation. We want to move along with our panel so we can get to the other witnesses. 

Thank you very much.

Mr. Sidney Goldish, the editor of the Minnesota Poll of Public 

Opinion and Minneapolis Tribune.

STATEMENT OF SIDNEY S. GOLDISH, DIRECTOR OF RESEARCH, MINNEAPOLIS STAR AND TRIBUNE, AND EDITOR OF TRIBUNE'S MINNESOTA POLL OF PUBLIC OPINION 

Mr. GOLDISH. Senator Humphrey and Senator Wiley, may I say for the record my name is Sidney Goldish, director of research, Minneapolis Star and Tribune; since 1944 the Minneapolis Tribune, our morning and Sunday newspaper, has maintained and wholly financed as a public service the Minneapolis Poll of Public Opinion. 

Since 1946 I have been editor of the Minnesota Poll and since 1947 both its editor and director. Copies of the statement, Senator Humphrey and Senator Wiley, have been prepared and turned over to the committee.

POLLING PROCEDURES EXPLAINED 

During the more than 20 years that people's views on major issues have been studied through public opinion polling procedures, the subject of disarmament frequently has been a survey topic. 

I should like to make it clear at the outset that my reference to polling procedures is to the kind of methodology—evolved through the application of principles derived from the social sciences-which involves a controlled, systematic sampling of the population under study, through personal interviews. It is the kind of procedure utilized by George Gallup and Elmo Roper and the National Opinion Research Center at the University of Chicago, and many others, to study national samples; and by political scientists and psychologists and sociologists in their investigations; and by many regional and 

State research groups, including our own Minnesota Poll. It is not a straw vote. It is a type of study which is scientific in concept and design, and fully capable of producing reliable and useful information.

USE OF POLLS BY FEDERAL AGENCIES 

The fact is that for many years various departments and agencies of our Federal Government have been relying on just such kinds of sampling surveys to obtain data for their own guidance. 

My stress on these points emerges from my belief that the members of this committee may obtain much useful background information, in considering the vastly complex topic it has under analysis, by reference to some of the studies that have been made since the middle 1930's on questions pertaining to world peace and disarmament. 

My statement today is intended only to illustrate the nature of the materials which are available to you, and to emphasize two points: 

POLLS ON DISARMAMENT AND RELATED SUBJECTS 

1. It is as true of disarmament as it is of any other public question or issue, that what the people think and say about it is a factor that cannot be disregarded.

If what the people think and say coincides with the judgments and convictions of the members of this committee, then the committee should know it and govern itself accordingly. If the people are in- 72880-56-pt. 9- -2

clined to disagree, then it would be evident that other steps-other approaches to the problem of gaining public acceptance and supportwould need to be taken, and the committee would want to know that. 

2. My second point is that disarmament necessarily poses questions on scores of related fronts; and that what the polls can show this committee about the nature of public thinking in some of these other areas may be even more helpful than would, say, a poll directly on some aspect of how disarmament might be carried out. 

It seems to me that you cannot talk of, or deliberate on, disarmament without at the same time knowing whether the people want disarmament, or consider disarmament feasible; whether the people are confident of the good will and good intentions of other nations, or cynical and suspicious; whether the people share a sense of common purpose in the destiny of America to strive ceaselessly for world peace and the means that may make it some day possible, or whether they are basically isolationist in their outlook and want only to remain aloof from international frictions and tensions and take refuge behind some imaginary kind of defense line-which might call for more, rather than less, armament. 

There are many more such questions that will occur to you, I am sure.

FINDINGS DRAWN FROM NATIONAL POLLS 

In these next few moments, I should like to present to you first some findings drawn from national polls, and then some data obtained from Minnesota poll surveys in the last year on statewide samplings of the adult population.

Two decades ago, in 1936, a Gallup Poll survey in the United States indicated that a majority of Americans favored the calling of an international conference "to limit and reduce armaments." 

About 9 years later, in the fall of 1945-shortly after the close of World War II-the Gallup Poll asked this question in a nationwide study: 

It has been suggested that the best way to preserve peace in the world is for all nations to get together and agree to do away with standing armies and military training. Do you think the United States should or should not enter into such an agreement? 

Only 1 person in 4-24 percent-answered that he felt we should participate in that kind of disarmament agreement. 

In July 1955, almost a year ago, the Gallup Poll published two reports that appeal to me as bearing specifically on the problem before you. In one instance, the question was asked by Gallup interviewers throughout the Nation: 

What do you regard as the biggest issue, or problem, facing the Government in Washington today?

Almost half of the national sample-48 percent-referred to the problem of establishing a firm and enduring world peace, and of abolishing international tensions. 

As a matter of fact, Dr. Gallup wrote, "World peace has been 

Americans' principal concern since the cessation of hostilities in Korea."

Another Gallup report last July dealt with this question: 

Do you think we are likely to get into another world war in your lifetime? 

Three-fifths of the national sample or 62 percent answered "Yes"; 

21 percent answered "No"; the remaining 17 percent were undecided or unwilling to hazard a guess.

To me, the importance of the data lies not in the answers themselves, for I do not ascribe to the public any singular powers of prophecy; but rather in the general atmosphere, the climate of opinion, which those answers define. 

If it were true today-as I suspect it might well be-that many Americans expect to experience another world war during their lifetime, then how readily would they endorse and favor recommendations for reduction of our Armed Forces, or a substantial reduction in national preparedness, or an agreement on international disarmament?

Certainly one might conclude that their uncertainties or misgivings would need to be banished before they could be expected to become disarmament proponents. 

Gallup's report last July went on to say:

A review of surveys in the country over the last 10 years shows that disillusionment over the chances for a lasting peace set in very quickly after the end of World War II ***. The change in attitude was due primarily to a change in United States thinking about Russia * * * 

We have read, in recent days, about Soviet Premier Bulganin's proposal to President Eisenhower that certain disarmament steps be undertaken, including the matching by the United States of an announced Russian plan to reduce its Armed Forces by 1,200,000 men. 

Shortly after the Geneva "summit conference" of July 1955, the 

Gallup Poll asked a representative cross section of Americans:

If all major nations agree to reduce their armaments, do you think the 

United States should agree to do so? 

About two-thirds, or 67 percent, of the sample said, “Yes”—but the report added immediately:

Many persons *** qualified their answers by saying they would favor the plan only if control and inspection of such an agreement were satisfactory.

10 80 5 100 Having moved into the area of Minnesota reactions to questions related to disarmament, I should like to refer to a report we published just a year ago, in the June 25, 1955, edition of the Minneapolis Morning Tribune-several months after the President's appointment of Mr. Harold Stassen as a special presidential assistant to advise him on disarmament problems.

The report contained the findings of one of our statewide surveys, in which we first asked this question: 

President Eisenhower has asked that a special study be made of ways by which the United States can help bring about world disarmament and preserve the peace. If you were asked by the Government for one suggestion on what we could do to persuade the countries of the world to disband their military forces and disarm-what would you suggest?

Forty-six percent of the people we interviewed were undecided; they had no suggestions to offer. Another 5 percent said, "Nothing can be done." And an additional 4 percent said, "I don't believe we should disarm."

That accounted for 55 percent of the sample. From the remaining 

45 percent came suggestions of these kinds : 10 percent urged a religious course, like "follow and teach Biblical principles" or "spread Christianity throughout the world"; and 8 percent proposed education to "promote understanding" and "work out better communication among countries."

Five percent wanted the United States to set an example for the rest of the world-by halting selective service, or stopping the production of atomic and hydrogen bombs, or disarming ourselves. 

Four percent favored international conferences on disarmament; 

3 percent advocated that we work through the United Nations to achieve the goal; 3 percent suggested that we "make ourselves so strong that other countries won't be willing to start a war"; and 14 percent offered an assortment of ideas, like "improving the world's economic situation" or "promoting world trade" or "taking the profit out of war."

Rather than 45 percent, these answers totaled 47 percent, because some respondents offered more than a single suggestion. 

Another question we reported in the same June 25, 1955, article was this:

How would you rate the chances that there will be world disarmament during your lifetime; would you say the chances are very good, fairly good, fairly poor, or very poor?

The answers divided in this way:

Chances for world disarmament in my lifetime are: 

Very good or fairly good- 

31 

Fairly poor or very poor….

No opinion_-_. 

67 2 100 

This, I would emphasize, must be construed as Minnesotans' belief about what the future is likely to bring-not what they hope it will bring.

In August 1955 we took note in a Minnesota poll survey of a report, issued in London in July by a group of internationally celebrated scientists including the late Dr. Albert Einstein-warning that mankind faces extinction unless the world renounces war. Interviewers asked a sample of men and women in cities and towns, and on farms, in all parts of our State:

A group of scientists has urged the world to give up the idea of any future war. 

They say the use of hydrogen bombs in such a war might wipe out mankind. Do you think the world is ready to outlaw war, or not?

Twenty-two percent said, Yes, they believed the world was prepared to outlaw war; but 63 percent disagreed; they said the world was not ready; and the remaining 15 percent gave varied answers or reserved judgment. 

We also asked, in the same sampling:

Instead of an agreement to outlaw war, suppose all countries agreed never to use A-bombs or H-bombs in any future war. Do you think we could count on all countries living up to such an agreement or not? 

Only about 1 person in every 7 (14 percent) said he thought such an agreement would be complied with by all nations; the great majority were skeptical.

In the fall of 1955, in a different study, the Minnesota poll asked: 

Because Russia has turned friendlier and the cold war has eased, there is talk about cutting down our spending for military purposes. Do you favor or oppose a reduction in spending for our Armed Forces ? 

The replies: 

In favor of cutting United States spending for our Armed Forces_ Against reduction_ 

Other answers.. 

18 76 2 100 

POLLS REFUTE MYTH OF MINNESOTA'S ISOLATIONISM 

Lest anyone conclude from any of these Minnesota poll findings that the myth—and I would if need be explain my concept about the myth-about the dominance of isolationism in our State is influencing people's reactions, I would like to cite the findings of a study we made a year ago, at the time of the 10th anniversary of the signing of the 

United Nations Charter.

After a series of questions intended to ascertain how Minnesotans feel about the U. N., and how it fared in its first decade, we asked 

Do you think the United States should continue as a member of the U. N., or drop out? 

Nine out of ten respondents throughout Minnesota answered that the United States should remain active in the United Nations. 

Other studies in recent years have shown public approval in Minnesota of foreign aid proposals, of NATO, of point 4 assistance for the world's underdeveloped areas, and so on-a number of checkpoints which, singly and in the aggregate, constitute effective refutationas I see it of the notion that Minnesotans are, on the whole, isolationist; and that their opinions about disarmament are affected thereby. 

But I would say, on the basis of public opinion studies in Minnesota since 1944, that the men and women of this State are as wary as are people elsewhere in the United States of the Communist bloc's intentions; that the attainment of a lasting world peace is a consummation they devoutly long for but do not expect during their lifetime; that they are opposed to any major reduction in the size or strength of our Military Establishment, or in spending for the Armed Forces, in the present unsettled world situation; and that Russian amiability and peace talk have captured their interest, but not overpowered their prejudices or beliefs.

As a final illustration, let me quote the results of a study this spring, April 1956, in which we asked:

Russia has proposed a 20-year treaty of friendship and cooperation with the 

United States. Do you favor or oppose signing such a treaty with Russia?

The answers: 

In favor of signing 

Opposed to signing 

Qualified 

Undecided; no opinion_

29 49 5 17 100 It has been my thought and intention today only to call to the attention of the committee the existence of those kinds of information which may give greater depth and meaning to your evaluation of problems attendant on disarmament proposals; and to outline for you some of the kinds of data that the public opinion polls of the last 21 years make it possible to consider.

It is my hope that, in some manner, these may contribute to the committee's mission.

Senator Wiley?

FURTHER QUESTIONS ON POLLING PROCEDURES 

Senator WILEY. I want to compliment the gentleman also. I think it is very interesting. I wish he came from Wisconsin. 

I have just one question because before I was handed a package by the kingmakers in my State, under the direction of the Gallup poll people a poll was taken over there in relation to my opponent and myself, and it showed 74 percent for me and 24 percent for him, but I have never known just how they reached that result. 

You might tell us whom you interview and how you get at it, because you speak very highly of the Gallup accuracy and I am sure they are right. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I just amend that statement, Mr. Goldish, to say that my esteemed colleague wants to be sure they are right. 

Mr. GOLDISH. Senator Wiley, the question you pose is actually the subject for a day-long discussion on its own, but let me suggest that basically public opinion polling involves reaching a representative sampling randomly drawn from an adult population by a variety of methods, basically 2 or 3.

In one instance the interviewers are assigned to specific blocks in specific communities throughout the States and then are told that within those blocks they are to interview given types, specified types of persons, never a specific individual by name or by occupation, education or any other factor.

In effect, that is what is called a probability or area sampling approach to the problem of a representative cross section sample. 

Generally there is another major concept which indicates that if you know the characteristics of people throughout the State and if you will disperse your interviewing so that you reach into all segments of a State, a community or the Nation and apply your knowledge of the characteristics of people to the random selection of respondents who will fit the bill as it were, who will meet the qualifications or specifications of the total population in the application of what is in the trade called quota sampling, you also are able to establish contact with a good, a representative cross section sample of people.

From that point on the interviewers are admonished to—and do actually-follow the specifications.

They read all questions in identical sequence, in identical language. 

All elements of discretion on their part are removed in the interview situation itself.

In answer to your implied question: yes, I do have a great deal of confidence in Dr. George Gallup and the Gallup poll operation. 

Senator WILEY. Did you hear that, Senator?

Chairman HUMPHREY. I heard it. 

Mr. GOLDISH. The pioneering work of Dr. Gallup in this country has made all of this kind of public opinion research largely possible 

I feel.

Senator WILEY. It is absolutely impartial. It is scientific as far as the human mind can be scientific, I presume. 

Mr. GOLDISH. As scientific as the social science techniques that are involved have made it possible to be scientific and systematic. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Now you can relax all day. 

Senator WILEY. Senator, I am relaxed all the time.

POSITION OF THE UNITED STATES PREDICATED UPON INSPECTION 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Goldish, as I said earlier, this is a unique contribution to our hearings. I believe again this is the first time that we have had an expression of this sort that would give us an indication as scientifically as one can put it under present methodology of attitudes relating to problems of direct interest to this subcommittee. One point that I noted with considerable interest and approval is that on page 5 of your statement you said that about twothirds of the sample qualified their agreement to United States reduction of armaments by saying: "Only if control and inspection of such an agreement were satisfactory."

I think it ought to be clear in these hearings that the position of our Government, the President, Mr. Stassen, the Secretary of State, the 

Congress of the United States, the Foreign Relations Committee, and every group that I have talked to, is predicated upon the best inspection that one can devise. The major difficulty in our negotiations under the U. N. Subcommittee on Disarmament at the recent London 

Conference, was over the issue of inspection.

SOVIET ATTITUDE TOWARD AERIAL INSPECTION 

I think it should be noted that in that conference the Soviet Union for the first time yielded in terms of a ground inspection system, which was presented by the Soviet representative at London. The 

Soviet proposal was much more comprehensive and much more inclusive than it had ever been before, and I gather from what Mr. 

Stassen had to say the other day, that part of the inspection system would have met with United States approval. 

However, the key to our Government's position at the present time is aerial inspection, aerial reconnaissance, aerial photography, aerial inspection.

I happen to believe that, if the Soviet would ever agree to that, it would indicate a basic, fundamental change in the whole Soviet orientation, because it would open their country to a wide variety of observations. This is what we are driving toward, and I was somewhat pleased to note our representative at the London Conference felt that the Soviets had not slammed the door completely shut on this particular proposal.

Marshal Bulganin continues to leave that loose thread out as a possibility. I only want to add one other new factor for consideration here. The physicists and the engineers and the scientists who have testified before us have told us that unless aerial inspection can be agreed to before the guided missile or the intercontinental missile has been perfected, and the launching platforms and the sites could be conveniently camouflaged or put underground, it may not be very meaningful even if you do get it. There have been those who have testified to us that aerial inspection would be primarily helpful in that period of time when there was a movement of materials for launching sites, platforms, etc. Once they are underground, covered over and camouflaged, it would be very difficult to ascertain if they were there.

For example, we revealed in this testimony before this subcommittee that there was no known device that could detect a hidden atomic weapon. Once the weapons are created, hidden, and encased with protective screen they cannot be discovered by any known scientific 

Now we have had proposals that there ought to be a pooling of all the engineering and scientific talent of the free world and the Soviet orbit to see if we could not perfect a device which would reveal the identity of hiden nuclear and thermonuclear weapons. 

AWARENESS OF PUBLIC OPINION INVALUABLE TO SUBCOMMITTEE 

We are cognizant of these very problems of which the public has revealed its knowledge, and of course this is indicative that the public is generally ahead of those of us that are holding the hearings. 

I think this is very true. I wanted to note for the record that on page 2, at the bottom of page 2 of your statement you say "It seems to me that you cannot talk or deliberate on disarmament without at the same time knowing whether the people want disarmament or consider disarmament feasible, whether the people are confident of the good will and the good intentions of other nations." That paragraph, I think, Senator Wiley, would state the objective of this subcommittee. 

This is what we are really trying to ascertain right here, and your samplings are very reassuring to me in the sense that there is a great public awareness of the intricacies of this problem. 

The American people do not intend to be caught off guard. They are taking a very mature objective look at what is going on. I think this is a very heartening report despite the fact that someone might say that it does not seem to yield too many concrete realities at once. 

I don't think anybody is expecting immediate results. 

Senator WILEY. I want to say, Mr. Chairman, that he has made a contribution, and those are political terms nowadays, contributions. 

This is the best one I have heard so far. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. This one, may I say, is the subject of public opinion.

IS PUBLIC OPINION IN THE U. S. S. R. CHANGING? 

Senator WILEY. There is one other thought there along the line he suggested. He suggests we could have a poll taken in Wisconsin. I am very much interested in knowing that there is coming out of Russia a very significant thought and idea, just as at the time of the French Revolution and at the time of Cromwell in England there had grown up a middle class who were shopowners and little homeowners and so forth that insisted on what you call their political rights and the right to own property, and so forth. Then when the king tried to exercise his absolute power which he had had previously, his head went off. 

Now that may be one reason why the poll indicates that Khrushchev and the rest of the Soviet leaders may have sensed there is something different than there was before. Maybe that is why they have resurrected Stalin and then buried him so far that you don't know what has happened to him.

Senator WILEY. That is a hopeful sign.

Chairman HUMPHREY. The Soviet Union is producing a larger professional elite from its technical and scientific institutions and from its academic institutions, which it exports. This is part of its new methodology of diplomacy today, the export of talent in its exchange programs. It may turn out that this new elite may be a little more difficult to deal with and have some ideas of its own. Once that elite goes forward into the world, it will be very difficult to track every movement of it, and if it is openly exposed to other areas of the world, it may have an impact upon its return. 

These are hopes. I think one has a right to hope.

I do not underestimate the methodology and the power of the police state. I do not agree that the whole thing has changed. Liquidation is still a part of the political apparatus, but I like Senator Wiley's optimism on these things, because I think it is a helpful sign and I also think it is a healthy one.

STATEMENT OF DAVID R. INGLIS, ARGONNE NATIONAL LABORATORY, UNIVERSITY OF CHICAGO Mr. INGLIS. My name is David R. Inglis, 3748 Ellington Avenue, 

Western Springs, Ill. I am a scientist at the National Argonne Laboratory. I made my first acquaintance with these problems as a member of the wartime team at Los Alamos and I had a preview of the potentialities of atomic destruction at Alamogordo.

I want to turn the discussion from the very important side of public opinion to that at least as important, perhaps even more important, side as to how the ultimate decision in this business is to be made in Washington.

In starting to discuss these very serious and sensitive matters, one is tempted to go off in a lot of discussion to show that his heart is in the right place, to show that he is not given, as Senator Humphrey has intimated, to fuzzy idealism.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Not me, sir.

Mr. INGLIS. That was Senator Wiley. Senator Humphrey did say he does not just want to disarm for the sake of disarming but is interested in the national security.

I think I will skip any more of that. I have a prepared statement which I have submitted in which I have tried to approach the subject somewhat circumspectly. I think I will go a little bit more directly into a discussion. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Just do as you wish, sir.

Give us the benefit of your observations in your statement in the oral discussion. We will print your statement as if read. 

(The statement submitted by Mr. Inglis is as follows:) 

I am David P. Inglis, a senior physicist at the Argonne National Laboratory, but here giving my personal views. I was a member of the wartime team at 

Los Alamos and had a preview of the potentialities of atomic destruction at Alamogordo.

I wish to express my great gratification both for the work of this committee and the work that has ensued from the appointment of Governor Stassen as the 

President's Special Assistant for Disarmament matters. 

"ARMAMENT CONTROL" RATHER THAN DISARMAMENT ADVOCATED 

In this discussion I prefer to speak of "armament control" rather than "disarmament" because of a feeling that disarmament in the old sense, meaning getting rid of national armaments down to police levels, is technically impossible. You have had ample discussion of the fact that nuclear stockpiles, once produced and carefully hidden, cannot be detected by any practical inspection. 

Some years ago a small group of us at the Argonne Laboratory were concerned about the apparent failure of Government planners to appreciate this fact, as evidenced by the retention of the Baruch plan in negotiations long after it was for this reason obsolete. As a sort of pilot-plant operation typifying the kind of searching effort we felt should be going on behind the scenes within the Government, we privately undertook an exploratory study of the possibilities of armament control plans that it might be in the interests of our Government to espouse. After more than a year of part-time effort, we submitted what suggestions we had to the proper authorities, but felt greatly frustrated by lack of complete access to all relevant information. It was also obvious that, even with the information, a great deal more effort would be needed to make a thorough exploration and assessment of the possibilities. 

Many scientists have seen the intimate workings of research teamwork in some great project that performed apparent miracles. At the start there were a few ideas, some of them dubious. In the way were enormous obstacles, some of them appearing almost insurmountable. On the way there was tremendous devotion to the task, continual and enthusiastic exchange of ideas that sparked new ideas, patient testing. At the end came a new and revolutionary tool in the hands of man, radar, or the atomic bomb, for example. The stuff that was being molded here was technical. It would be naive to hope that just the same approach would work on political and social problems. Yet there are parts of the approach that could be used and I believe should be used more than they are. 

The most important of these parts is the continual and enthusiastic exchange of ideas between many active participants having a wide variety of skills and special talents, without any "compartmentalization of ideas." It is, of course, essential that the participants have access to all relevant information. 

NEED FOR FURTHER RESEARCH IN UNIVERSITIES 

The feeling of a need for further research in armament control problems has recurred in these hearings. At the Boston hearing, for example, during the testimony of Mr. Max Millikan, director of the MIT Center for International 

Studies, Senator Humphrey remarked, "It appears to me that every great university ought to provide this kind of basic research." It is characteristic that, when the going is hard, we all want to pass the buck. But here is one place where the buck should not be passed by Government, unless great changes can be made in the secrecy situation. I agree that university teams should be working on these problems. Universities are probably our greatest source of new ideas. Yet in this field they work at a great disadvantage, as do all of us on the

outside, for lack of complete information. The trouble is that those on the outside who might have time and inclination to think deeply and imaginatively don't have the information, and those in positions of responsibility who have the information don't seem to have the time and associations to lead them to think sufficiently deeply and imaginatively on this subject. 

ADVISORY RESEARCH GROUP PROPOSED 

The idea of an advisory research group working with the broadest possible approach in the field of armament control is a very unconventional approach to a political problem, but the challenge of the nuclear age is an unconventional challenge. It is perhaps natural that a more conventional approach should have been taken with the State Department and more recently in Governor Stassen's organization, which have carried the heavy responsibility for immediate negotiations and felt the urgency of clearing the details of short-range policy with other departments. 

In addition to performing these immediate tasks with enthusiastic initiative, the Stassen group has also set up the machinery for assessing at least one straightforward, long-range approach to the arms limitation problem. No report has been announced, but the nature of the approach investigated is suggested by Mr. Stassen's statements in this subcommittee and by the assignments of the eight task groups involved. These groups apparently operate separately as part-time committees, their key personnel being intensively occupied with other affairs, as is typical of committees and perhaps even subcommittees. 

The broad advisory research project that I propose could perhaps best be set up as another adjunct of the Stassen Office, or it could be a parallel organization reporting to the President and the National Security Council, or elsewhere high in the Government. It is even possible that the research contract method could be employed, as so widely and successfully used by the armed services, if sufficiently extensive information could be entrusted to a contract. 

It seems to me important that such a project should be purely advisory and not have responsibility for making executive decision, so that it may be allowed to roam freely in the realm of ideas, without step-by-step checking with the viewpoints of various departments. If a favorable plan could in this way be developed, it would then be the function of the project to "sell" it to the highest authorities on its merits, in one broad judgment taking into account as realistically as possible all aspects of both short-range and long-range national security. 

If the judgment should be to follow a new course, there would also be the essential job of selling it to the public on its merits, for it cannot be effective without public support. Any progress toward world stability will involve a lessening of tensions. In any modest armament control plan, one of the most urgent problems needing solution is how to keep up public interest in paying for adequate deterrence while improving the international atmosphere through limited agreement. While improving security, a false sense of complete security must somehow be avoided without fostering tensions. Plans for handling such problems as this should be included in proposals made by the research project. The lack of solution of this problem, the fear of an economy move following relaxation of tension, is probably responsible for much of the present opposition to thought about armament control within Government circles. 

APPROACH TO PROBLEM SHOULD BE COMPREHENSIVE 

The research project should have a large central working group including imaginative men of a diversity of backgrounds, and of the highest ability available. Backgrounds in natural science and technology should be included, and in statecraft and modern logistics and the humanities and psychology and other social sciences, men of scholarly bent being mixed with those of more practical experience. Such a variety of able men should work together full time in the central group because it is most important that there be mutual stimulation between those tending to emphasize different aspects of the problem if they are to have any chance of success in a flexible approach to this most difficult and often very discouraging task. Even if the chance of success seems small, the gamble should be made, for the search would not interfere with our other efforts to preserve the peace, and the expenditure involved would be tiny indeed compared to some of them.

Such a group would start by assessing all aspects of armament control plans and suggestions now available, but it is to be hoped that out of the debris new ideas would arise which would eventually make the group a sparkplug of con-

structive action within the Government. It should not be considered necessary to have attractive proposals at hand to prove that we need an intensified professional search for a way to put a lid on the arms race. 

Nevertheless, I do want to make a proposal that suggests one line of approach. 

It is not a new idea but a combination of old ideas. I believe it is a good proposal, but because of secrecy I cannot be quite sure of the assumptions that lead me to think so. It is doubtless quite easy for some Government department, against whose immediate interests it would be, to find reasons for calling it a bad plan. It is possible that a fair and informed assessment by the research group I propose would show on the basis of secret information that it is a bad proposal. But in studying its faults, they might find a suggestion for something better, and they would have time to follow it up as perhaps a small part of their search for ideas.

The proposal which I want to promote has three principal aims: (1) To discourage or prevent the appearance of other nuclear powers, which is very important for the future stability of the world; (2) to keep the technical problems of arms limitation from becoming much more complicated than they are already, and thus saving the possibility of a more effective agreement for a future decade when there may be less distrust; and (3) to make it unlikely that there will be a revolutionary technical breakthrough from some unexpected corner of scientific development that might give a sudden advantage to one nation and upset the balance of deterrence.

THREE BASIC REQUIREMENTS FOR ARMAMENT CONTROL 

This proposal is a combination of three proposals that are already familiar to you. They reinforce one another and together would have a profound and I believe favorable effect on our long-term national safety. They are: (1) A ban on the testing of nuclear weapons, monitored by an international agency depending on long-range detection techniques and localized observation posts not requiring unlimited access to national territories; (2) a ban on the testing of long-range missiles, monitored by radar, as has already been discussed in these hearings by Colonel Leghorn; and (3) internationalization of scientific research and development in those fields from which a dangerous breakthrough might seem remotely likely, but upon which we do not yet depend for deterrence, perhaps along the lines suggested by Senator Clinton Anderson. 

I may not take enough of your time to make an adequate plea for this proposal. I have written some of my reasons for favoring it in the current issue of the Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, where I am pleased to find other arguments for the same proposal by Colonel Leghorn. I can supply you with copies and hope you will read them.

Briefly, the desirability of this proposal compared with continuing in the unlimited arms race depends on the answer to this question, among others: "When the Soviets get the capability of destroying all relevant targets on our side three times, say, will we be safer with the ability to destroy all targets on their side five times, assuming we can stay that far ahead, and with several other nuclear powers in the world all prepared for instant ICBM delivery, or would we be safer with saturated destructive power merely equal to that of the Soviets, but without a lot of other nuclear powers and without their having the capabiliy of surprise represented by the ICBM?"

Nineteen hundred and fifty-six may be the last year that this particular proposal makes sense. Next year or the year after may be too late to make a proposal as reasonable as this, because long-range missiles may be too far developed by then. For this reason I would urge that this committee try to initiate direct action on this proposal now. In addition, I would urge the establishment of the advisory research project on arms limitation, to keep trying to improve on the available proposals, and to develop new ideas based on firm facts. 

Mr. INGLIS. In the statement I have made two principal proposals. 

First, that what America needs is a good lively research project in armaments limitations; and second that, without even waiting for the ideas that were developed from such a project-time is running out so fast-that we should now be trying to negotiate an agreement for stopping the development of the surprise weapons of the future, leaving us dependent upon our present techniques of deterrence.

I think we need these procedures because, as has been intimated by some of the remarks already made this morning, deterrence, while essential for our present safety, is not really safe in the long run. 

Senator WILEY. You remember what the Kaiser said about a treaty, don't you? It was just a scrap of paper. What is an agreement unless they keep faith?

Mr. INGLIS. It is often said in this connection that an agreement, especially as regards the Soviet Union, but even in general among nations, only has meaning that we can depend upon as long as it remains in the interests of all parties to abide by the agreement, and I think it very important that we should not go beyond the dictates of this precept.

The second reason is that changing our course away from our present unlimited arms race involves an enormously difficult decision, as you well know. It involves so many aspects that relate to one another and it seems likely that the correct decision cannot be made except from an enormously broad point of view taking into account various aspects of the national security, weighing one against the other, the short range against the long range. 

A third reason for going ahead on these lines I think is that a more drastic type of arms limitation, something in the lines of real disarmament in the old sense, does not seem likely in our time. 

I think it is necessary, therefore, to grasp at those possibilities of limited approach to the subject where we can make some advance in our time, and not just sit idly by reaching for the moon. 

In connection with the first of those proposals that I made, that we need a research type program, I want first to say that Governor Stassen is doing a wonderful job. In this connection I want to remark that it is really a privilege to come up here to Minnesota this morning and take part in this exercise in practical democracy. 

Here in Minnesota where there has been such forward thought along these lines, as evidenced by the fact that two of the most prominent sons of this State are the leaders in the Nation in the pursuit of this type, Governor Stassen and Senator Humphrey. It is a wonderful job that Governor Stassen is doing to the utmost of the possibilities and within, I might add, some limitations of his office. 

LIMITATIONS OF THE OFFICE OF SPECIAL ASSISTANT 

The limitations are perhaps unfortunate, for the job is enormously important. In the first place, Governor Stassen, being a realist, a man who wants to get action, has undertaken the enormous responsibility of the immediate negotiations in this subject. This immediately concentrates the attention of his small staff on the short-range view, on accomplishing what can be done in the negotiations this week and this year, and making progress in proposals that are to be made to him requires of course getting some assurance that there will not be objections from other departments of the Government, which means then that at each step and without thinking very far ahead, the opinions of all the other departments must be taken into account. Now this is just as it should be of course for the immediate negotiations. We don't want to make some bad blunders by ignoring some important national interests.

Chairman HUMPHREY. You perhaps noticed, Doctor, the study that we made on the line of command and of coordination and of cooperation relating to Governor Stassen's office and the other departments. 

When you study that chart, you will see the difficulties that you are so ably indicating here, of coming to decisions even upon minor details. 

It requires a great amount of clearance all the way up and down the line, as it should. I am not complaining about it. It just shows the intricacies again of these relationships in the Government. 

Mr. INGLIS. Yes. And the difficulty is that in each one of these interrelated departments, some opinion has to develop on this extremely difficult problem.

Now the problem is really so large and so difficult, it is very hard to decide is there or is there not some added national security to be gained from some type of control or limitations of armaments as compared with going ahead in the way that seems natural with the momentum of our historical development, with unlimited development along these lines. It is the kind of a decision that seems to require an extremely large, an all-inclusive type of investigation. It is too much to ask that so large, and so broad a view could possibly be taken by the men in any one department who are so very busy with their own affairs.

I don't mean here in any way to criticize the opinion of any department. I don't want to imply that Senator Wiley's remark about trying to avoid the kind of realism which may be only blind cynicism, has anything to do with this question. Each of the departments is most sincerely and most patriotically trying to achieve a part of the national security, but of course it has to concentrate its attentions, to keep its eyes on the ball.

FABLE CONCERNING BOMB PROJECT THAT FAILED 

Perhaps the most delicate way to handle this would be to give a little fable that I dreamed up this morning.

Back in 1942 there began to be some glimmerings of a hope that appreciable supplies of separated isotopes of uranium or of plutonium or perhaps of both could be supplied, so it became very important to ask the question: "It is possible with these new materials to make an important military weapon?"

The Nation was at war and people were very busy.
