There were technical people busy on a number of fronts and a few left in the universities trying to carry the educational ball, and it was decided that it was necessary to have a quick and thorough investigation of these matters, so a conference was called, and people came from the group of technical people that were working on the proximity fuses, they sent their representatives, and people from the radar project sent their representatives, and people working on conventional explosives, trying to get more bang out of them, came, because, of course, all of these were relevant activities.

There were other single scientists and other scholars, engineers, mathematicians that came from various places, and they were posed the problem: We have to invent an atomic bomb. Here are the facts as known.

And they went back to their places of work and continued, of course, for the period of urgency with their main line of activity. But on the

side they thought seriously, very seriously, about this business of inventing an atomic bomb. 

And various difficulties obviously came up. And a couple of years later along in early 1945 when the Germans had folded and it looked as though progress was being made against the Japanese, it was decided now is the time we really have to make this bomb if it is possible, so the opinion was gathered from all of them and the various opinions were, "No, it is too difficult," or some of them thought, "Well, maybe, but I am not quite sure," just going along these lines.

The sum total of all of these considered opinions by very busy men was that the thing probably could not be done. There was great doubt, and in particular one man had gone back to his nuclear physics laboratory and measured some cross sections of contaminants that were inevitably to be found in these materials, and some of them were found to have enormously great neutron absorption cross sections, and this was the final straw that showed it could not be done. 

Well, nobody was too greatly disappointed about this because, well, the Japs by this time were beginning to fold and by the end of 1945 the war was over anyway and that was the end of the matter. 

I hope you see the point of this fable. People are so busy. I am sure that you Senators in Washington are aware of the tremendous pressure that is on people in Washington to get the immediate job done.

FREEING RESEARCH GROUP FROM PRESSURES 

Well, this pressure comes also upon Mr. Stassen and his small staff, and it is natural with the rather small resources that are available to him, that he has to depend on all of the other departments for advice and even for the supply of an assessment of technical information or at least the supply of the people who are going to do the assessing for him through his task groups, so it is inevitable that the somewhat discouraged departmental points of view will be operative here. 

It seems to me that what is needed instead is a larger endeavor in which more people are brought together, people of a variety of abilities and of backgrounds, very able people and very imaginative people, but a group that is large enough that it can do its own assessing of the facts according to its own views as these views develop in pursuing the problem of arms limitation and pursuing that as a single purpose. 

Now I don't mean to say that these facts that have led to the departmental point of view should be ignored, not at all. They are important to the national security. I only mean they should be judged in the larger context of the whole problem. 

I think it is important that such a group should be in a way independent, not have immediate pressures on it, be independent, for example, of the problem of the immediate negotiations. 

It should perhaps be another branch of Mr. Stassen's office, a large branch. If such a group should, with thorough investigation, come up with the idea that it is in the national interest to go ahead in these matters, then it would not ignore the bases of the departmental points of view at all. Instead, this being an advisory group, would make its recommendations fully documented with all the reasons. This recommendation would be to the highest authorities in the Government, I would think appropriately to the President and his Cabinet, and 72880-56-pt. 9—3

presumably the final judgment would be made there in the Cabinet where the departmental points of view are represented, where there are people who look out for the interests of all of these special jobs that have to be done by the various departments, but where these interests would be weighed in relation to the job as a whole, in the proper perspective, so that a really mature and overall and valid judgment could be reached. 

So I feel that the Stassen office, and Mr. Stassen personally, needs all the encouragement that can be given along these lines. It seems to me that you and your colleagues in the Senate are in a position to give him some of the encouragement he needs. I have no doubt you have already, through the very mechanism of these hearings. 

But even further to encourage him to expand, increase his influence in every way possible. I even suggest, perhaps it seems naive from an outsider in the Government who is not quite aware of all of the niceties of protocol, that you in the legislative branch through your control of the purse strings might somehow urge on Mr. Stassen some larger appropriations that are for his use, earmarked for his use alone to be used as he sees fit, so that he could build up this type of staff. You might want to sound him out first, but I cannot help but feel that he might respond very favorably to this type of encouragement. 

POLITICAL-TECHNICAL PROBLEMS FACED BY STASSEN GROUP Chairman HUMPHREY. Dr. Inglis, this is a very provocative, and I 

think constructive, proposal. With my limited information I can say that while it is true that Mr. Stassen has a working arrangement with all the departments, he has a directive from the President that should and does give him certain priority on work with these departments; 

I think the fact is that in most of the departments through which he must clear, the man in department B or in department C, for instance, with whom Governor Stassen is to clear and to work, is not always a man of high position in that particular department. 

Mr. INGLIS. Yes.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I am not being critical. We are just talking about this. It is true that ultimately, of course, it is the Secretary of the Department of Defense or the Secretary of the Department of Air, Army, or Navy, or the Secretary of State who takes the responsibility for clearance and for policy decisions with Governor Stassen's office.

From my own limited observation, the study made by our staff and the subcommittee, we find that all too often the man that is in direct line contact with Stassen's office may be, for example, a colonel in the 

Department of Defense, not an Assistant Secretary, bureau chief, or some one that has been given the kind of administrative and legislative stature that cannot be bypassed or ignored. 

REASONS FOR SUCCESS OF BOMB PROJECT 

I doubt the significance of your fable. In other words, if we had proceeded according to the fable we would never have had the atomic bomb, because everybody was too busy and everybody could figure out reasons why it would not work. We did not operate that way. 

We operated on the basis of assigned programs, concentration of effort,

and we were able to produce, and that is what you are asking for in analysis here; is that right?

Mr. INGLIS. Yes, that concentration of effort, it was a thrill to be a part of it, to see the way mutual stimulation of people with different backgrounds and different talents worked so effectively. One man was worried about one part of it, the other man worried about another part of it, but they shared their problems together, and through sharing the problems together and sharing enthusiasms and modifying the demands of one side to fit the demands of another as in this problem modifying the technical demands to fit the political demands might be necessary.

A sort of flexible working together was the most important part I felt of the success in that type of approach, and this is a thing which I feel should be carried over and is not carried over into this combined political-technical problem that Mr. Stassen faces. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I just want to say I think your observation is valid because as of the beginning of this year our Government had not resolved its differences in the Department which are legitimate differences as to policy on armaments reduction and control. 

I know it had not. When I said, for example, that the testimony of the Secretary of State in February before our subcommittee was of vital importance, it was because some of these problems were resolved almost on the spot.

Now everybody is busy and you are so right. We are so busy that we don't have time to even keep up with last year's reading much less this year's. It is a difference between applied research and basic research.

Governor Stassen, before he went to London, came before our committee at 3 in the afternoon; it was a hastily called conference. He had just then received the clearance to come before the committee and present the anticipated proposals of our Government before the London Conference.

Now this is not being critical of him. It is merely criticism of the process and the procedure to which you are directing your remarks. 

LIMITED APPROACH TO DISARMAMENT RECOMMENDED 

Mr. INGLIS. Yes. The time is running short. I want to skip over the reasons for my suggestion that we should go ahead with a limited approach to disarmament, or rather to arms limitation by only limiting the rate of development through a ban, of course multilateral, on testing of nuclear weapons, on testing, as Colonel Leghorn suggested, of long-range missiles, and an internationalization of the brave newworld type of technical developments that Senator Anderson has suggested.

I think there are very good reasons for doing this, but perhaps I had better just say I have written some of those reasons in the current issue of the Bulletin of Atomic Scientists, and I would like to supply you with copies in the hopes that you may find time to read them in spite of the world being so busy.

Chairman HUMPHREY. We would like to have the whole article, by the way, printed as a part of your testimony in the record, and if you would be kind enough to supply individual copies we would appreciate it.

(The article referred to is as follows:) NATIONAL SECURITY WITH THE ARMS RACE LIMITED By David Inglis 

1 

Our awakening

National security has ceased to exist in any absolute sense. tomorrow to participate in the life of a happy and healthy nation is subject to the whim of a foreign totalitarian regime—a regime armed with H-bombs and presumably with the means to deliver them almost unhindered. That whim is fortunately kept in check by our ability to retaliate, giving us a perhaps illusory feeling of confidence that we will work and enjoy many tomorrows. But national security has been reduced to a question of probability instead of certainty that we will survive any year without being blown to bits. 

The scale of the potential disaster is so vast that our foremost national concern should be to minimize the probability of devastating war while preserving the essential freedoms we cherish. It has often been said that the new technical realities require an entirely new approach to statecraft, but the world has remained insufficiently influenced by these new dimensions of destruction. Political evolution does not seem to have been hastened by the hot breath of technical advance.

Though there has been no development of new forms for maintaining peace, there has been an encouraging toughening of the diplomatic skin. This manifests itself in a new restraint in reacting to border incidents, such as the shooting down of a plane, that formerly might have been considered a casus belli, and leads to a hope that the stalemate may keep its precarious stability for a long time, perhaps much longer than the time since the Korean war. But toleration of minor offenses can go only so far before it encourages larger adventures. The danger of small wars can hardly be overlooked in view of the military buildup taking place in north Korea and elsewhere. And if a small war should start, there is always a probability, at least, that it may grow into the dreaded big one. 

While the mutual deterrence of stalemate has an apparent stability that seems gratifying for the short term, it must not be allowed to leave us complacent over the prospects for the long-term survival of civilization. The existence of some probability of failure in any one year adds up to an enormous danger in the course of many decades.

Such concern for the future-for our own old age and the lifetimes of our children--is normally shrugged off with the thought that things will be so different in the future that some way will then be found to stabilize the world. The tragedy is that through our astounding and unabated technical developments in this generation, we are making it utterly impossible for future generations to bring the ever new terror under control unless they can accomplish political utopias far beyond our dreams, while we in this generation remain apparently unwilling to take the slightest step toward an international political revolution. 

Such a blanket indictment of our generation is, of course, unfair unless we point out that some nations have seemed more reluctant than others to discuss the possibilities of progress in good faith. But the fact remains that there has been no progress toward disarmament or arms limitation. There has been no enthusiasm for seeking substantial agreements since 1946, and the recent London talks ended, as did their predecessors, in discouraging failure. 

The problem of any international armament limitation or control is already so very difficult from the technical point of view that tenable objectives in this direction are severely circumscribed. It is generally regarded as impossible to detect nuclear weapons once they have been produced and hidden. The fact that many already exist makes an agreement to retire them from national weapons stockpiles unenforcible.

There are benevolent organizations, here and abroad, promoting universal, enforced, and complete disarmament of all weapons and all nations down to the level of police forces. It must now be recognized that this could be achieved only under the protection of a worldwide, supranational government armed with nuclear weapons-numerous and mobile enough to cover all possibilities of secreted national arsenals. In the present sad state of international distrust, there is only a faint hope of achieving such a step in political evolution. It is, therefore, necessary in the present world climate to examine less drastic palliatives. Yet, from the premise of the enormous danger which hangs on the slender 

1 Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists (Chicago) vol. 12, No. 6, June 1956, pp. 195-201.

threat of mutual deterrence, it must be admitted that the logical conclusion is the search for supranational government. Statesmen continue to do lipservice to hopes for a happier future in which the arms race may be brought to an end, either through agreement between sovereign nations or through peaceful establishment of a world police power. Yet the prospect is that neither course will be technically possible if weapons continue to be developed at their present alarming rate.

The difficulties of establishing confidence in a supranational government, permitting acceptance of its authority as a substitute for the sovereign right to wage war, now seem formidable indeed. Even in a fancied future time of easier trust it is hard to imagine that the transition to such a government could be satisfactorily arranged if nations were equipped with the concealed apparatus of "push-button warfare”—the capability of destroying any other nation at will. If, on the other hand, it should, in that friendlier time, still be necessary to mount the relatively cumbersome offensive of present weapons to destroy another country, the establishment of the authority of a world army would remain entirely possible. While our present H-bombs have a ghastly power and cannot reliably be ferreted out, the planes or submarines that must carry them could be controlled, leaving no question of the supremacy of the world armed force. 

The alternative way to end the arms race is agreement between sovereign states to limit armaments and eventually reduce their scale to safer levels, guaranteed by mutual inspection and coupled with various political and economic arrangements to make aggression unattractive, if not impossible. But again it does not seem that any such agreement can ever be made once the “pushbutton" stage is reached, with the ICBM's poised in inconspicuous holes in the ground beyond the reach of inspectors.

PRESERVE THE POSSIBILITY OF AGREEMENT 

Here, then, is a very real and tangible incentive for trying to limit the development of weapons of mass destruction (including their delivery vehicles): i. e., to save the technical possibility of an agreement ending the arms race for a later time when broad agreement may be politically feasible. We speak of uniform limitation all over the world, not of unilateral limitation by any one power.

This is a real incentive, but perhaps it is not tangible enough, immediate enough, to compete with the frantic needs of the arms race for our immediate competitive national security. There is another, more impelling and less virtuous, incentive.

PREVENT THE ARMS RACE FROM BECOMING MANY-SIDED 

The deterrence of stalemated armaments is a logical deterrent. So long as each side acts sensibly, we should be safe from the scourge of modern war. The danger is that someone will make a mistake. The United States and the 

U. S. S. R. are living on a certain respect for one another's sanity. Each feels some confidence that, with somewhat restrained diplomacy and the threat of obliterating retaliation, a surprise blow can be avoided. .

It is a diplomat's nightmare to think of trying to keep a stable world with dozen's of nuclear powers. With so many sources of the spark to touch off the holocaust, with so many statesmen in a position to make the final error, it would be a miracle indeed if a decade could pass without disaster. Yet this ugly day of many nuclear powers will surely come if we continue to follow the conventional course of unlimited rivalry between sovereign nations. 

Here, then, is the common motivation to an agreement that could increase the national security of both great powers-an agreement to prevent, or effectively retard, the rise of other nuclear powers without upsetting the balance called stalemate. It is often said that we can only trust international agreements, particularly with the U. S. S. R., to be honored so long as they remain in the selfish interests of the contracting powers. This, then, is the basis for an effective agreement.

NUCLEAR TEST BAN 

A worldwide nuclear test ban agreement is the simplest possible step of guaranteed arms limitation and would prevent the rise of other nuclear powers, or at least minimize their potential effectiveness. It is the simplest step because it requires only a minimum deviation from conventional diplomatic and military attitudes, upon which our present partial security is based. It leaves us with

our present nuclear weapons and the freedom to build more of them to keep the stalemate effective. It merely interferes with the rate of development of new weapons, treating the great powers equally so that neither can gain a decisive advantage. The step is simple also because it does not require the admission of inspectors with free access throughout the various countries. 

The step is guaranteed against significant evasion because nuclear tests can be detected from afar. It is necessary to consider, at greater length than we shall here, the possibilities of special evasions, the limits of small air bursts that might not be detected by monitoring atmospheric radioactivity, the dependability of seismological detection of deep underground tests, and so forth. It seems very likely that a complete study would show that technically possible evasions would be of a minor nature and would not upset the stalemate. If it should, nevertheless, be deemed necessary, special provisions could be made to cover this difficulty which would only slightly complicate the otherwise simple scheme, such as admission of inspectors to seismic observatories at a few agreed spots in large countries. By and large, then, an adequate guaranty against evasion would be provided by an international monitoring agency whose primary tool would be airplane observation of atmospheric radioactivity and ground observation of fallout at various places throughout the world. Such an agency could very appropriately be set up under the United Nations. Its sole task would be to report any evasions, so that observing the ban would be in the self-interest of each nation.

The primary motive for the present H-bomb powers to enter into such a ban agreement would presumably be to prevent other powers from developing nuclear weapons. This motivation is primary because it is most immediate for the national interest. It is, perhaps, not the deepest motivation, nor the one that sounds best to the small countries. Yet, it is in the national interest of each small country to be kept from developing weapons so long as its immediate rivals are also prevented from doing so. No smaller nation can hope by its own nuclear arms to keep war from its borders or to maintain the world at peace. Its chances are better with our present arrangement in which two great powers keep each other in check. (England is a third nuclear power, so closely alined with the United States as not greatly to increase the chances of trouble.) 

A second major motive for a nuclear test ban is to try to preserve the technical possibility of more far-reaching agreement in the future. The nuclear test ban alone is not ideally suited for this purpose, but it would help a great deal. 

With this ban alone, development of ICBM's would continue apace. We cannot know just how well developed the warheads are that they would carry. The most authoritative hint that this development is still far from complete came recently from President Eisenhower. Replying on April 25 to Adlai Stevenson's inconsistent suggestion that we stop H-bomb tests but continue with ICBM development, the President said that, "It would be futile for the United States to continue at 'top speed' on the ballistic missile if it were to stop testing the hydrogen bomb that would arm the projectile." He went on to say that "one weapon would be 'useless' without the other." It appears from these remarks that the ultimate destructiveness of the H-bomb has not been streamlined to fit into the severely limited payload of prospective multistage long-range missiles, and that a program of future nuclear tests is necessary to accomplish this development. Insofar as this is true, the nuclear test ban could indeed serve to prevent the transition to the next stage of technical difficulty in the control of weapons.

India has repeatedly suggested a nuclear test ban in the United Nations, and others have joined in the plea. The main motivation was to get rid of the dangers of radioactive fallout. These dangers seem very real indeed, particularly to the unfortunate Japanese. Yet the number of people likely to be severely affected by the continuing program of testing is so small compared to the expected loss of human life in nuclear war that the motivations concerned with avoidance of war remain the more powerful incentive to ban tests. That the unpleasantness associated with the tests themselves would also be eliminated is an added gain, but a small one.

Banning the tests of large nuclear weapons has been urged also with a view to correcting the imbalance between defense and offense, slowing down the development of offense and giving defense more of a chance to catch up, thus reducing the temptation to sudden aggression. This now dubious motive was based on the notion current a year or two ago that defense might be developed to such a degree of perfection that it could play a significant role in discouraging attack. Though we still have conflicting reports and no single voice of authority

on this subject, the most competent opinion seems to be that defensive interception is not able, and even with unlimited development will probably never be able, to interfere seriously with the effectiveness of strategic attack and retaliation. This being so, we do not need to worry unduly about the fact that a ban of all nuclear testing limits the development of defense as well as offense. 

This dim view of defensive interceptors does not imply that in the throes of the present unlimited arms race we should not try as hard as we can to develop them for what they may be worth. Of course we should, for our defensive capabilities compared with those of the U. S. S. R. will be a factor in determining our relative strength even if interception capabilities never get beyond the range of 5 to 30 percent. In all discussion of arms limitations, we must take care to distinguish between unilateral renouncement, which might be motivated by economy and lead to a loss of relative strength, and renouncement by agreement, in which our loss is compensated by the same loss on the other side. 

BANNING MISSILE TESTS 

A more effective way to prevent the modern Armageddon from growing beyond the technical possibility of control would be to ban the testing of long-range missiles. If instituted soon, this ban would effectively forestall their being brought into existence as usable weapons. The missile test ban has been vigorously proposed by Col. Richard S. Leghorn before the Disarmament Sub committee of the Senate Committee on Foreign Relations, March 7, 1956. He pointed out that such a missile test ban could be monitored by a network of international radar picket posts set up at agreed points in national territories. 

This requires more relinquishment of national sovereignty in the traditional sense than does the nuclear test ban which can be monitored from outside. If this point looms large in the minds of statesmen, the missile ban could be considered as a separate and even secondary proposal. Yet the admission of international inspectors to relatively few agreed locations within a country is a very small concession compared to granting free access as would be required by more ambitious arms limitation plans. It seems unlikely that this small concession to inspectors at isolated spots would loom nearly as important as deeper considerations of national safety.

COMBINED NUCLEAR AND MISSILE TEST BAN 

The most convincing case can be made for the combined nuclear and missile test ban. Such an agreement between nations of the world would accomplish the objectives of both of the major motivations we have discussed, besides a host of minor ones presumably accompanying a reduction of tension engendered by the actual working of a very significant agreement. It would put a lid on the most threatening branches of weapons development. It would tend to leave long-range weapons technology about where it is today. Nations would be expected to work on development within the limitations of the agreement, but would find that they could not go far in these directions without further tests. More H-bombs could be built by the big powers, but not the most threatening vehicles to deliver them, for neither side yet knows how to make these missiles. Yet we could continue to rely on deterrence because we already have sufficient techniques. Save perhaps for quantity of bombers, we have essentially reached stalemate already. 

Complications arise in the rumors that the U. S. S. R. may be nearly ready to test an intermediate-range ballistic missile (IRBM) which, when fully developed, will be a direct threat to our European friends, as Senator Symington pointed out during the recent disarmament hearings. It is necessary, therefore, to convince our European friends that they are protected by our deterrence, and to realize that it is a long way from an initial test to a completely developed and sufficiently accurate weapon. It would be highly desirable, if possible, to introduce the combined test ban so soon as to prevent the development of the intermediate-range ballistic missile (IRBM) into an accurate, operational weapon with a highly efficient warhead. Insistence on this point might, however, lead to lengthy wrangling over the probable relative advancement of the two competitors in this field and stand in the way of any agreement. If it is not possible to keep such strategic missiles of shorter ranges from national arsenals, it would still be very desirable to stop the development of the ICBM by means of a test ban. A ban on one without a ban on the other would help future world stability (which is also vital to our European friends) because a nation plotting to go its own way by sudden aggression will have to be prepared

to take on the whole world at a stroke, not just its near neighbors, if the future stabilizing force of the world (be it the United States or a world armed force) remains alert, as it must.

These considerations make it urgent that the combined test ban agreement be made an objective of national policy very soon. 

SECURITY WITH AND WITHOUT A LID ON DEVELOPMENT 

A short discussion like this of so weighty a matter, carried out without access to secret information, is always in danger of oversimplification. These are vital balances of advantage and disadvantage which need to be considered in detail and without bias. The fundamental question is whether we are safer with an agreement to limit the development of armaments or with the arms race wide open.

Weapons development, while largely engineering, involves science as its nursemaid and its source of new ideas, and science has a long tradition of unlimited development. It is heresy for a scientist to suggest that limits should be placed on a field of scientific investigation. Fundamental knowledge is good-understanding, an end in itself. One can never tell beforehand from what alley of idle curiosity the next fundamental advance will come.

It is tempting by simple analogy to extend these principles to weapons development. The analogy may be used to make a test ban sound morally reprehensible, like thought control or the burning of the books. More practically, the danger is that confidence may be developed in control, only to have a new horror weapon spring up where least expected, perhaps suddenly to dominate the field. It must be admitted, for example, that our proposal puts no lid on the development of bacteriological warfare. If bacteriological developments are going to provide an ultimate weapon, they will provide it if we ban tests and do no more. If there is to be any deterrence it won't make much difference whether there has been a test ban or not. It seems likely that deterrence carried by bombers will be as effective as deterrence carried by ballistic missiles. 

Senator Anderson has suggested that research and development in new and demanding fields be promoted by establishing large international projects. He suggests weather control, controlled thermonuclear power, and space travel as examples. Many others, including bacteriological development and immunology, could be added. This plan would make a valuable addition to the combined nuclear and missile test ban. It would assure that all the cooperating nations would learn of any revolutionary technical developments at the same time. This would remove or greatly reduce one of the very real dangers of our present unrelenting national competition, the danger that one nation might suddenly find itself the sole possessor of a new principle with such important military applications as to make that nation feel immune from retaliation and tempted to aggression.

Unlike fundamental science, it is possible in weapons technology to delineate certain fields of development in which the important contributions in the fairly near future, at least, are expected to be of a preponderantly destructive nature. 

In banning nuclear bomb tests we might possibly prevent the discovery of a secret of nature essential to the development of controlled thermonuclear power. In any rational analysis it is well worth taking a small chance of such a loss if we thereby appreciably increase the likelihood that civilization may survive another century. If the missile test ban should demand it, we might likewise rationally decide to forego interplanetary travel for a few more generations for the sake of keeping this beautiful planet habitable. 

With the present unlimited arms race, our feeling of security lies in our confidence that we can run a good race. Through a combination of scientific and technical ability, devotion, the effectiveness of our free institutions, a head start, and good luck, we have had the experience of being definitely ahead. By trying our best, perhaps we can remain so indefinitely, but we cannot be sure. If an essential advance occurs first on our side, we feel that it is safe in our hands, for we will not attack. But one may occur on the other side and put us in danger. Even with development unlimited-from the point of view of cold technological competition alone, quite apart from mistakes of statesmen-we are not entirely safe.

Our present aim is to remain ahead because we feel that it gives us an added margin of safety. It is not clear that there is as much short-term safety in remaining merely equal to our rivals in retaliatory power. As we approach saturation we inevitably approach effective equality, that is the symmetry of stalemate. The main difficulty is that we do not consider the two sides as equal in

willingness to launch surprise attack. If an attack might be launched against us, we feel that it is worth while for us to have twice as much force as necessary to destroy all targets, because the attackers may destroy half of our retaliatory force before we can strike back (or even more if their force is equal to ours). 

If we cannot keep far ahead in numbers of operational aircraft, we hope to do so in refinement of weaponry. Herein lies the partial security of technological dynamism that is the basis of our present policy in this thinking we make the cautious assumption that for deterrence to be effective it is necessary for us to be able to inflict as much damage on them after being surprised as they can inflict on us in a surprise attack. This is effective deterrence. 

This assumption is, however, not a part of the simple concept of mutual deterrence. The concept arises from the fact that nuclear weapons are so devastating that even a small part (say a quarter) of the destructive power of one of the rivals if delivered to the other would cause damage and human suffering so intolerable as to be unacceptable in any rational decision. It assumes that neither side can wipe out almost all of the other's retaliatory force in a simultaneous blow everywhere. Such deterrence is in principle effective between equals, even if one of them is looking for a chance to overwhelm the other. The only flaw is, as we have seen, that the congenitally aggressive side might achieve a technical breakthrough that would make it feel able to overwhelm the retaliatory force. Such a breakthrough would have to be a radical one, indeed, and in this case it is very doubtful whether having twice as powerful an established retaliatory force would be decisive in discouraging attack. There is thus an essential symmetry and rugged stability to the idea of mutual deterrence, a tolerance of slight inequalities, and it does not seem that much is added to our immediate security by our attempt to beat the symmetry and stay far ahead. 

If we should decide to buy long-term national security by entering a test ban agreement, we would have to give up some of the competition in which we intend to stay ahead. Insofar as we are at present ahead, in entering a test ban agreement, we could expect to remain ahead for some years, but eventually to approach mere equality and to be dependent on the deterrence of approximate equality.

If we continue with the arms race unlimited, we will probably maintain our lead in refinement of nuclear weapons (on the assumption that we have it) for a decade or so-as far as one can hope to foresee. Within this time the transition will probably be made to operational ICBM's and we can feel no assurance that we will be the first to have them. This transition must be viewed as a danger period. Probably in the decade after that, and perhaps sooner, we should expect several other nuclear powers to enter the field. Then the future diplomacy of our present course begins to be really dangerous. This might engender strong pressures to control armaments, but it will be too late. 

If, instead, we should succeed in negotiating a combined test ban agreement, we could presumably expect to continue with our lead in refinement of nuclear weapons and especially with our preponderant numbers, approaching equality toward the end of the decade. This approach to equality in itself would not constitute any real danger. The transition to intercontinental ballistic missiles would not take place, so the danger inherent in this transition would be avoided. 

The rise of other nuclear powers and the danger from a multilateral diplomacy getting out of hand would also be avoided. If internationalization of bacteriological and other developments is included in the agreement, the danger of unexpected surprises of this sort would be greatly reduced. If it should later become desirable and politically feasible to bring weapons under detailed international control, this would be technically possible with a practical amount of inspection because there would be no hidden intercontinental ballistic missiles to worry about.

The balance sheet is a complicated one to draw, and includes items hidden behind the veil of secrecy. However, this simplified look at today's balance book indicates far greater national security with the test ban than without-it would be hard to believe that the truth is otherwise. 

DETECTION OF HIDDEN NUCLEAR WEAPONS 

Mr. INGLIS. One further remark which I can't help feeling is not quite so far outside of my capacity as a scientist, that is it seems to me most important that we judge properly whether or not there will be progress in the type of thing to which Senator Humphrey just al-

luded, the possibility of a technical breakthrough to make possible the detection of hidden nuclear weapons.

When asked whether some future development in science is possible, it is always necessary to be very circumspect. One cannot say bluntly a development is impossible. Someone makes a qualified answer, and such answers have been made in this committee in the previous hearings of this committee. It seems to me that the qualifications are apt, to a layman, to carry too much weight. I would just like to say that it is hard to judge these matters, but it seems to me-rather than allowing ourselves to be optimistic about this and therefore endeavoring serious thoughts about disarmament until somebody make a new invention, we should realize how hopeless it is.

My statement is that there is about one chance in a hundred that in the next 50 years an invention, a gadget will be invented which is capable of detecting a hidden atomic bomb, a nuclear weapon at a range of more than 10 yards. That if a gadget inside this limitation is invented, it is a little bit better than the proverbial screwdriver but not much, not nearly good enough to warrant going ahead with complete guaranteed disarmament down to the level of police force or anything of that sort. So that we should not stand by hoping for improvement in this line and neglecting the smaller steps toward arms limitation which seem possible and are within our reach in our time. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Are you emphasizing, Doctor, the impor tance of adequate inspection of any type of armament control and reduction?

LOW LEVEL INSPECTION NECESSARY 

Mr. INGLIS. Of course, from the background of the discussions in this committee I take it for granted that we are interested only in a really significant reduction of armaments down to levels which might be less burdensome and less of a threat of surprise attack, when making sacrifices because we expect that the Soviet Union is making sacrifices.

We can do this only if we have a guaranty that they are making the sacrifice, too, and this means if we go down to a low level, it means only if we can really inspect. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I do feel that this is terribly important and to keep the record clear on this, may I say that your proposals have been supported by suggestion of Senator Flanders and Colonel Leghorn before our committee, and by some of the scientists we have heard from at MIT and Harvard.

Mr. INGLIS. I have been interested to read their statements. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. As you will recall, that is excellent testimony.

Senator Wiley. 

Senator WILEY. No questions. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Dr. Inglis, we want to thank you very, very much. May I say it is almost a shame not to spend enough time with you to probe that very brilliant and learned mind of yours on these subjects, but we have so many witnesses I think we will have to proceed.

The next witness is Ralph G. Jones of the science department, University of Minnesota.

STATEMENT OF RALPH G. JONES, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I hope our other witnesses understand this panel has been given the privilege of more extensive reporting and observation because prior to the hearing they were asked to concentrate their attention upon the subject matter. 

Mr. JONES. Senator Humphrey, Senator Wiley, I am a visiting associate professor of political science at the University of Minnesota this year and I want to say how much I as an individual appreciate your coming out here not only because you want our ideas but because it makes us search our own minds and come to conclusions of our own.

DISILLUSIONMENT CONCERNING DISARMAMENT 

As I have listened to this discussion and as I have thought of the problem of disarmament in preparation for this meeting, I began with an attitude of disillusionment. 

As a matter of fact, I discussed the problem with my friends, with my students, with almost anyone that was willing to talk about the subject, and I will be quite frank that at the point of impact, the initial discussion, I met almost, shall we say, a solid wall of disillusionment.

And yet I think there was a change in the attitude of myself certainly and some other people for reasons which I will dicuss in just a moment.

TENSIONS IN THE 20TH CENTURY 

But first of all, if you will let me, I will give just a little bit about some of the reasons for disillusionment with respect to disarmament. 

I think that most of us know as Americans that we and the free world are engaged in a tension which is total in this 20th century. 

I think we know that there are very definitely at least two views of life, two attitudes as to the nature and the dignity and the purpose of man, and I think that we know that we are determined that our particular view will be implemented by any resource at our disposal. 

And I think we know, of course, that the same thing is true on the other side with respect to the Soviet Union. 

And so, therefore, it is highly unlikely that in a global struggle for the minds of men, which is total and which has such tremendous possibilities for, shall we say, victory or defeat, that neither side is going to deny itself the ultimate weapons, if it does come to that decision, because we know that so many of the decisions in our history of the development of freemen have come on the field of battle. 

The decision at Yorktown, for example, the decision at Appomattox, if you will, the decision which came in the first war at the forces of Compiegne when the Germans surrendered to Marshal Foch, the opportunity which came to the little schoolhouse in France or at Tokyo: those decisions were military, and so let us search for a moment to see if we can discover what those decisions gave us. They did give us something positive. They gave us an opportunity to work toward the perpetuation of our view of life, our scheme of government, our system of operation. That opportunity is something that war gives to us if it does come to that ultimate possibility. It gives us nothing less. But may I emphasize, Senators, that it gives us nothing more.

And this is the positive element which I would like to talk about with respect to disarmament today, if I may.

WEAPONS IN THE WAR OF IDEAS 

I think that there are more weapons than the military. I think that there are other instruments and other ways in which you can win the loyalty of men. The positive elements, so far as I see, have been neglected in the Western World in the last 5, the last 10, the last 20 years, perhaps throughout our history, because we had an assurance in earlier days that the tide of democracy was moving forward throughout the world. All that we had to do was to remove the obstructions, the roadblocks, give men a chance to make the decision for themselves, and we had complete confidence that the decision would be in the democratic fashion. 

We have seen in the 20th century, on the other hand, that there are mass movements which are not democratic in character, which are not democratic in purpose and do not pursue democratic ends. 

In that sense of the word, it seems to me, that we have to do something more than just remove obstructions to the operation of men and let them make the decision. The decision has not always been positive, has not always been democratic in this, the 20th century. That is the tragedy of our time, I think.

And in that sense of the word, it seems to me that perhaps we do need a little positive thinking with respect to the contest for the minds and the loyalties of men. We never needed it in the 19th century. 

The sympathies were with us. I think that the American knew that his country was the hope and the promise and the example to the world. 

I think that Governor Freeman said, with a sense of tragedy, I think, that this is perhaps not true with respect to the entire world in the 

20th century.

Perhaps we are losing the battle of ideas, and perhaps we are losing it because here we are emphasizing it too much, the military armament. I don't want to underestimate military armament one bit. But 

I would point this out, that it is a matter of degree. You need the armament which is necessary to defeat the armament which the enemy can place before you.

In that sense it is relative, and I think that we can say that if we do develop in the world an attitude of trust and cooperation and agreement, something which has to be developed in this 20th century, the degree to which we push that forward will make it possible for us to reduce armaments in the military sense of the word. The two go hand in hand.

INTERNATIONAL INSPECTION AND NATIONAL SOVEREIGNTY 

And may I suggest this as a last possibility as an international lawyer. We have to be very careful in our propaganda, if we want to call it propaganda, in this connection, because in a very peculiar sense of the word, the Soviet Union can say that technically, tongue in cheek to be sure, but technically the Soviet Union can say that it is upholding international law as much, sometimes more. 

I heard the Russian judge on the International Court of Justice say at The Hague in 1947 that the Soviet Union was upholding international law more than were the countries of the Western World. And

may I suggest that in a peculiar sense he could make a case for this, because he said the Baruch proposal, the control of atomic energy, inspection by international teams, this is an invitation to deny sovereignty. This is the establishment of supranational law, the destruction of international law. 

The Soviet Union's proposal for a multilateral treaty where each nation pledges its word is as far as any nation can go and respect sovereignty. And the respect for sovereignty is the keynote of our present international law.

In that sense of the word, particularly, tongue in cheek, perhaps, Krylov was right, Senators. If we are moving forward to this new period which demands international inspection, I haven't heard anyone today suggest that this program had any possibility of success unless there was international inspection.

We have to recognize that. And I think that most of us realize the importance. Most of us are willing to make sacrifices, but there are some questions as to whether American public opinion is really willing to give up its ideas of sovereignty at present. 

I don't say the fact of sovereignty. I think that so far as that is concerned, that disappeared a long time ago, certainly with the bomb over Hiroshima; but the idea of sovereignty. Perhaps the American people are hesitant about that. It might be, so far as I can say, that some members of legislative bodies have grave doubts. 

In that sense of the word let us not put forward any proposals that we know we can't back up. It takes good faith on both sides. We will talk many times about the fact that the Russians might not keep faith and I think that we have grave and justified doubts in that connection. But there is the possibility also that we might not be able to deliver the goods at this stage of the game in the year 1956. 

NEED FOR BETTER AMERICAN PROPAGANDA 

The great hope and the great problem, it seems to me, are to continue relations to develop the idea that this is a struggle for men's minds, that it has positive elements as well as negative, that the positive element is showing the people of the world the thing which is true, that we are the revolutionaries of all time. We are the ones who have very definitely pushed back the frontiers of tyranny. We don't see those frontiers rolling back in other countries today. 

We are the people who have established the Government of the people, by the people, and for the people. We know those things in 

America, and we know them so well we don't have to tell them to ourselves. But it seems to me that in some peculiar way they are not told to other countries, and the idea has come that we are the reactionaries, that the Soviet Union are the revolutionaries, the hope of the future.

We are losing that battle in the field of world public opinion, or if we are not losing it, we are backing up several steps in order to move forward. 

My suggestion, Senators, would be that if there is going to be disarmament, disarmament in the military sense of the word can come only through rearmament in the realm of ideas, in the mind and in the spirit of freemen. 

Thank you.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Senator Wiley. 

ARE WE LOSING THE BATTLE FOR MEN'S MINDS? 

Senator WILEY. Well, now, I have heard several times that phrase:

We are losing the battle in the world of public opinion. You know that isn't what General Romulo said when he came back from Bandung. He came back and said he went to that conference with fear and doubt and worry and thought the people of Africa and the people of Asia were all going in together with the Communists. Instead of that he said he came back with a song in his heart. He heard them tell of the ideas of the Declaration of Independence. He said they even recited the American poem, The Ride of Paul Revere, and he said he threw it back in the teeth of the Chinese Communists. 

Now why should we always take the negative viewpoint that we are failing when we know that that isn't the case? You have a few people coming back from Europe and elsewhere saying that that is what has happened. Well, it isn't so. We have made tremendous progress in public opinion and there isn't any question that the Kremlin is trying to confuse wherever it can. But why take the negative when a man like Romulo comes back and says he himself had his eyes opened after being at Bandung.

ATTITUDE OF INDIA 

Mr. JONES. Senator, we have got some awfully good weapons and we have fired some of them, as you said, the Declaration of Independence, the Battle Hymn of the Republic. That is the strength of our country and it is not unknown. It is a matter of degree that I am talking about.

For example, I believe, from statements which have been quoted today with respect to the people of India, that perhaps 30 percent of them think that the Americans would touch off another world war. 

A very much smaller percentage of them think that the Russians would touch it off. Now that is a dismal fact. I don't think we are going to lose this battle, Senator. But I think we can say this much, that it is a continuing struggle. No victory is ever complete. No defeat is ever final.

But I do think-I have a feeling that we haven't made the utmost use of our intellectual, spiritual, and democratic resources at a worldwide level. We have done much, but we haven't done as much as we can, and we need to do more. 

Senator WILEY. The only trouble is that we misinterpret points of history. India is coming up in her field, you know that. In India they still think the cow is a sacred animal. They still have their religious differences. They still have their troubles with the nation to the north, and tens of millions of them are only a meal away from starvation. 

Mr. JONES. They are. 

Senator WILEY. And how are you going to interpret there the mental attitude of people under those conditions and say we are losing the fight. That isn't the attitude of many people who have been there among the intellectuals.

You have got the story there, of course, that Nehru believes that the nation should be like we were in Revolutionary days, neutral. Well, with the Communists to the north, with conditions like they are, coming out of a political and communistic morass, then you are going to judge that everything has gone haywire because we in America aren't doing everything.

Why do millions the world over want to come to America, hundreds of millions? Why do they want us to take down our barriers? This is a land of promise and they know it and they know we keep our faith. We have put our billions into this. Let us not give the impression that we are a failure in that respect. 

Mr. JONES. Why, Senator, if I have given that impression, let me make the utmost apology to myself because I consider myself a good American, too. But I would say that there is the question of degree, and that the decisions which are made in this world are going to be made by Americans, yes; but they are also going to be made by neutrals who have ideas about America. Those ideas may be correct, they may be incorrect, but they are going to decide for themselves, and I think that there is a possibility that we might be able to present a truer picture of the American promise to people in other lands. 

I worked with people in other lands and I do have the impression that they have a distorted picture of America. 

Senator WILEY. What would you suggest? What is the remedy briefly, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5? We will take it back and give it to the Government so they will find the solution. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Before you go into that description, which would be helpful, I think the record ought to note that while Dr. 

Carlos Romulo made a very heartening report of the results of the Bandung Conference, I think it is fair to say the thing that impressed the representatives of the Bandung Conference are the very things that we talk about the least. 

Mr. JONES. Right. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. We haven't been talking very much about the ride of Paul Revere. We have been talking about massive destruction and retaliation.

Mr. JONES. It strikes home when it is done, sir; but it is done so seldom.

PUBLICIZING OUR MILITARY OBJECTIVES 

Senator WILEY. I asked General Gruenther in public hearing a question along these lines. He had been discussing with us some of the problems of NATO. I said, "General, is it true that the constant talk in the United States about our new airpower, our new weapons, and where we are going to deliver them-because we always mention where they are going to go. We say, 'This one is on Stalingrad, this one on 

Moscow' we name the place." I said, "does this have an adverse effect?" 

He said, "Indeed, it does." 

I said, "Do the Soviet propagandists talk in the same way?"

He said, "No, they are talking about defense. They have very few times in Soviet literature talked about dropping an A-bomb on Pittsburgh, on Cleveland, on Chicago. They are constantly saying, 'All we are trying to do is defend ourselves from these "terrible" people that are going to send these waves of bombers over us.' 999

Whether we like it or not, we have let ourselves into this. You have to face up to it. One of the first things you have to do is to recognize what you may be doing yourself.

Now the American people are a peaceful people and I think what you are trying to say is that we maybe have misinterpreted at times or not properly interpreted our own purpose and our own objectives. 

But you just take a look at the press which is only printing the news, and you will find that time after time we have dropped more bombs, I am sure theoretically, than are in our stockpile. 

Now I ask any good American to take a look at the same press and see how many times you can find outbursts from the Soviet leaders that indicate the same kind of direct attack. Khrushchev over in

London said, "You had better be careful with us or we will take care of you," and what did it do to him? Right away he loses his impetus and his impact in that visit because this exposed exactly what they had in mind. They have been a little more careful. 

ATTITUDE OF THE PHILIPPINES 

I might also say with reference to the Philippines:

within the week the Foreign Relations Committee of the Philippine Government has told the United States Government they don't intend to be an appendix to the United States. They have told us to get out. They have 3 bases over there and they want them on a 10-year lease. They said, "If you can be good to the Japanese, be good to us; we were on your side." 

This has become a very serious problem in the Philippine Islands, vis-a-vis our relationship with the Philippines. We have insisted on owning the bases, not just leasing them and because of that insistence on owning the bases, owning the land, now we have gotten ourselves into the position where the Philippine committee comparable to our 

Foreign Relations Committee has said, "You are not only not going to own; you are not going to lease for 99 years. We will give you 10 years and you will cut them down to 3 bases, and after 10 years we will talk about whether you are going to have any more. وو 

I think this is what you are saying in a sense.

Mr. JONES. It is. All I ask is that we try to put ourselves in the other person's frame of mind, and in that sense of the word perhaps it is a little bit understandable that the Filipinos adopt this view. do feel that we sometimes overemphasize the fact that we are losing. 

I think Senator Wiley has a very valid point there but I think we ought to have our own standards. I don't think we ought to be willing to say we are competing with the Soviets. If they want competitive coexistence, we ought to give it to them. When we compete I believe we ought to compete hands down.

FAILURE TO APPRECIATE AMERICA 

Senator WILEY. I don't want to get into an argument about this. 

I just want to say this about the Philippines, that is not the fault of America that we have been so good to them. It is the failure of the human mind everywhere to appreciate the greatness and the generosity and, let us say, the leadership of America. You have got it everywhere.

I remember one time a woman came back to Wisconsin from Italy. 

She said the Italians didn't like Americans. She based it upon the fact she understood Italian-that she went into a shop and she heard a woman say there, "I wish those Americans would go home." On that she formed the opinion the Italians were all against us. 

Well, you can't do that. The only point I want to make is that we haven't failed. It's the other people who have failed. It's their failure to appreciate the significance of America's generosity and leadership in the finest sense of the word. 

Now that doesn't mean we can't probably do better. How about the missionaries that have failed? Have they failed? No, it is the lack of others to appreciate the doctrine and the principle of the missionary the fact that human minds won't grasp the spiritual values. Šo let us get it straight that we in America haven't failed. 

We have put $50 billion in trying to resurrect this world after wars, and I think that story should be told. 

Mr. JONES. But Senator, excuse me one moment please. I don't think that anyone should attribute to me any idea that I think that we in America have failed. 

Senator WILEY. I am glad to get that statement.

Mr. JONES. I don't think, however, that I would be doing my job or exercising my role as a citizen properly if I didn't suggest that anyone can always do better, including Americans. 

Senator WILEY. We have jackasses in every class. 

Mr. JONES. We do.

Q.'s are entirely different, their approach to moral responsibility, et cetera. We seem to have gotten off on a tangent. Anyway, I thank you.

STATEMENT OF OSCAR F. LITTERER, BUSINESS ECONOMIST, FEDERAL RESERVE BANK OF MINNEAPOLIS Mr. LITTERER. Senator Humphrey, Senator Wiley, ladies and gentlemen, I am Oscar F. Litterer, business economist for the Federal

Reserve Bank in Minneapolis. In my testimony I would like to emphasize the national security expenditures in relation to the Ninth 72880-56-pt. 94

Federal Reserve District economy, I should probably say at first what this district covers. 

From the east to the west we cover the upper Wisconsin Peninsula, the 26 northwest counties of Wisconsin. Senator Wiley- 

Senator WILEY. That is my county in there. 

Mr. LITTERER. Yes; I know it is. Minnesota, North Dakota, South

Dakota, and Montana. I think often this region is referred to more or less as the upper Midwest.

BASIC INDUSTRIES AFFECTED BY DEFENSE EXPENDITURES 

I would like to point out that the war and defense expenditures permeate the entire economy, but the direct impact of these expenditures is on certain basic industries. In the Ninth Federal Reserve District agriculture, mineral production, the wood and pulp industry, the manufacture of food, of certain soft goods, and a few hard goods are such industries where a proportion of the output has been diverted into military channels or into relief and rehabilitation in the reconstruction period.

By studying the expansions and contractions in the output of these industries the influence of war and defense expenditures may be deciphered and appraised. Furthermore, some insight may be gained into the implications of a reduction in the current national security expenditures.

Ever since the Korean conflict came to an end, some industries have been in the process of adjusting their output back to civilian markets, and a few have completed this adjustment. On the other hand, others have continued to hold military contracts and, therefore, have not been forced to find civilian markets for their output. A brief description of problems encountered when Government contracts were terminated may be a harbinger of what is to come when current security expenditures are reduced.

AGRICULTURE 

In this district agriculture is a large industry. The greatest impact of military expenditures in this area, no doubt, has been on agriculture. This Nation has become involved in two world wars. 

In these hostilities American agriculture has been called upon to supply food and fiber not only for our Armed Forces but also for those of our allies. The American farmer did a spectacular job in meeting these greatly expanded needs for farm products. 

Agriculture in the past several years has been faced with the problem of adjusting to a lower peacetime demand. Despite the rapid growth in the Nation's population, which adds annually 2.8 million persons to the number to be fed and clothed, the production potential of American agriculture is so great that surpluses readily appear and tend to depress the prices of farm products. 

The pertinent question for this discussion is: Will a reduction in national security expenditures reduce further the demand for agricultural products? The answer, of course, is found in the outlook for foreign and domestic markets.

Foreign markets, in the aggregate, may not be affected by a reduction in national security expenditures. Only a relatively small quantity of our farm products is exported, but an increase or decrease in

the volume, nevertheless, is reflected in the surpluses that accumulate. 

In the regular trade channels where the bulk of the trade is found, foreign countries are limited in their purchase of American agricultural products by the amount of dollars they can accumulate. This, in turn, depends on the volume of commodities we are willing to import from the rest of the world, the amount of money Americans spend on travel abroad, and the investments Americans make in foreign countries.

In the foreign-aid program the United States has contributed to both military and civilian relief abroad, and I understand the foreignaid program is now under consideration in Congress again. 

Senator WILEY. It is. 

Mr. LITTERER. What Congress or the administration may do about the disposal of surplus farm products will have a bearing on the total foreign market. However, any policy adopted in disposing of surplus products, I assume, is a separate problem from the one under consideration here.

Demobilization would have some bearing on the domestic market for farm products. If the almost 3 million persons now in the armed services were demobilized, purchases of food and clothing by the quartermaster of the armed services would be cut off, but the demand for farm products would rise in the civilian market. 

However, the personnel in civilian life probably would vary their intake of the different kinds of food. For instance, military camps have been supplied with such surplus products as butter, milk, fats, and so forth. The diets in camps, no doubt, are heavier with surplus products than they are in civilian life. Of course, if the personnel were demobilized over a period of years, the effect on the market would be minimized.

FOOD INDUSTRIES 

Since the Upper Midwest is not only a part of the bread basket of the Nation but an important food processing area, it is pertinent to trace the impact of military contracts on the manufacture of food. (Much of this information was obtained from Dr. Ernest G. Booth, business analyst, U. S. Department of Commerce, Minneapolis field office, who was in touch with the Government's food procurement program during World War II.) The production of dairy products may serve to illustrate the effect of war and defense expenditures. 

Military contracts let for dairy products in this region required investment in new capital. Near the beginning of World War II 

such contracts were let for condensed milk. The essential building materials and equipment were made available for the construction or expansion of these plants. As the war progressed and submarine warfare intensified, making it necessary to conserve shipping space, contracts were let for dried milk and dried eggs. 

Again allocations were made of materials and equipment to build about 25 drying plants in this region. The springing up of military camps in the United States, with many of them located in areas producing inadequate quantities of milk for the purpose, led to a need for stainless steel tanks to transport fluid milk at great distances to these camps.

As meat processing is the largest manufacturing industry in this region, many contracts were let to firms in this area for meat, poultry,

and poultry products. Fourteen poultry-processing plants were built in Minnesota alone. Cold storage facilities were erected. 

Following World War II and again after the Korean conflict, some firms have found civilian markets for their equipment and products, while others found themselves in possession of excess capacities. 

For example, stainless steel tanks for the transportation of fluid milk were in demand for the transporting of fluid milk to rapidly growing population centers. Cold storage facilities were sought by the frozenfood industry to expand the output of their products. 

On the other hand, some equipment could not be converted, and some products became surplus commodities shortly after hostilities ceased. The Commodity Credit Corporation, as we know, has held large stocks of agricultural products. For example, dried milk has been a surplus item. According to the United States Department of 

Agriculture, the CCC on April 30, 1956, held a stock of dried milk valued at over $30 million.

Demobilization of the Armed Forces would change the method of marketing a portion of the food products. The quartermaster of the armed services has purchased hundreds of carlots of meats, poultry, and dairy and poultry products from this region. These products generally are purchased in areas where there are surpluses, to avoid, as much as possible, an interruption in the markets. 

Through these purchases the United States Government has guaranteed a small portion of the market to processors. However, the aggregate market for food may not differ much from the one that prevails today, assuming a comparable level of employment so that the civilians have the necessary purchasing power.

PAPER AND PULP 

The paper-and-pulp industry, another basic industry in this district, has had military prime contracts for all kinds of paper. During 

World War II it was necessary to replace antiquated equipment to fill the orders. As a result, many firms came out of the war better equipped to meet competition in the civilian market.

Demobilization, no doubt, would cut off orders, but the strong growth in the civilian demand would quickly offset them. The volume of paper and paperboard products used in the Nation is growing at a faster rate than the population.

According to the American Paper and Pulp Association, the annual per capita use of paper and paperboard in the United States has increased from 382 pounds in 1950 to 418 pounds in 1955. In such a strong growth industry, termination of military contracts would only retard momentarily the rate of growth.

MINERAL PRODUCTION 

Mineral resources in this region were heavily drawn upon in the war years. For example, in 1942, over 92 million gross tons of iron ore were shipped from the Lake Superior region, whereas in the decade of the thirties less than half of this quantity was shipped annually. 

Production of other minerals, such as copper, was also high in the war years. Thus, the war and the armament program have hastened the depletion of high-grade minerals, and industry has now turned to the mining and beneficiation of low-grade iron and copper ores.

Certificates of necessity for accelerated tax amortization have encouraged firms to invest capital in plant and equipment. To date, certificates have been granted to many firms engaged in the mining of low-grade ores.

Reduction in armaments will affect the mining industry only if it leads to a lower level of industrial production. From the viewpoint of mineral production, it is immaterial whether the minerals are used to build tanks in Cleveland, aircraft carriers at Norfolk, or a superhighway across the Nation.

EMPLOYMENT AND ARMAMENTS 

The level of industrial production and of employment leads to the crux of the economic problems associated with disarmament. Is armament production essential to economic prosperity?

Some observers apparently have come to the conclusion that it is. 

They reason that consumers and business firms may not take the entire output of a highly efficient industrial machine and so a margin of the output must be directed into armaments or foreign-aid programs. Nevertheless, no matter how efficient management may have become in cutting costs, the stages of production and distribution-from raw materials to finished goods take enough labor, and generate enough wages and profits to furnish purchasing power for the entire production output.

Obviously, taxation to maintain armament or foreign-aid programs transfers a portion of the income from consumers and business firms to the United States Government. Both consumers and business firms could hold back on their purchases, but there is no evidence of it at this time.

Back of this reasoning that consumers and business firms cannot clear the markets of goods produced lies the fear of unemployment. If the armament program is reduced, some workers, for example those in aircraft factories, may find it necessary to seek employment in other types of industries.

It may even require some workers to migrate to other communities. 

The armament program undoubtedly has concentrated workers in communities where they cannot be used economically to produce civilian goods. From this district many workers migrated to the large war and defense production centers on the west coast, Milwaukee, Chicago, and Detroit. When a reduction is made in the armament program, some workers may return to this region. Their return, of course, will depend on the opportunities for employment. 

In a highly dynamic economy, such as ours, workers cannot be assured a specific job in a certain community indefinitely. As a result of research and experimentation, a constant stream of new products is placed on the market and as these products are produced and accepted by consumers, old jobs are eliminated and new ones are created. 

The classic example is the automobile factory supplanting the buggy shop. Even if Congress through its appropriation maintained the current armament program indefinitely, workers would not be assured specific jobs in the production of armaments. 

Technological changes in methods of defense require different equipment. For instance, the perfection of the guided missile, I presume, may relegate the aircraft to a less important position. Consequently,

a worker in the Boeing Aircraft Co. in Seattle, where about 1 out of every 5 workers is engaged in armament production, is not assured a job up to the time of his retirement. 

The Employment Act of 1946, in my opinion, has been misinterpreted by some of our citizens. They assume Congress intended to assure workers specific jobs. The intent, of course, is to maintain an economic atmosphere in which private enterprise would provide sufficient employment opportunities to hold unemployment to a relatively low figure. In an economy where technological changes occur daily, a portion of the labor force must remain mobile. 

MILITARY CONTRACTS IN NINTH DISTRICT 

Now let's return to the ninth district to see the impact that the military contracts have had on the economy of this region. The fulfillment of military prime contracts have been a part of the industrial output of this region since the beginning of World War II. 

Of the total amount of contract submitted since 1950 the proportion let to firms in this region has ranged from eight-tenths of 1 percent to 

1.2 percent annually, and you will see that at the bottom of the charts.

You will notice in terms of military prime contracts they were at a peak in 1952, they declined in 1953, they declined still more in 1954. 

I might say that subcontracting in this area undoubtedly is more important than the military prime contracts, but we have no information on those because the Department of Defense has not required prime contractors to report their subcontracts. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. To what do you attribute that sudden advance in 1952? Of course I can recognize it is the Korean war period. 

What kind of contract was that?

Mr. LITTERER. Ordnance production. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Was that primarily the employment at the Twin City Arsenal and the Northern Pump? 

Mr. LITTERER. Yes. And then those contracts were cut back. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. With changes in weapons and designs and so forth?

Mr. LITTERER. That is correct; yes. Now, then, of course the important question is this: What effect has the decline in military prime contracts had on the manufacturing employment in this district? 

At the top of the chart you will see total employment in our four States in the ninth district-Montana, the Dakotas, and Minnesota. 

You will notice it rose to a peak in 1953, then it declined in 1954 and now it is again moving on up. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I ask a question here? Fifty-three, the red line at the bottom of the graph, that does not mean delivered goods? That means placed contracts, doesn't it?

You notice that has been going straight across the chart there, varying very little with the change in the contracts issued- Chairman HUMPHREY.
