Chairman HUMPHREY. Your seasonal gyrations? 

SHIFT FROM PRODUCTION OF MILITARY TO CIVILIAN GOODS 

Mr. LITTERER. That is a typical seasonal gyration; yes. But even the total number of people employed in the industry, Senator, has not varied, you see, with the contracts that were issued. 

Now then, since 1954 in this district there has been a gradual shift from the production of military to civilian goods. The shift in employment that has taken place in Minnesota has been summarized by the Department of Employment Security in the May 1956 issue of Employment Trends: 

Durable goods employment in Minnesota made substantial recoveries from post-Korean lows in early 1955, despite continued cutbacks in ordnance jobs. 

In April 1956, 113,113 Minnesotans were employed in manufacturing durable goods. This is some 10,500 above the low, but 10,200 under the Korean conflict's high in mid-1953.

Two major groups within the durable goods categories-nonelectrical machinery and ordnance-were the main factors in this trend. 

Nonelectrical machinery, which accounts for over 25 percent of the employment in the durable goods group, has recovered to within 2,500 of its peak 1953 employment. Conversion to peacetime contracts with continued industrial expansion has helped bring this about. 

At the same time ordnance employment has continued to decrease to the point where it is nearly 7,400 under its peak. This ability of durable goods manufacturing firms to make the shift from Korean war production to a peacetime basis with a minimum of adverse effects, has been very noticeable. 

Actually disregarding the ordnance decline of 7,400 and the nonelectrical decrease of 2,500 from the mid-1953 war induced highs, the 'group is within 300 workers of the 1953 total. Minnesota is currently at a record peacetime peak in stable goods factory employment.

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I ask a question there? 

Mr. LITTERER. Yes. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Has this been related somewhat to the construction and building program, particularly in housing? 

Mr. LITTERER. Senator, primarily in nonresidential construction. 

We are having a very large volume of commercial and industrial construction.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I mean in the last year or so, our residential construction was a little higher in the last 18 months, starting, let us say, about January 1. Moving it back we had a high peak of residential construction.

Mr. LITTERER. Very high. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. We had a number of industries related to that construction; wallboard, for example, furnace, heating equipment, and so forth. Was there an absorption of displaced workers from defense contracts in this housing construction and related industries?

Mr. LITTERER. There was. Some of the men that were in these defense industries secured work in residential building and some of the suppliers of building materials; for example, I could name 1 company that is running its factory 24 hours a day and they put on extra workers to run their factories. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. This is a substantial factory in this region, is it not?

Mr. LITTERER. Yes. It is a growing industry, too. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. It is a growing industry, wood and wood products, of course, and also metal products for ducts? 

Mr. LITTERER. Yes; air conditioning. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Wallboards, heating plants, and so forth. 

HIGH LIVING STANDARDS AND ECONOMIC PROSPERITY 

Mr. LITTERER. That is correct. Now, can manufacturers continue to make this shift in production from military to civilian goods if prime contracts are terminated?

Evidence is accumulating that this shift can be made with a minimum of adverse effects on economic prosperity. American people are interested in a higher standard of living. This is reflected in the rapid rise of family incomes. Family incomes in the current period of high employment have risen faster than per capita incomes. 

An increasing number of wives have entered the labor market to raise the economic welfare of their families. They are interested, for instance, in better housing, in appliances, and in furnishings. In the kitchen a semiautomatic range, refrigerator, freezer, automatic dishwasher, and garbage disposal unit; and in the laundry an automatic washing machine, dryer, and mangle with increasing frequency have become the fulfilled dream of the housewife. 

Between 1940 and 1950 the proportion of working wives nearly doubled. Partly due to wives moving into the labor market, families have steadily moved into higher income brackets in recent years. 

According to United States Census data the chances that a family had an income of $5,000 or more in 1951 were twice as great if the wife worked than if she stayed at home. American families want a higher standard of living rather than more leisure time. 

A reduction in armament would materially assist them in achieving this goal. It would release production facilities for civilian goods; and a reduction in taxes comparable to the cut in armament would provide the necessary purchasing power. 

The material presented may be summarized in this fashion: When armaments are essential for the security of this Nation, both business. and labor in this district are anxious to secure their share of the contracts. The fulfillment of military contracts creates payrolls and expenditures for plants and equipment.

Some of the capital formation can be converted later on to civilian uses. However, when armaments are no longer needed for the security of this Nation, the people are anxious to turn to the production of civilian goods.

In the production of these goods we have not only payrolls, expenditures for plants and equipment, but also the end products which will raise our standard of living. This is the prime objective in our economic endeavor.

PROBLEMS OF REGIONAL UNEMPLOYMENT 

Senator WILEY. All right. Now, have you given thought to the difference between this area and say an area such as in Michigan, Ohio, and in my own State?

We are having a little taste of what happens in the automobile world right now as you know. Can you carry in mind the difference between that and this area and give us your reactions as to demobilization in the two areas? 

How can that situation be handled? Is there any difference between that and this?

Mr. LITTERER. Well, Senator Wiley, I think there are some prob lems that differ. May I go back to the years right immediately before 

World War II? You see, we were described as a labor supply area during World War II. Labor moved out of this region to the industrial centers of the Nation, and I sat in on one businessmen's meeting that lasted most of the afternoon, where these men were very worried as to what to do with all the workers when they returned after World War II, and some concrete suggestions were made in this committee. 

Well, as it turned out, there was no net return of workers to this area. 

In fact, we have had a net loss in population after World War II.

Now let's assume that we demobilize very gradually, I mean cut these contracts back gradually, are these people going to move back to this area?

I am not so sure anymore, I don't know. It depends on the industrial opportunities we have for employment in this area. When you ask what problems it will create for a city like say Milwaukee, if you have alternatives for employment there, if these firms can turn to production of civilian goods, I think the labor is apt to stay right there. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. You have got the possibilities of school construction?

Mr. LITTERER. Yes.

Mr. LITTERER. And as soon as credit can again be eased, I think some of those issues will come back on the market.

I am very grateful that you have remained, those of you that have waited this morning, because I think that you get as much good out of this as we do, to hear the expression of ideas of other people who are well qualified to express themselves and give us guidance.

AFTERNOON SESSION Chairman HUMPHREY. Senator Wiley is momentarily detained in the lobby and I thought we would just proceed with the testimony because time is running short. 

Is Mr. Merle Harris here?

STATEMENT OF MERLE HARRIS, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. HARRIS. My name is Merle Harris. My address is 3509 Stinson

Boulevard, Minneapolis. I am on the staff here of the University of 

Minnesota but I am speaking simply as an individual.

I might say that my remarks here will follow along the lines of my written statement but are not identical with it. I count it a great privilege to have the opportunity to express my views on matters of national and international concern to you members of the United States Senate and of this subcommittee. 

I have often been critical of persons in Washington who in referring to some bold proposal may say, "Yes, that is the solution, but the people are not ready for it."

My criticism has fallen along two lines. Too often the people are not given the facts so they can be expected to be ready for the proposed solution.

In addition to that, I often wonder how the person who makes the statement that the people are not ready can know this because they are seldom asked.

However, the hearings in which you participated 2 years ago with Senator Wiley as chairman and the present hearings are setting new precedents in this respect and I am most grateful for them.

I should make clear at the beginning that in this discussion I am using the term "disarmament" to mean total and universal disarmament. By this I mean all nations stripped of all armaments, both nuclear and conventional, with which to make war. I define it in this way because I think that in the long run this is the only kind of disarmament that has a chance of being successful and therefore it is the only kind worthwhile for us to discuss. 

OPEN SKIES PROPOSAL ONLY A GATEWAY 

What we hear most about in connection with disarmament these days is President Eisenhower's open skies proposal. While I have the highest regard for the President's bold proposal as a means of capturing the imagination of peoples and of convincing them of his sincerity, I think it must be admitted that in and of itself the open skies proposal has nothing at all to say about disarmament as such. 

The President has in fact referred to it as the gateway to disarmament. 

What I assume is that we are all here today to discuss those matters that must be faced once we are through the gateway. If my information is correct regarding the recent conversations between Mr. Khrushchev of Russia and Mr. Stassen, Khrushchev stated that he was not interested in the open skies proposal unless it was part and parcel of an overall disarmament plan. 

And while I have no idea what kind of overall plan Khrushchev might have had in mind or would accept, I do think that he raised a very pertinent and proper question with Mr. Stassen and a question that all the American people should also ask Mr. Stassen. 

We seem to be taking the position that we won't even discuss disarmament unless the open skies proposal is accepted. At best there are two possibilities: this may indicate an inverted sense of values or at worst the possibility that we don't have an overall disarmament plan to propose. 

To other nations it is very likely to appear that we don't really want disarmament and that we are merely playing around the fringe of the idea for propaganda purposes. 

But whatever may be the reasons why we place open skies first, it is to be hoped that we do not assume an inflexible position with regard to it as we did the now admittedly obsolete Baruch plan several years ago.

AN OVERALL PLAN ESSENTIAL 

Many seem to assume that the way to approach the disarmament situation is by degrees, a little at a time, like cutting off the proverbial dog's tail. But partial, limited and voluntary disarmament has been tried repeatedly with disappointing results.

Each time war has swept away our good intentions. 

May I suggest that the reason we have made no greater progress toward disarmament than we have may be that we have not been courageous enough to face the whole problem and seek a comprehensive solution to it.

It may be that we must start implementing any plan a little at a time. But it does not do much good to propose a small aspect of

a plan unless one is able to follow through and relate it to the whole structure. It would be very embarrassing if after all of our efforts to get the U. S. S. R. to accept the open skies, we were only to find that in the United States, and I might say in the United States Senate for example, that we would not accept open skies.

I think both sides are much more likely to accept if we know the whole plan. We insist on living in the past I think when national armaments could provide at least a measure of security. The nuclear age has changed all that, but we have not been willing to face it. 

Our new weapons, and I am including here the intercontinental ballistic missiles, provide the means for inconceivably greater destruction, much greater than ever before, but virtually no protection or security against it.

Now we see that a new plan is required but a little plan or half a plan will not do. We won't buy it any more than we would buy a half of an automobile. This leads me to what I believe to be a fundamental principle for the discussion of disarmament. 

It is this: it is futile to attempt to discuss disarmament apart from an overall plan to provide security for all by means other than by national armaments, and inseparably tied up with this there must be a plan for effectively outlawing war as a means of settling international disputes. 

It is just as irrational or perhaps even more so to expect a nation to give up its arms while war is still countenanced as it is to expect a golf player to surrender his clubs while the tournament is still going on. 

INADEQUACY OF MILITARY ALLIANCES 

To even forcibly outlaw war in the world we must have a world law to this effect. World law is just as necessary to world peace and disarmament as our national laws are in preserving peace and disarmament between Minnesota and Wisconsin.

The objectives set forth above cannot be obtained, as I see it, by NATO, EDC, SEATO, or any other combination of nations that does not include them all. 

The very obvious reason for saying this is that these arrangements are primarily military alliances which depend on the strength of national armaments, the very thing we propose to eliminate. Moreover, we are all aware that the history of military alliances in preventing wars is a tragic one.

UNITED NATIONS AS ENFORCEMENT AGENCY 

An organization, the United Nations, already exists for the very purposes we seek, but it has never been given the power to do more than to recommend. As presently constituted it cannot pass a world law. 

Here is where I think we must begin if we would do anything effective toward disarmament.

First, we should so revise the United Nations Charter that it has the power of enforcible law in the area of preserving the peace. 

Second, the United Nations should be provided with a police force adequate to its needs. sal. 

Third, membership in the United Nations should be made univer-

In this framework I feel that disarmament might be possible. It could be made enforcible and if it were made enforcible it would be demanded. 

That is the conclusion of my statement. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Senator Wiley, do you have any questions?

Senator WILEY. No questions. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Harris, I want to thank you very much for your statement.

We are going to have to limit our questions in order to accommodate our witnesses. (The prepared statement of Mr. Harris is as follows:)

DISARMAMENT: CAN IT BE MADE ACCEPTABLE AND ENFORCIBLE? 

Let me make clear at the outset that in this discussion I am taking the term "disarmament" to be synonomous with total and universal disarmament. By this I mean all nations stripped of all weapons, both nuclear and conventional, with which to make war. I define the term in this way because it is my belief that this is the only kind of disarmament that has any chance of success in the long run, and that is the only kind worthwhile for us to discuss. We have had experience with partial, limited and voluntary disarmament and have been so embittered by it that the word has fallen into disrepute for a number of years. 

It required the military prestige of President Eisenhower to reinstate the term to respectability. I do not pretend to know the precise meaning that the President attaches to the term but from some of his pronouncements I think we have some reason to believe that he sees beyond the older, limited application of the term.

Presumably there are two major objectives in the minds of those who would promote disarmament, in any form, namely: (1) To remove or greatly to reduce the probability of global war; and (2) to relieve mankind of the crushing burden of participating in the arms race. Some may feel that limited disarmament would serve these purposes, but if our experience has taught us anything in this regard it is that the results of such action are temporary and illusory-finally reversed and consumed by war. It is because of my belief that limited disarmament is futile that I propose the meaning outlined above, and address myself to it.

When we ask ourselves whether disarmament can be made acceptable and enforceable we are, of course, asking the most difficult of questions. However, if we are to discuss the subject of disarmament in a realistic manner these questions are inescapable, it seems to me. It would be most presumptious indeed for me to pretend to provide any positive or direct answers to them. However, I do have some thoughts, for whatever they may be worth, as to the general framework under which I think disarmament may be possible and without which it will be neither acceptable nor enforceable, as I see it.

Let us begin by asking ourselves why nations have armaments in the first place. The traditional answer that many would still give is: "To provide security." Another answer, less readily understood or admitted, is that armaments constitute the ultimate instruments of foreign policy. Other answers can be found but these will suffice. They will suffice because they are the leading arguments for armaments-and they are both obsolete with the advent of modern weapons. We are all familiar with the testimony of various military experts to the effect that we have very limited prospects of intercepting an attack using present weapons. When intercontinental ballistic missiles become a reality our defense will become essentially zero. Our weapons provide kindling for the conflagration but little or no protection against it. 

The growing realization that security is no longer provided by national armaments may force us to think boldly enough to arrive at and to accept a plan that could make disarmament enforceable and that would regain for us the security we have lost. But we are hardheaded. We will not accept half of a plan, any more than we will buy half of an automobile. We want something that will run, something that will do the job. And this brings me to what I believe to be a fundamental principle for the discussion of disarmament, if we are really serious about it. The principle is this: It is futile to discuss disarmament apart from an overall plan for providing security for all by means other than armaments. 

And inseparably bound up with this there must be a plan for effectively outlaw-

ing war as a means of settling international disputes. We are only deceiving ourselves if we pretend that we will not want our weapons, all of them, so long as war is not enforceably outlawed. To do so would seem to be as irrational as to throw away our golf clubs while we still play golf-and just as likely to be accomplished.

To enforceably outlaw war in the world we must have a world law to this effect. World law is just as necessary to world peace and disarmament as our national laws are in preserving peace and disarmament between Minnesota and Wisconsin. The objectives set forth above cannot be attained, as I see it, by 

NATO, EDC, SEATO, or any other combination of nations that does not include them all. A very obvious reason for saying this is that these arrangements are primarily military alliances which depend on the strength of national armaments-the very thing we propose to eliminate. Moreover, we are all aware that the history of military alliances in preventing wars is a tragic one. 

Such organizations and alliances are merely stopgap measures, and even on this basis are of doubtful value. At best they alienate otherwise friends and neutrals while attempting to coerce those we deem most valuable to our cause; at worst they are a means of choosing partners for the next dance-war. In my opinion, our present efforts to resuscitate NATO are most unfortunate, partly for the general reasons just given and specifically because it indicates a lack of comprehension of the political and military situation as it exists today. 

An organization, the United Nations, already exists for the very purpose we seek but it has never been given the power to do more than to recommend. As presently constituted it cannot pass a world law. Here is where I think we must begin if we would do anything effective toward disarmament: (1) We should so revise the U. N. Charter that it has the power of enforceable law in the area of preserving the peace. (2) The U. N. should be provided with a police force adequate to its needs. (3) Membership in the U. N. should be made universal.

In this framework disarmament could be made enforceable, and if it were made enforceable it is my belief that it would be acceptable, in fact demanded. 

In the above discussion I do not mean in any way to depreciate President Eisenhower's "open skies" proposal. I have only the strongest praise for it as a means of capturing the imagination of people. I do not believe that the President considers it to be much more. He has, in fact, referred to it as "the gateway to disarmament." My discussion refers to those matters to which we must set our minds once we are through the gateway. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Is Mr. Langton in the room? 

Mr. Langton, we will pass by, and when he comes in we will return him to his position on the list of witnesses. 

Mr. J. N. Eisendrath?

STATEMENT OF JACK N. EISENDRATH, REPRESENTING THE MILWAUKEE PEACE EDUCATION COMMITTEE 

Mr. EISENDRATH. Senator Humphrey and Senator Wiley, my esteemed Senator from Wisconsin, my name is Jack N. Eisendrath.

I am an attorney from Milwaukee, Wis. I represent the Milwaukee 

Peace Education Committee, a small group of men and women from various walks of life professional people, housewives, church people, factory workers, and so forth, who for about 5 years have tried to encourage individual and group exploration of creative, peaceful means of dealing with tensions in today's world. We work in cooperation with the American Friends Service Committee; we develop study programs and have initiated such projects as two citywide all-day seminars on U. N. Charter Revision, participated in by delegates from many of Milwaukee's civic organizations. 

Groups similar to the Milwaukee Peace Education Committee have been organized by citizens in other cities in Illinois and Wisconsin. 

This coming fall we hope to invite all interested community organizations to attend a full-day seminar on world disarmament. Per-

haps the job of this committee and the planning of this Government might be easier in this field if similar committees could be organized throughout the land.

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I say to you the American Friends 

Service Committee has testified before our committee and we are in consultation with them. I thought you would like to know that. 

Mr. EISENDRATH. I understand that. Thank you.

My wish today is not to discuss the technical details of disarmament, the progress of nuclear weapons, the impact of Soviet-announced reduction in manpower, the relation of taxes and armament costs, and the other myriad aspects of disarmament.

FULL-TIME STUDY OF DISARMAMENT NEEDED 

I have observed in my limited reading of the excellent testimony before your committee that in addition to your own keen interest and study of the problem, you have had the benefit of many of our country's outstanding experts on various issues related to disarmament. I am certain that my experiences cannot add anything to the testimony on these specialized items. 

It is, however, interesting to note that, with the exception of Governor Stassen, I cannot recall any other witness who devotes his full time to the special subject of disarmament. 

Most of the witnesses have gained their background because they have been able in their main vocation to do some incidental thinking on issues allied to disarmament; in that category you heard men like 

Professor Sohn, who, in connection with his work as law professor, has developed a keen interest in and theory of international law; and 

Mr. Walter Levison, who is an expert on inspection and control of nuclear weapons as an adjunct to his vocation as assistant director of 

Boston University Physics Research Laboratory. 

PROPOSAL FOR ORGANIZING A DISARMAMENT INSTITUTE 

This thought leads to the theme of my discussion: How much more valuable would these experts be if they and others were fulltime members of a permanent disarmament institute? 

We have discovered that an increase in armament does not necessarily mean an increase in security, and more terrible armaments may even decrease security feelings. It is not necessary to repeat the thought your committee has heard so often throughout the country concerning the importance of universal disarmament. All of us know that the heedless scramble of the two major contenders for world supremacy in weapons capable of destroying the other side, if unchecked, can lead only to self-destruction. Great historians of the world, atomic scientists, political scientists, economic experts and other students today agree on the foregoing. 

If this is true, what is more important for our country to excel in than a knowledge of how to implement a plan for universal workable disarmament? If this is true of what value are our material advances, our great cities, our sleek high-powered motor cars, our advanced skill in medicine, chemistry and industrial production, if all may be reduced to dust particles by the release of some unfortunate 72880-56-pt. 9-

button-pushing missile madness which we always said we hoped would never happen?

United States experts give full strength to overbrilliant advances. 

Why not disarmament, upon which continued existence to other accomplishments depends? We will spend more than $33 billion this year. We did the last year and we will spend a like amount next year to retain military strength as a means of seeking security. We are now forced for survival to think in terms of disarmament for permanent security and the preservation of democracy. If this is true, as a matter of defense measure, we should be willing to spend a small percentage of our defense budget exclusively for disarmament studies. 

We have funds to maintain an outstanding academy at West Point, a Naval Academy, marine training schools, and now a carefully planned Air Academy in Colorado. The time is ripe to plan a permanent disarmament institute which should be disassociated from any branch of the Armed Forces. Such an institute could be located at a great university such as the University of Minnesota or in connection with the Princeton Institute of Advanced Studies. As we subsidize candidates for the Armed Forces training centers, could we not select interested students to attend the disarmament institute for training under a director of international repute and ability? 

OBJECTIVES FOR INSTITUTE STUDY 

A study of disarmament is more than an attempt to devise means of agreeing to reduce armaments. 

A few of the institute study courses could be, for example, the history and background of disarmament; the technology of disarmament and the relation of nuclear weapons to conventional armaments; the economics of disarmament, an overall plan for transition, bearing in mind present-day figures at about 11 percent of our national income and one-eighth of our labor force is connected with arms spending; the sociology of disarmament; how can savings in armament expenditures be utilized for encouraging world peace by financial and technical assistance to underdeveloped areas; the politics of disarmament, the role of the United States in world disarmament. What type of international law, international police force, legislative body, judicial system, and so forth would be necessary under a system of world disarmament?

RECRUITMENT OF STUDENTS AND TEACHERS 

The above are only a few of the aspects of disarmament that demand continuous analysis. In colleges and universities there are many highcaliber students who, with help, financial or otherwise, could be encouraged to complete their studies in some field related to disarmament. An annual prize of high value similar to the Nobel prize could be awarded to individuals who have contributed most to disarmament each year. Part of the problem in reaching a peaceful accord is to understand the minds and emotions of those with whom we differ. A disarmament institute could sponsor scholars who would live and study for the purpose of understanding the people in areas of the world with which we have to reach agreement. A system of exchange students might be developed.

There are countless capable men in the United States eager to teach and study at such a disarmament institute. An outstanding director could be secured for a whole year for the same pay as it costs for one general in the Army. The entire program could be operated on an experimental basis for several hundred thousand dollars a year. We have spent millions on experimental bombers and submarines. If the United States represents to the world that we are really interested in world peace, we must be willing to spend money for new ideas on disarmament, just as we do for development of new weapons. 

This subcommittee is urged to consider a proposal to recommend the early organization of a permanent disarmament institute. Such an idea would capture the approval and enthusiasm of peoples throughout the world. Such an idea would help demonstrate that the United 

States is willing to assume moral leadership in today's anxious world. 

Thank you. (The prepared statement submitted by Mr. Eisendrath is a follows:) 

My name is Jack N. Eisendrath; I am an attorney practicing law in Milwaukee, Wis. I represent the Milwaukee Peace Education Committee. This is a small group of men and women from various walks of life-professional people, housewives, church people, factory workers, etc., who for about 5 years have tried to encourage individual and group exploration of creative, peaceful means of dealing with tensions in today's world. We work in cooperation with the 

American Friends Service Committee; we develop study programs and have initiated such projects as two citywide all-day seminars on U. N. Charter revision, participated in by delegates from many of Milwaukee's civic organizations. Groups similar to the Milwaukee Peace Education Committee have been organized by citizens in other cities in Illinois and Wisconsin. This coming fall we hope to invite all interested community organizations to attend a fullday seminar on world disarmament.

My wish today is not to discuss the technical details of disarmament, the progress of nuclear weapons, the impact of Soviet-announced deduction in manpower, the relation of taxes and armament costs, and the other myriad aspects of disarmament. I have observed in my limited reading of the excellent testimony before your committee that in addition to your own keen interest and study of the problem, you have had the benefit of many of our country's outstanding experts on various issues related to disarmament. I am certain that my experiences cannot add anything to the testimony on these specialized items. 

It is, however, interesting to note that, with the exception of Governor Stassen, I cannot recall any other witness who devotes his full time to the specific subject of disarmament. Most of the witnesses have gained their background because they have been able in their main vocation to do some incidental thinking on issues allied to disarmament; in that category you heard men like Professor Sohn, who in connection with his work as law professor, has developed a keen interest in and theory of international law; and Mr. Walter Levison, who is an expert on inspection and control of nuclear weapons as an adjunct to his vocation as assistant director of Boston University Physics Research Laboratory.

This thought leads to the theme of my discussion: How much more valuable would these experts be if they and others were full-time members of a permanent disarmament institute?

But we have discovered that an increase in armament does not necessarily mean an increase in security, and more terrible armaments may even decrease security feelings.

It is not necessary to repeat the thoughts your committee has heard so often throughout the country concerning the importance of universal disarmament. 

All of us know that the heedless scramble of the two major contenders for world supremacy in weapons capable of destroying the other side, if unchecked, can lead only to self-destruction. Great historians of the world, atomic scientists, political scientists, economic experts, and other students today agree on the foregoing. If this is true, what is more important for our country to excel in than a knowledge of how to implement a plan for universal workable disarmament? 

Of what value are our material advances, our great cities, our sleek highpowered motorcars, our advanced skill in medicine, chemistry, and industrial

production, if all may be reduced to dust particles by the release of some unfortunate button-pushing missile madness which we always said we hoped would never happen?

The time has come to study and plan disarmament full time, all time, spare no expense, no holds barred. The United States experts give their full mental and physical strength to other brilliant advances, why not to disarmament, upon which the continued existence of the other accomplishments depends? We will spend more than $33 billion this year; we did it last year, and we'll spend a like amount next year to retain our military strength as a means of seeking security. We are now forced for survival to think in terms of disarmament for permanent security and the preservation of democracy. If this is true as a national defense measure we should be willing to spend a small percentage of our defense budget as an expenditure devoted exclusively to disarmament studies. 

Our country has funds to maintain an outstanding Academy at West Point, a Naval Academy, marine training schools, and now a carefully planned Air Academy in Colorado. The time is ripe to plan a permanent disarmament institute which should be disassociated from any branch of the Armed Forces. 

Such an institute could be located at a university, such as in connection with the Princeton Institute of Advanced Studies. As we subsidize outstanding candidates to the Armed Forces training centers, so could we carefully select interested students to attend the disarmament institute for intensive study and training under a director of international repute and ability. The study of disarmament, as we know, is more than an attempt to devise means of agreeing on reducing armaments. Endless ramifications of this central theme demand attention. Following are only a few of the institute study courses that could merit scholarly research: 

1. History and background of disarmament: 

(a) Successes and failure in previous disarmament attempts. (b) Needs of the modern world in relation to disarmament. 

2. Technology of disarmament: 

(a) The relation of nuclear weapons to conventional armaments. (b) Aspects of inspection and control in relation to the difficulties involved in detecting fissionable materials. (c) Changing patterns of warfare; recognition that 1 bomber may equal 

1 division of men and similar disparities in ratio of men to modern weapons. (d) Aspects of total universal disarmament contrasted with reduction or partial limitation of arms. 

3. Economics of disarmament: 

(a) How would disarmament affect the economic and industrial life of city and national economy? (b) An overall plan for transition, bearing in mind present-day figures that about 11 percent of our national income and one-eighth of our labor force is now connected with arms spending. 

4. Sociology of disarament: 

(a) How can savings in armament expenditures be utilized in part for encouraging world peace by financial and technical assistance to underdeveloped areas? (b) How can agreement be reached between opposing sides; what is the psychology of negotiating with those who differ from us; are the most successful negotiations done in public or private meetings; what type of leadership is most advantageous in negotiation conferences, etc.? 

5. Politics of disarmament: 

(a) The role of the United Nations and world disarmament. (b) What type of international law will be needed to enforce universal disarmament? (c) What type of international policing force will be required to control universal enforcible disarmament? (d) What type of legislative body will be called upon to make the laws for enforcible disarmament? (e) What type of judicial system is fitted to aid in enforcement? (f) How best can internal differences within armed services in our own country reach a practical accord? 

Obviously, the above are only a few of the many aspects of disarmament that demand continuous careful study and analysis. In all the colleges and universities there are many high-caliber students who, with proper help, financial or otherwise, could be encouraged by the institute to complete the Ph. D. studies in some field related to disarmament. An annual prize of high value, similar to the Nobel prize, could be awarded by the Government to individuals who have

contributed the most to disarmament each year. Part of the problem in reaching a peaceful accord is to understand the minds and emotions of those with whom we differ. The disarmament institute could sponsor scholars who would live and study, for the purpose of understanding the people, in areas of the world with which we have to reach an agreement. Perhaps a system of exchange students could be developed. 

There are countless capable men in the United States who would be eager to teach and study at a disarmament institute. The curriculum is not difficult to devise. An outstanding director could be secured for a whole year for less than it costs for the pay of one general in the Army. The entire program could be operated on an experimental basis for several hundred thousand dollars a year. 

We have spent millions of dollars on experimental bombers and submarines. 

If the United States represents to the rest of the world that we are interested in world peace, we must be willing to study and spend money for new ideas on disarmament just as we do for development of new weapons. 

This subcommittee is urged to consider a proposal to recommend the early organization of a permanent disarmament institute. Such an idea would capture the approval and enthusiasm of peoples throughout the world. 

Such an idea would help demonstrate that the United States is willing to assume moral leadership in today's anxious world.
