STATEMENT OF RUSSELL P. COOPER, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. COOPER. I am Russell Cooper, 1894 Southeast Minneapolis. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. My next-door neighbor. 

Mr. COOPER. While on the staff of the University of Minnesota, I speak of course only as an individual.

Since I have submitted a statement to the committee, I won't take time to read it in detail, but I would like to summarize very briefly simply the two points that I have made in this statement. 

SECURITY THE STUMBLING BLOCK IN DISARMAMENT PLANS 

The first point is that while there has been a good deal of effort at disarmament ever since the Hague Conferences of 1899 and 1907 which were called partly for the purpose of disarmament, all efforts up to this point have proved futile. I think it is important to examine the reasons for the failure of disarmament as well as the possibilities for success because we may get lessons from that experience. 

As one reads the story, particularly at the time of the League of Nations activities when they were seeking for a period of over 10 years to call a disarmament conference and were continually frustrated, it became very clear that the reason for such frustration was because the nations were unwilling to lay down armaments until some kind of security could be given to them to replace the defenses which they were being asked to set aside. Many treaties were proposed such as

the Treaty for Mutual Assistance and the Geneva Protocol of Interna tional Disputes and the Locarno Treaties. 

After the Locarno Treaties were adopted in 1925 and 1926 and went into effect, it looked as though there might be some possible measure of security with the guaranty of the Rhine frontier and France, and our Allies were for the first time willing to participate in a disarmament effort which led to the calling of the disarmament conference in 1932 to 1934.

It was my privilege to spend a year there during that period attending most of the sessions of the conference, studying in detail its possibilities for success and also, as it turned out, the reasons for its failure. 

Among the 2 or 3 reasons for its failure I should say the major one was simply this problem of security. The nations were simply as yet unwilling to lay down their arms until they had something which they felt was reliable, that they could turn to for their defenses thereafter. 

If this is the case, and if this has been true with the United Nations, as I think it has, similarly, I think then we should ask ourselves whether it is possible to approach security by degrees just as we have talked about approaching disarmament by degrees. 

THE GRADUAL APPROACH TO SECURITY 

If security is the great stumbling block perhaps we don't have to get it all overnight but perhaps we could approach it one step at a time, and it would seem to me that the situation is ripe today, when we have a number of minor disputes around over the world, to demonstrate whether it is possible to set forth a security system that can really be operated.

I think particularly, right now for example, of the dispute between the Arabs and Israel where you have two nations with age-long conflicts seeking now to work out their problems but each desperately fearful of the other, each unwilling to make serious concessions for fear it might weaken their security as against the other. 

Supposing we had a similar situation here in the Twin Cities where possibly we had a dispute between St. Paul and Minneapolis, maybe over the reversions of waters to the Mississippi River. Possibly Minneapolis would like to use waters for industrial development at the expense of St. Paul industries downstream. Now if each party became intransigent in this struggle and it looked as though Minneapolis by its diversion was going to create widespread unemployment in the St. Paul area, this could degenerate into a time of anarchy, confusion, rioting and actual bloodshed as armed bands between the two partieslabor, industrial and political leaders--found themselves warring over these water rights.

It is like the problem you have with the Jordan River today. What would we do in Minnesota if this should happen? Would we arm the marauding gangs on both sides in order that we could have equality of armaments and maintain a balance of power? I think not. Would we call on Duluth or Milwaukee or some neighboring city to come in and support one side or the other? I think not.

We would ask the Governor to call out the State militia and restore order where anarchy was now prevailing, and the State militia would remain in the area until the warring parties were disarmed and until

the various forces were required to come to the courts that were available and settle their disputes equitably in that fashion. 

Neither side would probably be satisfied with the solution realized, but it would be a workable system and it would be a system that would replace the anarchy theretofore prevailing. 

I would suggest that a system of this kind could be employed in the Israel-Arab area and I don't know of any other system that can be applied with equal likelihood of success. The sending in of British or American marines, as has been proposed, would almost surely be balanced by the proposal of bringing in marines from the Eastern nations. Certainly we would be accused of seeking an imperialistic prize as a part of our motivation.

POLICING TROUBLE SPOTS WITH NEUTRAL FORCES 

The only way in which this situation can be pacified through outside action would seem to be by neutral impartial forces being brought in. And if they were brought in, and it became clear they were going to remain in the Gaza area and other points until peace were restored and then each nation could disarm with the understanding that it need have no fear from its antagonists thereafter. If that happened, we could then get a measure of disarmament in the Near Eastern area as between Israel and Egypt and the other Arab states. 

If it can be done there it can be done in Indochina, or possibly in Korea, or in other areas which are tension spots around the world, and I would suggest that possibly we can begin at this stage with the development not of a universal world police force but with a smaller police force which is capable of at least policing the smaller areas and smaller disputes of the world, thereby gaining experience as to the problems and the techniques of such police activity, and thereby also demonstrating to the peoples of the world the fact that there is a possibility of a security system other than national armaments for defense.

SYSTEM OF WORLD LAW VERSUS ANARCHY 

If this were done one could then have hope I think that the anarchy which now prevails in the world might gradually give way to larger and larger systems of law, until eventually even the largest nations would be willing to reduce their armaments because there would be in the world an international force sufficiently strong to come to their defense.

Now I recognize that this would involve some modification of the present United Nations although I think not much, but it would involve some.

I think there is not time today to go into the technicalities of that, but these are questions which men of good will, if they are really intent upon disarmament, I think can cope with and solve. 

My major point is that I believe that efforts at disarmament without making a constructive and imaginative approach to the security issue are going to be doomed to failure as they have been doomed for the last 60 years.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Cooper, first of all I want to express my gratitude to you for your very farseeing proposal and also your thoughtful suggestions. Again may I say that this is why the sub-

committee wants these hearings. I regret that I am the only Senator here today at this time, but I assure you and all of the witnesses, as I said earlier, that this will be carefully reviewed by others. 

SECURITY REGULATIONS AND THERMONUCLEAR WEAPONS 

The point that appears to me in all of the hearings that I have participated in is that the people of the world just do not seem to comprehend what has happened. I am of the opinion that some of the security regulations pertaining to thermonuclear weapons have almost kept back from the people of the world the terrible consequences that are before us.

I venture to say that there is not 1 person in 10,000 that has any appreciation whatsoever of the destructive capacity we already have in the stockpile. May I say most candidly, from my own point of information, I have every reason to believe that the Soviet Union has developed weapons equally strong and may very well have some lead time on us in certain other weapons. I don't think people are really going to come to grips with this problem until they understand what is at stake, what could happen and what might happen. 

Mr. COOPER. I agree. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I hesitate to say that one should use the specter of fear as an incentive to creative thought, but it is not unfair to say that before one makes any modification of existing institutions, he has to reconcile his plans to limitations which the present world situation imposes, I for one do not believe that the people have the slightest idea of what is going on.

I showed a film, for example, in this area of the tests in Bikini 2 years ago around the county fairs, before clubs, and people. The audiences were amazed at the power and potential devastation, yet this film had nothing of any consequence in terms of what really has happened. If people could hear some of the things we have heard, I think they would have a better understanding. 

We welcome your suggestions. I want to say that a boundary commission between the United States and Canada has settled innumerable disputes. We are not dealing, however, with Canada. 

This is just one point I want to make. 

SEIZING THE INITIATIVE FOR PEACE 

You said men of good will. To ascertain the good will of the totalitarian states is indeed a very difficult assignment. I do feel, however, that by constantly pressing forward, you have an opportunity to test sincerity, and what I seek for our Government is to be on the psychological and political offensive. 

Mr. COOPER. I think that is very important, Senator.

Chairman HUMPHREY. It seems to me that one of the major requirements at the moment is to seize the initiative again for peace in the world which we seem to be in danger of losing to the Soviet Union. 

Some imaginative bold proposal such as this would help in that direction. 

I heard 2 days ago that we did not drop the first H-bomb from an airplane. Did you read that any place?

The Soviet Union dropped the H-bomb several weeks before we did and perfected the dropping of a thermonuclear weapon from airplanes before we did. When we dropped ours it was 4 miles off course. 

This information took time to get out too. I don't know why the 

American people are not entitled to know these things. By the way, you would be interested to know it was in my hearings on the security questions in our Government that we finally got the information as to fallout. This was being withheld from the American people, the impact of fallout. In fact the doctors in charge of civilian defense in the United States came before my committee and wanted to know why they were not being told about the dangers of fallout. 

Do you know where we found out about it? From a group of 

Japanese doctors touring throughout the world, making speeches telling about the pattern of fallout in the Pacific area. After the 

Japanese doctors had exposed it to the whole world, our Government finally admitted it happened. 

Are we so immature that we cannot hear these things?

I think people have to know the horrible truth and sometimes the kind truth too. There are some horrible truths to know and I don't think we are going to do very much about any of these things until we realize the alternatives. 

Thank you, Mr. Cooper. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Cooper is as follows:) A FRESH LOOK AT DISARMAMENT 

While modern technology has made disarmament critically important for national survival, the lesson of history shows it is very difficult to achieve. Indeed, save for very limited and temporary reductions at the Washington Naval Conference in 1921-22, all efforts at international agreement for disarmament in modern times have proved futile.

HISTORY SHOWS DISARMAMENT WITHOUT NATIONAI. SECURITY DOOMED TO FAILURE 

As one studies the records of past disarmament efforts he soon discovers that they have foundered primarily on the rocks of national security. Governments have been unwilling to surrender tangible weapons of immediate defense, even though admittedly fallible, unless they could be assured that these defensive weapons would be replaced by other guarantees that would clearly protect their security. Until the present time, no such guarantees have been forthcoming and no disarmament has proved possible.

For example, at the Hague Conferences of 1899 and 1907 there initially was considerable hope for disarmament but when the only political guarantees forthcoming were those of the Hague Court of Arbitration, with neither compulsory jurisdiction nor means of enforcement, the nations failed to disarm and indeed soon embarked on a military and a naval race which led directly to the tensions of 1914.

The limited reduction of capital ships and aircraft carriers at the Washington Naval Conference in 1921-22 was achieved only after a Five-Power Treaty and a Nine-Power Treaty were drafted to promote security in the Pacific. But again these pacts were only consultative in nature with no reliable guarantees of aid to ward off possible aggressors. Hence the nations soon embarked on cruiser and submarine building programs to strengthen their defenses and the game of competitive armament went on.

The League of Nations Covenant recognized in article & that "the maintenance of peace required the reduction of national armaments to the lowest point consistent with national safety and the enforcement by common action of international obligations." However, the peace machinery set up under articles 10 to 

17 was so weak and unpredictable in nature that no nation regarded it as an adequate substitute for its own military defenses. Indeed, the Third Assembly recognized the realities of the situation clearly in its Resolution 14 which declared: "In the present state of the world, many governments would be unable to

accept the responsibility for a serious reduction of armaments unless they received in exchange a satisfactory guaranty of the safety of their country." 

During the 1920's, delegates to the League of Nations strove valiantly to close the gaps in the covenant and to provide nations with a sufficient sense of security that they would be willing to undertake a measure of disarmament. Thus the 

Draft Treaty of Mutual Assistance of 1923, the Draft Protocol for the Pacific 

Settlement of International Disputes of 1924, and the Locarno treaties of 1925, were all efforts to this end. Through the Locarno treaties, actually negotiated outside the League of Nations, Great Britain and Italy guaranteed to protect the Rhine Frontier and France and her allies for the first time began to talk seriously of disarmament. Indeed they participated in the discussions of the League's 

Preparatory Commission which led directly to the Geneva Disarmament Conference of 1932-34. For a time it appeared that the Geneva Disarmament Conference might prove successful, particularly in view of America's participation and our declaration of May 22, 1933, that the United States would participate on a consultative basis in security efforts if disarmament were achieved. However, the rise of Hitler in Germany and the aggressions of Japan in Manchuria demonstrated so clearly the essential weaknesses of the League security system that all nations ran to cover, abandoned their disarmament efforts, and began feverishly to build their several national defenses-a natural reaction of fearful nations but one that failed dismally to keep the peace. 

Efforts at disarmament within the United Nations have been equally unavailing. To be sure, the intimate link between security and disarmament was recognized more clearly than in the League Covenant, for article 47 of the U. N. 

Charter authorized a Military Staff Committee to make plans for action against potential aggressors and thus to permit "the regulation of armaments, and possible disarmament." But the veto powers in the Security Council and the limited functions of the Assembly have so deprived the United Nations of quick and decisive action that no nation feels able to disarm and throw its hope for security solely upon the United Nations. The General Assembly defined the problem in 1948 when it declared that before substantial disarmament could take place there must first be found a substitute method of security through placing national armed forces at the call of the Security Council as provided in article 43. 

WORLD SECURITY SYSTEM NOW TIED TO NATIONAL ARMAMENTS 

Strangely enough, this very defense system, as contemplated in the United Nations Charter, tends to militate against disarmament. This is because of the paradox that the national armaments which tend to create fears and insecurities in the world are also the ones that the United Nations must call upon to effect sanctions in case of aggression. To the extent that nations are brought to disarm, therefore, the military forces at the disposal of the United Nations are weakened, the international defenses available to nations are therefore jeopardized and a restraint is placed on their further disarmament. This anomalous paradox of the world security system being tied to national armaments and in a sense therefore contributing itself to continued insecurity can be remedied, but only by a drastic revision of the conception of the United Nations military contingent.

INSPECTION BLOCKED BY MUTUAL DISTRUST 

While extensive and commendable efforts are being employed by various governments in seeking ways to reduce armaments and to establish watertight systems of inspection, these activities continue to be blocked by mutual mistrust. 

Nations are too fearful that the enemy will succeed in concealing some weapons or in precipitating a sneak attack which would leave its disarmed adversary helpless. Surely some new, imaginative and bold system of security must be established if the long frustrated efforts at disarmament are finally to be successful. 

The world today is in a state of lawlessness similar to the old western frontier now so familiar to TV children. Men on the frontier insisted on carrying their own guns for defense until sheriffs were elected, courts established, laws clearly defined, and a man's security was thus protected by community agencies. With the coming of such security, sidearms were quickly abandoned and individual disarmament achieved. Similarly the quarreling 13 States in the 1780's were brought to lay down their arms against each other when a national system of protection was established under the Federal Constitution of 1789. While the international situation is admittedly more complicated than this American analogy, the principle surely is clear that disarmament is possible only when people can be assured that their basic interests will be protected in some other manner.

The present proposals before the United Nations Disarmament Commission, even the imaginative suggestions of President Eisenhower for photographing and blueprinting military installations of possible adversaries, fail to give the kind of security required. Such proposals are of course constructive and should be effected if possible, but lessons of history indicate that they are not likely to result in substantial disarmament by anyone. 

Now from all this it seems logical to conclude that universal disarmament can be achieved only through a drastic strengthening of the United Nations in such fashion that an international police force is continually on hand to apprehend the violators of disarmament agreements and to restrain potential aggressors, making it unnecessary for nations to establish these protections for themselves. 

There is some evidence that the common people of many countries are ready for this next step. However, it is doubtful whether governments will put their faith in a new and untried international police system until its merit has been decisively demonstrated.

U. N. EXPERIMENTAL POLICING OF DANGER SPOTS CAN SERVE AS PILOT PROJECTS 

The world needs a pilot experience wherein international police forces can relieve tension in a danger spot, manifesting the worthwhileness of the police idea and gaining technical and organizational lessons that can be used on a wider scale later on.

It happens that a desperate need for precisely this kind of international intervention exists at the moment in the Israel-Arab controversy. While the recent visit of the U. N. Secretary-General has effected an uneasy truce, the basic causes of tension remain and a war could break out at any time. Such a war might easily engulf Russia and the Western nations and bring on World War III with its attendant atomic horrors.

How can the world make sure that the fuse to World War III is extinguished in the Near East? Does the policy of arming both sides yield any hope for relief from fear and tension? On the other hand would the landing of British or 

American marines to preserve order be any better solution? Not only would this latter alternative be a most unpopular investment of American lives and treasure but would lay us open to the charge that we are engaged in an imperialistic grab for Middle-Eastern oil and strategic position. Surely something better is needed.

HYPOTHETICAL DISPUTE OVER MISSISSIPPI WATERS 

Let us compare the Near-Eastern conflict with a hypothetical dispute that might arise between Minneapolis and St. Paul over the waters of the Mississippi 

River. Suppose that Minneapolis should declare that it must divert the waters of the Mississippi for the use of new industrial developments even though this would be at the expense of present and future St. Paul industries a few miles downstream. Both cities regard these waters as essential to their development, just as Israel and the Arabs regard the Jordan waters as essential. 

And suppose 

that each of our Twin Cities uncompromisingly demanded its rights, that industrial, labor and political leaders entered the fray, and that armed gangs attacked each other at night in the "midway Gaza district,' that law broke down and anarchy took over? What would be the solution? Would it be to arm the forces of both sides in order that they would have closer balance of power? Or should 

Duluth or Milwaukee intervene on one side or the other to restore order? Hardly. 

We would expect the Governor of Minnesota to immediately call out the State militia, to disarm the marauding gangs, punish all individuals engaged in violence, and insist upon submitting the entire dispute to the courts for legalistic and equitable settlement. The resulting solution would probably not be fully satisfactory to either side but it would be workable and most important of all anarchy would be ended and peace based upon law would be restored. 

It is proposed that precisely the same action is now required for the Israel- Arab dispute. An international police force should be organized at once composed of nationals from small neutralist nations and commended by officers from small countries. The function of the police force would be to move into the Gaza area and all other troubled spots, arresting any lawbreakers and protecting both sides from further aggression. The police force would make clear its intention to remain until peace and order were fully established and then would turn over to the International Court of Justice or some other arbitral body the responsibility of working out a suitable solution to the controversy. The aggressiveness of the parties would now be transferred to the courtroom, armed action would clearly be futile, and fear of attack would at last become groundless. Under such circumstances and only such can we expect Israel and the Arabs to disarm.

ORGANIZATION AND UTILIZATION OF U. N. POLICE FORCE 

Now to achieve even this limited police activity in the Near East, some important developments in the United Nations structure must take place. The new police force should properly be placed under the General Assembly which is freed from veto restrictions and is more responsive to the peace hunger of the great masses of the world's peoples. Moreover the police action would require heavy financing running into several billions of dollars, far beyond the $50 million per year that the United Nations is able to beg now from its member states. То raise such sums, the U. N. must be given access to tax funds resting upon nongovernmental activities. There are many possible sources of such funds and they need not be elaborated here. One might note, however, that the Baruch plan which called for the internationalization of atomic energy under the United Nations, might be reactivated with the assumption that the United Nations would not only protect the atom from abuse but could help to establish atomic power projects throughout the world and gain vast returns from the sale of power. 

Similarly, a tax on international commerce and carriers collected readily at ports of entry would yield large sums corresponding to revenues from the national taxation of imports and interstate carriers. Surely the financing of international action is possible if men are sufficiently concerned and imaginative to work out the details.

Obviously if the international police system were made to work effectively in relieving Near Eastern tensions and achieving disarmament in that area, the same forces would also be available for maintaining the peace in Africa, Indochina, Korea and any other tension spots which may become so inflammatory as to require police activity. The experience thus gained through moderate and limited action would form a sound base for further expansion of the United Nations police system. With its growing prestige and universal popular gratitude for its prevention of wars, nations would come to look to the United Nations for security and would accept a still larger United Nations police force. Then a general reduction of armaments would become possible even among the great powers. Each would disarm confident of its security in the same fashion that most American citizens will go to sleep tonight without wishing a gun under their pillow.

A proposal of this character would be a decisive even though limited step for preserving peace. It would make possible disarmament that the world is anxious for and would restore to America the moral leadership for peace which at the moment seems likely to be grasped by the Russians. In making this proposal, America would simply be following her own tradition of establishing Federal agencies to replace interstate conflict and we would be giving to the world a tested and proven method for peace that could be built on and perfected through the years to come.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Richard Newby? 

STATEMENT OF RICHARD P. NEWBY, MINNEAPOLIS FRIENDS MEET- ING OF THE RELIGIOUS SOCIETY OF FRIENDS, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. NEWBY. I am pastor of the Friends Church in Minneapolis and 

I speak for the Friends Church, Senator Humphrey. "War is sin" is the judgment rendered by the Christian churches individually and by the great ecumenical gatherings of recent years. 

From the beginning of the Religious Society of Friends many Friends have been led not to take part voluntarily in war or preparation for it. Our involvement with all mankind has led us to be not merely conscientious objectors but also to work positively to prevent war. 

Under the guidance and power of God's spirit, mankind can have freedom from the scourge of war. We have the knowledge of political institutions, the means of communication and resources to provide national security without resort to military violence or its threat. 

Mankind, led by the United States, has developed atom and hydrogen bombs capable of destroying much of our civilization, and is

rapidly on the way toward perfecting intercontinental missiles against which there is no foreseeable defense. This situation points to the great truth and great urgency in President Eisenhower's statement that "there is no alternative to peace." 

NEED OF MORE PROPHETIC LEADERSHIP 

The world desperately needs more prophetic leadership. This leadership should be directed toward a strengthened United Nations; toward more abundant sharing of our economic resources, technical assistance and agricultural surpluses; toward more liberal trade relations, more exchange of students and others, more provision for education at home and abroad. 

However, priority must now be given by our Government toward taking a much bolder lead in universal disarmament and the renunciation of war itself. 

One not in on the intimate discussions between nations cannot be too dogmatic about the most promising means of making progress, but one feels there is value in trying to combine the best features of the various disarmament proposals before the General Assembly of the United Nations. Disarmament discussions have been characterized often by too much propaganda and too little negotiation. I would like to add here we are told from the London Conference there was much more negotiation and not so much propaganda. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Very, very definitely so.

Mr. NEWBY. And I think we should be encouraged by that. 

One suggestion would be that the United States couple its aerial inspection plan with a program along the lines of the British-French proposal of March 29, 1955, for the reduction of forces of the United States, the Soviet Union, and China down to a level in the range of 1 million to 12 million, and 650,000 for France and the United Kingdom.

IMPORTANCE OF POLITICAL SETTLEMENTS 

We must also strive harder for political settlements which point to or include steps toward disarmament. This would include such matters as the unification of Germany, the unification of Korea, the totalitarian threat of communism to democratic liberties around the world, the question of military alliances, the question of supplying arms to the Middle East, the future of Formosa, and the role of China in the Far East and in the United States. These questions could be more easily solved within the framework of a disarmament agreement. 

PROPOSED STEPS TOWARD DISARMAMENT 

Pending a general disarmament agreement, it is also important to take positive action in some area on the disarmament problem. The following proposals should merit close attention: 

Cessation of experimental explosions of nuclear weapons. 

Establishment of an armaments "truce" or freeze with adequate safeguards under which there would be no expansion of military forces or increase in military budgets, or further production of atomic weapons. 

Unification and disarmament of presently divided countries, thus providing an opportunity to ease tension and work out inspection and other procedures on a pilot project basis.

Agreement not to be the first to use weapons of mass destruction. 

Begin disarmament in areas where adequate inspection is now possible. 

Let us also encourage more contacts and exchange of persons and ideas. The United States and the Soviet Union as well as other countries in the U. N. this last fall referred to the fact that an increase in mutual confidence and trust among the great powers will greatly hasten the reaching of a disarmament accord. 

The role of armaments in our economy and the spreading military influence, while more indirectly related to disarmament, are highly significant in indicating potential resistance, either conscious or unconscious, to the realization of the goal of disarmament. 

These factors may mean that pressure in the business community, press, education circles, labor's ranks, and in the minds of ordinary citizens is being built up to buttress the growing Pentagon resistance to disarmament.

They lend weight to the widespread belief that the way to achieve peace is to be fully prepared for war. 

It is our firm conviction, however, that the United States can offer leadership in steps toward the reduction of armaments. We encourage this Senate committee in its efforts, with full confidence that peace is the will of God. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much, Reverend

Newby. I think one point needs to be brought out from your general observations.

THE INTERNATIONAL STATUS OF COMMUNIST CHINA 

I have visited the exhibits, we have had technicians discuss aerial inspection and reconnaissance and aerial photography by the hour. I think we have a good working knowledge of what it is all about. The proposal was not only aerial inspection but also an exchange of blueprints and ground inspection, and mobile teams that move rapidly from one point or another. It is an intricate work of inspection apparatus.

I wonder how many Americans have thought air inspection is going to work just between the United States and the Soviet Union if there is no aerial inspection, for example, of Red China. How are you going to negotiate aerial inspection with Red China, in the light of our diplomatic or nondiplomatic relationships? I only want to emphasize that we must face these problems. I asked the Secretary of 

State about this. He suggested that we drop the discussion at this point. 

Now don't misunderstand me, I am not advocating recognition.

I do think, however, we Americans have to be willing to talk these things out. I have many reasons for not supporting diplomatic recognition of Red China in the light of her past intransigence and attitudes, but I think if you are going to discuss disarmament, you must discuss all of it, including China's international status. 

Nuclear weapons or thermonuclear weapons are not always going to be just in the hands of the United States, the U. S. S. R., and Great 

Britain. Maybe France, maybe Germany, maybe the Argentine, maybe India, maybe somebody else. I mean all of these are potentialities and possibilities.

The strange thing about all these industrial and technical developments is that they have a way of springing up all over the world about the same time. There seems to be a pattern of simultaneous development among the highly industrialized nations. 

INSPECTION OF ALL POSSIBLE AREAS OF ATTACK ON THE U. S. 

We must not deal with only part of the facts, because when we deal with only part of the facts we are placing our lives in danger. For example, I could never approve an aerial inspection system unless I was sure that we were going to have aerial inspection of every possible area of attack against our country, because we know that an attack could be launched from the most unlikely areas. It is not entirely improbable that attack could be launched from Latin American and South American countries if they should fall into unfriendly hands. 

I just mention these things. I think people need to think about this. I am not drawing judgments. I am trying to be provocative. 

Mr. NEWBY. I think we should be encouraged by the steps that are being taken.

I still feel that we should provide as much opportunity as possible to give our money and our time and our efforts to this great problem before us, which is disarmament, and I would encourage you in your interests. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much.

Dr. Charles J. Turck. Dr. Turck, we welcome you before the subcommittee in the presence of just its chairman at the moment, but I am very pleased to have you testify.

STATEMENT OF DR. CHARLES J. TURCK, THE COMMITTEE ON WORLD PEACE, MINNESOTA COUNCIL OF CHURCHES 

Mr. TURCK. I would like to speak on behalf of the Committee on 

World Peace of the Minnesota Council of Churches, a committee of 52 persons drawn from 14 Protestant denominations. Their purpose is to express the deep concern of the church for peace and to stimulate study and action with regard to disarmament and other problems related to the achievement of peace.

I can assure you that disarmament is no new concern within the Judeo-Christian heritage. Many centuries ago the prophet Micah declared- they shall beat their swords into plowshares, and their spears into pruning hooks nation shall not lift up a sword against nation, neither shall they learn war any more (Micah 4:3).

I shall quote sections from An Affirmation of a Christian Policy for Peace, a statement prepared by the Committee on World Peace and approved by the unanimous vote of the Minnesota Council of Churches at its annual meeting of February 1956.

This statement represents the careful and considered convictions of dedicated Christian leaders from the 11 member denominations of the council having more than 300,000 confirmed (or communicant) members. You will find this Affirmation included in the Congressional Record of March 1, 1956 (through the kindness of Senator Humphrey).

SUPPORT OF THE UNITED NATIONS BY UNITED STATES 

The first proposition I make is that our Government in the matter of disarmament should work in and through the United Nations. 

The United Nations is the only practical alternative we have today to world anarchy, and the only hope that the nations may develop a system of world law.

We cannot say that a proposal advanced by the United Nations or by any one of them, including Russia, should be accepted by the American Government, but we can say that it should be courteously received and carefully studied. 

I quote from the affirmation: 

We call upon our Government at all times to demonstrate clearly our faith in united action under law by working in and through the United Nations to relieve international tension and establish the moral authority of law. 

We believe that our foreign policy should be conducted always in ways consistent with the United Nations Charter and that we ourselves should lead the way in using the United Nations processes of conciliation and arbitration. 

OBJECTIVE: MUTUAL DISARMAMENT WITH EFFECTIVE GUARANTIES 

Second, I speak on behalf of the general ideal that disarmament must be brought about by mutual agreements and step-by-step reduction in the armed strength of the great powers, accompanied with effective guaranties. To say that such guaranties cannot be given or relied upon is to admit the defeat of all possible efforts even before they begin. Unilateral disarmament is regarded as folly, but stubborn persistence in an arms race that is already costing this country $36 billion a year is likewise folly and could be equally destructive of our rights and liberties.

I quote from the affirmation: 

Believing that the present armament race, like all others, can lead only to increased suspicion and fear and to the threat of another war, we pledge ourselves to support all efforts toward mutual disarmament with effective guaranties—including the renunciation of nuclear weapons that would threaten the very existence of life upon the earth. We note with gratitude in President Eisenhower's state of the Union message that "we shall persevere in seeking a general reduction of armament under effective inspection and control." "Effective guaranties" might well include such steps as enforcement through international courts, the provision of United Nations inspection teams empowered to go anywhere, plus continual scientific search for methods to make possible effective inspection. In the reduction of arms there may well be risks of evasion, but in the continuance of the arms race there is the risk of total disaster.

ABOLISHMENT OF WEAPONS OF MASS DESTRUCTION 

The third proposition I make is that the United States should take the lead in gaining universal assent to the abolition of all weapons of mass destruction. There was a time when the foreign policy of the 

United States was distinguished by its unfailing advocacy of measures that would protect civilians from the ravages of war. 

For example, the Hague Convention and the rules of land and sea warfare were largely influenced by American advocates of mercy, like Elihu Root in one generation and Woodrow Wilson in another. What 72880-56-pt. 9- -6

a strange reversal of role it is that the United States is now described as the advocate of the mass destruction of civilians by hydrogen and atomic bombs, and Russia is the nation that appears to protest against such mass slaughter.

We know that this is not the true picture, but it is the picture that has been created in the minds of the majority of the human race. 

Our Government is strangely insensitive to this misrepresentation, which requires instant and repeated refutation. 

I quote from the affirmation: 

We remind our churches of the united declaration of the Second Assembly of the World Council of Churches at Evanston in which Christians from every part of the earth called for "the prohibition of all weapons of mass destruction; including atomic and hydrogen bombs, with provision for international inspection and control, together with the drastic reduction of all other armaments." 

BUILDING FOR PEACE AT HOME AND ABROAD 

It may well be added that we believe steps toward disarmament. ought to be an integral part of a total effort to relieve international tension, allay fear and build for peace. Along with the blueprints for disarmament we need to continually develop blueprints for peaceful means of settling conflicts between nations and creating law and order in an interdependent world community. 

Here we may call upon the combined wisdom of political scientists and experts in international law-somewhat in the manner of the physical scientists of the world who met in Geneva in 1955 to pool their scientific knowledge.

Along with blueprints for disarmament there may well be drafted a blueprint for economic conversion to full-scale production for human welfare embracing such fields as slum clearance; the construction of highways, schools and more adequate public institutions; the development of the resources of the so-called backward areas of the earth; and the provision in our land as well as abroad of educational programs that build in the minds of men the defenses of peace. Here we may call on the economists, the leaders of industry and labor, to address their talents to the adjustments necessary to develop economic stability in a world of peace. 

I quote again from the affirmation:

We find ourselves also in complete agreement with the proposal of President Eisenhower in his five-point program for peace (as stated April 16, 1953) that a substantial part of the funds saved through disarmament may well be used in an all-out effort to work with underprivileged nations in banishing forever hunger and disease.

We believe that the people of America-and of the world—will respond to leadership willing to explore with persistence and daring such new and untried ways. We will follow paths that are dangerous and full of problems if our leaders are "thinking ahead" with courage and vision and the actions taken by them are founded on basic concepts of freedom, law, responsibility and justice. 

It has been a privilege to present to this committee the views of the church groups who value peace and brotherhood above all human conditions and who seek to promote mutual disarmament of the nations as a way that leads toward peace and brotherhood. God helping us, we cannot do otherwise.

EDUCATION FOR DISARMAMENT 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to say to Doctor Turck that we had some excellent testimony from the representatives of the National Council of Churches of Christ at the hearing in Washington, and at that time we discussed informally the urgent necessity of the churches in their adult groups, and even from the pulpit, of trying to explain not only the desire for disarmament but also the problems involved in it, the relationship of disarmament to a total national foreign policy or international policies on the part of our Government. 

In other words it is necessary to take this subject and give it the mature discussion that it needs. We can do great harm to ourselves and to others by just talking in favor of disarmament without appreciating the complexity of it. 

Mr. TURCK. That is correct; that needs education as well as preaching.

Chairman HUMPHREY. It does need it. Father Conway, very active in this field, has worked with this subcommittee and me. I have attended informal gatherings in Washington of top businessmen as well as intellectual leaders throughout the country who are giving disarmament education their time and energy. 

There is a good deal of work being done on it. It is not as yet formalized into a final pattern, but we feel we are moving along the right way.

We are hopeful that through this subcommittee we can establish an advisory board of top people throughout this country that are willing to give patriotically and voluntarily, their advice and counsel, because I cannot help but believe that by thinking constructively along these lines we do make an impact.

SOUTH DAKOTA CHOIR SINGS FOR VOICE OF AMERICA 

For example, these hearings are reported overseas, and it is very encouraging, may I say. The Voice of America has recorded many of our hearings. I wish they were here today. 

In fact, I think it is more important for the Voice of America to be out with us when we are in the field than when we are back in Washington. I recall I heard comments about some movies that had been sent overseas, American movies that portrayed American youth in what I considered to be a very unkindly and unfair manner. 

I became disturbed about it and I just happened to be down in a neighboring community in South Falls, S. Dak. I heard a choir from Washington High School sing, and I want to tell you it did something to a man. It made you feel almost reborn. It was terrific. 

I got on the telephone and called up the Voice of America and told them I wanted them to come out and record this choir and I said, "I want this to be the Voice of America instead of some of the stuff we are hearing." Believe me, they accepted the suggestion. It was played over the air waves all over the world.

Those little things may not amount to much for the moment, but they have an impact, and the voice of the people at these hearings has an impact. Mark my word that these hearings are read and read carefully, and in great areas of the world the voices of witnesses before

these hearings will have as much impact as the voice of generals and even diplomats.

Mr. TURCK. The church people are very grateful for the privilege of making this statement this afternoon. Thank you, Senator.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I see my friend York Langton is here. He is a busy man and I told him we would pick him up just as soon as he came in. Mr. Langton.

STATEMENT OF YORK LANGTON, REGIONAL CHAIRMAN OF AMERI- CAN ASSOCIATION FOR THE UNITED NATIONS, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. LANGTON. Senator Humphrey and friends, my name is York 

Langton. I live at 4301 Abbott Avenue, South Minneapolis. I serve as the regional chairman of the American Association for the United Nations which is Wisconsin, North and South Dakota, and Montana. 

Minnesota works directly with our national office but on the authority of the president, Dr. Jerge, who is in the room.

I speak also for the Minnesota United Nations Association. In business I work for coast-to-coast stores covering 16 States and have a chance to meet many people. I have spoken, had the privilege of speaking, to over a thousand audiences about the United Nations since 1943.

In fact, Senator Humphrey, that is when I first met you 

Chairman HUMPHREY. That is correct. 

Mr. LANGTON. When you were working for world organization. 

And I have not had any serious arguments with anyone about the 

When you talk about Russia's changing, I think we could analyze our own experience back in 1920. We couldn't muster enough support to join the League of Nations, one of the tragedies of history. 

Now we were told this morning 90 percent of the American people favor the idea of United Nations, and of what we are trying to do. 

Our association strongly commends your Disarmament Subcommittee for conducting these hearings, and it is a tribute to you, Senator Humphrey, who started these hearings, created the idea. We are grateful for the work you have done.

Senator Wiley is not here but I want to say if you will pass it along, we are very deeply grateful for the work he has done. I have been in Wisconsin on many occasions and he has gone from Ashland to Jamesville, from Milwaukee to Hudson across the river, talking about the United Nations and world organization. 

Betty Goetz, the last time we had her here in Minnesota she helped conduct a series of conferences in the upper Midwest, so your gain was our loss.

CHANGING CONCEPTS IN THE SOVIET UNION 

We believe that every student of world affairs knows that an atomic war would be mutual suicide. Mr. Stassen said at our recent United 

Nations conference at Washington at which Senator Humphrey shared the honor of speaking that afternoon and gave a brilliant presentation, Mr. Stassen said he believes even the Russians understand that atomic war would be mutual suicide.

Now, they are changing, I am sure. They for many years would not give any funds for technical aid. A couple of years ago they gave a million dollars. We didn't know what to think about it, and it was the pressure of public opinion from Asia that perhaps influenced the Russians to change their thinking. 

So on the basis that they have minds, that they are reasoning people to some degree, it seems to me that changes the picture. If they recognize that atomic war is mutual suicide, then it seems to me we ought to move ahead in this field of disarmament. 

COST OF WORLD ARMAMENTS 

Today 85 cents out of every dollar that we pay as income taxes goes to pay for either past wars or a defense against a possible future war. 

Our defense bill today is $40 billion a year. That is $200 for every man, woman and child in America, and the world bill for armaments is $100 billion a year and that is about $600 for every man, woman, and child in the United States. If you applied it all to ourselves and the world crisis for just 2 or 3 billion a year of economic aid, an international flow of capital would be established to raise the standard of living.

INSPECTION THROUGH UNITED NATIONS AGENCY 

We believe that a disarmament plan must apply to all nations. 

There must be a United Nations organ to enforce the treaty. Disarmament must be enforceable. Thorough inspection is necessary at all times and at all places. The question was asked at one of the disarmament meetings whether this organ could inspect a button factory. The American delegate said we want to inspect a button factory because we want to make sure it is a button factory, so there cannot be any restrictions on inspection. 

The inspectors ought to have the power to issue cease-and-desist orders where violations are found. Violations must be stopped quickly. A safe method of disarmament would probably be a gradual method.

The armaments race is largely due to fear and mutual suspicion and we must continually and persistently grapple with political problems whose solutions would ease tensions. I often say in the business world it is a problem of getting a meeting of minds. We lack the meeting of minds, and so the situation today merely reflects and manifests the tensions that exist in the world that we must grapple with.

The United Nations must be made stronger. It is hard to see how reductions of armaments can succeed without making the United Nations stronger through strengthening of collective security, the strengthening of international law commission, expanding technical aid through the U. N., setting up of international police force. The 

United States ought to take the lead. We have heard nothing about our willingness to negotiate a treaty to give our Armed Forces in the event of aggression.

We should use the U. N. machinery constantly. We did it recently in Palestine. Our only criticism is we ought to have done it sooner. 

We have no more rights, I presume, in Palestine than the Russians, but the Russians didn't dare veto when we said let the United Nations

send Dag Hammarskjold over because he represents the world organization. 

UNITED STATES LEADERSHIP IN THE WORLD COMMUNITY 

There is a vast difference between the U. N. and unilateral action. 

We should use creative imagination in keeping with the atomic age in which we live. Our thinking is not up to date. We are living in the past in so many cases. This is the age of the world community. We must have the imagination to grapple with problems in that light and seize the initiative against the Communist world in building peace. 

Let us take the lead. In the business world if you try to counter, if you try to meet, you are licked. You have to lead. You have to do better than the other person on your price and the quality of your merchandise. And so let us take the lead, and not be countering what the Communists are doing.

Let's devote one-tenth of 1 percent of our military budget to research for peace. Let's put the best minds to work with ideas. One-tenth of 1 percent of $40 billion would be $40 million. Even a fraction of that should bring creative and imaginative results. 

We see it in the business world. Minnesota Mining, for instance, 70 percent of their products, I understand, are the result of research in the last 10 years. Let's have research, because this armaments race is continuing. I tell you it's like the Secretary of the Air Force said at our 

Veterans' Day just a year ago. He said one bomb dropped today can cause as much destruction, chaos, loss of life, loss of property as all of the bombs dropped in World War II.

Now that is why, Senator Humphrey, you are a symbol and you just can't quit. This is a tough, hard job, but it has to be done and you are doing something for yourself, for us and for all humanity when you carry forward without discouragement this vigorous program until things break our way, and I think it can happen.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. J. Linn Nash. 

I think I ought to say at this point in light of the fact that a number of references have been made to the United States conducting its negotiations on this matter of reduction and control of armaments through the United Nations, that this is now the official policy of our Government.

I think this ought to be a matter of record. We are not conducting what you might call bilateral negotiations as such except as they relate through the activities of the United States subcommittee. The exchanges of correspondence between the President of the United States and Marshal Bulganin, while they are bilateral, are related, of course, directly to our policies in our commitments to the United

STATEMENT OF JAMES LINN NASH, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. NASH. My name is James Linn Nash. I have been a member of several organizations interested in world peace and the United Nations, but I am speaking as an individual today. 

I appreciate this opportunity to address you, Senator Humphrey, as a representative of the Subcommittee on Disarmament of the 

United States Senate. 

ONLY A WORLDWIDE POWER CAN EFFECT DISARMAMENT 

War starts when one nation attacks another nation, or it may be started when one group of nations attacks another group of nations. 

In either case aggression is the spark which sets war's fires burning. 

But, without the ability to make war, nations cannot indulge in it. 

The possession of an adequate supply of modern weapons and a trained personnel to use them is essential to the waging of international conflict. Hence disarmament must be one of the first objectives in a campaign to bring about permanent world peace. But, no nation is going to strip itself of arms while another nation remains armed. 

In this age of the airplane and atom and hydrogen bombs even a small nation well equipped with modern weapons of mass destruction can become a menace as a potential aggressor. Therefore, in order to bring about international disarmament, it is necessary first to establish a worldwide power composed of a large group of nations united in a permanent and unbeatable combination of actual and potential military power and able to make and enforce world law against aggression. 

The United Nations can be transformed into such an organization

by suitably amending its charter. Its present charter, like that of the 

League of Nations, is based on the theory that collective security can be achieved through voluntary cooperative action by member states or nations, particularly the great powers. History shows this cannot be done. Repeated attempts to so achieve it have failed. 

FAILURE OF PARTIAL DISARMAMENT THROUGH TREATIES 

Now does partial disarmament based on treaties which are wholly dependent on the good faith of the contracting parties meet the situation? This has been tried and found wanting. After World War I various attempts were made to forestall another such catastrophe. 

One such attempt was the holding of a disarmament conference in 1920. This conference resulted in an agreement by the great powers, minus Germany which was already disarmed, to reduce their armaments according to a prescribed schedule and ratio. Accordingly, the 

United States and Great Britain destroyed a considerable portion of their own navies, but, there being no provision for international inspection or enforcement, Japan went on secretly building up her armaments and used them effectively in World War II, beginning at Pearl Harbor.

Mere reduction of armaments to some established quota will not suffice to maintain world peace, unless aggression is inhibited. It may temporarily reduce the kind of aggression a nation is able to start, but, once armed aggression begins, armament limitations will no longer be observed and a small war with limited armaments is likely to quickly progress into an all-out war with all weapons. Moreover, the flame of war once ignited may spread throughout the globe. World War I started when Austria-Hungary made certain demands on little Serbia, her neighbor, and World War II began when Hitler's legions invaded neighboring Poland.

OVERALL APPROACH TO DISARMAMENT 

Thus universal, total, and enforceable disarmament is the only way to assure international peace and security. This can best be brought about by so amending the United Nations Charter as to empower that organization to make and enforce laws providing for it. This necessarily implies the power to raise, equip, and maintain armed forces sufficient to enforce world law prohibiting aggression or the threat of aggression. Indeed this is a step which must be taken before the nations will agree to disarm. 

But Russia has steadily opposed the establishment of a United Nations police force and, even in these amiable post-Stalin days, has indicated a preference for settling international questions through direct negotiations with one or more of the Western Powers rather than by dealing with them through the United Nations. This is shown by 

Premier Bulganin's reference to last year's efforts of the U. N. Subcommittee on Disarmament as a waste of time, and his urging the United States, Great Britain, and France to join with Russia in withdrawing their troops from Germany as a step in the right direction. 

All of this shows that, since Russia is one of the Big Five exercising the veto power in the Security Council to which such matters must be referred for action if brought before the United Nations, the Sov-

iets are likely to block any attempt to establish universal, total enforceable disarmament through the United Nations. 

POLICE FORCE: AMENDMENT OF U. N. CHARTER 

Hence, as a first step the U. N. Charter must be amended so the veto will not apply to disarmament, aggression, or the establishment of a powerful U. Ñ. peace force.

There are two ways that can be done and one is by simply inserting in paragraph 2 of article 27 after the words "procedural matters" the words or matters concerning aggression, threats of aggression, disarmament or the establishment of a United States police force. 

The second way would be giving the General Assembly where there is no veto the primary authority to deal with matters involving aggression, threats of aggression, disarmament, or a United Nations peace force. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very, very much, Mr. Nash. We appreciate your comments.

I want the audience to note and the record to note that Mr. Nash appropriately and accurately stated that the representative, the Prime Minister of the Soviet Union, did chastise the U. N. Subcommittee on 

Disarmament. I would hope that we in our country would recognize that the Subcommittee on Disarmament of the United Nations has been pursuing a rather creditable and constructive course. It hasn't had too many results as yet, but it is still in the constructive posture. 

The next witness is Mr. John E. Larson. Is Mr. Larson here?

Mr. Larson is not here. He will be permitted along with Mr.
