Mr. Stanley Platt. 

Mr. Platt, we welcome you to testify.

STATEMENT OF STANLEY K. PLATT, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. PLATT. Thank you, sir. 

Senator Humphrey, my name is Stanley K. Platt. I live at 2002 

West Lake of the Isles Boulevard, Minneapolis, Minn. I am an investment counselor and I speak here as an individual. My remarks refer to a paper that has already been submitted. 

First of all I want to thank you, Senator Humphrey, for the very sincere efforts that you have been making and to this very important problem, and it certainly is my hope that your committee will be able to produce constructive results.

A great deal of time and effort has been spent on this question of disarmament, and I believe that much progress has been made in developing its complicated interrelated nature with many other problems, but nevertheless we realize that there has been much frustration connected with it, and it is in connection with this frustration that I wish to discuss two topics. 

One is the world's trouble spots in relation to disarmament, and the second is the piecemeal as opposed to the overall approach to disarmament.

MAJOR TROUBLE SPOTS OF THE WORLD 

All of us have heard the statement made that little can be done toward disarmament until some of these major trouble spots of the world find a solution: such as the division of Germany, the situation between Israel and the Egyptians, the Kashmir problem, Vietnam, Korea, Singapore, and Cyprus.

Now it just happens if we examine these problems, we find that in each instance, their military importance is of great significance, and that means that a settlement would involve the sacrifice of a military position by one side or the other, and that is why this approach has such great difficulty and why so little success has been developed in this manner.

If we take the example of Cyprus as one among many-and I cite others in my paper-we find that Britain there occupies a military base. They feel it is essential to their security. They say they will not give up their position in Cyprus, but as long as they remain in Cyprus there will be tension and discord as between Britain and Greece and Turkey and also propaganda value to the other side. 

So it develops that if we are to solve some of these local trouble spots, we must develop a system of security which will bring equal security to all nations.

FUNDAMENTAL DIFFICULTIES OF STEP-BY-STEP DISARMAMENT 

Now if we examine this second question, there is the genuine thinking that the approach to this problem should be step by step, and of course there is value, there is logic to this kind of a development, and that was back of the proposals which our Government made for open skies and inspection and all that went with it, and I think that there is value in that and we can say that that might have had more success if the Russian attitude had been different. 

But if we examine that question we will find there is an inherent impasse in that kind of an approach, because we find that any step-bystep approach involves the sacrifice by one side or the other which jeopardizes their military position and therefore they are unwilling to move along in that direction.

Our open skies proposal was turned down by the Russians because secrecy is part of their diplomatic and military policy and they did not want to give that up.

On the other hand, some years ago the proposal was made by the Russians that all the nations give up atomic bombs, but the United States said that we could not give that up because that was our area of strength, that was our basis of security. And so we cannot do so. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I just say at this point I think there was mighty good reason that we did not. 

Mr. PLATT. Yes.

DETERRENT VALUE OF UNITED STATES ATOMIC STOCKPILE 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I think as you indicated we found ourselves with but one means of military strength-namely, the stockpile of limited atomic bombs-while Soviet strength was based on 412 to 5 million men in uniform with powerful mechanized forces and an air force that was much superior to ours.

I think Winston Churchill made it quite clear that he thought that this stockpile, this temporary monopoly upon atomic bombs, may very well have had a decided deterrent effect upon Soviet military aggressive patterns, but that situation has changed now. 

I think that part of our problem today is to recognize that the race of thermonuclear and nuclear weapons is pretty well evening off, at least from the layman's point of view. I don't have accurate figures. 

I don't know how many bombs we have and frankly don't want to know, since it is a matter of high security. 

Mr. PLATT. Yes, sir; and I thoroughly concur in the decision of the

United States Government that we should not give up our atomic bombs, because they have been essential to our security in this kind of a world, and we cannot give them up until we have some other sure, foolproof method of getting security.

But I think that this does point out that this step-by-step approach does have certain basic fundamental difficulties that involve a sacrifice of the military sovereignty of individual nations and the possibility of jeopardizing their position on a military basis. That means that if we are ever to arrive at a foolproof system of disarmament it must be on the basis of equal security for all nations, and that must stress particularly the peaceful settlement of disputes. 

ORGANIZATION TO ENFORCE PEACE MUST PRECEDE DISARMAMENT 

I feel that the peaceful settlement of disputes comes ahead of disarmament, that we must develop the control organ, we must develop a system of law that will provide for the peaceful settlement of disputes, and that we cannot have disarmament until we are sure that disputes can be settled on a peaceful basis. 

Now the Russians made the point that they could not accept inspection until we have a complete disarmament program. It happens that the United States foreign policy for many years now has been one of advocacy of a foolproof system of universal disarmament. That has been stated by both of our Presidents and by many of our officials in the Government.

But again I stress that we must have this control organization before we can effectively talk about disarmament and that step-by-step programs are apt to meet with frustration. I believe that advancing such a program on the part of the United States would have very great moral and legal value. I believe that world opinion would swing in our favor because every nation would find advantages in that position.

I also believe that the Russian leaders are influenced by world public opinion and by the opinion of people within their countries, and that that world public opinion will swing the Russian leaders eventually in support of such a program because such a program opens the way to peace, freedom, and a higher living standard for all peoples. 

Thank you very much. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Platt is as follows:) 

My name is Stanley K. Platt. I am an investment counselor. I plan to comment on two topics: (1) The world's trouble spots and disarmament. (2) Piecemeal versus overall approach to disarmament.

My point of view can be identified by these conclusions:

1. National armed strength is essential until nations and peoples provide a more realistic system of international security against war. 

2. No nation or limited alliance can by its own armed strength assure either lasting peace or security. This means that the only practical way to national security lies in the formulation and enforcement of a system of law which will require the peaceful settlement of disputes and make aggressive war impractical as a result of disarmament operated under controls administered by an international agency. 

3. Soviet leaders are not likely to accept effective armaments control so long as they think that armaments equality or superiority is essential to the success of their foreign policy. Future acceptance of armaments controls by the Russian leaders requires a knowledge on their part that aggression will not pay, and leadership on our part for amendment of the United Nations Charter in a manner which will provide the means and authority to require the peaceful settlement of all disputes between nations, will eliminate the burdens and risks of heavy national armaments and will thus make possible a major advance in living standards and national independence. 

4. Progressive disarmament must come after and not before an agreement on a comprehensive plan to assure the peaceful settlement of disputes has become effective. Until such a comprehensive plan becomes effective, national armaments will continue to be essential to national security. After the establishment of the legal machinery for peaceful settlement of disputes and for control of armaments, national armaments will become increasingly unnecessary. The 

United Nations or some other agency will become the community's agent in protecting every nation against armed aggression by any other nation. 

THE WORLD'S TROUBLE SPOTS AND DISARMAMENT 

For the same reasons that provisions for the peaceful settlement of disputes must precede disarmament, or be agreed upon as part of a plan for progressive disarmament, a solution to the problems involved in many of the trouble spots of the world is also not likely until such an overall plan is agreed upon and made effective.

Cyprus is important to Britain and the West only because of its use as a military base. But in maintaining a base in Cyprus, Britain is antagonizing both Greece and Turkey. No unilateral British decision there can please both countries. By using force to remain in Cyprus, Britain is playing into the hands of those who seek to breed discord and hatred by the charge of imperialism. 

Britain has made it clear that she has no intention of withdrawing from Cyprus. 

A solution there must await an overall plan for peace which will make Cyprus unimportant as a British military base. Cyprus will continue to be important as a military base so long as the settlement of differences between nations is effected by force rather than by peaceful means and so long as the possession of national armaments makes aggression a continuing threat. 

Germany has a military potential which can be used against either Russia or the Western European nations. It is, therefore, unlikely that an agreement for unification of Germany can be arrived at which will satisfy both Russia and the NATO nations. Again, a solution will be postponed until an overall plan for the peaceful settlement of disputes and universal disarmament can be agreed upon and made effective.

Armed conflict between Israel and one or more of the Arab nations will continue to threaten until a just and peaceful basis of settlement is worked out and made enforceable by a United Nations peace force of sufficient size and backing to command respect from both sides. If the solution is dictated by the major powers, resentment will smolder and continue to threaten the outbreak of war.

But if the solution results from application of an overall plan for the peaceful settlement of international disputes, then the solution will also be honorable to both of the peoples most vitally concerned and peace will have a firm foundation.

Pakistan and India both claim Kashmir. In addition to questions of selfdetermination there are other complications. Our military alliance with Pakistan and supply of arms has aroused the resentment of India, whose friendship we cherish and seek because it is one of the great democratic governments of the world. Our military strategy may be losing us a friend in our fight for freedom from Communist domination. Fear and resentment between India and Pakistan will continue and will carry with it a major risk to our interests until an overall plan can become effective which will make impractical military aggression by one country against another and until disputes, such as the one over Kashmir,

can be settled as part of the regular processes of international law agreed to in advance by all nations as the price for peace. 

Talks on Singapore self-rule have collapsed because self-rule which would freeze Britain out of Singapore is unacceptable to the British. But so long as 

Singapore remains under the military control of the British, a sore will fester in southeast Asia. The results can only be unfavorable to our interests and to the cause of peace. So long as national power is necessary to protect the interests of any nation such as Britain, or to advance the interests of such a nation as Malaya, armed conflict will continue to be a threat hanging over Singapore. An overall solution will protect the trading interests of every nation and Singapore will cease to be important as a military base for the Brtiish. Self-determination can then be honored in deed as well as in principle. 

Korea and Vietnam will continue to be divided, contrary to the economic interests of all concerned, until military strategy between nations is outmoded because replaced by community security and justice. 

Many individuals have said that the solution of these local problems must precede disarmament, but these examples illustrate the futility and dangers in continuing to neglect efforts for an overall solution. The most logical answer to present local dilemmas is to develop understanding, agreement, and law at the world level. Such world law must be drawn and made effective as the means for assuring the peaceful settlement of disputes and for the enforcement of universal disarmament. This can best be achieved by amendment of the United 

Nations Charter. 

Amendment of the United Nations Charter and accomplishment of the twin objectives of a method for peaceful settlements and disarmament will come about: (1) When all nations face the facts of the suicidal potentialities of modern armed conflict; (2) When leadership develops for an overall solution involving amendment of the Charter of the United Nations; (3) When the amendments agreed to in advance by the preponderant majority of the nations in accordance with their own constitutional processes come to have the force of law; and (4) When progressive universal disarmament is made effective as a result of such agreement.

PIECEMEAL VERSUS OVERALL APPROACH TO DISARMAMENT 

The second notion which is delaying an overall solution is that it is more practical to seek progress on a piecemeal basis. The' open-skies proposals could have been productive first steps were it not that Russia has demonstrated an unwillingness to accept effective controls and inspection, probably for the reasons cited in Boston by Mr. Rostow of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Russia apparently considers secrecy and military power essential elements in the conduct of her foreign policy. Russia has given as her reason for not agreeing to inspection that she would consider it to be against her interests unless it becomes part of a "complete disarmament program.'

Whatever our mental reservations may be, we have nothing to gain in questioning the sincerity of Russia's stated reason for turning down mutual inspection. It was actually a logical reaction. We asked Russia to give up her advantage of secrecy. Russia refused. Earlier Russia asked us to give up atom bombs. That was our point of superiority, so we refused. Actually, it is illogical to assume that we can arrive at disarmament on a step-by-step basis, when each step would mean loss of advantage to one side or the other. The only practical solution is to work out in advance an overall plan for the peaceful settlement of all disputes and to include in that plan a program designed to bring about over a period of years complete, universal disarmament, in stages which would maintain the present balance of power but at decreasing levels of strength, while at the same time the world community's power for peace under law could be built up and made effective. 

Thus, instead of saying: "It is hopeless to strive toward disarmament so long as present Russian attitudes and leadership continue," we should test the sincerity of Russia's statement. We should welcome Russia's awareness of the need for a complete disarmament program. We should propose and invite suggestions for such amendments of the United Nations Charter as are necessary to assure the peaceful settlement of disputes and to make realistic a program for progress toward complete universal disarmament. That is the only valid way to assure the mutual security of all nations.

Such proposals would demonstrate our sincere interests in both peace and disarmament. By associating disarmament with increased aid to underdeveloped countries through the United Nations, the self-interest of all nations in the proposals would become clear. Such a statement of objectives, accompanied by proposals for accomplishment, would open up a new approach which can capture the imagination of the leaders of the world. We will be suggesting that peace be based on law and justice rather than on a continuing, unstable, and dangerous equilibrium of terror based solely on the armed strength of individual nations. 

This is the American way to peace. As Chief Justice Warren has said: "The 

United States, as Americans have always known and loved it, cannot subsist without law." Law prevents chaos. Law enables us to live peaceably. Law protects individual rights. But, as Chief Justice Warren also said: "It is not just the United States that needs law, it is the entire world. The world's chief need in these next decades will be peace and order; and of all human institutions, law has the best historical claim to satisfy this need." 

International law for peace must be written law, so that all nations will know in advance both their benefits and their obligations. The law must be in the form of "constitutional legislation" so that no nation will fear an expansion of powers without specific delegation of new authority by amendment. There must be courts to apply the terms of the agreement to specific situations. There must be a world peace force and system of inspection to make sure that all nations are conforming to the law. The freedom of every nation to govern its own internal affairs must be preserved. The constitutional guaranties of all citizens of the United States and the national rights of other peoples must be protected. We want peace, but not at the loss of freedom. We can have both peace and freedom.

CONCLUSIONS 

(1) Many of the world's local threats to peace will be solved only when an overall basis of law has been established to assure peaceful settlements and disarmament. (2) Attempts at piecemeal solutions cannot be expected to solve problems which require an overall solution providing for the mutual security of all nations. (3) Agreement on international disarmament will not be reached until all nations are sure that a better way to provide national security has been established. That agreement must be written into law, preferably in the form of amendments to the United Nations Charter. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Platt, we want to thank you. I know you have given many years of very thoughtful analysis to this prob- 

Ïem, and we surely do welcome your suggestions.

SHOULD POLITICAL SETTLEMENTS PRECEDE DISARMAMENT NEGOTIATIONS? 

May I say that one of the recurring questions that is posed in our hearings with the executive officials in Washington, and the men who are entrusted with this responsibility in whole or in part, is whether we must have political settlements before we have disarmament negotiations, whether we must have them simultaneously, or whether we should proceed with disarmament negotiations leaving political settlements in the hope that they will fall in line afterward. 

We are not quite sure about this yet, but we must keep searching for an answer. Having wrestled around with this problem in a very inexperienced manner, when I see people who concentrate their full time upon it, I have come to understand the gravity of the situation and the difficulty of it. I want people to talk about it and to see if we cannot get a fuller expression of views about it. 

I think that I can say very candidly that this record is read by Mr. 

Stassen's office; it is read by the officials in the Department of Defense; it is read by the officials in the Security Council, so that I suppose ideas do come out.

1 12 

AERIAL INSPECTION TESTS IN LIMITED AREAS 

For example, on testing aerial inspection, you may recall that Mr. 

Stassen proposed that we test it on a limited area. He suggested an area about the size of Alabama in the Soviet Union and in the United States, just a pilot plant operation. 

One of our witnesses came up with the suggestion that we test the idea among our allies with the United States and the United Kingdom for instance. For practice they would put barriers in the way of our being able to get accurate information, camouflage every conceivable thing that they could to hide their installations. This is a possibility. 

Now this possibility has been studied by our executive branch. 

These proposals which are coming here will be studied, and I am more and more impressed by the number of witnesses who speak in the vein in which you have spoken.

Mr. PLATT. Yes, sir. Well, my basic proposition is that all approaches should be used to the full extent of their capabilities and possibilities, but that there are some limitations to the step by step approach and to the trouble spot solution approach, and that more time and attention should be devoted to an overall solution that will provide for the security of all nations on an equal and just basis. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I just want to add one word so that we will have it as a matter of record. 

WORLDWIDE STUDY ON COST OF ARMAMENTS NEEDED 

Somewhere along in our studies I hope that we can make an analysis and economic appraisal of the total amount of public revenues that are going into defense establishments in all the nations of the world as far as we are able to ascertain. 

It is difficult to get the exact amount in the countries behind the Iron Curtain. However, we do know that in the budget of 1955 the 

Soviet Union increased its military budget by 12 percent despite the fact that they were talking much more amicably and their countenances revealed only smiles. Now it may be coming down. This is an example of our need for all available facts on Russia. 

We must discuss this subject from as many known facts as we can. 

We have been trying, for example, in this subcommittee staff to release a study on the total aggregate military forces of all nations in the world. We have completed the study. We have had some mild objection from some sources about it, but will probably be able to release it.

I doubt that many people appreciate the number of men under arms and the industrial output that is consigned to our Military Establishment. Our industrial potential and capacity, our mechanized units, the quality and quantity of equipment, and the latest technology mean so much.

OBSOLESCENCE OF CONVENTIONAL WEAPONS 

I think we are at a stage in this world armament picture where conventional weapons become negotiable because of the new development in guided missiles and in the field of thermonuclear power and electronics, etc. We may very well be approaching that stage. If we are, then some of our proposals for political settlements will be very

different, because part of the problem of political settlements today has been, for example, 30 divisions of troops in East Germany. 

The Soviet Union has insisted upon having that as a staging area. 

In fact, within recent weeks there have been tremendous maneuvers taking place there. We had information as to the large quantities of conventional weapons that have been moved into East Germany in certain staging areas this past year. This was brought out as a point to show that even the Soviet Union has problems of overproduction of weapons and it does not quite know what to do with all of them. 

As General Gruenther said, they are worthless. I have never forgotten his statement. He said the equipment is worthless, tanks by the hundreds, new cannon, new equipment, worthless. He said it means nothing. Well, now this is from the man who is the commander in chief of all of the NATO forces. This is in terms of the new kind of warfare that is available and ready to be touched off. 

I just thought I might toss these things out for your consideration as you think in the weeks to come. 

Mr. PLATT. Thank you very much.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Cyrus Barnum. 

STATEMENT OF CYRUS P. BARNUM, JR., PROFESSOR OF PHYSIO- LOGICAL CHEMISTRY, UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA 

Mr. BARNUM. I am Cyrus P. Barnum, Jr., on the staff of the University of Minnesota and I am testifying this afternoon as an individual.

Rather than discuss any specific disarmament proposal I should like to discuss briefly the context within which I feel any specific proposal should be considered.

DEFINING GOAL OF UNITED STATES FOREIGN POLICY 

I should like to argue the case for stating clearly, explicitly, and repeatedly, the goal toward which our foreign policy is aiming so that there can be no doubt in the minds of our own citizens nor in the minds of any peoples in the world what we consider as the desirable goal. 

Furthermore, if such a goal is always kept foremost in our minds, then we cannot help but evaluate each specific proposal in terms of its likelihood of advancing us toward our goal.

How should such a goal be defined? There has been a good deal of careful, detailed consideration of such an ultimate goal, but for our purposes today, I should like to state it in simple and general terms without attempting to spell out in detail the conditions on which it would rest. I believe one might define the goal as a world at peace in which no nation need fear military aggression from any other nation.

ESSENTIALS OF THE GOAL-ACHIEVING WORLD PEACE 

Stated in such terms it would be understood and, I am sure, devoutly desired by all peoples-particularly in this atomic era. It could be phrased in slightly more sophisticated terms as "enforcible disarmament under law," and in these terms would suggest three ultimate conditions that are probably the minimum essentials of the goal to be

sought, namely; complete and total disarmament, a world police force capable of insuring peace, and a system of world courts empowered to adjudicate disputes that might arise. 

I fully recognize that these conditions may be difficult to attain and may appear utopian to some but I submit that in a world in which nations possess such fantastic destructive powers as they do today, this is the only realistic goal. The alternative, it seems to me, is balance of power diplomacy with the world sitting forever on an atomic powder keg.

Under this alternative, the absence of war could not properly be termed "peace" but rather an armed truce. It surely would not dispel the fear of aggression nor allow nations to most effectively turn their talents toward the betterment of mankind.

While some may feel that the goal of enforcible disarmament under law is utopian, I would hazard the prediction that merely the emphatic and sincere reiteration of such a goal by our Government would greatly enhance the probability of its early attainment.

IMPORTANCE OF UNITED STATES LEADERSHIP 

If, as I firmly believe, this is the only realistic goal for the future of mankind, then I would say let us take the lead and tell the world that this is where we hope to arrive. 

I realize that diplomacy, like politics, may be described as the art of the possible, but in defining what may be possible at any moment in history one should never lose sight of what one considers essential to the continuance of civilization. 

Any specific proposal in the area of disarmament should be carefully evaluated in terms of our ultimate goal. For example, if decreases in national armaments could be linked to strengthening of the United Nations, these two steps together would advance us substantially along the road we wish to go.

When you speak of strengthening the United Nations I think in terms of its enforcement powers, of its power to mediate and adjudicate disputes and of its ability to render technical and financial assistance, to increase the living standards of less fortunate areas. 

In conclusion I feel that the prime requisite is a carefully considered, clearly stated and oft-repeated goal that can serve as a guiding star in destroying and sponsoring the immediate and specific proposals. with which your committee, Congress, and the administration are so vitally concerned, and I should like to urge that this goal be stated in some such terms as "enforcible disarmament under law."

In conclusion, let me cite briefly an example from my own field of work that I feel is pertinent to our discussion here today. In my research I have set for myself, the goal of trying to understand why a cell in the body grows and divides and by what mechanisms the body exerts a controlling influence over its growth. 

I have set this goal because I feel that if we are to understand malignant growth, which is not under control of the body, we must first understand normal cell growth and the mechanisms by which the body controls such growth. Some would say that this goal is utopian and unattainable even though desirable. 

However, since it does seem to me to be a highly desirable goal, I go about designing experiments that I hope will advance me toward it. Not all experiments do advance me toward my goal but these are set aside in favor of more promising lines of research. Also, even though some experiments are failures, each experiment is carefully designed in the hope that it will mark an advance. 

Returning to the field of international relations, I feel that the prime requisite is a carefully considered, clearly stated, and oft-repeated goal that can serve as a guiding star in devising and sponsoring the immediate and specific proposals with which your committee, Congress, and the administration are so vitally concerned. And I should like to urge that this goal be stated in some such terms as "enforcible disarmament under law." 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much, Mr. Barnum. I shall not ask any questions. STATEMENT OF MRS. M. J. PRITCHARD, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mrs. PRITCHARD. Thank you very much.

I am Mrs. Pritchard and very pleased to be able to present my views to your committee. I am not able to qualify as an expert. I received no letter urging me to appear, but if you were earnestly seeking grassroots opinions, I feel I am qualified in that category.

UNDESIRABILITY OF UNITED STATES DISARMAMENT IN VIEW OF SOVIET AGGRESSION 

Defense today is a complex and technical matter. Even experts must disagree on its nature and efficiency, since many weapons have not yet been tried. As citizens it is our duty only to discuss whether 

America should be armed for defense.

No one can say we are preparing for an offensive war because we even refused to take the offensive when our boys were being killed in 

There are around 500 newspaper dailies in the United States, and not one has ever mentioned an offensive war. There are 531 Congressmen and Senators, and not one has ever advocated offensive war. 

The next point is: Why do we need defense? Simply because

Russia declared war on us in February 1946 when Stalin made a 

Now that Hitler is dead, we are back on the road to world revolution, which means England and America must be destroyed. 

This we ignored and thereby encouraged Soviet aggression while they took over the many satellite countries-friendship pacts notwithstanding and then China, while we rejoiced in our material progress and prosperity.

Finally, in 1950, their advance engulfed us and enacted a heavy toll of over 30,000 dead and 110,000 wounded in Korea. Only then did we seriously think of the need for national defense. 

Why is Russia aggressive? Because they believe in class struggle, international war, and the destruction of the capitalistic system. 

The Communist blueprint for world conquest is as real and as well advertised as Hitler's aims in Mein Kampf. Is it possible we can ignore these avowed sins again?

Lest the superficial people among us take the Geneva smile too seriously, we have Khrushchev's own words that Soviet Russia has not abandoned its plan of world conquest in his famous statement to the effect that if romantic liberals think we Bolsheviks have abandoned our program for world domination, they might as well wait to hear a shrimp whistle.

When you listen today to advocates of disarmament, remember it was Molotov less than a year ago who initiated this whole propaganda campaign. If you are sincere representatives of the people, the survival of our people and our Nation should be your prime objective. 

The only question before us today is, "Does disarmament guarantee our survival in the face of Soviet goals, Soviet power, and Soviet deeds?" By deeds, I mean just that, not smiles and speeches.

The Korean truce is a concrete example. Central inspection is impossible. The Communist neutrals, Poland and Czechoslovakia, wrecked the truce team while the North Koreans openly built their war machine.

The events of the last month in expelling the Communist members of the Armistice Committee show the futility of postwar agreement and cooperation with the Communists. 

PROPOSAL THAT THE SUBCOMMITTEE DISBAND 

There is, therefore, one concrete suggestion I wish to make: that this Committee on Disarmament disband immediately and dedicate the taxpayers' money to the task of educating all those victims of selfdeception who are present here to the realities of survival-and to the necessity of preventing another sacrifice of American boys_by the folly of self-deception and naivete that is represented here today.

AIMS AND OBJECTIVES OF THE SUBCOMMITTEE

tends to reach its goals. They try it sometimes militarily, sometimes politically, sometimes economically.

I think that what we need to do at all times is to keep the pressure on to the best of our ability. In the first place, if we could reduce the armaments struggle it would surely leave us with a much greater force economically to do the job of defeating them in the economic aid area. 

I am not ready to see our Government disarm unilaterally, or any other way unless I have incontrovertible evidence as to the certainty and not just the willingness of a potential enemy to do the same. 

In fact I welcome your testimony, because I think it demonstrates the gravity of the problem. I want you to know that despite my political differences with many people, I have yet to find anybody in our Government that did not recognize the gravity of this problem. 

I think our President seeks peace, but I think he seeks it with honor. I have disagreed with some of our foreign policy. I am not here to argue that today. I know of no man in the Government of the United States that would sell out the security of the United States. I really mean that, and I have watched them pretty carefully.

The next witness is Dorothy Schultz.

STATEMENT OF DOROTHY SCHULTZ, REPRESENTING THE SOCIALIST WORKERS PARTY, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Miss SCHULTZ. I am Dorothy Schultz, speaking for the Minnesota section of the Socialist Workers Party, 10 South Fourth Street, Min neapolis. I am a dissenting voice in a different direction than the lady who just preceded me.

My party, the Socialist Workers Party, is fighting for a world socialist order in which war would have no place, in which mankind could devote himself to peaceful purposes and utilize the great tech-

nological advantages for gratifying the human urge for an ever-better life.

SOCIALIST WORKERS PARTY OPPOSES "PERMANENT MILITARIZATION OF AMERICA" 

My party is therefore against the ever-growing militarization of the United States and the ever-growing expenditure of the country's resources for armaments. The billions devoured by militarism come out of the meager pay envelopes of the workers. This misuse of the people's money takes place at the expense of adequate schools for our children, housing for the poor, and urgently needed medical facilities for the sick. 

Is there any hope that these huge military expenditures will be cut?

Not so long as the United States Government continues its bipartisan policy of trying to smash the world revolutionary movement for a better social order. Not so long as the United States continues its efforts to prop up the dying reactionary regimes in various countries against the will of the majority of the people of those countries. 

The United States Government pours arms and dollars into the war chest of the Fascist dictator Franco in the name of "democracy." According to Mr. Dulles, almost $12 billion will be spent in the next year to provide arms for the dictatorial regime of Syngman Rhee in Korea and the fictitious "Republic of China" represented on the island of Formosa by the equally tyrannical Chiang Kai-shek. Even if your committee should recommend certain cuts, they would amount to nothing more than a token reduction of the biggest military program in world history. 

Discussing what he, perhaps ironically, terms "peace insurance, the Secretary of State spelled out a policy of the permanent militarization of America. 

It will be askedsaid Mr. Dulleswill the cost go on forever? Can we see no end to this gigantic expenditure, totaling about $40 billion a year, as the cost of our peace insurance policy? The answer ishe added- as long as the danger persists, for so long must we pay to combat that danger. 

Attempting to shift the burden of responsibility onto the Soviet Union, Mr. Dulles contended that so long as the Russians maintain their present armament levelso long * * * we may have to expend about 10 percent of our gross national product, as we are now doing, for peace insurance purposes. 

Negotiations between the United States and U. S. S. R. for joint arms reduction will no doubt take place periodically in the future as in the past. But this much is certain. The United States will not abandon its basic military plans, and the ever-present danger of the cold war erupting into an atomic one will not be eliminated. 

CONFLICT OF TWO ANTAGONISTIC SOCIAL SYSTEMS 

The existing conflict between the United States and the U. S. S. R. is not the result of an arms race. On the contrary, the arms race is

only one expression of the conflict between two antagonistic social systems. The United States Government, which shapes its policies not on the basis of the needs or interests of the majority of the American people, but on that of a tiny clique of powerful industrialists and financiers, is determined to establish the economic and political domination of the American capitalist class over the entire globe. It cannot accomplish this by peaceful means. That is the primary reason for its vast, permanent arms buildup. 

That the United States is the pacesetter in the world armament race is demonstrated by the simple fact that it is the only country in the world that can afford such an enormous sapping of national resources. 

UNITED STATES CAPITALIST ECONOMY MAINTAINED BY ARMS RACE 

For the United States the production of weapons of war is absolutely indispensable for the maintenance of its capitalist economy. 

For the Soviet Union, with nationalized production-for use, not profit-the effort to match the level of United States military power creates an enormous, unneeded drain on its economy. 

During the terrible depression of the Thirties, all of Roosevelt's pump priming could not get the wheels of industry turning. It was only from 1938 on, when the New Deal became the War Deal that relative prosperity was again reestablished on the basis of massive arms production.

In 1947, when a new recession began to engulf the country, the economic crisis was averted when the Truman administration plunged the American people into the bloody Korean "police action." It was the production of munitions which once again set the wheels of industry turning.

Today, armaments constitute an indispensable prop for the economy. Even if one accepted Mr. Dulles' contention that armaments represent 10 percent of the gross national product, it is clear that any substantial reduction would result in an economic crisis. 

It is already being demonstrated that arms production is a palliative that can only postpone, but not eliminate capitalist crisis. There is today a crisis in agriculture and in farm equipment production, in the automobile industry, et cetera, even while arms production is booming. 

The propaganda for disarmament by those who support capitalism and approve of United States foreign policy, can only serve to deceive the great mass of the people who genuinely yearn for an end to the insecurity and tension which militarism engenders. This is the essential function of your committee as well as that of the President's Special Assistant on Disarmament, Mr. Stassen. There is no one in the 

Pentagon in any way in danger of losing his job as a consequence of the disarmament proposals of either your committee or of Mr. Stassen. 

STALIN'S TYRANNY ENDURED ONLY BECAUSE OF IMPERIALIST THREATS 

A great movement is on foot throughout the world to end the rule of imperialism. The colonial people are fighting for and winning their independence. They are demanding an end to economic exploitation and political tyranny. They want no more militarism and no more war.

The Soviet workers have once again entered this worldwide freedom fight. By their pressure they have won important concessions from the Kremlin dictators. The Soviet workers are determined to eliminate the bureaucracy that has deprived them of their political rights. 

The Russian workers are pushing for an end to the bureaucratic privileges taken at the expense of their living standards. 

The Soviet masses have endured Stalinist tyranny because of the imperialist encirclement which threatened the conquests of the 1917 revolution. This encirclement was smashed as the consequences of the victories of the Soviet Army in World War II and as a result of the Chinese and Yugoslav revolutions. The attempt to impose a new encirclement-the so-called containment policy—which is the keystone of United States foreign policy, is doomed to failure. 

A war unleashed against the Soviet Union and China under present world conditions would mean a war of nuclear devastation, which capitalism could not survive. 

WORLD COOPERATION THROUGH A WORLD SOCIALIST ORDER 

The American people will inevitably come to realize that they have no actual conflict with the people of the Soviet Union, China, or the colonial world. The aspirations of the American people for peace and security will not be realized in conflict with the oppressed of the world but in cooperation with them. 

The interests of the American people can be served only by replacing the present government of the monopolists with a workers' and farmers' government. A workers' and farmers' government in America would extend a hand of genuine solidarity to the Soviet workers in their great struggle to redemocratize the Soviet Union. Such an action would topple the Kremlin bureaucracy. This would create the basis for a world Socialist order where barbaric militarism would be ended forever.

For the present, the Socialist Workers Party demands: 

1. An immediate halt of nuclear weapons tests. Scientists throughout the world have urgently warned of the grave danger to the health of humanity resulting from the radioactive fallout of these explosions. 

The people throughout the world are insistently demanding a halt to these tests. Let your committee join in this demand. 2. The very destructiveness of a coming war demands that a decision on war or peace be removed from the hands of Congress and the President and be placed in the hands of the people. Let your committee demand that the question of war or peace be submitted to a people's referendum.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much. 

We have a full expression of points of view before this committee. 

I could not, of course, let the testimony pass without saying that I must disagree with its basic assumption, but that is my point of view, just my personal point of view, and with its basic conclusions. 

The next witness is Carl Miller.

STATEMENT OF CARL S. MILLER, RESEARCH CHEMIST, MINNESOTA MINING & MANUFACTURING CO. 

Mr. MILLER. My name is Carl Miller. I live at 1670 Upper Afton 

Road, in St. Paul. I am a chemist at the research laboratories of 

Minnesota Mining & Manufacturing Co. My statement is quite brief so I will read it and then make a couple of comments if I may. 

To be effective, a disarmament program must be supervised by some nonprejudiced authority such as an international police force. While this fact places the day of total world disarmament many years in the future, its eventual achievement is still dependent on what we do today. 

The best first step is, I believe, for the United States to assume world leadership in a cooperative effort to set up a small international police force, and an international democratic body to run it. It should be large enough to prevent today's "little wars." 

Participation of a nation in it would depend on the nation allowing its fair share of these cosmopolitan police soldiers to reside on its soil and to travel about freely on it. The voting powers of the nations would have to be such that no power group could control the force. 

Two bodies similar to the United States House and Senate might be effective. The existence of such a force should, I believe, create a new mental atmosphere in the world and could be a very powerful part of our foreign policy.

As the world confidence in collective security grew and the nations learned to rely more heavily on the protection of the world community, we would truly be approaching the day when we could talk intelligently and hopefully of enforcible agreements to reduce national armies and of eventual total world disarmament. 

SIZE AND CONTROL OF INTERNATIONAL POLICE FORCE 

I would like to reiterate a couple of points because I think sometimes they are missed.

It seems to me that it is not necessary for a world police force to be large in the beginning. The United States has shown by its relations with other nations in the United Nations that we could very well rally around us the majority of the world's nations. In its first 10 years I believe that we consistently had over 50 of the 60 nations with us on most of our proposals. I think if the United States called the nations together or asked them to participate in discussions on setting up a small world police force, we would have a great many of them right with us.

The second point is of course we are all worried that a big power might get control of this police force. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Yes, that has been expressed again and again as you know. 

Mr. MILLER. And I am sure there could be checks and balances worked into the thing. One that occurs to me immediately would be that any country that participated in the operation of such a police

force would have to give the police force hospitality on its own soil and great freedom of movement. Just how far that freedom could go at first I don't know, whether they could actually inspect factories. 

They would certainly have to travel around the way any free civilian can in the United States today.

There might be other checks and balances which would tend to keep out a country that was not going to play the game. With regard to representation in the control body, which is the other sticker as I see it, I think that good parliamentarians could probably work out formulas of representation which would be acceptable to most of the nations of the world and would be fair. 

I believe that in our early history our own country had that problem and we came up with bicameral house system. That might be a possibility here.

I think that covers my thoughts for the moment. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much, Mr. Miller.

A moment ago I heard some reference to the breakdown, I won't say complete breakdown but at least the great difficulties being encountered by the truce team supervising the Korean armistice. I consider these to be very flagrant violations of the armistice terms, but I want the record to be clear that the inspection machinery provided under those armistice terms was very inadequate. I hope we have learned a lesson from that particular experience. 

The kind of inspection that is being talked about today and has been discussed with this subcommittee and by the Executive offices of our Government is one that goes much further than any inspection we have known before. It provides for inspection of factories, complete and unlimited inspection of military installations, airports, actually examining and counting new equipment, inspection of arsenals-not only inspection but plans for the development of new arsenals and new weapons.

I only make the record clear on this. I think, very frankly, that some of the terms of the Korean armistice, as finally agreed to, lacked much that was desirable in terms of an effective inspection. 

At least, however, it brought termination to the hostilities. 

Mr. MILLER. I would agree with that. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much, Mr. Miller. 

The next witness is Mrs. Harold W. Ruopp.

STATEMENT OF MRS. HAROLD W. RUOPP, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mrs. RUOPP. I am Mrs. Harold Ruopp, 2205 Pleasant Avenue, 

Minneapolis, Minn.

I am a former teacher and a minister's wife and a grandmother and a worker for better human relations and peace. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Those are good credentials, Mrs. Ruopp. 

Mrs. RUOPP. I am president of the Children's Plea for Peace but 

I am here today as a private citizen. I am deeply grateful to you, Senator, for making this democratic discussion possible and bringing this subject out into the open. I feel it has needed to be in the open. 

But I am not going to speak for myself today; I am going to speak for those who cannot speak for themselves.

PLIGHT OF THE CHILDREN OF THE WORLD 

My testimony for disarmament is in behalf of the 900 million children in the world, who cannot be asked to testify for themselves. I am concerned about the children. They are new and neutral citizens. 

We were all children once. We need to remember that, I think. What rights do they have? Are they being remembered in the midst of vital decisions that will affect every moment of their lives? They are the ones who will inherit the results of what governments decide to do about an accelerating arms race that may destroy them and all life or may free them to experience the normal joys and sorrows of growing and trying to attain some semblance of brotherhood in the world. 

What would they say if they could speak for themselves? The voice of these infants and boys and girls of every land would speak in every tongue to tell you of the shocking plight of half the children around the world.

Today, and I have two reasons for presenting that, their present and their future. I am not going to go into a lot of statistics because there is not time, but I do think that we need to remember that half of these children are hungry all or part of the time, destitute, inadequately housed and clothed or ill of preventable diseases. 

Four hundred and fifty million are illiterate-and we know what that means; that they are at the mercy of hearsay, any type of propaganda, dishonesty, and the vicious cycle of overpopulation, malnutrition, underproduction, and endemic disease. 

Disarmament could change these conditions. In Europe, Japan, and Korea, 300,000 or more bear the total scars of war. They are maimed, blinded, crippled, and incapacitated mentally and emotionally by the consequences of war, ignorance, and greed. 

Five hundred thousand in Korea alone are orphaned, vagrant, displaced; add to these, refugees in other lands, the friendless, victims of race prejudice, broken homes, alcohol, and narcotics and you get a picture of a spiritually bankrupt civilization pouring 80 percent of its tax revenue-not into a better life for children, but into arms and more arms even though they know armaments are not the answer for a secure world. Liddell Hart, military expert of England for 40 years, calls the arms race, "piling absurdity upon insanity."

The Japanese children, who have been dying in indescribable agony 

7 to 10 years after the first A-bomb fell on Hiroshima, might ask, “Why must children be the target? Is there anything that can be done to keep this from happening to other children?"

The Russian children, our target for "massive retaliation"-and if anyone has read the Hiroshima Diary I think they know what massive retaliation means-must ask their mothers in secret, "Why do men kill each other? Why do they make bombs?" While our children, their target, ask it out loud, "Tell us, why?"

Yes; children themselves are asking questions. They know about the building of bomb shelters. At school they are drilled for air raids. Since the perfection of the H-bomb the high-school students of Connecticut are required to take a course in primitive living in order to prepare them for the remote chance of possible survival if this weapon is used. Is it any wonder youth feels insecure and fearful?

They hear or read speeches by retired military leaders about the futility and uselessness of war. Is it any wonder that they have little confidence in their adults or their future?

They know what is going on in the fields of science and they say, "If the scientists are right, that no one can win another war, and there is no defense, can't we use the money spent for armaments to settle things another way?"

PSYCHOLOGICAL DAMAGE CAUSED BY EMPHASIS ON MILITARISM 

Many of us realize that children do not live in a vacuum. They are very much a part of everything that happens in the world. We now have a society where children-your children and grandchildren, and mine-are no longer protected from war, and even the unborn are under a constant threat.

If we care about what happens to our children, we will recognize the psychological harm of the constant emphasis upon militarism and the arms race. With no hope in the future of anything but a heavily militarized existence, with the threat of annihilation constantly dinned into their ears, with the knowledge that all our knowhow, our inventions, our wealth, our education do not enable us to be creative in our world approach-apathy, indifference, dread, and lack of constructive goals engulf many young people.

INSECURITY A CAUSE OF JUVENILE DELINQUENCY 

We already have evidences of this in increasing juvenile delinquency, distrust and defiance of parental authority; also in disinterest in many areas, such as science, which seem to lead to a deadend street. Many a young person, unable to look ahead to the natural things of life, decides to live for the moment and get what they can while there is time. I think Karen Horning describes this for us in her book. I have forgotten the title but it is on our schizophrenic society. 

Children and youth need disarmament so that they may grow in trust, faith and creative attitudes; so that they may learn how to meet the problems of life, not with arms of steel, but with the moral arms of the spirit.

Five thousand years of history show that all arms races end in war. 

It shows that war and violence have been tried over and over again and have failed to bring lasting peace. Wars stand out in the laboratory of mankind as out of harmony with all the moral laws, unscientific, out of date, wasteful, and worst of all, completely futile. 

Would a scientist-I wish Mr. Inglis were here to answer thisgo on making the same experiment over and over again if it was unsuccessful? No. He would try a new formula-many new formulas-until he got the result he wanted. 

If our formulas for disarmament do not seem to work, I am glad we are discussing this and maybe we will find some new formula that will work.

The formula-violence to overcome violence-has brought no answer of peace. Only good can overcome evil. This is a moral law. 

And it is just as unchangeable and fixed as any physical law that we can think of.

મી 

THE LEGACY OF ARMAMENTS 

Disarmament would give the world an opportunity to find out if this law works; if it is true. Let's think just for a moment then of the future of our children if we do not have disarmament. If the arms race continues they will inherit higher and higher taxes as more deadly and costly weapons are invented. 

They will inherit the necessity of finding deadlier and deadlier weapons. I understand from my reading that the work on the intercontinental missile is underway and someday the launching tubes may be hidden underground and the weapon be fired from thousands of miles away. Camouflaged under a barn, this article said. Then someone must invent a way to launch them beneath the sea-and finally to launch unmanned tiny death-laden moons, ready to drop instant death upon any foe. 

I wonder if we remember how quickly Hitler changed children to obedient robots. Will our children also have to learn that only the goal matters and you can use any means to reach the goal that you want to? 

RELEASE OF CREATIVE ENERGY THROUGH DISARMAMENT 

Is that what man was created for? Is this what we wish for our children? Disarmament will create an atmosphere wherein we can once again release this vast energy creatively to resolve differences that separate nations today.

Science is ready to end want and poverty. Mr. Gerald Piel, editor of Scientific American, tells us that human want is now technologically obsolete. Whether or not we use this knowledge to end want, war, ignorance, and fear, depends largely upon our willingness to begin a disarmament program.

Biology now tells us that every amoeba and cell of our bodies illustrate that the basic forces of life are: interdependence, dependence, and cooperation. We are also slowly beginning to realize that everyone is dependent upon everyone else, and it is absurd to go on flouting this fundamental law of life and expect that life to survive. 

Let the children speak for themselves: 

One for Germany says, "We hope all children all over the world can become friends. Then when we disagree we will not need to kill each other." 

A letter from a Hiroshima orphan says: "Why should we fight?

It is only a few years since we lost our parents. War is inhuman and should not be done by us, the head of all living creatures. It seems to me those sleeping under the ashes of Hiroshima were crying something for peace. We hope the people could go hand in hand to make a safer world."

In our office at the World Affairs Center we read thousands of letters from young people all over the world. We now have correspondents in 61 different nations. We know that is what our boys and girls want, to go hand in hand to make a safe world, a happier world.

We have it so clearly demonstrated in our American field service students who come over here and ours who go to Europe.

VALUE OF STUDENT EXCHANGE PROGRAMS 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I just want to interrupt to tell you that I am very much interested in that program. At the time of the State 

Department appropriations this year I was able to make a plea for a little more consideration, and we got it and we also got an assurance of extra help if we need it before the end of the year. The teen-age exchange program is, I think, a dramatic program of good and constructive purpose. 

Mrs. RUOPP. I am grateful for that. And the foreign student exchange and the pen pals programs that are going on are very important, because the children find that they can have an appropriate part in world affairs in this way. 

EDUCATION FOR PEACE 

We need education for peace. It is one of our greatest needs now, and we are doing it in a very humble and small way from the World 

Affairs Center. 

Young people can learn what to do about differences other than killing one another. As President Eisenhower said in one of his last speeches, "Let us not make the ignorant, uninformed decision that only in armaments are we going to find the solutions to our foreign problems."

That is the end of my written statement, but I do have a few suggestions if you have time for them. They are very short. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Go right ahead and take just a few moments if you will.

Mrs. RUOPP. One is that since there is a struggle for the minds of men, let us recognize that the minds of young people are open to education and to spiritual and psychological understanding. 

Also I would suggest that we take our battleships and send young people, thus grounded, all over the world with games and dancing and music and art and surplus food and let them speak for us. Let's listen to what young people have to say. They have a natural charity which we, with our jelled ideas and our vested interests, do not always have. 

U. S. S. R. OUTSTRIPS FREE WORLD IN ITS YOUTH PROGRAMS 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I might just add that if more American citizens could see some of the movies which our intelligence people have been able to get out from behind the Iron Curtain as to what the Soviet Union has been doing in the development of young people, they would thoroughly agree with what you are saying. The emphasis which the Communist system places upon youth is beyond any proportion to what we have ever done in this country or in the free world. 

Every dictator has done this. As a matter of fact, the dictator may restrain himself occasionally and be willing to let the old die off. 

Some are willing to accelerate the process, but they are all willing to indoctrinate youth.

I have seen movies of a million and a half young people in Eastern Germany in games, festivals, in parades, in demonstrations, and this is going on in country after country. Why in the name of commonsense we don't understand this is beyond me, except we never hear about it.

Mrs. RUOPP. May I suggest that we don't try to indoctrinate them but just help them to be themselves, because I think they understand these things better than we do. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I don't think you have to indoctrinate them but give them a chance to be exposed to constructive thoughts, and they respond quite well. I recall in 1951 or 1952, the Soviet Union was conducting or sponsoring a huge youth conference in East Germany. Our great and rich country decided to sponsor one, too. We had 80 people. We had 80 young men and women that came here and spent 6 weeks at Cornell University and other places. 

This was our answer. They had 750,000 at that 1 meeting and then they doubled it the next time. Yet when you get up to speak for an exchange program, just an exchange program in the Congress, you would think you were taking the last ounce of gold out of Fort

Knox, Ky. I want to get this off my chest because I really think that the exchange programs have tremendous value. I think we have everything to gain from them, and I think that all the standards and principles in which we believe are all the more strengthened by these programs, and I hope the citizenry will just watch these budgets as they come up. We are fighting to get it now. 

We must continue something like the Fulbright exchange program, the Smith-Mundt Act, costing about 21 millions of dollars. It is a substantial sum of money, may I say, for any citizen to talk about, but it is insignificant in the great struggle in this world. 

FUNDS FOR UNICEF AND WORLD HEALTH NEEDED 

Mrs. RUOPP. We hope that some of the money can be released for 

UNICEF, because we feel that UNICEF and World Health are doing a tremendous piece of work for peace. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. They are, and I wish more people knew about it. We fought the battle for UNICEF every year in Congress trying to get it up from 9 million to 12 million. That is hardly enough to repaint the ships that are in mothballs, and yet we have to fight for it and plan and work and connive to get an extra vote for 

UNICEF and World Health. 

Mrs. RUOPP. I have just two more suggestions.

One is that perhaps the best way to protect the children is to protect them just as we do here in our beautiful cities with a police force and the restraint of the offender. I don't know how to work this out now- 

I am not an expert in this but I believe that we have been so inventive about weapons, we certainly should be inventive about this. 

Then I have one more, and this is not at all egocentric, but I feel that we should put women on every disarmament committee. I think that women should work with men because they have a way of fighting for their young and they would do a great deal to help. 

CULTURAL MISSIONS OF THE SOVIET UNION 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I think that is a very practical suggestion. 

I would just like to add one other thought. Again it would be well if the interested groups here would look into the so-called cultural missions of the Soviet Union and the satellites. I have tried to make a very cursory examination of this, but again I confess we just don't

have the time for all of it. It is a shame, because we ought to have this in the papers. 

People ought to know what is going on. There are literally thousands and thousands of young people from Czechoslovakia and Bulgaria and the Soviet Union and East Germany and China, every state in the satellite system, going into Indonesia, into India, into Burma, into every place. I am talking about young people, 15, 16, 18, 20 years of age. And what are they doing? They are doing just what you said,

Mrs. Ruopp. They are giving their cultural dances, going down there with their books, they are there on visits, games, athletic events. We had to fight like Trojans to have even a trade fair, mind you, from the United States of America. It is almost unbelievable when you think about it. I get irritated when I mention it. The greatest productive nation in the world still is not capable of putting on a firstclass trade fair all over the world.

I heard witnesses 3 weeks ago telling how in New Delhi the Soviet Union has machinery on the spot for immediate delivery, machinery that is as competent, I mean machinery as fine as any that we can produce, printing presses, plows, and tractors. Everything is right there. Write out the order, and they are there with people that speak the language.

We always have to send somebody over that has just learned to speak English, plus an interpreter. Not in all instances, because I must say we are doing better. We are improving, but under the Soviet education system, out of the first 14 years of education, 10 years are dedicated to the study of 6 or more languages, and only 14 percent of all the students in all American institutions of learning take any modern languages. The figures, in terms of what the Soviet Union is doing today in educating engineers and scientists as compared to what we are doing, are staggering.

TECHNICAL EDUCATION IN THE U. S. S. R. 

We have lost 53 percent of all the teachers of mathematics and science in American high schools since 1950. The Soviet Union has doubled our number of scientists in the last 2 years. They export many technicians and with this technician comes indoctrination. 

They are no longer the typical image of the Bolshevik with the big bomb and the whiskers, any more than the capitalist is the big fat fellow with the dollar signs all over him.

We have to recognize this problem, and this is part of what I call the new offensive that we are up against. We had better be doing something about it before it is too late, because they are making tremendous inroads. 

Mrs. Ruopp, you set this all off. It is your fault. I had no intention of saying that, but I surely have some strong feelings about it. 

Mr. Pierce Butler, I believe that you asked to be heard at this time.

I mention these last things, ladies and gentlemen, because I know there are a number of groups associated with the World Affairs Center and I hope you can develop this material and get it out to the citizenry of our community. This is a very great obligation. 

I think we are prone to underestimate the opposition. I think we are almost premeditatingly underestimating the nature of a competitor in this contest between our way of life and the Soviet totalitarian way

of life. If we continue to do it we are going to wake up some day, as we did after Pearl Harbor, when our admiral said that war would be over in 6 weeks and the pessimists said it would take 6 months. 

It was 5 years, and the next time it might not be quite so easy. 

Go ahead, Mr. Butler.

STATEMENT OF PIERCE BUTLER III, ST. PAUL, MINN. 

Mr. BUTLER. My name is Pierce Butler III. I am a lawyer from

St. Paul. The views I state are entirely my own. 

About a half hour ago a lady read a statement that I thought was quite good and she stated among other things that you cannot trust the Russians and that there should be no disarmament unless they demonstrated by deed and not by words their peaceful intent. I agree.

She also stated that she felt that unless this committee, which is considering methods of disarmament, concurred in that proposition, it ought to disband, and I agree with that, too. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I would agree with that, too. 

OUR POLICY OF MUTUAL DETERRENCE 

Mr. BUTLER. Disarmament is not an end in itself. The proper end is security and we are merely seeking to determine, as I understand it, whether by disarmament we might not be more secure than we are at present. We have a policy essentially of mutual deterrence, as Secretary Dulles said, with the sword of Damocles hanging over both sides. 

I think President Eisenhower recently stated that modern armaments, where major wars are concerned, have value merely as a deterrent to attack. He was saying in other words that once a major war was started, no amount of preparedness can offer an effective defense. 

Secretary Dulles in testimony before this committee defined our foreign policy as one of mutual deterrence and further stated that it is not a very satisfactory policy. Most people including President 

Eisenhower agree that mutual deterrence is not a satisfactory policy. 

At Geneva last summer the open skies plan was proposed as a possible alternative. This plan as finally formulated calls for an exchange of military blueprints with the Russians, accompanied by air and ground inspection without disarmament. It is for inspection only with the object of preventing surprise attack. 

THE IMPORTANCE OF PSYCHOLOGICAL INITIATIVE 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I am glad that you are emphasizing that point, Mr. Butler, because I think some people have been prone to feel that the President's proposal was a disarmament proposal as such. He has tried to make it quite clear as have the representatives of the executive branch, that it has one prime purpose; namely, to ascertain the possibility of surprise attack. I happen to feel that if the Soviets would ever accept aerial inspection with all of the intricate mechanism that is involved in it, that it would really indicate a fundamental change in Soviet attitude. I doubt if there are any absolute safeguards, but as has been said, it is the best we have been able to contrive and I just want to add this: I thought that the open skies proposal put us momentarily out in front. 72880-56-pt. 9- -8

In other words, there have been two things that I have seen in the last 3 or 4 years that I want to commend. One was the President's proposal of the peacetime use of atomic energy-the international body to do it. This set off the chain reaction in the world. It put us out in front in that area temporarily. The President's proposal at Geneva surprised many people, including the Congress who knew nothing about it, and I don't say that is wrong. He is our spokesman in foreign affairs, and he is entitled under that constitutional prerogative to speak for the country.

It surely caught the Soviet Union off base. In fact they were caught so much off base that they literally had to recess that part of the negotiations, and it is still a matter of consultation. I think again it shows the importance of psychological initiative, particularly if we are willing to back it up. 

Mr. BUTLER. I was not saying that this proposal was not very useful. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I understand that. 

Mr. BUTLER. It was, because it demonstrated to a great many people that the United States is really in favor of peace. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. That is right. 

UNITED STATES AND U. S. S. R. INFORMATION TECHNIQUES CONTRASTED 

Mr. BUTLER. And that we are not intent on becoming an aggressor. 

I think that there are a great many people, particularly in the Middle East and the Far East, who fear that aggression is much more likely to come from our side than it is from the Russian side and from the United States. They are afraid of us because of the ineptitude of our information service over the last 10 years, over the postwar period.

Chairman HUMPHREY. It might also be because the Soviet Union has been most clever in its diabolical purpose of being able to get people of their own kind amongst the native group to carry their message. Here has been part of our problem. We have tried to superimpose an information system of Americans so to speak throughout the world. The Soviet has been able to subvert our efforts, and get agents within the community to act as their spokesmen, which gives them much closer contact with the public. 

Mr. BUTLER. What you are saying is that we should be using the same techniques?

Chairman HUMPHREY. I should say that Mr. BUTLER. Of subversion essentially. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I don't say that we should use subversion but I think we should utilize, particularly out of our own midst, the tremendous number of cultural groups that we have. It might even be better in certain areas to take the cultural groups in our own country to another part of the world. 

Mr. BUTLER. I think it is true, is it not, that the President later suggested that the inspection plan, if accepted, might facilitate at a subsequent time a comprehensive and effective system of inspection and disarmament, of inspected disarmament I should say, and I wish to devote my remarks here to an examination of these proposals.

RUSSIANS UNLIKELY TO ACCEPT AERIAL INSPECTION PLAN 

The first and most important matter seems to me to be the fact, I think, that it is really very unlikely that the Russians are going to accept any inspection plan, whether it is accompanied by a limited disarmament scheme or whether it is simply an inspection plan to insure against a surprise attack. The reason is that Russia is a totalitarian regime capable of cloaking its policies and its warlike preparations with a security which can never be available to any free country.

In free countries like the United States and its allies, citizens may move about within and without national borders with little or no restriction. They are free to talk with whom they please and their newspapers and other organs of information are free to publish what they choose. It is impossible to create in the United States a system of internal security which will even approach that of the Russians without violating the United States Constitution.

It is also impossible to do so in the nations which are allied with the United States, and where the United States directly has bases upon which its defense now depends, without violating the fundamental traditions and laws of these free peoples. It follows that the Russians are always likely to have far better intelligence regarding our preparations for war than we can have as to theirs. 

Even if secrecy is not an advantage to the Russians the presence of the Iron Curtain shows they think it is. Why then should the Russians, by accepting the open skies proposal or some modification of it, providing for first steps so-called, voluntarily give up the great intelligence advantage they now have or think they have?

Chairman HUMPHREY. I recognize the basic validity of your analysis. That is why I said that if they did accept an inspection plan it would mean a fundamental change in their overall policy. 

However, it was intriguing to me that in the London Conference the Soviet representative did agree to and advance a plan, a very detailed inspection plan, of conventional weapons, military installations, factories, practically everything except the thermonuclear and nuclear installations. 

No aerial inspections. Of course, the vast expanse of territory makes it impossible to roam around in jeeps as you can on the Gaza strip or even over in Korea but it was quite interesting at this stage they should have made this concession which they refused to do pointblank in other conferences. I think they are beginning to appreciate the lack of importance of some conventional weapons. 

Mr. BUTLER. I was just about to come to that point. There is a qualitative problem and it is a question that any disarmament scheme, bilateral or multilateral is bound to get into. 

I think without exception-I don't know that I have read all of the transcript of previous hearings of this committee-but I think without exception so far every scientific and technical witness that has appeared before this committee has testified that no form of inspection, ground or air, is going to be thoroughly effective as to nuclear and thermonuclear weapons, and also that such ground and air inspection will not be effective as to the ICBM when that gets developed and there has been time to conceal launching sites. 

Isn't that correct, Senator?

IS THE U. S. S. R. STALLING FOR TIME? 

Chairman HUMPHREY. That is right, and one of the worries that I have as one citizen and one Senator is that the Soviets stalled us for years on the Baruch plan until the Baruch plan was outdated once they were able to get their own stockpile of atomic weapons. 

Now the concealment of atomic and thermonuclear weapons is a matter of fact as was pointed to here today, and the scientists that we have had testify tell us that if the aerial inspection proposal can be stalled by the Soviet Union for the next 3, 4, or 5 years, or whatever time it may take to develop an intercontinental missile, and they can get their launching sites underground there will be no way that you can ascertain that they are even there. 

So we may be in a political stall or a political drag again, while the current proposal loses its effectiveness. That is why we had to scrap most of our disarmament proposals here in the last year or two, because the world weapons scene had changed so rapidly that the older plans that we had in 1946 and 1947 on up were no longer applicable. 

Mr. BUTLER. That is right.

DANGERS OF BILATERAL DISARMAMENT 

The weapons picture is not static. The picture changes very rapidly, and this brings me to the third point I wanted to make, which is simply this: That if the Russians were bolder and craftier, they might well accept an inspected disarmament plan, after having concealed supplies of nuclear and thermonuclear weapons. These apparently cannot be discovered at present if they are concealed. The 

United States, in all likelihood, would live up to the spirit and the letter of a treaty for disarmament and inspection and refrain from concealment of such weapons, and make honest disclosure.
