The Russians would then have us at their mercy and we would not be able to retaliate. This shows the danger of merely bilateral disarmament. I think this prospect is unlikely, just because the Russians are not quite that smart and they lack the real daring and cunning that such a scheme demands. And also the Russians probably attribute to us motives such as those we attribute to them and believe that we would only pretend to disarm. 

The relative difficulty with the so-called open skies inspection plan is then that it fails to offer the Russians any more security than it offers us and that is why they won't be likely to accept it. 

DIFFICULTIES OF ENFORCING AERIAL INSPECTION 

Now the testimony of Secretary Dulles was that our fundamental plan is to dispel fear and insecurity and that the United States advocates as a first step a system of inspection which will dissipate fear and distrust by giving warning of any sudden surprise attack. The present inspection plan is not going to have that effect. It is not going to dispel fear because it cannot be enforced. If the Russians pretend to disarm or pretend to submit to inspection and actually conceal weapons, there is no way to retaliate against them if they suddenly, after having waited until their enemies have disarmed, threaten an attack.

Chairman HUMPHREY. We asked Mr. Stassen and Secretary Wilson what we would do in case there is a violation of an agreed-upon inspection plan. The question was, "How could we enforce it?" and the reply was, "Well, you just have to start up all over again and rearm. 

Mr. BUTLER. You withdraw from it.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Withdraw from it. 

Mr. BUTLER. In other words, it leaves you essentially in status quo, and a further point is the judging of whether the treaty is being violated. There is no neutral third force or enemy neutral judicial body that can determine whether a violation has occurred. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Under a bilateral system. 

Mr. BUTLER. Under a bilateral system, that is right. It follows that the only way to protect any nation which disarms is to create simultaneously a third party agency with the power and the necessary forces with which to retaliate upon a violator of the disarmament treaty. We are not secure otherwise and neither are the Russians, or at least they will think they are not.

It is obvious, moreover, as Dulles said, that our essential problem is to devise a plan which will give a suspicious Russia a feeling of security that she now lacks. That is why any disarmament plan, in addition to being accompanied by inspection, has to be enforceable. 

A number of workable suggestions as to the detail of such an enforcement system have been put forward; that of Louis B. Sohn, which has been recommended to this committee and is certainly feasible. 

Any scheme to obtain disarmament a step at a time without defining in advance what the later steps will be and what kind of enforcement system we end up with affords no security. You cannot expect the 

Russians or anyone else to agree to take limited disarmament measures now and talk later about further measures when meanwhile the risk of violation of the current agreements exists. 

COMPREHENSIVE VERSUS PIECE-MEAL PLAN 

It is a legal commonplace that a contract to make a contract, all the terms of which will be defined later, is illusory and legally unenforcible. As a practical matter, no nation is going to forgo some security now in exchange for a solemn promise to negotiate later about further steps.

The fundamental point then is that no plan for disarmament will be acceptable to Russia or say to us, unless it is comprehensive, and the testimony before this committee of Adm. Louis Strauss of the 

Atomic Energy Commission was precisely that. 

He said he thought "that any plan that might be advanced ought to be a comprehensive and not attempt to be a piecemeal plan." 

The only further thing that I have to say is I think the primary activity of this committee, which has devoted such a lot of time and effort to the problem involved in security and disarmament, should be to make the public aware of the fact that a comprehensive rather than piecemeal plan is necessary and that indeed is Strauss' feeling about it and he ought to know. 

(The prepared statement is as follows:)

My name is Pierce Butler III. I am a lawyer from St. Paul. The views I state are entirely my own.

President Eisenhower recently stated that modern armaments, where major wars are concerned, have value-merely as a deterrent to attack. 

He was saying, in other words, that once a major war was started no amount of military preparation can offer a defense.

MUTUAL DETERRENCE UNSATISFACTORY POLICY 

Secretary of State Dulles in testimony before this committee defined our foreign policy as one of "mutual deterrence" and further stated that it is not a very satisfactory policy. 

Most people, including President Eisenhower agree that our present policy of mutual deterrence is not very satisfactory. At Geneva last summer the open skies plan was proposed as a possible alternative. This plan as finally formulated calls for an exchange of military blueprints with the Russians, accompanied by air and ground inspection. This plan is for inspection only with the object of preventing surprise attack. The President suggested that it might facilitate a comprehensive and effective system of inspection and disarmament. 

FALLIBILITY OF OPEN SKIES PROPOSAL 

Examination of this plan discloses certain grave risks to United States security as well as other problems.

First, it seems most unlikely that the Russians will accept it. Russia is a totalitarian regime capable of cloaking its policies and its warlike preparations with a secrecy which is never available to any free nation. In free countries, like the United States and its allies, citizens may move about within and without national borders with little or no restriction. They are free to talk with whom they please and their newspapers and other organs of information are free to publish what they choose. It is impossible to create in the United States a system of internal security which will even approach that of the Russians without violating the United States Constitution. It is also impossible in the nations allied with the United States, and where the United States has the bases upon which its defense depends, without violating the fundamental traditions and laws of these free peoples. 

It follows that the Russians are likely always to have far better intelligence regarding our preparations for war than we can as to theirs. Even if secrecy is not an advantage to the Russians, the presence of the Iron Curtain shows they think it is. Why then, should the Russians, by accepting the open skies plan, voluntarily give up the great intelligence advantage they now have or think they have?

In other words, from the Soviet point of view, acceptance of the open skies inspection plan would be foolhardy, since it lacks any concomitant, agreed provision giving the Russians security as great or greater than that afforded by their present uninspected secrecy. 

The primary target of each country is the strategic air command of the other. 

The bases for the Russian strategic air command are within Russia. Why should the Russians disclose to us the location of those bases when we have overseas bases far closer to Russia than Russia has to the United States and which the Russians can readily locate by reading the newspapers of free countries or visiting the countries where our bases are. 

A second objection to the inspection plan is that without exception every scientific and technical witness who has so far publicly testified to this committee has stated that no form of inspection is thoroughly effective. No inspection system known can discover concealed nuclear or thermonuclear material. It seems likely that if an inspection plan is not put into effect until after the ICBM has been perfected, launching sites can be concealed underground in such a way that even ground inspection might not reveal them. 

This brings me to my third point which is that if the Russians were bolder and craftier, they might accept an inspected disarmament plan after having concealed supplies of nuclear and thermonuclear weapons. The United States in all likelihood would live up to the spirit of the treaty for disarmament and inspection and refrain from concealment and make honest disclosure. The Russians would then have us at their mercy and we would not be able to retaliate. That awful prospect is an unlikely one only because the Russians probably lack the real daring and cunning that such a scheme demands and probably believe that the United States would likewise only pretend to disarm.

PRESENT PLAN FAILS TO PROVIDE SECURITY 

The real difficulty with the so-called open skies inspection plan is, therefore, that it fails to offer the Russians any more security than it offers us. 

The testimony of Secretary Dulles was that our fundamental plan is to dispel fear and insecurity and that "the United States advocates as a first step a system of inspection" which will dissipate fear and distrust by giving warning of any sudden surprise attack.

The present inspection plan, however, is not going to dispel fear because it cannot be enforced. Whoever disarms pursuant to the plan remains subject to attack by whoever manages to evade the inspection. Therefore, the only way to protect a nation who disarms is to create simultaneously a third party agency with the power and necessary forces with which to retaliate upon a violator. We are not secure otherwise and neither are the Russians-or at least they will think they are not. It is obvious, moreover, that our essential problem is to devise a plan which will give a suspicious Russia a feeling of security that she now lacks. 

PLAN UNENFORCIBLE 

This is why any disarmament plan, in addition to being accompanied by inspection, must be enforcible. A number of workable suggestions as to the detail of such an enforcement system have been put forward. That of Louis Sohn and 

Grenville Clark has been recommended to this committee and is feasible. 

Any scheme to obtain disarmament a step at a time without defining in advance what the later steps will be and what kind of enforcement system we end up with affords no security. You cannot expect the Russians or anyone else to agree to take limited disarmament measures now and talk later about further measures when meanwhile the risk of violation of the current agreements exists. 

It is a legal commonplace that a contract to make a contract, all the terms of which will be defined later, is illusory and legally unenforcible. 

As a practical matter, no nation is going to forego some security now in exchange for a solemn promise to negotiate later about further steps. It would be just as improvident for a nation to accept in advance the decision of an international body on a problem that it might negotiate about privately and directly now. I would certainly not want the United States to do that. 

A COMPREHENSIVE DISARMAMENT PLAN REQUIRED 

The fundamental point, then, is that no plan for disarmament will be likely to be acceptable to Russia-or safe for the United States unless the program is a comprehensive one. The testimony before this committee of Admiral Louis 

Strauss of the Atomic Energy Commission was precisely that. He said he thought "that any plan that might be advanced ought to be a comprehensive and not attempt to be a piecemeal plan."

In the absence of a comprehensive plan detailing each step in the disarmament process and providing for an armed and effective third force with the responsibility of policing the agreed-upon steps in the disarmament process and with the actual power to make agreed-upon inspections and to retaliate against possible violations, no country, particularly the United States, can safely risk disarmament. Countries like Russia and China whose domestic and international policies are instinct with suspicion could not be induced to risk it. Further attempts to make "first steps" or "beginnings" are bootless and can only generate hopelessness, for no real disarmament or security can be so obtained. A primary activity of this committee, which has devoted much time to the problems involved, should be to make the public aware of this fact. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Butler, what is it that leads you to believe that the Russians would regard a third party agency as a guaranty of their security, in light of the fact that they have persistently stressed national sovereignty in arguing against the Baruch proposals and other disarmament proposals. 

Mr. BUTLER. Because they feel insecure now.

The attitude of the Russians I am absolutely sure is that we are encircling them, that we are aggressive. That is their own theory; their own definition of capitalism is that it is an aggressive antisocialist force. They have observed us building up a system of bases

which do in fact geographically encircle them. It is perfectly understandable to any of us here that those are for defensive purposes and defensive purposes only, but to the Russians, with their special definition of non-Communist policy, this is no doubt an act of aggression, and I would say that the behavior of the Russians in diplomatic exchanges and in international politics and in domestic politics in Russia over the last 25 years discloses that their leaders are suspicious of each other and of everybody outside of Russia. 

I don't think there is the slightest question that it would surprise greatly most competent Russian politicians and intellectuals to learn that the United States is really afraid of Russia. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. You have the feeling in other words that the third party agency such as a U. N. or 

Mr. BUTLER. It might give them a greater feeling of security than they have now. In other words, the Kremlin is occupied no doubt by opportunists. I do not think there is any doubt about that. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Hardheaded ones? 

Mr. BUTLER. Hardheaded opportunists, tough boys from the villages, and these fellows want to stay in office. I am sure you know what I mean.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Sometimes I wonder why, but I surely do know what you mean. 

Mr. BUTLER. And if they think their chance of avoiding domestic difficulty in Russia, or a war which would be a draw or which Russia would lose, is better under some international system of controls, they are more likely to accept that than the present system of mutual deterrence, which, as you cited, their behavior after Geneva shows they think is dangerous. The Russians have obviously got some real doubts about mutual deterrence, too.

Chairman HUMPHREY. According to some of our scientists and others assigned to our government, there is at least a growing understanding of the Soviet Union on the part of their leaders as to the awful aspect of the thermonuclear war, but I am not in any position to know whether this is added deterrence. We can only accept or at least try to evaluate what is presented to us.

I cannot but believe that there is a time element in all of this, if you are going to get any disarmament agreements with the Soviet Union that are enforcible. If it is not enforcible it will be very limited in its effectiveness. If you are going to do it the time element is very important.

Mr. BUTLER. Yes, there is a very real possibility that countries like Guatemala will soon know how to make a hydrogen bomb. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. It is not at all improbable. 

Mr. BUTLER. Not at all improbable. I am sure a competent Guatemalan scientist could today produce a bacteriological weapon which would be effective and dangerous. 

I have nothing further. Thank you. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Butler, we appreciate your testimony very much. May I suggest we take about a 5-minute recess and then we will proceed to the other witnesses. 

The next witness will be Mr. Richardson B. Okie. 

(Short recess.)

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Richardson B. Okie is here, I believe, at the witness table. Will those in the back of the auditorium kindly come up here and join us? 

Mr. Okie, would you proceed? 

May I just make one request? I have been a violator of this myself because I have tried to tie down some of this testimony a little bit. If you have anything that has been presented, would you just allude to it, and if you can give us something new or a personal observation that has not been properly emphasized today—this is not directed to you, Mr. Okie, as such but as to all the witnesses-just try to shorten it up because we have a number left and I would like to get everybody heard today. 

Proceed, Mr. Okie.

STATEMENT OF RICHARDSON B. OKIE 

Mr. OKIE. My name is Richardson B. Okie, of 1961 Goodrich Avenue, St. Paul, Minn.

I am engaged in writing and research. I have been interested in international affairs for some years and the views that I express are only my own. What I have to say I think is not new in all its aspects but I think I have certain things to add to what has been said. 

In the first place, the very existence of this committee recognizes the great danger of an arms race as well as the danger that we face from Russia, and we have heard certain testimony here leading us to think that the danger of the arms race is also recognized in Russia. 

We have heard the discussion of President Eisenhower's open sky inspection plan and the method which seems to have been preferred by the Russians of stage-by-stage conventional disarmament. They seem to have reduced their armed forces by 1,200,000 and are challenging us to match this, and are waiting in the wings with a type of proposal having to do with nuclear weapons. 

In all of this the safety of the United States and of the world is at stake and it is certainly true that the most cautious approach is necessary.

BUILDING A BASE OF CONFIDENCE ABROAD 

I doubt if either of the types of plan mentioned before, or any other plan, can be carried out very far until a basis of confidence is laid both here and abroad, so our problem is partly how can we build a firm base of confidence in a way that is realistic enough to develop support for a cautious disarmament. 

Let's consider for a minute these steps of reasoning.

First, it seems to be agreed both here and abroad that nuclear war would be folly.

Secondly, local wars we know very well can involve the interests of great powers and can lead to nuclear war. 

Therefore these so-called local wars must be prevented and a mechanism for doing this must be made effective. 

It is not enough in thinking of disarmament to consider only the matter of the Soviet Union and the United States nor to prevent a direct sudden clash of these great powers. The causes of war, big and small, are still present. The big powers still tend to become

embroiled, nuclear age or not, and it seems to me that this is our greatest danger today.

For example, we have the situation now in the Middle East between Israel and the Arab countries and we have turmoil in North Africa, a developing situation in Korea again and in Indochina. 

Smoldering situations are not new in the world, but the catastrophe which they can touch off is immensely new. I suggest to this committee that the time has come to explore more fully the mechanisms which are already written into the United Nations Charter designed to deal with these very situations. 

We know that the functioning of the United Nations was made dependent on great power cooperation and we know also that the frustrations of the U. N. have arisen quite largely from the separate purposes of Soviet Russia.

But now the new Soviet leadership has rather recently joined in sending Mr. Hammarskjold to the Middle East for the purpose of preserving the peace. It looks from this as if they have recognized, at least to some extent, this danger of an uncontrollable war growing out of a smaller war.

The problem in the Middle East therefore now is to make the United Nations effective, and it seems to me that the Soviets might quite well be willing to explore a further step in this direction. It also seems to me that there is some value to us in pushing them a little further to see what more they will do. In other words, test out their intentions. Anyway, it seems to me that the United States has a duty of leadership just as other responsible nations do, and we also have a great interest in it. 

URGES CREATION OF A SMALL UNITED NATIONS POLICE FORCE 

Senator Humphrey, I propose that your committee should consider recommending to the President that our delegate to the United Nations press for the establishment of a small United Nations police force to back up Mr. Hammarskjold and the United Nations truce team in Korea.

Chairman HUMPHREY. In where? 

Mr. OKIE. In the Middle East, I beg your pardon. Such a force would underline the determination of nations to keep the peace, and I think it would be important for the force to be small. I should think as little as one battalion, perhaps, from each large nation ranging down to a company or platoon from the smaller, well-equipped troops. The small size would pose no threat to the sovereignty of any nation, but like a policeman in a small town, this small force would represent the serious intent of the community to enforce the law, in this case the law of the United Nations Charter against aggression. 

And I suggest to you that this force would not be lightly defied.

Such a force, if of this size, could scarcely be objected to except by a nation harboring the intent of aggression, and I suggest to you that it is of value to us to expose any intent of aggression. 

It seems to me that article 43 in the charter with its emergency measures which can be taken by the Security Council in the event of danger to peace, gives the authority for setting up such a force. 

Mr. Chairman, I urge this course.

I believe that the building of peace is a slow and laborious matter, that we should not neglect any small step and that this particular step might do something to create that greater degree of confidence which is necessary for any real progress in disarmament in which larger steps might be more hopefully advanced. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Okie is as follows:) 

Mr. Chairman, Senators, members of the committee, as I understand it your committee's work was undertaken in the atmosphere of public interest generated by the efforts of the administration to explore all possible avenues to disarmament, in full recognition that the continuance of a nuclear and conventional arms race between the Communists and the Western Powers would grow more and more dangerous until it might lead to a fatal explosion. 

At the present time two major types of proposals are in the foreground for serious consideration by the governments: (1) President Eisenhower's "open sky" aerial inspection proposal, designed to prevent surprise attacks and to allay fear of them, through laying military establishments, troop movements, and major arms developments open to the effective surveillance of both parties; and (2) the stage-by-stage conventional disarmament apparently favored by the Russians, who claim to be releasing 1,200,000 men from active service, and challenge the United States to do the same. 

Obviously the safety of the United States is so important that such sweeping proposals call for the most cautious approach. I question whether either type proposal, or any other large-scale disarmament proposal, can be very far advanced until a certain climate of support is generated for it, both in the United States and abroad. How can we find a realistic basis of confidence, in which support for a cautious disarmament can be developed? 

Consider the following steps of reasoning: 

(1) Nuclear war is folly. Both sides have apparently come to recognize this. (2) Local wars-whatever their origin—can lead to nuclear war if they touch vital interests of the great powers. (3) Local wars must therefore be prevented. A mechanism must be made effective for this purpose. 

It is plainly not enough to seek means of preventing a direct sudden clash of the great powers. The play of border rivalries, resource rivalries, conflicting ambitions, real grievances, and inherited hatreds which have tended to embroil nations, whether small or large, for centuries, still tend to embroil them; and the great powers still tend to become engaged, nuclear age or no. This constitutes the greatest danger we have to face today. 

For one single example I call your attention to the situation in the Middle East, where the hostility between Israel and the Arab States amounts to no less than a huge powder-keg, with its fuse smoldering. Other such situations exist elsewhere in North Africa, in Korea, in Indochina. These smoldering situations are nothing new in the world. But the catastrophe they could touch off is immensely new.

I urge upon this committee that the time has come to explore to their fullest extent the mechanisms in the United Nations Charter designed to deal with the very type of situation above named. 

Functioning of the U. N. was deliberately made dependent on great power cooperation. Separate purposes of the U. S. S. R. have been responsible for much of the U. N.'s frustration. But it should be noted that the Soviet Union recently joined in the decision of the Security Council to send Dag Hammarskjold, U. N. 

Secretary-General, to the Middle East to preserve the uneasy truce and to explore avenues of peace. This indicates that the Soviet under their new leadership recognize the danger to everyone of an uncontrollable war. Since the current problem in the Middle East is to make U. N. influence effective, the Soviet might be willing to explore further means of doing this. At any rate it seems to me that the United States, like other responsible nations, has a duty and an interest in leadership for this purpose. 

I therefore propose that your committee consider seriously a recommendation to the President that our delegate to the U. N. be instructed to press for the establishment of a small United Nations Police Force, such as would be useful in backing up the findings of Mr. Hammarskjold and the U. N. truce observation teams now in Palestine.

Creation of such a force would serve to underline the determination of the overwhelming majority of the world's peoples, including the great powers, to keep the peace.

Such a force might consist of a single battalion of well-equipped troops from each of the larger nations, ranging down to 1 company or 1 platoon from the smaller. Its significance, like that of a policeman or small police force in a community, would lie in its representing the serious intent of the community to enforce the law-in this case the law of the charter against aggression. 

In its small size the police force would pose no threat to the sovereignty of any nation. It would not be "anti" anybody except lawbreakers. It would be hard to find a rational objection to it, except by some nation harboring a serious intent of aggression. It would be in the public interest to expose any such intent.

Article 43 of the charter (with articles immediately following) gives the Security Council authority to call such a force into being. 

Mr. Chairman, I urge consideration of this course.

The task of building peace is slow and laborious. We should not neglect forward steps because they appear small. This small U. N. police force might well prevent the fearful prospect of small wars breaking out and becoming the uncontrollable big war which everyone fears.

This one forward step might do much to create that climate of feeling in the great powers in which the larger steps toward disarmament might be more hopefully advanced. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much, Mr. Okie. We appreciate your coming here. 

Mr. Arthur Sternberg?

STATEMENT OF ARTHUR STERNBERG, REPRESENTING THE MINNE- SOTA FELLOWSHIP OF RECONCILIATION, ST. PAUL, MINN. 

Mr. STERNBERG. I am Arthur Sternberg, 1563 Fairmont Avenue,

St. Paul. I am a legal editor with the West Publishing Co. I am speaking today on behalf of the Minnesota Fellowship of Reconciliation of which I am executive secretary. The Minnesota fellowship is associated with a national and international organization of the same name concerned with problems of world peace, particularly in reconciling differences between people by love and basic sociological principles.

Rather than making a formal statement at this time, I would like to make two or three brief comments in the general area that I think will pose a slightly different point of view. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. And we shall incorporate your full state ment in the testimony. (The prepared statement of Mr. Sternberg is as follows:)

A STATEMENT BY THE MINNESOTA FELLOWSHIP OF RECONCILIATION ON DISARMA- MENT AND THE ATTITUDE TO ARMAMENTS 

The Minnesota Fellowship of Reconciliation is a branch of the American organization of the same name, which in turn is associated with other fellowships of reconciliation in the international fellowship. We were born during World 

War I and have as our objectives the encouragement of genuine reconciliation between and among groups and peoples. We do not think this reconciling process can take place at a superficial level; rather must it attack human problems at their roots, if true reconciliation is to be possible. Economic and political changes and rearrangements must be accompanied by changes in attitude and outlook; and the latter do not necessarily flow automatically from the former, or vice versa.

It is in this context that we wish to present our view on disarmament. (1) We believe that the existence of huge armaments represents an insecure state of mind and inadequate world organization. We also think that competitive armaments prevent our tackling the political and economic problems of the

world at their roots. Psychologically, armaments act as a bar because we become so intent on "keeping up" with the Soviet Union that we do not have the will to deal imaginatively with the political and economic questions out of which future wars emerge; and our confidence in armaments tends, subconsciously at least, to lead us to minimize the importance of discussion of fundamental problems. Economically, armaments consume so many of our resources that we have only driblets left for the task of world reconstruction and development, which is so vitally important if basic causes of world tension are to be resolved: We simply cannot keep up an armaments race and finance the economic development of Asia at the same time, even with our large national income. (2) While we fully recognize that an aggressive Soviet Union plays a not inconsiderable part in the failure of the world to stop the arms race, we deny that the existence of the Soviet Union is the only factor, or even the major factor, in the existence of the arms race itself. We do not believe that any international issue can ever be explained in simple terms and one of the great errors in present day thinking is to pin all or most of the blame for huge armaments on the Soviet Union. When we do so, we forget that the world did not disarm after World War I either (the level of armies and navies throughout the world during that period was generally higher-with the exception of the military forces of Germany-than it had been before 1914). We may also forget that the postwar military expansion of the Soviet Union may be explained in part by the fact that in 1945 the United States alone had the secret of the atom bomb, which we chose not to disclose, while nonetheless we were engaged in stockpiling bombs to increase the margin of our military superiority over the Soviet Union. (3) The failure of the world to disarm after 1919 and our failure thus far to make much progress today are due fundamentally to a commonly held belief that armaments somehow can make for security (and "security" is almost never defined). This is the faith, basically, of both the Soviet Union and the United 

States and is the great stumbling block to all our earnest efforts. We believe that this faith is a misplaced faith and that if we ever cease to have it we shall begin to disarm.

Despite widespread recognition of the probably disastrous consequences of a future war, most men still somehow believe that armaments will prevent such a war, or, if it breaks out (and this very expression assumes that we are not responsible), that they will enable us to win. We believe that both aspects of the faith are utterly unjustified: While armaments may prevent a war for relatively short periods—although this is far from certain—their long-run effect is and can be only to help develop an atmosphere in which war becomes more certain; and if a war does break out, armaments will not enable us to win, for there will be, literally speaking, no victor in the next war-Eisenhower, Bulganin, MacArthur, and many others have recognized this fact. (4) If, then, the faith in armaments is a misplaced faith and they will not in fact do what is claimed for them, and if the other observations of this statement have validity-the whole problem of disarmament is put in a new light. Our attitude to disarmament will not be "we shall disarm if proper inspection plans are developed," but rather "let's disarm, inspection plans or not, since we have lost all faith in armaments for any ends claimed for them." Armaments, far from being an advantage, are a liability and a curse. (5) If progress in diasarmament is to be made we must avoid the stalemates of past disarmament efforts. The attitude in the past on all sides of disarmament conference tables has been one of offering to sacrifice what is not needed, while demanding a real concession from the other side. Such proposals are rejected and a similar counterproposal comes from the other side. Each party tries to strengthen its position, or at least maintain the same relative position while looking for someone else to make the big concession. Negotiations will be doomed to failure unless some nation is willing to say, "Disarmament is so necessary that we will make a proposal that will weaken our relative position." 

We are the strongest nation, or so we assume. If there is a responsibility on some nation to take that step, the responsibility rests with us. 

This new attitude to some implies the acceptance of unilateral disarmament as an example to the world and as an expression of utter lack of faith in the whole armaments scheme. But at the very minimum, the new attitude would imply a complete skepticism as to the value of armaments under almost any conceivable modern circumstances.

We believe that if this new attitude to armaments could be developed, in the context of the other points made by this statement, we might proceed more rapidly in the direction of disarmament. Instead of believing, as most of us do

today, that "while armaments are a curse, we need them as a last resort," our position would be "armaments are a liability, period." 

We believe that this is the only realistic political position to take and that the prevalent attitude, both in the Soviet Union and in the United States, is an utterly utopian and unrealistic outlook. 

ARMAMENTS GIVE FALSE SENSE OF SECURITY 

Mr. STERNBERG. First, I would suggest this, the proposition that armaments no longer give security. Senator, you have indicated your primary concern that we should not play fast and loose with security. 

I do not feel that we have security at the present time. 

In recent years it has been said that there is no defense against the atom bomb. Probably there has been some defense. But certainly the scientists seem to agree that with the new weapons that we are developing, possibilities of defense become less and less, and particularly with the intercontinental ballistic missiles which are contemplated. There the scientists say there is even less opportunity for defense than there is at the present time. 

Hanson Baldwin, military analyst of the New York Times, writing in the Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, May 1956, made just that statement, that is the dilemma of military policy today is that there is no security. 

DISARMAMENT IMPERATIVE AND FEASIBLE 

The second proposition is this: That disarmament is imperative.

Disarmament is imperative first, as I have said, because there is no security in armaments; and, in the second place, disarmament is imperative because with a concern on our part for military defense we cannot do the other things in the world that we know that we should. 

I would like to underscore many of the things that Mrs. Ruopp commented about. Also, I would like to recall your own comments about the difficulties in the United States Congress of getting through appropriations for exchange students, foreign aid, the activities of the specialized United Nations agencies and that sort of thing. 

You commented about the contrast of our policy and that of the Soviet Union where they are doing many things to win the minds of men over the world.

The Soviet Union- 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Don't misunderstand me, I think we are trying to do it too. As an American citizen I feel I have the right to point up our shortcomings. This does not mean that I am not aware of our great potentialities and capabilities in what we are doing. 

Mr. STERNBERG. You have merely deplored the fact that we are not doing what we should do in that regard. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want us to do more, that is all. 

Mr. STERNBERG. Well now, the difference: Soviet Russia, of course, is a dictatorship and they can dictate a military program and they can also, the leaders, dictate a program of winning the minds of men across the world. Here in the United States we are a democracy and we have to get our people to support what we do, and at the present time we are placing our emphasis so completely upon military defense and weapons that the other things that we should be doing and that many of us would like to do are just being shunted aside because of our overbearing concern with the military point of view.

PROBLEMS RELATING TO FOREIGN AID 

Chairman HUMPHREY. This is part of the argument on the foreign aid bill, and I hope people will be somewhat charitable with others as they may disagree on the foreign aid bill. I have been reading a great deal about it. I know there are people that will disagree with my point of view, and I am not so sure my point of view is 100 percent right, the more testimony I listen to in committee. 

Eighty-some percent of the foreign aid bill is for military assistance, and the question is whether or not it is even the right kind of military assistance.

Of course the other question is whether or not the areas that are getting the military assistance have the present technological competence to use it.

These are some very grave problems, and I am asking people to be a little charitable with one another for a while as we discuss this foreign aid bill down in Congress, because I think it is kind of hard to know just who is right and who is wrong. I never had more trouble myself trying to wrestle around with a decision than on that one. 

Mr. STERNBERG. I am sure there are differences of opinion. I would presume that the organization for which I am speaking would in general favor a higher percentage of the foreign aid to be for nonmilitary purposes than is presently contemplated. 

COST OF ARMAMENTS 

The third point that I was going to mention under disarmament being imperative is the matter of cost. I noted in the paper this week some figures that have not been cited by anyone today, much to my surprise: That at the present rate of expenditure, over a 15-year period beginning in 1951 we will have spent over a half a trillion dollars (whatever sum that is)-$528 billion for defense as we call it, over the 15-year period, which would be almost $200 million more than we spent in the preceding 15-year period, which included the waging of World War II. 

The third proposition that I would make is this: that disarmament is feasible. I suppose I must qualify that by saying that disarmament is feasible only with some changed attitudes. Trust and confidence and that sort of thing have been mentioned. Dr. Inglis this morning mentioned that very thing.

REARMAMENT OF IDEAS REQUISITE 

He said there must be a rearmament of ideas, and certainly I concur in that.

One point under that, I think there must be a rethinking of our attitude toward the Soviet Union. The Soviet Union is a tough competitor, there is no doubt about that. However, I feel that there has not been a sufficient concern with trying to understand why the Soviet Union acts the way it does; that is, there is plenty of propaganda to the effect you cannot negotiate with the Soviet Union, they won't keep their agreements, they are bent on world conquest. Perhaps so. 

However, I think we should remember that the Soviet Union is made up of a large land area with land boundaries. They have been invaded many times during the history of the world. They were the

greatest loser in World War II, I believe, in terms of property damage and loss of life. They have an economic ideology that is foreign to that most of the rest of us believe in, and they know that because of that the people in the free world generally are not sympathetic with their point of view.

They realize that they were tolerated as a military ally because of the value that they could give in winning World War II, and I think that after that war was over in the situation in which they were, with the United States having the atom bomb which they kept secret as long as a scientific matter could be kept secret, that they had every reason for being concerned.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Don't underestimate the thoughts of people about the Ribbentrop-Molotov Pact of 1940. There are grounds for suspicion there. I am sure you recognize that. This was an alliance of convenience to be sure but I think many people throughout the world are mindful of the fact that Stalin and Hitler negotiated a nefarious pact which both of them intended, or at least history would so indicate, to violate. These are the things that stand in people's minds.

The Soviets can make a record for having kept some of its agreements too. I am not an advocate here. I am just trying to kick this around so that we get people to talk up and I am glad people are talking up.

ATTITUDE OF THE U. S. S. R. RATIONALIZED 

Mr. STERNBERG. Yes. Well, in that connection it would seem to me that if the people who have been the makers of military policy in the United States since World War II had been the makers of military policy in Russia in the same period, that is since the end of World War II, they probably would not have done too much different than the military leaders in Russia have during the same period. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I would like to call to your attention one or two things. I do feel that while I respect every man's right to express his point of view, I think we ought to recognize when the Marshall plan was first announced, it was announced to all nations. That ought to be recognized.

I think it ought to be recognized equally that the Soviet Union had made a request for a substantial monetary grant from this country, a long-term loan which we rather quickly turned down. 

These are patterns that indicate that our policy following World War II of rearmament and of containment and of mutual security were not a willful desire on our part. The Berlin blockade, the threat to Greece and Turkey, the knowledgeable fact of conspiracy between satellite states in revolution or the civil war in Greece are facts of blood and tears, and it is because of these facts that our country did rearm.

Remember our country demobilized. A lot of people complained about that, but no living mortal could have kept the United States from demobilization, that is, and remain in office. 

It was utterly impossible, and the only way you could mobilize would have been to leave office, so it would have been impossible to prevent a demobilization. 

Mr. STERNBERG. I recognize that, but we had no use for 10 million men under arms, that is, we assumed at that time that for purposes of

security against some potential aggressor across the water, that the atom bomb would do it; isn't that correct?

Chairman HUMPHREY. I respect your judgment. 

Mr. STERNBERG. The only other point which I wish to make then is that if we are really going to have disarmament, that someone must make the first step. Most people here are aware of previous attempts at disarmament over the years and they have not been particularly successful. The reason is that each party at the conference table is usually seeking to advance its own interests, and ends making a concession that does not count, with the idea and hope of getting the other party to make a concession that will count as far as they are concerned. 

Each side is seeking to promote its own interests. 

It seems to me that the only way that a stalemate of that sort can be broken is by one side being willing to step out in front, and so I am merely making this proposition. I feel then that the United 

States must make the first step. Now, in conclusion, anything and everything that would bring a greater degree of confidence and trust 

I think should be followed out.

I believe it has been pointed out that President Eisenhower's opensky or early warning plan, whatever you call it, is not a disarmament proposal; that it is an idea, a plan for preventing a surprise attack with a continuation of the armament program that we have. 

Some people might hope, and I think it might be desirable if it could be worked out and if Russia could be gotten to accept the inspection provisions, that that in itself might well be a step in building up a greater degree of confidence.

Dr. Inglis this morning recommended the possibility of disarmament in future weapons, that is, those that have not been developed yet, such as the missile weapons. That, too, I think is something that should be explored very definitely. There are many aspects to the problem of world peace, and the different phases of disarmament are merely a part of the picture, and I think we must have a continuing concern for all aspects of the problem. 

Thank you. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much for your testimony and your willingness to appear. 

Mr. Fred Blum?

STATEMENT OF FRED H. BLUM, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. BLUM. I am glad to be here, Mr. Chairman, and I appreciate the opportunity to testify to the committee. 

My name is Fred Blum. I live at 50 Arthur Avenue in Minneapolis.

I am connected with the University of Minnesota, but I am testifying today as an individual concerned with the socioeconomic aspects of disarmament.

I will not read my statement but I will briefly comment on it and bring out some of the major points which I think should be taken into consideration in a discussion of the socioeconomic implications of disarmament. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Blum is as follows:) 

72880-56-pt. 9-

THE SOCIOECONOMIC IMPLICATIONS OF DISARMAMENT 

My name is Fred H. Blum. I am an assistant professor and cochairman of the social science program in the department of interdisciplinary studies at the University of Minnesota. I have served in 1955 and in 1956 as a consultant to the Subcommittee on Labor of the Committee on Labor and Public Welfare of the United States Senate. However I am testifying today as an individual who is concerned with the socioeconomic problems arising in connection with disarmament.

My testimony is not concerned with the problem of disarmament as such. I take it for granted that the development of atomic energy makes disarmament a necessity for sheer survival and that we are rapidly moving toward a world in which a combination of an economic and an ideological struggle will replace the outworn and outdated struggle based on military alliances. I can only express the strong hope that this country will maintain the lead in unilateral disarmament which it assumed after World War II and speed up an inevitable process rather than leaving the psychological triumph of initiating further steps toward disarmament to the Soviet Union. 

The main question to which I will address myself is this: In which direction must our economic system be developed in order to cope best with the economic and ideological struggle of what I want to call, for brevity's sake, the postdisarmament world? The criterion for the ability to cope best with these problems is not primarily a materialistic criterion of technical efficiency and of total output figures, but it is our ability to further genuine democratic values and to make sure that these values will win out in the coming world struggle. The ultimate prize of this struggle is nothing less than the preservation and development of the integrity of the individual and of a society which is based on the respect for this integrity as well as the greatest possible self-realization of the individual in a democratic community. 

From this point of view, most of the present-day discussions on the economic implications of disarmament are woefully inadequate. The prevailing attitudes range from a doubt in the ability of our ecomomic system to adjust to the new conditions-a doubt of which the New York stock exchange seems to become an eloquent apostle-to a superficial brushing aside of the whole problem by denying the severity of the issues and by refusing to deal with the deeper implications of disarmament. A good example of the latter attitude is an article published in the New York Times on May 20, 1956 under the title "How would arms cut affect United States prosperity?" This article denies the dependence of our present prosperity on arms expenditures and states that a reduction in defense spending of as much as $10 billion would “not necessarily” entail a "cataclysmic" decline of the economy. The evidence brought forward to support this point of view is twofold: (1) a decline in the yearly rate of defense spending of about $14 billion between the second quarter of 1953 and the end of 1954 caused only one of the "mildest slumps" in our history; (2) there were then and there are now compensatory possibilities of increased spending, particularly expenditures by consumers.

Such an argument-though correct to a certain extent-fails to deal adequately even with the first aspect of a meaningful discussion of the economic problems posed by disarmament. This first stage comprises what I consider a technical problem; namely, to increase total demand at such a rate that high and expanding levels of employment and income are assured. Prof. Kenneth 

E. Boulding from the University of Michigan who testified on June 8 on behalf of the Friends Committee on National Legislation before this committee has clearly outlined this problem. The impact of disarmament on the solution of this aspect of the problem can be seen in a clear perspective if we take a broad historical view and briefly state some of the main economic trends. These trends are: (1) Up to the great depression of the thirties, our economy was primarily sustained by an increasing private investment demand. It was private investment rather than consumers' demand which has been the strategic element in our economy. (2) Since the depression of the thirties, private investors have not been able to sustain the economy at a level sufficiently high to be humanly tolerable and politically acceptable. When World War II broke out a decade after the onset of the great depression of the thirties-this country still counted about 10 million unemployed. (3) For over 25 years the Federal Government has been the ultimately decisive element in determining total demand-and hence the level of employ-

ment and income in this country. The Employment Act of 1946 formalized this situation by giving to the Federal Government the responsibility for maintaining maximum levels of production, employment, and income. 

These three major trends are irreversible. They are due to a combination of (a) technological changes, and (b) the inability of private investment to develop either the natural resources or the human potential of an industrial society. The demand in fields not directly accessible to private investors, such as housing, education, public health, social security, etc., is increasing, whereas investment demand in fields immediately accessible to private investors is, relatively speaking, declining. In 1929 gross private domestic investment amounted to about 20 percent of the gross national product. In 1954 it amounted only to about 15 percent of the gross national product. 

The significance of these trends is clearly expressed in a forecast of the total output of our economy in 1965 and of the role of private investors, consumers, and of the Government in purchasing this output. The Joint Committee on the 

Economic Report published during the 2d session of the 83d Congress a report on the potential economic growth of the United States during the next decade. 

According to this report the gross national product in 1965 may amount to about $535 billion as compared to about $400 billion at present. Such a level of production presupposes a total demand of the same amount. The report forecasts the size of the 3 major elements composing this demand; namely, (1) consumer expenditures of about $360 billion, private investment equal to about $80 billion and Government expenditures, both State and Federal, of about $95 billion. Out of this $95 billion, $40 billion is earmarked for "major national security."

These data illustrate the possibilities of expansion of our economy—which incidentally fall short of a more courageous alternative evaluation of our actual productive potential. But they also illustrate the magnitude of the problems to be solved. The reduction of armament expenditures may be partially compensated by increases in consumer expenditures provided taxes are lowered. 

However, consumer demand is not an adequate substitute for a decline in governmental investment expenditures. Undoubtedly there will be a need for either increases in private investment or for increased alternative Government expenditures. It is more than unlikely that private investment could be increased beyond the projected $80 billion, unless certain governmental measures are taken in the field of housing etc.-measures which may stimulate private investment. There is, however, no dearth of investment opportunities for the 

Government. Schools, colleges, hospitals alone can absorb tens of billions of dollars.

It must, therefore, be concluded that there is no need for a slackening of our economic growth as a result of a reduction in armament expenditures provided the Federal Government fully implements the responsibilities which it assumed in the Employment Act of 1946. The real questions raised by a reduction in armament expenditures are, therefore: (1) What alternative expenditures should receive priority in the Federal budget? (2) In the absence of a consistent and adequate planning in this regard, how much of a recession are we willing and able to take? It should be mentioned in passing that a large-scale depression is economically unnecessary and hence inexcusable. It would, furthermore, be so destructive of the internal and external position of this country as to be politically unbearable. 

We can best. understand the problems posed by a reduction in armament expenditures if we realize that such a reduction does not create entirely new problems but rather enhances the major trends outlined above, thus bringing out more sharply the already existing problems of our economy. 

In this sense, a reduction in armament expenditures will be a major test for our ability to assure growing levels of employment and income within the framework of a democratic society. 

These conclusions bring us to the second aspect of the socioeconomic problem of the postdisarmament economy. So far we have dealt with maximum levels of employment without indicating what we mean by "maximum.” This is a basic question which Congress debated for many months prior to the passage of the Employment Act of 1946. It is, furthermore, a difficult question which has many facets. Only two criteria can be mentioned in this context to evaluate the nature of the maximum which is to guide our policy, namely (1) the amount of frictional unemployment deemed normal and (2) the structure of wages and the distribution of income.

Economists disagree as to the percentage of total unemployment that can be considered frictional in the sense that it is due to people looking for jobs,

changing from one job to another, etc. Most of the estimates vary between 

2 and 5 percent of the labor force. Even the lowest of these estimates is, in my opinion, all too high. It is based on outmoded ideas on the so-called labor market which are not compatible with the ideals of a democratic society. This means that during the past 30 years the level of unemployment was higher than it should have been-with the only exception of the war period. 

Little evidence is needed to indicate how much our economy even now falls short of our democratic values in terms of the structure of wages and the distribution of income. The hearings held during the last session of Congress on the amendment of the Fair Labor Standards Act have shown that in 1954 almost one-fourth of the production workers in manufacturing earned less than the amount necessary to maintain a minimum standard of living of a single woman without dependents. And hearings held during the current session of 

Congress revealed that in 1954 about one-half of the seven-odd-million exployees in retail establishments earned less than the amount necessary to maintain a bare minimum standard of health and decency for a single woman without dependents. It is, therefore, not astonishing that recent statistics on the distribution of income show that in 1954 almost one-half of all American families earned less than $4,000 a year. 

These problems may be considered unrelated to the question of disarmament. 

Actually they are at the center of the problems which must be solved if our economy is going to meet the needs of the postdisarmament world. It is of little use to talk about "dynamic capitalism" and about "free enterprise" and to try to "'sell' the rest of the world on the value of modern American free enterprise as a bulwark of human freedom"-as we have been recently advised to do. We must constantly work to remedy the shortcomings of our economic system and to prove our allegiance to democratic values by deeds rather than by words. 

To raise this question means to deal with the third aspect of the socioeconomic implications of disarmament; namely, our ability to build up an economy which can be a model for the rest of the world-an economy which is so attractive as to induce that part of the world which has not yet decided whether to follow our lead or the lead of Soviet Russia, to turn toward truly democratic values. 

Recent studies made by the Joint Committee on the Economic Report on a "comparison of the Western powers and the Soviet bloc" and investigations carried through by the United Nations have shown that totalitarian economies are rapidly becoming as technically efficient as our economy is and that the actual rate of economic growth of the Soviet economy has been higher than the rate of growth of our economy during several post-World War II years. 

Efficiency and rate of output will be important factors in the postdisarmament world. But they will not be the only and maybe not even the most important factors. I venture to say that the genuine democratic content of economic organization, the extent to which deeper needs of personality development and growth can be satisfied, and last but not least, the meaning which work can assume I venture to say that these factors will be of decisive importance for the attractiveness and hence for the survival of alternative economic systems. 

The chances of our socioeconomic system to win in the forthcoming struggle are the greater, the more we foresee now and plan courageously for the needs of a postdisarmament world. The test for our foresight and courage is our willingness to initiate those modifications of our economic system which a commitment to democratic values necessitates. These values must not be left in the realm of vague and meaningless generalities and slogans but they must be spelled out in precise terms which can give concrete meaning to the ideal of a free economy. Limiting myself to the few criteria mentioned in my testimony 

I would define these values as follows: (1) In a truly democratic society there are always more jobs than people wanting jobs since there is no other way to assure the greatest possible development of man's abilities and potentialities. (2) In a truly democratic society power relationships are of a cooperative nature because there is no other way to assure a proper balance of control and responsibility-another basic tenet of democracy. (3) In a truly democratic society power always emanates from the people who in the economic as well as in the political realm—should have an opportunity to participate in the decision-making process as fully as their capacities enable them to participate. 

Few discussions of the socioeconomic implications of disarmament penetrate even the first of the three aspects of the problems which this testimony mentioned. Yet the problems to be solved become the more significant as we move

from the first to the second and the third aspect. Indeed it is in the realm of values and power relationships in which the ultimately significant decisions for the future of democracy will be made. 

ADDITIONAL STATEMENT SUBMITTED BY FRED H. BLUM ON THE NEED FOR DEMOCRATIC PARTICIPATION IN INDUSTRY 

The essence of the concept of participation which I am advocating is the Greek ideal of the greatest possible development of the potentialities of the individual and of the opportunities to contribute the fruits of this development to the community. Ideally industry should be organized in such a way as to give to all those connected with it the opportunity to develop and express their abilities.

This is an ideal which, in its purity, is unlikely ever to be realized. But there is a world's difference between a constant attempt to move into this direction and the complacent acceptance of forms of industrial organization which thwart an attempt to realize this basic democratic ideal of participation. Since it is a democratic ideal it must be primarily expressed in a certain conception of man and of his relationships to the social order (which constitutes the basic content of the ideal) and only secondarily in a certain type of industrial organization (which constitutes the form through which the ideal is being expressed). Yet democratic relationships necessitate democratic forms of expression. Hence there is a need of establishing new channels for participation. One approach which deserves further experimentation is the possibility of transforming the grievance machinery from an organization primarily concerned with grievances, which implies a negative approach, to an organization which develops the positive potential of workers, thus enabling them to contribute their ideas and to take part in the decision-making process to the extent to which they have developed their abilities.

In order to be fully democratic such a transformation should be a gradual one and the actual participation in the decision-making process should proceed according to the development of a democratic consciousness. If this procedure is followed, a futile power struggle can be avoided. Indeed the development of new channels of participation will make it possible to transcend the present struggle for power between unions and management. It will eventually make management as responsible to the workers-while maintaining its responsibility to the stockholders and to the public-as the President of the United States is responsible to the people. But it will not inhibit the free exercise of the managerial functions. Quite to the contrary it will free management to devote itself to its basic functions and will create a genuine community of goals by making the workers a real part of the enterprise. 

Mr. BLUM. First of all, and maybe this is the major point, I want to plead for a very broad approach to the question of the economic implications of disarmament.

It is a much bigger problem than the admittedly big problem as to what is going to happen to our economy if expenditures for armaments are being reduced considerably.

ECONOMIC AND IDEOLOGICAL WEAPONS IN THE POST DISARMAMENT WORLD 

Ultimately it is a problem as to the nature of the post disarmament world. We can see very clearly a trend toward the use of economic and ideological weapons in the struggle for world domination. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to make this point. I am becoming somewhat moved by the belief that the Soviet Union has a program in mind which may be away from armament, by building great areas of association, which some people would call neutralization, but which I think would be more than that. It would be friendly association, trade areas, economic areas, cultural areas, so that the preponderance of world power finally falls not necessarily directly in their orbit, but 

---

I think this is something that the public is not fully aware of and I regret to say I don't think our foreign policy is very much aware of it at the moment.

Mr. BLUM. Well, this is a point I will make shortly but I like to mention first three major trends which I think we have to bear in mind once we choose this approach.

MAJOR ECONOMIC TRENDS TO BE CONSIDERED 

The first trend is the profound modification of our own economy. 

Our economy has moved from an economy which was primarily determined by private investment to an economy in which the Federal Government is now playing a strategic and, in my opinion, an ultimately decisive role in the determination of production, employment, and income. The Employment Act of 1946 gives official expression to this fundamental change in our economy. 

I will briefly comment later on what these changes mean for the problem under discussion.

Second, we have to realize that the totalitarian economies are very quickly approaching the technical efficiency that we have achieved. 

You commented on the situation in regard to delivery of machinery in India. I think that is a good example. A recent study by the U. N. has shown that the rate of growth of Soviet Russia in three post- World War II years was greater than the rate of growth of our own economy.

Third, there is the rest of the world which is now rapidly industrializing. The point I wanted to make and which you have already made in a way is that where the rest of the world goes there will go the decisive weight in this struggle. 

In other words, the struggle will be determined not directly between Soviet Russia and this country, but it will be determined to a very large extent by what is happening to the rest of the world. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I was just trying to rack my mind here concerning a recent negotiation between India and the U. S. S. R. wherein our industry was unable to deliver the goods on the same time schedule that the Soviet Union was able to deliver it. 

I mean it was not a matter of price, it was a matter of delivery date. 

This is something that we are just underestimating and I am so happy to hear what you are saying because this is not getting across to the people. We are deluding ourselves into believing that somehow or other we have got the world by the tail on a downhill pull when there is evidence to the contrary that we are in for the fight of our life. 

Mr. BLUM. I would like to say that what the world wants is not only bread in the sense of higher standards of living, but also freedom, and it is at this point that our real chance arises. What is going to happen to our economy is extremely important to assure that the world will have both bread and freedom.

I would say that a realistic approach, and by realistic I simply mean dealing with the problem in its whole scope, has to take three aspects of the economic problem into consideration.

EXPANDING LEVEL OF PRODUCTION IN THE UNITED STATES 

Number one is the problem of assuring an expanding level of production, employment, and income. I would like to emphasize expanding. I don't think we should always talk about "stabilization" and "maintenance."

We have a fundamentally dynamic potential in our economy and we should realize it. We should not be satisfied with maintaining and stabilizing at a certain level. The testimony given to this committee by Prof. Kenneth Boulding from the University of Michigan has clearly dealt with that aspect of the problem. I think Professor 

Boulding has clearly pointed out what is involved and has shown that this problem can be solved; that economists know how to solve it. 

Therefore I feel that we should work out plans and start now to assure an expanding economy.

However, we talk about "full" employment or we talk about "maximum" levels of employment and we do not often enough pause and ask ourselves what we mean by "full" and what we mean by "maximum." 

This question has been debated for a long time before the present Employment Act was enacted by Congress, and I want to suggest that we should elaborate a much more daring interpretation of what "full" and "maximum" is. This is the second point I want to make. 

ELIMINATION OF LOW WAGES 

We should think in terms of a situation where there are more jobs than people. We should go ahead full steam in such things as the 

Fair Labor Standards Act which attempts to eliminate low wages. 

We have still too many "pockets," if you want to call it this way, of low wages and low income. To eliminate these shortcomings is an important aspect of making our economy attractive in the coming world struggle.

Chairman HUMPHREY. What is the percentage figure of income levels in this country in the lower income level, Doctor? 

Mr. BLUM. One of the most recent percentage figures is about 48 percent having less than $4,000 a year-this figure is only for families, not for individuals.

Chairman HUMPHREY. In the United States? 

Mr. BLUM. In the United States. To me this situation should not be tolerated and it does not have to be tolerated because we do have the productive resources to change it. If we were a country without these resources we might have to take it, but we do not have to take it. 

Since you asked me about specific figures, I want to mention that the recent hearings on the extension of coverage of the Fair Labor Standards Act have shown that there are still one-fourth of manufacturing employees who earned less than the amount necessary to support a single woman without dependents, and the current hearings in the current session of Congress have shown that in the retail industryand there are 7 million people in that industry-one half of all employees earn less than necessary to support a single woman without dependents.

MORE COOPERATIVE RELATIONSHIPS IN INDUSTRY 

It is this kind of situation which I have in mind when I say we should be more daring in our definition of what "full" employment is. 

It should exclude such a situation. 

The third point, a point which follows from the others, is the democratic nature of our economy. We should do all we can to support those trends which establish more cooperative relationships. 

Guaranteed wages may serve as an example along those lines. I will not take the time now to discuss this point further but I want to say that we should be very clear cut and explicit when we speak about democratic values in our economy. I want therefore to make three points.

DEFINITION OF A DEMOCRATIC ECONOMY 

I would say an economy is democratic if there are more jobs than people who want jobs, because there is no other way to assure the selfrealization of individuals and the real development of their potentials. 

I would say, furthermore, that an economy is democratic if it survives constantly to establish cooperative power relations, because there is no other way to keep a democratic balance between control and responsibility.

Chairman HUMPHREY. What do you mean by that? 

Mr. BLUM. Well, I mean for example in the organization of industry. Unions have certainly modified this organization by giving the workers some voice. But I hope that we can move away from a purely mechanical determination of what is participation. We must move to a concept that everybody can participate to the extent of his abilities and potentialities. One argument that is always thrown at you if you speak about cooperative industrial organizations is what about management? It is feared that somebody may "tell" management what to do.

I feel that what is true for the President of the United Statesnobody tells him what to do but he is elected every 4 year I feel that this principle should eventually be applied to all fields of our society. But my main point is that we should seek new ways of making it possible for people in the shops, in the offices, in the factories, on the farms to take a more active part, to participate more strongly in the decisions affecting their lives. 

This again raises many questions but I am convinced that the appeal of our economy to the rest of the world would be tremendously enhanced by a genuine experimentation in that direction. 

There are developments now going on which I think are in that direction, but we should support them, we should build them up, and we should also bring them to the attention of other countries. 

I do feel that any real effort along those lines, is much better than an empty talk on "dynamic capitalism" and "free enterprise." 

You cannot "sell" the world the benefit of our way of life in an advertising sense. I believe that the principles of freedom and democracy on which we stand are ultimately stronger as we fully realize them. They are stronger than arms and they are stronger than totalitarian principles. We would be blinding our eyes if we would deny that totalitarian principles can have certain short run effects

that democratic methods do not have, but I am taking here a longrun view.

If we do not have a policy with long run objectives then we can never win. Ultimately the democratic objectives and principles are stronger, and I think if we believe in them and have faith in them, and sometimes even take a chance on them, then we can win out in this struggle.

The coming struggle needs a mixture of economic and ideological weapons. It is not just a struggle about technical efficiency. If it were that, we had no chance to win it. It is not just a struggle about total production and total income. It it were that, we would quickly be overcome by several countries in this world. 

But if it is a struggle about basic democratic values, a struggle about respect for the individual, if it is a question of honest experimentation with those values in all aspects of life, including industry-where it may hurt and where many suspicions have to be overcome-in this kind of a struggle we can win, and give the world both bread and freedom. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I do thank you so much for your statement. 

Professor Sibley.

STATEMENT OF MULFORD Q. SIBLEY, DEPARTMENT OF POLITICAL SCIENCE, UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA 

Mr. SIBLEY. Mine is a 412 minute summary of a fifteen minute statement, Senator Humphrey, and I will try to stay within the time limit.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you, sir. 

Mr. SIBLEY. I appreciate it particularly because my position has been called foolish several times this afternoon, the position I will espouse, and I appreciate very much your willingness to tolerate fools gladly.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I have a much higher regard for you, Professor, than that. 

Mr. SIBLEY. My name is Mulford Q. Sibley. I teach in the department of political science of the University of Minnesota, and I live at 2108 Fairmont, St. Paul. I represent however in this paper only myself and my wife.

I am grateful for the opportunity to appear before your honorable body, which is doing so much to call the attention of the United States and the world to the pressing and momentous problem of disarmament.

The position for which I argue is an eccentric one and an unpopular one. It has, I believe, been presented to the subcommittee at previous hearings. It is a position which in my judgment is one of the more "realistic" attitudes to take in the present state of the world. 

DEFENSE OF UNILATERAL DISARMAMENT POLICY 

Briefly and bluntly put, this statement defends the proposition that if a general universal disarmament agreement cannot be reached in the immediate future, it would be desirable and highly realistic to adopt a policy of unilateral disarmament.

The considerations which lead to this conclusion may be outlined 

THE FAILURE OF WAR AS AN ARBITER OF INTERNATIONAL DISSENSIONS 

1. It is widely accepted now by many if not most of the major leaders of the world that another great war, if it should unhappily "break out," would result in mutual suicide. Thus Gen. Douglas 

MacArthur has been quoted as saying:

War is no longer rationally a means of settling international problems. Its destructiveness has become so great that there can be no winner. Both sides lose. It is almost a form of mutual suicide. 

Commenting on the Korean war, the general remarked: 

This experience again emphasizes the utter futility of modern war-its complete failure as an arbiter of international dissensions. A nation has been gutted, and we stand today just where we stood before it all started. Nothing has been settled. War is outmoded. Its threat must be abolished if the world is to go on.

President Eisenhower has been quoted along the same lines; and only recently Marshal Bulganin made a very similar statement. 

NO DEFENSE AGAINST LONG-RANGE MISSILES 

Many of those who have studied the possibilities of long-range missiles seem to suggest that it is fantastic to expect any real protection against their horrors. One reason both the American and the 

British people are so apathetic about civil defense is probably because they realize, perhaps subconsciously, that the whole idea is a farcethat "civil defense" cannot "defend," or, if conceivably it could be made to do so, would cost so much that even a wealthy nation would become bankrupt. All the agitation involving the alleged superiority of the Soviet Union in certain types of weapons, implying a fear that the United States may not be "keeping up," illustrates anew the futility of an arms race for the promotion of "security" (however that rather ambiguous term may be defined). 

We live in a world, in short, where the waging of war would be so destructive that in sheerly utilitarian terms it would not "pay," whatever the probable circumstances; a world, moreover, in which the mere preparation for such a war promotes national insecurity and even constitutes a serious hazard to human life—as in the atomic and hydrogen bomb tests.

OUR INABILITY OR UNWILLINGNESS TO FACE THE ISSUES 

2. Why, then, do we continue to arm against one another?

It is partly, I suggest, because we either will not or cannot face up to the implications of our very admissions about the nature of war and preparation for war.
