On the one hand it is admitted widely (and obviously I share the view) that losses from any conceivable war with modern weapons would be far greater than any imaginable gains; on the other hand, we still seem to be saying that, despite this admission, we are still willing to go to war under circumstances, knowing full well that no victor can emerge. This is not rational, and we should frankly admit that it is not. Either MacArthur, Eisenhower, Bulganin, myself, and many others are dead wrong in their estimates

1 about the destructiveness of a future war, in which case they should be corrected by someone, or they are right, in which event we should think in terms of a vastly changed policy. I happen to think that they are right. If this is true, our policies-which are still based on the notion that war, however undesirable, may somehow be necessarydespite the fact that it will lead to mutual suicide-our policies ought to be altered in a revolutionary way. 

ARMING FOR MUTUAL SUICIDE 

3. Now these policies assume today, that, in the absence of international agreement on armaments, we must still prepare nationally for what is admitted to be probably suicide. Sometimes such policies are defended by saying that, of course, their intention is to "threaten" others in order to keep the peace as if a threat means anything unless we are prepared to wage a suicidal war in case of presumed "necessity." Threats without probable action cease to be threats and socalled realists ought to recognize this. Building national armaments today, in short, is building for probable suicidal war, which can in no sense "defend" this country. National armaments, under modern circumstances, and if the objective be "national defense" (whether physical, economic, or political), are an inappropriate, ineffective means for the alleged objective.

UNILATERAL DISARMAMENT A "REALISTIC" CONCEPT 

They are, in other words, unrealistic, utopian means. 

At the very least, they are useless, and at their worst (as in hydrogen bomb testing) are a positive menace to both contemporary and future life. 

Unilateral disarmament, if this analysis be correct, is not some "idealistic" and "impractical" notion; rather is it an exemplification of good sense.

If I should take a poodle to assist me in hunting, I should be termed crazy, and rightly so the rational act would be to discard the poodle and get a hunting dog. Charles Lamb, in a famous essay, rightly ridiculed those who burned down their houses in order to get roast pig. So it is with the vain attempt to get "national defense" and "security" through armaments. All the paraphernalia of the national war machine—espionage, counterespionage, National Security Council, hydrogen bombs, guided missiles, and so on-constitute the poodle dog, or, if you will, the burning of the house. At international conferences it seems generally to be assumed that any surrender of armed might would be highly dangerous-hence all the talk of "fool-proof" guarantee, international inspection, and so on. But if the analysis of this statement be supported, a nation is not giving up anything really valuable when it surrenders armed might-rather is it giving up something valueless, something, moreover, which is probably a positive detriment in the quest for defense and security. 

POSITIVE CONSIDERATIONS IN DISARMAMENT 

4. This is not as outrageous a view as it may seem to be. In his suggestive study Power-which examines the ramifications of power

relations in human society-the well-known British philosopher Lord 

Russell says (p. 152):

The actual harm which a nation would suffer from unilateral disarmament is very much less than most people suppose. 

We have in this statement, of course, gone much beyond the negative of Russell's observation and have suggested positive considerations which would seem to point in the direction of unilateral disarmament. 

OBJECTIONS TO UNILATERAL DISARMAMENT DEALT WITH 

Now I am aware, I think, of most of the objections to such a policy and shall attempt to deal with them in conclusion: (a) It is often alleged that the United States actually disarmed after World War I and World War II and that the disarmament did not work to promote peace or security. Of course, the answer is that the United States did not disarm. Its level of armaments was always much higher than in prewar years. It is true that it may have disarmed or reduced its armaments more rapidly than the Soviet Union after World War II, but this was far from a policy of disarmament carried out within a context of positive alternatives such as we suggest below. (b) This leads to the second point-the meaning and context of unilateral disarmament, which are all-important if we are to criticize the policy intelligently. In a narrow sense, unilateral disarmament means the voluntary destruction of all our warmaking potential and an invitation to the rest of the world to send inspectors to check on our claims. But the narrow meaning of disarmament must be seen in the context of other proposals which are organically connected with unilateral disarmament. Simultaneously, we would immediately turn over to an international agency all the resources we had hitherto been using in preparation for war. We would do this each year for an indefinite period. The international agency would use the resources to develop underdeveloped areas, construct and maintain international libraries, establish and maintain an international university, and so on.

The cost of one destroyer would build 80 schools; the price of one bombing plane would build some four hundred $15,000 houses. (c) Objectors to unilateral disarmament often fail to realize how this revolutionary act would in itself probably change the political atmosphere of the world-and for the better. Even the Soviet Union's current modest reduction in armaments has shaken world opinion. 

What would not unilateral disarmament by the United States do, if carried out in the context outlined above? We cannot, of course, predict exactly because we have never tried it. But it is safe to suggest that the whole international climate would undergo drastic revision. 

It is entirely probable that other heavily armed powers would follow the lead of this country and we would have a competition in disarmament, rather than competition in armaments: it might be a repetition of the competitive actions of French nobles in giving up their privileges at the beginning of the French Revolution. (d) But, it may be objected, you have not insured peace by this act and aggression may and probably will still occur. The answer to this objection is twofold: (1) No action, however complicated and ingenious, can ever make peace certain, but I submit that an act of this kind, in the appeal to

human rationality, imagination, and sympathy, would go further in the direction of building the atmosphere conducive to peace than our present insanity, which, if we are honest with ourselves, we will admit must result in war. (2) No act of unilateral disarmament, even in the context suggested above, should be adopted without a clear realization that an ineffective and dangerous means of national defense is being surrendered as good riddance in favor of a more realistic method. That more realistic method would be some American adaptation of the Ghandian technique of nonviolent resistance. 

TRAINING IN NONVIOLENT RESISTANCE CALLED FOR 

Organized potentiality for nonviolent resistance should, in other words, accompany unilateral disarmament. If we were to devote only 

5 percent of our present so-called defense budget to training for possible nonviolent resistance which might never be needed-we would, in my judgment, have a far more effective and far less dangerous defense against possible aggression-even assuming that the Soviet Union remained heavily armed, which is highly improbable than our present unrealistic and utopian methods provide. 

It is a commonplace to say that we live in a revolutionary age. This statement has been a plea that we seriously consider revolutionary means to cope realistically with one of the crucial problems of that age. This revolutionary means would imply that we no longer have any faith in war and preparation for war and that what would begin ostensibly as a revolution in technique would prove to be basically a revolution in attitude and outlook. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much. I want to say that at these hearings what some people might call unorthodox ideals are not looked upon as though they were unwanted or undesirable. 

Mr. SIBLEY. I understand. I appreciate your attitude.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I am delighted to get such points of view as expressed here. Thank you very much. 

Is Mrs. Rudolph Peterson here? 

May I say that if any friend of Mrs. Peterson is here, if she wants to file a statement as if she had been here to testify, she will be permitted to do so, unless it has been presented to us. 

Is Francis Ceplecha here?

STATEMENT OF DR. S. F. CEPLECHA, REDWOOD FALLS, MINN. 

Dr. CEPLECHA. I am Dr. S. F. Ceplecha, a practicing physician and surgeon in Redwood Falls, Minn.

I am a member of the Ameri can Medical Association, American Academy of General Practice. I 

am also a private pilot and a member of the Aircraft Owners and 

Pilots Association: 

I volunteered for service in 1940 and served on active duty with the Medical Corps of the United States Army from January 6, 1941, to December 26, 1945; was released at the age of 34 in the grade of colonel. Forty-four months of this time were spent in the Aleutian and European theaters of war.

DANGERS OF DISARMAMENT 

My purpose in testifying is to express my conviction that any disarmament in this age would be dangerous. I wish to state here when we speak about disarmament we have to understand who we are disarming, whether we are disarming a country that has a government by the people, a representative government like we have, or whether we are disarming à totalitarian nation. 

Perhaps someone mentioned a goal of our foreign policy, I thought that perhaps the goal of our foreign policy is most ably expressed in the declaration of the independence of our country. 

Disarmament, in reality a synonym for unpreparedness, is a welltried formula for failure as a prevention of war. From personal bitter experience, I know what it is for a commanding general to tell his command that only 3 minutes of small arms ammunition and 10 minutes of artillery fire is available for defense. I understand the utter hopelessness of such a grim deficiency and what it means to the morale of troops, and the suicides it motivates. 

During World War II, our Nation faced a dilemma. Two powerful totalitarian regimes, the Axis on one side and the Communists on the other, were engaged in a fierce struggle. Both of these tyrannies, historical enemies of America, made our position rather awkward. 

Today, our position is definitive but far more serious. 

THE KREMLIN LEADERS AND THE RUSSIAN PEOPLE 

I think sometimes some people have brought this up-our position would be less awkward ideologically if we had not or would not at this time have recognized the Soviet Union because they do not represent the Russian people. They represent only the small group of ruling people in that particular country. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I might mention at that point, Doctor, I had a group of young Soviet refugees that came to me one time in the Senate. It was not planned on my part, but I did not refer to the 

Russian people in my statement, I referred to the leaders of the Kremlin, the Communist Party.

After all, the first people to be enslaved by the forces of communism were the Russian people. These young men who had come here and were starting a new life came to express a word of thanks; namely, that I had not defamed the Russian people as such. They said: "Just keep this argument on the basis of the leadership, the Kremlin, the Politburo, the Cominform, the Communist Party," because after all the Communist Party is in Russia a very small part of the total population.

I think that is what you are bringing out here. 

Dr. CEPLECHA. Yes, sir, and I also think that one of the major problems that the U. N. faces is the fact that it is really not a united organization because it represents two different systems, one-half of the world free and one-half of the world being held in slavery, and that makes our problem very different.

Our enemy is not two or more totalitarian regimes, but one militant, atheistic communism. It is an armed international conspiracy which controls 900 million people behind the iron and bamboo curtains, plus an additional 200 million followers in the neutral and free world, totaling 1,100 million people.

FIVE BASIC COMMUNIST WEAPONS 

The Communists are waging total war against the United States. 

In this struggle to conquer the United States and the globe, the Communists utilize 5 basic weapons; the first 3 are the familiar and conventional land, sea, and air forces. The fourth weapon is the psychosocial warfare with all its economic, diplomatic, and psychological aspects.

Slander, libel, blackmail, narcotics, counterfeiting, kidnaping, assassination, and conspiracy are all part and parcel of the fourth weapon for Communists. This is the type of weapon which is a big obstacle to any disarmament plan because we don't use this type of weapon. We are a peace-loving nation.

The fifth weapon is the Communist plan for the coordination of the first three traditional weapons plus the integration of psychosocial warfare resulting in their concept of total war. 

International communism is a total amoral philosophy of life; it fits in well with these five weapons. It is ever changing now. Since the inception of the Bolshevik revolution they have broken over 1,000 treaties, including treaties on disarmament. 

This treachery coupled with the fifth weapon, that is, psycho-socialmilitary warfare, gives the Communists the widest conceivable range and effectiveness; works clearly for a world Communist government and enables them in less than 10 years to conquer more than 600 million people and 5 million square miles. 

We should pay less attention to their smiles and frowns and more heed to their global plan. Their smiles should remind us of a diabolic poker player with a royal flush ready to win the grand pot, downgrading of Stalin notwithstanding.

Perhaps the Communists smile because they possess the T-37, a long-range intercontinental bomber with the superior bypass jet engine; or perhaps because of their rapid progress in the development of the intercontinental guided missile plus their recent successes in the Middle East.

The precise nature of weapons and tactics is, of course, the province of military experts but the overall national strategy of our destiny and survival is the province of American people. And I believe that is the purpose of your committee.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Indeed. 

Dr. CEPLECHA. Yes. American people do know that all materials and goods shipped to the Soviets and satellites are strategic because these release their workers for war production. Tito is the Red 

Trojan Horse. Instead of aiding this international Communist, Tito, who is and has been for years on written record dedicated to the destruction of America, we should provide adequate training and defense facilities for our Air Forces on our continent, and relieve the dangerously congested Wold-Chamberlain Airport as one example.

To this date we are losing this vicious war the Soviets are waging against us. Certainly it is folly to reduce arms while one is in battle. 

Our best and only promise of peace and survival is to maintain an unchallengeable superiority in all weapons, until such time that the principles of the Declaration of Independence which made this country what it is are accepted in the other parts of the world. 

Thank you.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I hope you understand this subcommittee is not a partisan committee in any sense. I don't mean just politically, I mean partisan in terms of viewpoint as such. We look upon ourselves as a receiving station for ideas, expressions of thought. 

We also feel that in these hearings we get a chance to get a genuine overall evaluation of our total national-security policy. It is impossible to discuss any aspect of reduction and control of armaments without discussing the whole totalitarian security, and I have never wanted to leave the impression with anyone that we are not seeking answers, because we are seeking answers. We are looking for solutions. 

But we are also not unmindful of the kind of a world in which we live.

Mr. Adamson Hoebel?

STATEMENT OF E. ADAMSON HOEBEL, PROFESSOR OF ANTHROPOL- OGY, UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA 

Mr. HOEBEL. I am E. Adamson Hoebel, a professor of anthropology at the University of Minnesota.

My address is 2273 Farrell Street, St. Paul. I am an anthropologist who has made the study of law and society and the needs, means, and ends of social organizations his main concern for more than a quarter of a century of his professional life. 

The anthropologist seeks scientifically to observe and analyze the widest possible range of human society to determine what are the common conditions of human living and what alternative solutions for getting along together and coping with the biological and physical demands of nature men have been able to work out. 

Much of the testimony previously heard by this committee has turned on technical problems of inspection and exposure of hidden armaments and for procedures in reaching agreements between nations on the limitation of armaments. I do not propose to offer further suggestions on these points, essential though they be. Rather, it behooves me briefly to discuss the relevance of the findings of my science to the problem of your concern.

In sum, they support the position so ably presented in your previous hearings by Mr. Fuchs, of Brandeis University, a position that has been reiterated a number of times here, in one point of view or another, namely, that enforcible disarmament in any community demands agencies of government constituted to do the enforcing. 

THE EVOLUTION OF HUMAN SOCIETY 

Every human society is forced by the nature of things to make a series of choices from the vast potential of behavior that human beings are capable of. It must decide unequivocally what goes on among its members and what does not. The gross result constitutes its culture. Each society must-and does-devise means to hold its members to the lines laid down. Most of these means are

informal. In a limited area, where issues are critical, the means become more explicit and formal. We recognize them as constituting law. Law is distinguished from mere custom in that it embraces the sanction of force. On its substantive side law is concerned with selection of certain kinds of behavior for social acceptance and the denial and elimination of other lines. Procedurally law is concerned with orderly determination of whether prohibited activity has actually taken place. It also specifies, once this first decision has been reached, who may apply the sanctions of force and in what amounts and in what manner.

In the course of my long researches I have intensively examined a number of primitive social systems at firsthand with respect to these problems. I have further surveyed the findings of other anthropologists and legal scholars on many more social systems. Here, in capsule form, are the results that are relevant to your concern. 

Human existence extends through a million years. The earliest human societies consisted of very small local groups of closely related people. The size of the group was limited by its meager technological culture. The archaeological record shows how, through the ensuing millenia, man has continuously expanded his technological inventory, until the coming of the Neolithic revolution he was able to domesticate plants and animals, so to achieve sufficient food surpluses to begin the founding of cities and lay the foundations of our great contemporary civilizations.

DEVELOPMENT OF LAW IN PRIMITIVE SOCIETIES 

The anthropological record also shows that with the expansion of technology there went a steady increase in the scope of societies, which came to embrace ever-larger populations with increasing heterogeneity of their internal groupings. Conflicts of interest steadily grew. 

Law came into being. Beyond the very simplest of primitive societies it is not possible to find a single one that has not developed a law system, for law is essential to the maintenance of society-and that means, human life.

Law is always a product of social invention. A need is recognized. 

Men use their intelligence to create a workable solution. 

CHANGING CONCEPTS OF LAW 

Simple primitive societies allocate legal authority to the kinship group of the wronged individual. It is up to such groups to initiate action and impose the penalties. This works a good deal of the time. 

But it has an inherent weakness. The kin group of the defendant may not acknowledge the validity of the plaintiff's claim. The proceedings break down. The aggrieved party must drop its suit and suffer its wrong unredressed. Or if it pushes its case, the result is feud-internecine war.

The solution commonly arrived at by the more advanced societies is to shift the privilege rights of prosecution and imposition of legal sanctions from the individual and his kinship group to clearly defined public officials representing the society as such. The judicial function steadily wins out over the merely legal. On the higher levels 72880-56-pt. 9– -10

of cultural development, feud is generally prevented by the action of a central authority representing the total social interest. 

Up to the present century the nation and the empire represented the largest unit of social entity. Today, for the first time in a million years of human evolution, we find ourselves at the point where technology has brought virtually every society into enduring contact with nearly all the others. We have crossed the critical threshold into the realm where incipent society has reached its ultimate bounds of magnitude.

INTERNATIONAL LAW IN A WORLD SOCIETY 

With reference to the social needs of the newly emergent world society our present legal inventions are precisely comparable to those that characterized the early primitive societies in their time. International law, so-called, is primitive law on the world level. 

The immediate concern with disarmament centers on the hope that feuding between nations may be made less deadly. It by itself ignores the simple and basic fact that the only way national feuding can ever be suppressed is to concentrate on development of means for universally acceptable judicial and legislative devices for resolution of conflicts of choices and interests among nations and blocs of nations. Simultaneously, national arms must be reduced according to whatever means your committee's findings may find workable. 

But it is clearly evident that a United Nations Government must be endowed with more effective force for legal purposes than any constituent member or coalition of minority members. 

For whatever the idealist may desire, as Jehring put it, "A legal rule without coercion is a fire that does not burn, a light that does not shine." "Law without force is an empty name."

The problem today, as forever, remains one of achievement of the supremacy of legitimate use of force on behalf of the whole society as against the unruly activities of its members. Force and the threat of force are the ultimate power in the determination of international behavior, as in the law within the nation or tribe. But until force and the threat of force are brought under control by the world community, by and for the newly born world society, they remain the insruments of social anarchy. 

DISARMAMENT ONLY AN ADJUNCT TO GOVERNMENT WITHIN LAW 

Nations will not disarm, as individuals did not disarm, until government and law provide the means for effective and reasonable solution of their disagreements along with swift and efficient control of criminal violaters. Consider the frontier West. Therefore, the mechanics of reduction of disarmament, of open sky inspection and ground inspection, are important necessities. To work toward their realization is a positive contribution in the right direction. If, however, we set our sights on these as ends rather than as limited means, we are feeding a soporific to ourselves and the great mass of humanity to whom this nation should provide cooperative leadership. We fool ourselves into thinking we are making progress and fail to face the real issue. Functionally, disarmament of and by itself is impossible. It becomes realistically meaningful only as an adjunct to government with law for the whole society.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Professor Hoebel. I want to thank you for your statement. I would like to comment that Mr. Valeo of the staff said to me a moment ago, "These hearings are a liberal education," and they are.

I regret that we don't get as much time to do all the constructive reading and thoughtful analysis as we should, but we should get a chance at a time like this to hear very interesting and provocative statements and I want to thank you for what you have contributed to these discussions. 

Mr. HOEBEL. Thank you, Senator, and in the statement for the record I express my appreciation of the approach that this committee is using.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you, sir. 

Mr. LOEVINGER? 

STATEMENT OF LEE LOEVINGER, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Mr. LOEVINGER. Senator Humphrey, my name is Lee Loevinger. I 

live at 5816 Queen Avenue, South Minneapolis.

I am a lawyer. I speak for myself only. I have no authority to represent either my wife or children who would, as usual, probably disagree with me.

I was not invited to appear but asked to appear because I have been thinking about this subject and thought that I might be able to contribute something.

I have prepared a statement which I ask to have incorporated in the record, and in order to save time and avoid repetition of prior statements I will merely outline my position. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Loevinger is as follows:) PRACTICAL STEPS TO DISARMAMENT 

It is useless to repeat that the present necessity for maintaining national armaments is a crucial problem for us. It is useful, however, constantly to appraise the character and magnitude of that problem. Economically our national armaments are, at best, a heavy burden and, at worst, the path to national bankruptcy. Clearly armaments are essential to our national safety in present circumstances. Yet they afford us no safety. Such security as we have depends altogether on the preservation of a most precarious balance of power. Worst of all, we can never be certain that this balance is in existence at any given time, and if we ever find out that we have lost the balance of power, it will probably be too late for us to do anything about it. 

DISARMAMENT BY MUTUAL AGREEMENT OR WORLD GOVERNMENT? 

In these circumstances disarmament is clearly the rational goal for us to seek. 

Yet unilateral disarmament would be suicidal folly and unthinkable. However, disarmament by mutual agreement or treaty with our principal antagonist seems hardly more practical. The difficulty is that mutual promises which carry no effective external means of enforcement are worth no more than the degree of trust which each party has in the other. But, since the basic reason for the existence of the armaments in the first place is the nations' distrust of each other, it seems rather absurd to expect that the armaments will be dispensed with on the basis of mutual trust without some change in conditions or institutions. To say that each nation will undertake to enforce the disarmament of the other obviously implies that each nation will seek to maintain superiority in strength over the other, and is meaningless except for the agreement to call our armaments race by the name of "disarmament."

We can hardly hope for disarmament while the conditions which have required us to arm ourselves remain unaltered. The consideration of alternatives possible in this area has led many thoughtful people to the conclusion that we

cannot hope for relief from the burden of armaments and the threat of wars until we have established some form of world government with limited powers to maintain world law and international order. Some of those who have reached this conclusion and many others who oppose it contend that the vision of a limited world government is too idealistic and is impossible of attainment within the reasonably near future. Nearly all would concede that such a goal represents a long step from our present position and is, at least, very difficult of attainment.

RATHER, DISARMAMENT THROUGH AGENCY OF THE UNITED NATIONS 

Is there then any middle course between the inadequate and impractical plan of bilateral treaty, which has always in the past failed to eliminate conflict or achieve disarmament, and the idealistic vision of a limited world government which we have not yet been able to attain or even closely to approach? I believe that there is, and that such a middle course would consist of a number of successive, practical steps, each of which is well within the range of practical political action, and each one of which would represent a significant change in present world conditions tending toward minimizing the possibility of international conflict and eliminating the national need for armaments. Briefly and summarily I think that these steps should include the following:

First, the creation of an independent military force under the exclusive control of the United Nations.

Second, the establishment of a staff or body within the United Nations which is able to command the U. N. force and use it to meet any threat to world peace and which is not subject to the veto. In this connection it will be recalled that the employment of military forces in Korea under the U. N. flag was due to purely accidental circumstances which prevented employment of the veto; and also that the fact that the U. N. forces were composed of separate national units with relatively independent commanders created substantial difficulties both during and after the fighting.

Third, promulgation of the principle that no nation is entitled to use force or the threat of force against any other nation, except in meeting and repelling an attack, and that any nation which is involved in a dispute with another nation may, if other means of settlement are unavailing, invoke the jurisdiction of the World Court for determination of the controversy. Many persons have the impression that this principle is now part of the U. N. Charter, but it is not. 

At the present time the use of force except in self-defense is implicitly, though not clearly and explicitly, outlawed, and the competence of the World Court is limited generally to matters submitted to it by agreement of both parties to a dispute.

Fourth, amendment of the United Nations Charter to give the U. N. the power to limit, control, and eliminate national armaments. 

Fifth, structural and administrative reforms of the United Nations to enable it to exercise this power more easily and effectively. Either elimination or curtailment of the veto power would probably be required at this stage. These amendments should include adequate guaranties that the United Nations would not have any power or authority to act beyond the limited subject matter of maintenance of world peace and effectuating national disarmament, and carrying out the other specific and limited objectives now permitted to it. 

UNIVERSALITY OF MEMBERSHIP IN THE U. N. 

Sixth, universal and ultimately compulsory membership of all national or quasi-national groups. It seems to me that the argument that some nations are wicked or dangerous is not relevant to the issue of whether or not they should be included in such an organization as the United Nations. If no nations were wicked or dangerous we probably would not need such an organization as the U. N. It is precisely those who do threaten the peace, whether individuals or nations, that it is most important to subject to the enforcement of the law. 

Therefore we should look toward membership in the U. N. and compliance with the principle of pacific international behavior to be established by the U. N. not as an honor or a privilege but as a duty which is obligatory for all groups that move or act in the international sphere. 

Certainly each of the points that I have mentioned is complex and difficult. I do not pretend even to have suggested all of the considerations involved and obstacles to be overcome in each of these steps. That is impossible within present limitations. However, we must bear in mind that the problem with

which we are concerned is a complex and difficult one. No solution will be simple or easy. The most we can ask is that a solution be possible. I think that the steps suggested do offer a possible solution. 

There is no promise that immediately or at any particular point all of our problems will vanish. It is quite possible that even if we follow the course I have suggested these measures will, in the beginning, be adequate only to deal with limited threats to the peace and not with a threat offered by any major power. 

But if we can begin to deal adequately with even the minor threats to the peace we shall be on the right course. If the United Nations today had a military force of its own adequate, for example, to insure that there would be no further war between the Arab nations and Israel, even that would be a substantial contribution toward insuring the prevention of another major war. If we can achieve this much as a beginning, then, I submit, we can reasonably expect that further advance along the same course will finally bring us to the position where we can depend upon the United Nations to defend us, or any other nation, against the aggression of any power. Then we will be able to disarm and to employ our immense resources and productivity wholly for peaceful and civilized uses. 

Mr. LOEVINGER. I think the importance and the desirability of disarmament as an end has been demonstrated in the statements here and is pretty much assumed. It should be mentioned perhaps that not only have armaments an economic cost but as this community here in Minneapolis has learned so tragically within the last few weeks, the cost of armaments even in peacetime must be paid at least partially in blood.

UNSATISFACTORY DISARMAMENT PROPOSALS 

The problem, however, is not whether it is desirable to disarm; but assuming to be desirable, how? I think that the impracticality and unreliability of mere treaties with antagonists as a path to disarmament has been established. Some have proposed a world government with limited powers, to maintain world war and international order. Others say that this is visionary and impracticable. 

In brief, the proposals and the positions of the protagonists before your committee seem to me to fall into four categories: those who say as Mr. Platt said, that a method for the peaceful settlement of disputes must precede any plan for disarmament; second, those who suggest that some mechanism of disarmament must precede any political settlement between the nations of the world; third, those who say that unilateral disarmament is possible, we should rely purely on moral force and the power of virtue; and finally those who say that there should be no disarmament since we can ultimately rely only on force alone.

Now it seems to me that these four positions leave out of consideration one highly practical alternative possibility which is that which I would like to propose, and that is that we establish a world police force with a substantial degree of force available to it prior to considering either disarmament or achieving a political settlement between the various nations; not that we should not strive for these things, but that we need not await them before we have the establishment of a world force which will permit disarmament to occur. 

WITNESS PROPOSES A MIDDLE COURSE; CONTROL THROUGH THE U. N. 

I believe that such a middle course consisting of a number of successive, practical specific steps can be taken over a period of time, and that these need not be simultaneous. Each of these steps is I think within the range of practical political action, and each would represent a significant change in the conditions which require arma-

ments on the part of this Nation and which other nations seem to think require them to arm. Each of these steps I think would tend toward minimizing the possibility of international conflict, and thus tend to eliminate the need for national armaments. 

Very briefly and very summarily, I think these steps should include 

First, the creation of an independent military force under the exclusive control of the United Nations. Whether this force should be minimal or substantial is I think a matter that needs to be worked out. 

It should have sufficient force to cope with any significant threats to the peace. It certainly should be built up to a point to where it can cope with whatever aggressive forces there seem to be loose in the world.

Second, I think this force must be under the control of a staff or body within the United Nations which is able to command it to meet any threat to world peace and which is not itself subject to the veto in this use.

In this connection it will be recalled that the employment of military forces in Korea under the U. N. flag was due to purely accidental circumstances which prevented employment of the veto. It will also be recalled that the U. N. forces in Korea were composed of separate national units with relatively independent commanders, and that this fact created substantial difficulties both during and after the fighting.

Third, I think we should promulgate the principle that no nation is entitled to use force or the threat of force against any other nation except in meeting and repelling an attack, and that any nation which is involved in a dispute with another nation may, if other means of settlement are unavailing, invoke the jurisdiction of the World Court for determination of the controversy. Many have the impression that this principle is now part of the U. N. Charter but it is not as you know.

UNITED NATIONS CHARTER AND THE USE OF FORCE 

The use of force in the U. N. Charter is implicitly though not clearly and explicitly outlawed and the competence of the World Court is limited generally to matters submitted to it by agreement of both parties to a dispute, so that if one party to a dispute does not agree to the submission, the other party is without remedy. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I say that that area is very carefully reviewed in the report of the Special Subcommittee on U. N. Charter 

Revision. I think there is some very interesting documentation on what you have just stated here.

Mr. LOEVENGER. There is a point here, Senator, that I think is perhaps different than has been made by other speakers. 

HISTORICALLY, FORCE PRECEDES LAW 

In general it is assumed that law or the specific settlements of disputes must precede disarmament and must precede the establishment of a world police force.

While I do not claim to be a thoroughgoing scholar on this I have studied something of the history of law and I do not believe that this I believe that historically the fact has been that the police force for the maintenance of peace within any given area has generally preceded the establishment of a thoroughgoing code of laws.

What we know as the common law, which is still the basis of our American law, is largely made up of the decisions of courts which are not based upon preexisting statutes, which are based on the moral code or the commonsense of the people if you will. 

But the way the common law came into being was by the extension of the power of the King in the British Isles originally over the various feudal principalities. The King sent out his army and his forces to establish and maintain peace, and following the police forces came the King's judges, who developed the common law as incidental to the maintenance of peace.

I think therefore that it is perfectly practical to suggest that we can establish a police force that will to some degree maintain peace in the world, even prior to establishing a code of laws and a whole judicial system.

AMENDMENT OF U. N. CHARTER TO EFFECT CONTROL OF ARMAMENTS 

In the fourth place and after these steps have been taken, then I believe that the U. N. charter should be amended to give the U. N. the power to limit, control, and ultimately eliminate national armaments.

Fifth, I think we should have structural and administrative reforms of the U. N. to enable it to exercise this power more easily and effectively. Either elimination or curtailment of the veto power would probably be required at this stage. These amendments should include adequate guaranties that the U. N. would not have any power or authority to act beyond the limited subject matter of maintenance of world peace and effectuating national disarmament and carrying out the other specific and limited objectives now permitted to it. 

UNIVERSAL MEMBERSHIP IN THE U. N. 

Sixth, and as a final stage I believe we should have universal and ultimately compulsory_membership of all national or quasi-national groups in the U. N. In other words, we should not permit outlaws merely because they choose to be outlaws, to put themselves outside the scope of the laws, any more than we permit an outlaw to declare himself outside the jurisdiction of our municipal governments. 

Certainly each of these points is complex and difficult. I do not pretend even to have suggested all of the considerations involved and the obstacles to be overcome in each of these steps. That is impossible within present limitations. However, the problem with which we are concerned is a complex and difficult one. No solution, not the solution of the maintenance of national armaments and the continuance of our relative position in the armaments race, no solution is easy or simple. The most we can ask is that a solution be possible. 

MEETING MINOR THREATS TO PEACE A BEGINNING 

I think that these steps do offer a possible solution. There is no promise that immediately or at any particular point our problems will vanish. It is quite possible that even if we follow the course 

I have suggested, these measures will in the beginning be adequate only to deal with limited threats to the peace and not with the threat offered by a major power.

But if we can begin to deal adequately with even minor threats to the peace, we shall be on the right course. If the United Nations, for example, today had a military force of its own adequate to insure that there would be no further war between the Arab nations and Israel, even that would be a substantial contribution toward insuring the prevention of another major war. 

If we can achieve this much as a beginning, then I submit we can reasonably expect that further advance along the same course will finally bring us to the position where we can depend upon the United Nations to defend us or any other nation against the aggression of any power. Then we will be able to disarm and to employ our enemies' resources and productivity wholly for peaceful and civilized uses. The important thing I suggest is that we make a beginning, and 

I am happy that that is what this committee is seeking to do. 

THE VOTING POWER IN THE U. N. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Mr. Loevinger, there is a point that I think has to be constantly kept in mind about these proposals relating to the U. N. and that is the voting power in the U. Ñ. One vote per nation regardless of its resources is the present arrangement, one vote regardless of population, one vote regardless of its role of responsibility. 

I wonder if you have given any consideration to this. I bring this up because I am sure the citizenry would immediately say, "Are you going to subject the United States of America with our resources, with our 165 or 170 million with just 1 vote in the U. N. to parity with Guatemala with 1 vote, Nigeria with 1 vote, and Libya with 1 vote?"

Mr. LOEVINGER. I think reapportionment of representation in the 

U. N. is as badly needed as it is in the State of Minnesota.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Then it is a deplorable situation. 

Mr. LOEVINGER. However, this again is something which certainly should be undertaken and I think that as we move along this path taking it step by step, this reform will be undertaken and can be achieved. Actually on the record we have not done badly. 

The majority of the nations are unquestionably devoted to the same ideals as we are, are prepared to vote with us, have voted with us, and would exercise such control as they have to the ends which we seek. I think that it would be safe to make a beginning along this path even without reformation of the representation in the U. N. 

Ultimately I think the representation in the U. N. can and must be reformed. There will be many changes that will be required but probably the method of representation in any one of the states is not perfect. Some states may have reapportioned recently and have perfect proportional representation but it is not essential that we wait for this to happen before we begin to take the steps that will so change conditions in the world as to permit progress toward disarmament. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I merely raise this point because these are factors or problems that I am sure occur to people that study this question and I think it is well that the committee or a representative of the committee just cite for the record that these are matters of very crucial importance and consideration. 

Mr. LOEVINGER. I am sure they are. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much for your testimony, Mr. Loevinger.

I would just like to have the record note in the light of what Mr. 

Loevinger said about the U. N., that I have done some checking on its activities. It is my privilege to be subcommittee chairman on U. N. 

affairs of the Foreign Relations Committee. I don't recall that the 

United States of America has ever lost a substantial issue in the United 

Nations. 

We have had on occasion, on a procedural matter, a reverse but with equal candor I must say within the last 2 years we have seen some defection from support for some of our positions in the U. N. However, we have been able to muster on the average 47 to 50 votes against 6, 7 or 5, and that is not a bad average, might I say. I would have settled for much less in some of my political activities. 

Mr. Ward Fleming?

STATEMENT OF PAUL KASNER, REPRESENTING A STATEMENT BY WARD FLEMING, UNIVERSITY OF MINNESOTA Mr. KASNER. Senator, my name is Paul Kasner, of 1305 West Ar lington Avenue in St. Paul, Minn.

Chairman HUMPHREY. And you are reading Mr. Fleming's state ment?

Mr. KASNER. Mr. Fleming is a lieutenant colonel in the Air Force 

Reserve and his duties in connection with that work have prevented him from coming here today and he asked that I be here and read his statement. I am going to read a portion of the statement at least. 

For brevity I am going to keep it down a bit.

All of us devotedly hope and pray that the day will come when this desirable objective of disarmament will be a reality, but the predatory nature of international communism is too well known to need discussion here. Promises which come from the Kremlin are worthless. 

Let us examine the recent Soviet proposal that they intend to disarm. 

RUSSIA'S UNILATERAL DISARMAMENT MOVE 

Now this statement by Colonel Fleming is made in light of the recent statements by the Russians that they are cutting back their Air Force and cutting back their Armed Forces, it is in light of that that 

Colonel Fleming is making this statement. 

On May 28, 1956, 16 days after the May 12 pronouncement of the

Kremlin that they intended to cut back their forces, General Gruenther, Commander of NATO, observed that Russia now has 175 divisions. Cutting 60 divisions from this force would leave 115 divisions. 

General Gruenther estimated that there are 85 to 80 satellite divisions mobilized from the conquered European nations and this would leave mostly in Europe a total of 190 to 195 divisions. 

U. S. S. R. MILITARY DIVISIONS COMPARED TO NATO's 

To date NATO has raised only 15 divisions, and General Gruenther counts upon the German contribution of 12 divisions plus various tactical aircraft and a few naval vessels to augment the current NATO forces.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I would like to keep the record accurate. I most respectfully suggest that that is not an accurate figure, because

we have 18 divisions in the United States alone and we are a member of NATO. What you mean is there are 15 divisions in Europe? 

Mr. KASNER. Fifteen in Europe, that is right.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Fifteen committed to NATO? 

Mr. KASNER. That is right.

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I say there are about as many Soviet divisions on the Chinese border today and in the Far East as there are in Western Europe. That is interesting by the way. 

Mr. KASNER. The 15 and 12 make a total of 27, and thus it is apparent that the remaining Red Army and its satellite divisions will outnumber the West by an enormous figure and leave the Soviet Union in a position of superior military power in the event any real war took place in the next few years.

RUSSIA'S AIRPOWER 

In addition, Russia has assured us that she will cut back three air divisions. This is a scant 300 planes and in all probability are outdated Yaks, TU-4's and other obsolescent types. It is a known fact 

Russia possesses 20,000 jet aircraft of all types. General LeMay has estimated that by 1959 the Russians will have a greater strategic air force than we have. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. That is our own fault I want to say. I agree with that statement. I think it is unpardonable. 

Mr. KASNER. Our air observers who witnessed the parade in Red 

Square recently in the fly-by of the modern Soviet Air Force came home deeply concerned about this growing influence of Soviet strategic power. So turning back 300 obsolete Soviet warplanes by the 

Red government bears no relationship whatever to any firm purpose of disarmament which Russia claims she is about to begin. 

THE RUSSIAN NAVY 

The third part of the Russian promise was to retire 375 naval vessels, to set an example to the West to cut back our naval forces. In 1955 alone Russia built 80 submarines. This was 5 more than the total of 

75 that Germany had when she began World War II. Russia has today an estimated 400 submarines of the Snorkel type which no doubt are equipped to carry atomic missiles and launch them offshore from our coastal ports. Russia says nothing about including any of her modern up-to-date submarines among the 375 vessels to be retired. 

To summarize, it is clear even if the Soviet carries out her proposed partial demobilization, she will be far ahead in Europe in land and air forces, and at sea will be equipped with a gigantic navy of undersea craft which makes her a formidable armed enemy capable of striking without warning whenever she thinks we have been lulled into a false security. 

If the Reds are sincere about the desire for peace and justice in the world they could withdraw from the satellite countries, which they have conquered by force, and throw them open to free elections so their people may enjoy what we in America have; namely, the right of selfdetermination and free institutions.

It is apparent that the Soviet move for disarmament is nothing but a propaganda device designed to delude the West that the days of

Russian predatory Stalinism are over and that a peaceful coexistence is about to begin. 

COEXISTENCE NOT IN COMMUNIST CREED 

The coexistence proposal of the Reds is a myth. Recently before the 

Senate Internal Security Committee, through an interpreter, the former director of anti-American propaganda in Communist Poland testified as to the speech which Khrushchev made to the Polish Communist Party Central Committee in Warsaw in April 1955. He quoted the Russian party leader as saying, "We must realize that coexistence cannot be eternal. One of us must one day go to the grave. 

We do not want to go to the grave. Americans and Westerners do not want to go either. We must push them."

Khrushchev's attitude is clear enough. To prevent ourselves and our friends from being pushed to our graves, we must remain strong. 

We must resist all temptation, based on wishful thinking, to give reality to a myth. 

Communism is rotten to the core. It will use armed force when it believes it possible to effect victory over us. Until God in His mercy removes this scourge from the world, the world cannot look forward to a future of real progress in peace, justice, and good will to men. 

As a veteran of World War I and member of the American Legion, member of the Veterans of Foreign Wars, with one son now in the infantry at Fort Riley, Kans., another who served in the Navy during the Korean war, I am just going to add my little piece to the comments on this session. 

I don't claim to be an expert. You have had a lot of experts talking here and frankly I am a little confused about what some of them are talking about. There are two missing links and this is going to be very elementary. I am sorry if I cannot lend a more erudite explanation to it, but there are two missing links in this whole disarmament thing. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. You don't need to apologize. You go right ahead.

THE CONSPIRATORY AND GODLESS NATURE OF COMMUNISM 

Mr. KASNER. The first is the conspiratory nature of the people running the Soviet Union, the gangsters who have millions of people under subservience without the will of the people. That is the first thing, trying to deal with them.

The second thing is the Godless aspect of the world organization trying to settle this.

UNITED NATIONS COVENANT OF HUMAN RIGHTS AND THE UNITED STATES BILL OF RIGHTS 

I realize that the U. N. has to be studied. We may be able to use it, but I am amazed at the willingness of some people to accept, for instance, the Covenant of Human Rights under the United Nations as a substitute for the Bill of Rights of the United States of America. 

These Americans don't know what they are talking about. It is a reversal of the Bill of Rights under the Constitution of the United States to have the United Nations talking about human rights which

are given by government and which can be taken away by government. 

The speaker who just preceded me talked about the common law of England being brought about by feudal chiefs. Let me read what 

Edward Coke, the Chief Justice under James the First of England from 1552 to 1634 said:

The law of nature was before the judicial or municipal law and is immutable. 

Human government is limited by the law of God. 

That does not sound like law set up by feudal chiefs. This is a Godgiven thing. The Founding Fathers reaffirmed this by these words: 

We hold these truths to be self-evident that all men are created equalcreated equal presupposing a supreme beingthat they are endowed by their Creator with certain inalienable rights; that among them are life, liberty and the pursuit of happiness. 

To secure these, governments are instituted by men. In other words governments are instruments whereby these basic natural rights are protected and our Founding Fathers made that the whole basis of our 

Government. 

It was the basis of the American Revolution, not the Boston Tea Party or taxation without representation. And the opening words to the Declaration of Independence affirm that. In England they were doing just the same as we are doing too much in this country today, saying there is no natural law, there is no right to human property, there is no such thing as the right to anything, it is a social arrangement. We are having that taught cross the country unfortunately in many of our institutions of higher learning. 

William Penn said that those who will not be governed by God will be ruled by tyrants, and history proves that Penn was right and that our Founding Fathers were right and that the Communists who place government above the natural rights are wrong. 

Let's strengthen the United States under the Constitution of the United States, and devote our efforts to establishing God-given rights to the peoples of the world; and this is the key to disarmament and the key to peace, not government-granted rights. 

I appreciate this opportunity to make this statement. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much. 

THE DOCTRINE OF HUMAN RIGHTS OUR SECRET WEAPON 

It has been my privilege to be one that reads the writings of Thomas Aquinas for example. I think there is a great body of tradition and heritage in this country, as you just pointed out, in support in the faith of the doctrine of human rights, of natural rights. 

I believe in these things and I think this is one of our powerful weapons in the world because in many of the less-developed societies, natural rights or God-given rights are a very powerful force. 

Mr. KASNER. It does not seem that we are using it. 

The associate professor of the University of Minnesota was one of the speakers here this morning and pointed out we are not using some of the things that we should. 

I think this may

Chairman HUMPHREY. I could not agree more. 

be our real secret weapon. 

Mr. KASNER. This is our secret weapon.

Mr. KASNER. I went to school with him. He is a personal friend. 

TRIBUTE TO GENERAL GRUENTHER 

Chairman HUMPHREY. He is a great American and a really great citizen. I think that this man Gruenther has much to tell the American people and he has been doing a pretty good job about doing it. 

One of the things that he told us the Foreign Relations Committee-was that one of the great needs in American society today is to have a little more definite knowledge about other areas of the world. 

We are somewhat superficial about that. Everybody has a solution for the Far East without knowing about the Far East. The farther the problem is away, the more positive our solutions are. It is sort of in inverse proportions.

I want to say further that we are not unmindful at all of the tremendous military buildup of the Soviet and her satellites, but a military buildup is one thing and the industrial output immediately available is the second thing.

At least within this decade, we have an industrial base that is far beyond what the Soviet orbit has. One of my concerns as a Senator, as a citizen, and as a dad is that if we don't watch out we may lose some of that edge.

SECURITY THROUGH STRENTGH 

I have been greatly distressed as one citizen to see the tremendous advance in Soviet military capabilities. I did not vote to reduce the 

Air Force by $5 billion, I want you to know that. At the time that that issue was up I was much more interested in a balance of forces than I was in a balanced budget and I make no apologies for it at all. 

I have felt that, until this world situation straightens out a little bit more to my limited knowledge, I was not going to be one of those that went along with trying to see if we could not make a headline play on reduced expenditures for national security.

I am not at all sure that we have security. I recognize the points of view that have been brought out here today. Maybe we don't have it. I must confess, however, that until I can see some semblance of law and order in the world, I would not feel very comfortable unless I thought our country was relatively strong. 

I appreciate your testimony and I appreciate your presenting Colonel Fleming's testimony. (The prepared statement submitted by Colonel Fleming is as follows:)

In the entire world there is no sane person who does not deeply desire peace, with liberty and justice for all men. All of us devoutly hope and pray that the day will come when this desirable objective will be a reality and men will not have to live in fear that their lives, freedom, and property will ever again be endangered by any power which desires conquest for any reason. 

But the predatory nature of international communism is too well known to need discussion here. Promises which come from the Kremlin are worthless. 

Only by their acts can they be judged. The Bible says, "By their fruits ye shall

know them." So let us examine for a moment, the fruits of the recent Soviet proposal that they intend to disarm, to some extent, and see what balance results even if this promise of theirs should be carried out. 

On May 28, 1956, General Gruenther, Commander of NATO, addressed the Commonwealth Club of California. This was 16 days after the May 12 pronouncement of the Kremlin that they intended to cut back their forces. General 

Gruenther observed that Soviet Russia now has 175 divisions. If she cuts 60 divisions from this force and returns them to reserve status, this would leave her with 115 divisions in being, the vast bulk of which would be based in Europe! 

In addition General Gruenther estimated there are 75 to 80 satellite divisions mobilized from the men of the conquered European nations. This would give the international Red Army in Europe a total of 190 to 195 divisions, after the proposed demobilization is effective. To date NATO has raised only 15 divisions and General Gruenther himself admits that even with the reduced Soviet total force of land troops, there is no guaranty that he could defend Western Europe against any Red invasion. He counts upon the German contribution of 12 divisions, plus various tactical aircraft, and a few naval vessels, to augment the current NATO forces. This will require another 3 or 4 years to develop. At that time, he said, he could give “reasonable assurance" that Western Europe could be defended, because of the German addition, and the greater deployment of tactical atomic weapons.

Thus, it is apparent that even with the proposed Soviet demobilization, the remaining Red Army and its satellite divisions will outnumber the West by an enormous figure, and leave the Soviet Union in a position of superior military power, in the event any real war took place in the next few years. 

It should be clear that any attempt to reduce the forces of the West in Europe, or the Army of the United States, would be a patent absurdity not justified in any way, even if the Soviet carries out its promise to demobilize the 60 divisions. 

In addition Russia has assured us she will cut back three air divisions. But this is a scant 300 war planes and nothing at all is said by the Reds as to what type they will be. The Russian's strategic air force is newly developed and in all probability the planes to be cut back are old, out-dated Yaks, TU 4's, Mig 15's and other obsolete types, which have no further tactical value. It is a known fact that Russia possesses 20,000 jet aircraft of all types. With the cutback, it leaves them with 19,700. General LeMay has estimated that within 3 years-by 

1959-the Russians will have a greater strategic air force than we, in terms of numbers. Our air observers who witnessed the parade in Red Square recently and the fly-by of the modern Soviet air force, came home deeply concerned about this growing influx of Soviet strategic power. So the alleged turning back of 300 obsolete Soviet warplanes by the Red government is obviously so much eyewash, and bears no relationship whatever to any firm purpose of disarmament, which Russia claims she is about to begin. 

The third part of the Russian "promise" was to retire 375 naval vessels. Το examine this a bit further, however, we note the absurdity of their claim that this should relieve tensions and set an example to the West, to cut back our own naval forces. In 1955 alone, Russia built 80 submarines. This was 5 more than the total of 75 that Germany had when she began World War II. 

Russia has today an estimated 400 submarines of the snorkel type which, no doubt, are equipped to carry atomic missiles and launch them off shore from our coastal ports. Russia says nothing about including any of her up-to-date modern submarines among the 375 vessels to be retired. 

To summarize, it is clear that even if the Soviet carries out her proposed partial demobilization, she will be far ahead in Europe in land and air forces, and at sea will be equipped with a gigantic navy of underseas craft, which makes her a formidable and armed enemy, capable of striking, and without warning, whenever she thinks we have been lulled into a false security which the proponents of disarmament seem to want us to adopt. 

If the Reds are sincere about the desire for disarmament for peace and justice in the world, they have only to do two things: (1) To withdraw themselves from the satellite countries which they have conquered by force, and to throw them open to free elections where their peoples may enjoy what we in America have; namely, the right to self-determination and free institutions. (2) To propose on a commonsense basis a substantial cutback of land, sea, and air forces to quantities sufficient only to maintain such police forces as necessary, and invite the United States and the Western World to do the same. 

This obviously would have to be guaranteed by an inspection system on both

3 sides, of such extent that subterfuge and concealed armaments would be impossible. In this way, neither side would have sufficient striking force to be able to mount an aggression, and the mutual fear and distrust of the East and the West would be effectively nullified. 

As things stand, however, it seems completely apparent that the Soviet move for disarmament is nothing but a propaganda device designed to delude the West that the days of Russian predatory Stalinism are over, and that a peaceful coexistence is about to begin. Russia has shown no intention of giving up her conquests gained without a single claim of right. She shows no willingness even after the proposed demobilization to permit any type of inspection system. The coexistence proposal of the Reds is perhaps the biggest myth of all. Recently, before the Senate Internal Security Committee, through an interpreter the former director of anti-American propaganda in Communist Poland testified as to the speech which Khrushchev made to the Polish Communist Party Central Committee, in Warsaw in April, 1955. He quoted the Russian party leader as saying: "We must realize that coexistence cannot be eternal. One of us must one day go to his grave. We do not want to go to the grave. Americans and 

Westerns do not want to go either. We must push them."

Khruschev's attitude is clear enough. To prevent ourselves and our friends from being pushed to our graves, we must remain armed with regular and reserve forces sufficient to prevent any attempt by the powers of darkness to reduce our Nation to rubble, and our institutions to a hollow memory of their former greatness.

We must resist all temptations based on wishful thinking to give reality to a myth. Communism is rotten to the core. It will use armed force whenever it believes it possible to effect victory over us-the last best hope on earth. 

Our guard must be up until God in His mercy removes this scourge from the world, and all nations can look forward to a future of real progress in peace, justice, and good will toward men. Until that time, let us say our prayers, and keep our powder dry. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. There are 1 or 2 points I would like to call to your attention.

I only correct the record. I do not believe the Soviet Union has 

400 snorkel submarines. They do have approximately 400 submarines, according to our intelligence.

They have been making tremendous advances in this. When are we going to understand that their capabilities are pretty big? They produce only 40 million tons of steel a year but they don't put it in automobiles. They put it in something else. 

I will let you go now.

The next witness is Mrs. Richard Scott. 

As long as the citizenry is willing to stay I am willing to stay. 

Mrs. Scott?

STATEMENT OF MRS. RICHARD SCOTT, REPRESENTING THE MIN- NESOTA BRANCH OF THE WOMEN'S INTERNATIONAL LEAGUE FOR PEACE AND FREEDOM Mrs. SCOTT. I am Shirley Scott and I live at 711 Pennsylvania Ave nue N., and I am a mother, a teacher, and a housewife and today I have the honor to represent the Minnesota branch of the Women's Inter national League for Peace and Freedom. The Women's International 

League or the WIL for short-was founded in 1915 and is an international organization dedicated to the proposition that social change can take place nonviolently if we but work intelligently for it. The only two American women to receive the Nobel peace award-Jane Addams and Emily Greene Balch-have been two of the international Presidents of the WIL. The Minnesota branch of the league has about 150 members.

TENETS OF THE WOMEN'S INTERNATIONAL LEAGUE 

The WIL has from its foundation advocated total universal disarmament; abolition of violent means for settlement of all conflicts and substitution of peaceful methods of settlement; development of world organization for political, social, and economic cooperation of peoples; and fostering by nonviolent means, of equality of opportunity for all, with no distinction of race, sex, religion, or opinion. 

We have always thought of freedom and peace as interconnectedthere can be no real freedom without peace and no true peace without freedom.

Our objective of total universal disarmament, therefore, must be thought of as only one of our priorities. But we have always argued that the removal of the means for waging war is an essential step toward the abolition of war. On the old problem of which comes first, security or disarmament, we contend that they will develop, if at all, simultaneously.

Our position, more explicitly, is one in favor of total and universal disarmament, not merely reduction of armament or control of armaments. By disarmament we mean that every nation would have to agree to give up all weapons other than those needed to maintain law and order within its own borders. 

TOTAL DISARMAMENT EASIER TO ACHIEVE THAN PARTIAL DISARMAMENT 

Total disarmament, we think, will be easier to get agreement on and easier to carry out than partial disarmament. Our reasons for holding this are: (a) Partial disarmament can afford but little security in an atomic age. (b) It is easier to carry out an inspection plan for total disarmament than for partial disarmament. (c) Elaborate schemes for percentage reductions are really very impractical and utopian, for they all tend to give some nations advantage over others and thus promote jealousies and failures to agree. True, complete disarmament will still leave some nations more powerful economically, and in other respects, than others—but the results of such inequalities are radically less harmful than military inequalities would be.

CESSATION OF NUCLEAR WEAPONS TESTS 

Control and inspection problems are admittedly difficult. But pending the working out of such schemes (and progress has already been made), we think there should be an immediate cessation of atomic and hydrogen weapons tests and a temporary arms truce in all armaments production. We are happy to note that a member of the 

Atomic Energy Commission agrees with us on the banning of atomic and hydrogen weapons tests.

ECONOMIC IMPLICATIONS OF ARMAMENTS AND DISARMAMENT 

The Minnesota branch would like to emphasize its concern for the economic implications of armaments and disarmament. In the first place, we believe that only through total disarmament can we obtain

adequate support for the world economic development so desperately needed if we are to have a stable world. In the second place, we are alarmed by the degree to which the American economy has become an arms economy-an economy increasingly dependent on so-called defense orders for its continued operation. 

Unfortunately, in an economy increasingly dependent on armaments, men tend to develop a rather stupid, though rather passive, faith in armaments. The W. I. L., by contrast, has absolutely no faith in military methods. We believe these methods have failed. This should be the first premise for all disarmament discussions. 

Thank you very much.

CORRECTING THE RECORD Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you, Mrs. Scott. I think I should make just one comment. That the record should show that Commissioner Murray, who is the senior member of the Atomic Energy Commission, did not advocate banning of all weapons tests, but a ban on the size of the tests.

In other words, I would suggest a very careful reading of his testimony, and it is a very moving bit of testimony. He has a great sense of social conscience and social justice. He was primarily concerned with these thermonuclear tests, because he said, "We have already proven that any weapon of any size can be set off."

I just bring that out to correct the record. 

I want to thank you very much for your testimony and commend you. We had your national representative testify last week in Washington.

I believe these are the grassroots; getting various opinions from all people is doing a good job for two reasons: No. 1, you get testimony which you could not get inside of Washington. For example, Professor Coryell's statement that we do have the means of testing, of finding out whether Russia is testing H-bombs, we can tell when they are doing it. 72880-56-pt. 9- -11

For example I have forgotten the professor's name this idea of testing the aerial inspection plan on other nations friendly to us, is also, I think, a very worth while suggestion. 

Also I believe disarmament has to be implemented, and implementation calls for education by the people, education of the people, and this is the way to do it by going out to the grassroots. 

Some of this testimony was so good here, the Foreign Relations Committee gave me this bushel of documents and I did not have a chance to look at them until last night. I started at 6:30, and at 12
