Chairman HUMPHREY. I am glad they were that interesting.

Mr. ROBINS. They were, especially the Cambridge hearings from private citizens. I thought they were especially worth while. Indeed they have caused me to change some of my opinions. I hope this is because of objectivity rather than immaturity. For example, in the first statement I make here that prospects have never been so bright for disarmament as they are today and that Russia wants disarmament, I am not so sure about that. Various testimony has convinced me that this may not be so.

However, I would say that Russia for the first time is beginning to take a serious interest in the field of disarmament. I think the 

London Conference which we have mentioned here before well illustrates that point.

If I may also comment, some people have suggested, I am afraid, that the United States should not disarm, because any time we go into any agreement we have the possibility of being tricked by the Russians. If we are going to take that attitude, we actually in effect will be the nation who will be opposed to disarmament. I believe this nation does seriously want to disarm.

DISARMAMENT PROPOSAL MUST BE MUTUALLY ADVANTAGEOUS 

I think this is a point that has been emphasized earlier but I would like to repeat it. All disarmament proposals must be mutually advantageous to both Russia and the United States. For example, when we suggested in President Eisenhower's letter to Premier Bulganin that all stockpiles be frozen, I assume that he believes that the United States has more atomic bombs than Russia and hydrogen bombs, and therefore it would obviously be to our advantage. Why should Russia agree to this proposal when it would be taking away the advantage where she might be able to catch up in a few years?

Chairman HUMPHREY. That is a question of great concern may I 

say to you, and I am not at all sure that we have such a great lead. 

I am only expressing my own opinion. There is reason to believe that some of that lead may have been overtaken, but I can see your point and I think you make a very valid one.

Mr. ROBINS. Here is one authority I use, the science writer for the 

Manchester Guardian estimates that right now Russia has the equivalent atomic materials to make 20,000 nuclear bombs and we have 35,000.

However, and the point I am going to come to later, there comes a point where it does not make any difference if you have 25,000, 500,000 or 5 million. There comes a point where you just have enough and that can do the job.

But then there is the other way too. For example, some people suggest that we possibly might set up a plan of banning long-range missiles without banning short-range missiles. Russia I imagine would be well in favor of this because with her short range missiles she can actually blackmail Europe because they can hit Europe, whereas the only short-range missiles we have that can hit Russia are from overseas bases which are, while not untenable, certainly questionable from a military standpoint of what we could do with them. 

Therefore, it seems to me that any disarmament proposal must be fair to both sides, and it is for this reason along with several others that I believe that the aerial inspection is not fair to Russia, for as a reason which has been pointed out before, the simple fact that Russia right now knows basically of our military bases, etc. They certainly do not know everything, but with superior intelligence and the fact that we are a democracy they know more than we do in this field. 

Why should they give up this advantage unless we give up something in return? This is a problem which I will come to a little later. 

On that it seems to me that there is more than this military thing. 

There is the fact that now we would be able to know what thinking was going on in Russia. I am sure if we took photographs I am sure we would not throw them in the basket if they were not related to military affairs. I am sure some way would be found to measure and evaluate all the photographs, plus the great difficulties in the problem itself. 

I can picture thousands of men scooting around in the country would obviously be quite difficult to accomplish. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. May I say it would not take thousands it might take a hundred thousand. It may take very many qualified technical people. 

As a matter of fact, one of the questions to Admiral Strauss was whether or not we had enough people trained and competent to really do that job of inspection. Frankly, speaking for myself, I doubt that we have.

Mr. ROBINS. There is another problem for a few months or years of study, this engineering thing.

INTERCHANGE OF IDEAS 

That is really serious. Then on this business of changing opinions, on my last statement in relation to this I say that I don't know of any answer to this question. Well, actually I don't but I would possibly suggest this and use an example from America's national pastime in relation to sports.

If two teams are trading, it does not necessarily have to be a pitcher for a pitcher or a shortstop for a shortstop. They can trade positions, and I suggest that possibly the same thing could be done in relation to the United States and Russia. If we get Russia to agree to give up the advantage of secrecy in this aerial inspection plan, we must agree to some other type of concession. We cannot give up our security, but possibly in relation to trade, two different things but they might equal à balance which would be mutually advantageous to both sides. In other words, we would possibly trade some materials with Russia while they would agree to an inspection system.

In continuing this problem of the technical aspect, and I admit I am getting most of it from these documents and various others- 

Chairman HUMPIIREY. You just keep reading. 

Mr. ROBINS. Let me say that right now the only thing as I understand it we can control in an inspection system of any kind is the ballistic missile and planes. We have no way of knowing what bombs they have got.

They can bury them in the earth and we cannot find them. As I understand it there is no way of detecting their nuclear bombs that they have made.

Chairman HUMPHREY. They are not much good in the earth. 

Mr. ROBINS. That is why I say that within the 10-year span until the intercontinental ballistic missile is developed, we have a chance at controlling this type of thing.

But after 10 years from now as you gentlemen well know, when the intercontinental ballistic missile is developed to a point where they can put it in the ground, around 10 square feet or something is all that is required, imagine the terrible problems you would have in inspection then. As a matter of fact I believe one authority in this book suggested that inspection for all practical purposes would become impossible.

A CRASH DETECTION PROGRAM NEEDED 

Therefore, I suggest along with a crash program to develop the intercontinental ballistic missile, we should adopt a crash program to find a way to detect nuclear bombs even if they are in the earth, that we somehow do it. How I don't know, so that after 10 years we will have a program available for the new scientific aspects of the problem. 

Also in continuing the technical aspects of this aerial inspection, I would like to suggest, and here I quote from Harold Stassen in his report, that related ground service would follow up the aerial inspection. It seems to me that this is impossible to be done. If you have to go in steps you have to take a full step. In other words, all inspection has to take account of the one type of thing, aerial, ground, blueprints or whatever there may be. Obviously Russia could agree to an aerial inspection inasmuch as it is ineffective we might disarm and Russia could keep on arming without us knowing it. We have to have a full inspection setup.

My next point will deal with the problem of oversimplification, which we seem to have done in connection with Russia and the United States assuming aerial inspection or any other disarmament plan can operate within these two countries, and this is a point on your statement about Red China.

However I believe it also applies to ourselves as well. How could 

Russia agree to complete disarmament of Russia and Red China without knowing Britain, France, and so forth, were disarmed? I believe any program of aerial inspection would have to include all countries of the world, every single one because we are getting to a point where every single nation in the world can develop these nuclear bombs.

Some supranational organization will have to do this. What type 

I cannot envision. I do not believe at this time possibly that the U. N. especially with the veto power could do it, but with some changes in the U. N. structure which we have heard discussed at great length

today, or possibly some other type of supranational organization could do this. 

We would need this rather than each nation because of the worldwide effects of the problem and the fact that other nations while not being able to produce these bombs, are vitally interested in the problem.

At this point I would like to come to the final point of my testimony. 

The thing which we can do unilaterally is, and here I would like to once again agree with the statement by Thomas Murray-not the statement that we ban all bombs and all military tests as you have pointed out that we ban these large thermonuclear tests, for this reason: I do not necessarily agree with all of Mr. Murray's reasons for the banning but I believe there are sufficient reasons for doing so. 

MORAL REASON FOR DISCONTINUANCE OF TESTS 

The first reason would be a moral reason, not the moral reason that we could destroy civilians, because I believe in any war civilians must be willing to assume the responsibility of a military establishment. I believe our civilians here would not assume that our boys are fighting but we are not fighting. I believe we would pitch in and 

I suspect we would be willing to sustain these risks which are inherent in any war, if this war becomes necessary. 

But there is the moral reason of the fact that we do not know the effects as of this time of radiation on people. Right now we are not testing these bombs in the United States where if there is a danger we sustain it. We are testing them out in the Pacific where the Japanese sustain it. This works out fine for us. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Or the Okinawans or the Marshall 

Islanders? 

Mr. ROBINS. Yes, I believe there was a test shown where 68 natives in 1 island in the Marshalls, received something like 15 roentgens of radiation and a year later we publish a text saying no one has died yet. 

This is very interesting but the rule suggests a law developed by H. J. 

Muller, Nobel prize winner, that 5 days of life is lost for each roentgen ray received. So 75 days is lost for these people. We have the statement here.

First we assume that the bomb was 2 points off course; now it is 4 miles; some people report 7 miles. Two people are assumed blind. 

We don't know the situation which is occurring in these tests, and I submit that there is a moral argument. 

As long as we test these bombs out in the area where other people are endangered, we have no right to endanger these people. If we want to endanger anybody the only people we can do it to is ourselves. 

It seems to me that the practical benefit of these bombs is drawing to a close. You know this rule as well as I do, the blasting power varies as the cube root, and the heat as the square root. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I am sure I don't know as much about it as you do. I am frank to admit it. I am sitting here in amazement. 

Mr. ROBINS. Thank you very much for this compliment although 

I am sure it is not true.

Chairman HUMPHREY. It is true. 

Mr. ROBINS. Well, thank you. 72880-56-pt. 9- -12

DIMINISHING RETURNS IN INCREASING SIZE OF BOMBS 

So, in other words, if we have an H-bomb which is possibly a thousand times as powerful as an A-bomb, in terms of megatons or kilotons that bomb can blast 10 times as much and no more. 

In other words, 10 A-bombs of possibly one one-thousandth the power can do the job so there comes a point. Now it helps to a certain extent to build them larger because 1 can do the job of 2 but there obviously comes a point of diminishing returns in this matter where rather than becoming more efficient in a larger package these bombs actually become less efficient. 

Then there is this other point.

As General Partridge estimated, something like 50 H-bombs could just about do the job. If they are dropped correctly by Russia, half our population and most of our industrial resources would be out, and, as I quote here from the Manchester Guardian, we have the stockpiles to make 35,000 bombs.

This is based on the idea that 40,000 kilograms were offered for the peacetime uses and around 5 kilograms for a bomb, and that is 8,000 bombs we would be giving away and obviously we are not giving away half of our strength so we would have around something like 30,000 or 35,000. So even if it requires a little more money, in comparison with the world opinion which we would get on our side, which 

I will go into a little later here, I still suggest it would pay to do this. 

It might cost us a little more money but in the assets which possibly cannot be measured by monetary terms but by good will, I think this would pay off.

BANNING OF H-BOMB FIRST STEP TOWARD DISARMAMENT 

No. 1, we have the security factor, namely, as Mr. Coryell pointed out we can find out if nations are testing H-bombs so that we can continue, we can set up mock tests if we have to so we don't have to lose much time.

There comes a point where each nation can destroy each other. The fact one nation can do it more efficiently than the other nation is not too important if the job is done. 

When you consider the propaganda effect all throughout the world it would help us greatly when you consider the fact that this propaganda effect may in the final analysis be more important even than the military effect.

I suggest that banning of the H-bomb, the large thermonuclear bomb, may be a logical first step toward disarmament.

Mr. ROBINS. Thank you very much, Senator. (The prepared statement submitted by Mr. Robins is as follows:)

DISARMAMENT 

It seems to me that prospects have never been so bright for disarmament as they are today, but also that there have never been the problems in disarmament that we have today. Both sides have seen by their own tests of atomic weapons that in the next war, if there is to be one, both sides would be losers. The second factor working for disarmament is that both Russia and the United States are now spending a good share of their resources in the political arena. They would like to spend even more in this, and the economic field. The costly arms race is just a hindrance rather than a help to both countries and it would be to everybody's advantage if they could spend all, not just some, of their planning, resources, and money in propaganda, economic development, and diplomacy. 

For these reasons there is today, in my opinion, a real chance for disarmament. 

There are, however, great hurdles to leap before disarmament can be accomplished. But the greatest immediate obstacle to disarmament is that the United 

States must be sure of an adequate inspection system and Russia demands that its advantage of secrecy be retained. We all know that it would be impossible and incomprehensible for the United States to disarm without guaranties that Russia was doing the same. What would all too obviously happen is that Russia would attack after we had disarmed.

But the reason for Russia's demand for secrecy in this matter is equally obvious. Why should it give up something for nothing? It knows the locations of most, if not all, of our airbases, atomic testing grounds, etc. Yet we do not know a great deal in comparison about its bases and testing grounds. It was for this reason, and not that she doesn't want disarmament, that Russia turned down the open-skies plan. For in such a plan we get knowledge of Russia's military plants and life, while in return Russia gets little new information. 

Here is the real dilemma of disarmament: how to get an adequate inspection system without disturbing anyone's privacy. I wish I had the answer to this question. Despite it, I still say the chances of disarmament are good because now

everyone wants it. In resolving this dilemma lies the hope of the world, for any nation that has a huge investment of arms is always tempted to use them. 

There is, however, one thing which I think we can do by ourselves in the field of disarmament. I believe this country should, unilaterally if necessary, ban the testings of hydrogen bombs. In referring to hydrogen bombs I do not mean the tactical type, but the mammoths we have all grown to know so well. All possible advantages of these tests, in my opinion, are gone. By stopping hydrogen bomb tests we would either force Russia to do so, thereby making the first stride toward disarmament, or gain a great propaganda victory for ourselves if she does not.

While I hardly claim to have solved the question of disarmament I hope that I have brought out some worthwhile points in relation to the question. Thank you for letting me appear before this committee.

Chairman HUMPHREY. Adelaide Enright? 

I gather she left. She has asked that her statement be placed in the record.

V. J. Weight.

Mr. WEIGHT. That is right, and was on the train last night too.

STATEMENT OF V. J. WEIGHT, BARABOO, WIS. 

Mr. WEIGHT. I want to thank you, Senator, and Senator Wiley, for coming here. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I hope you have not found this day a waste of time.

Mr. WEIGHT. No; I have been listening to this very closely. I have been thinking about it for 10 years. 

My name is Weight. I come from Baraboo, Wis. I am speaking for myself. It appears that there ought to be agreements among the people as to how they shall live together as nations. 

ARMAMENTS CONTROL THROUGH INTERNATIONAL AGREEMENT 

The control of all destructive weapons of warfare are to be included in these agreements. That these agreements through the democratic process be made into law, and that this law be enforced upon those that won't comply, so that there is harmony, justice, and peace among mankind. So that all humans everywhere can have safety, security, and freedom from disintegration by the atomic and hydrogen weapons that man has just lately invented. 

Because all people cannot gather to come to an agreement, then it seems logical that representatives be appointed or elected to act for the people. Since our own Republic has proved so fruitful, perhaps it would be best to pattern procedures after it, and let each nation élect its representatives. These representatives from all countries to gather someplace so they can set up the rules we shall live by, as a community of nations, that is to include prohibiting the acts that start war, and creation of controls over mass weapons of destruction. That an international police department be set up to enforce these rules or world laws upon those who make attacks upon their neighbor nations. That those who commit crimes against humanity be apprehended by the international police for trial in the World Court, and if guilty either imprisoned or executed.

STRENGTHENING THE UNITED NATIONS 

Since time is short and tension increases with every passing week and month, it seems best to strengthen the United Nations so that it can take legal and authoritative action to enforce and maintain world peace.

Was not this the purpose for which the U.N. was made? Then let us give it the sovereignty and legal authority so it can be effective to do the job for which it was created. 

If efforts to amend the U. N. Charter and change the United Nations be prevented by those now bent on world conquest through the use of the veto, then it seems logical that all democracies who desire to live as free communities, act independently, as was done in our early history at Philadelphia for the creation of our own federation of the United 

States. 

EXPRESSION OF FAITH IN DEMOCRACY AND JUSTICE 

Since free men have gathered together before to create lawful order, then we, the free of this day and age, can gather again to bring into being that international society that can maintain a lawful world order to control destructive weapons and those who would use them in air attacks upon their neighbors.

Let us rely on the principles of democracy and justice that are now in effect and have proved so efficient in all free nations. If we of the free world act together, we will have such a preponderance of power that those who will not now cooperate will change their views and take the right action so that we all can live as a peaceful global community. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. Thank you very much. May I ask you what is your work?

Mr. WEIGHT. I repair homes. 

Chairman HUMPHREY. You repair homes and you prepared this statement? 

Mr. WEIGHT. Yes; I do a lot of reading.

Chairman HUMPHREY. You surely do. 

Mr. WEIGHT. I have read all the reports on the U. N., and I have read the reports that you sent me too, and I want to thank you very much for sending them to me.

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you. 

Your presence here and your appearance here along with others is the reassuring aspect of this public work. 

I am very grateful to you. 

Mr. WEIGHT. Senator Humphrey, we are going to win out. The

Christian people will lead the way. The very fact that all these people were here will spread the word and, though we don't agree, we are going to finally awaken America and through our officials-we have statesmen, yourself and others included in Congress-who will do what we want, we will solve the problems, and I feel sure we will master this thing and that we won't be destroyed and that we will live at peace in this global community.

CONCLUDING REMARKS OF THE CHAIRMAN 

Chairman HUMPHREY. I want to thank you very much. That is a wonderful optimistic note upon which to conclude these hearings and 

I think a justified optimistic note. I am very grateful to you.

I believe that there is no other witness to be heard, and may I say if anyone wants to submit a statement, the record of this hearing will be held open for a week.

I want to say this: With the exception of the panelists that testified this forenoon, all other witnessess on their own initiative exercised their right of petition in asking to be heard here. 

The subcommittee notifies the press and the people who have been asked to be placed on our mailing list of hearings, and the rest is up to the people who want to be heard. In other words, they made their own application for the opportunity to testify. 

Anyone who wishes to be put on the mailing list can be accommodated if they will merely write to me, Senator Hubert H. Humphrey, Washington, D. C., chairman of the Subcommittee on Disarmament, and we will send you whatever documents we have or, at your request, any specific information that is published by this subcommittee. 

I do not recall that there is anything else that should be noted for this hearing except to express formally and officially our gratitude and appreciation for the cooperation which has been extended the subcommittee, and to express a note of regret that more of our colleagues could not be here.

I think you all know that this is a very busy time for them. It was most difficult for us to get away on this weekend. We are hoping to hold a similar hearing in one of our neighboring cities in the Midwest some time if we can this summer, and as I said earlier today, this fall we will have a number of hearings around the country, after the election, to take more testimony from the people. 

Thank you very much. (Whereupon, at 7:05 p. m., the subcommittee was adjourned.)

APPENDIX STATEMENT BY ADELAIDE M. ENRIGHT, ST. PAUL, MINN. 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am Adelaide Enright, a businesswoman living in Saint Paul.

UNIVERSAL PEACE: THE CHALLENGE OF THIS AGE 

The achievement of universal and enforceable disarmament is the great challenge of this age. The elimination of the scourge of war which has cursed mankind throughout history is now recognized by many as within the realm of possibility.

This is evidenced by the appointment of Harold Stassen by President Eisenhower and the appointment of this Senate subcommittee on disarmament to explore this field. These two developments indicate a great change in public thinking in the past few years, for it is not so long ago that thought was absolutely closed to such a possibility and when it was proposed, the answer was almost invariably, "There have always been wars-there will always be wars." 

Liberation of thought from the bondage of tradition, limitation, and apathy has given mankind hope and furnished the impetus for an exploration of this desirable course.

I have read with interest the scholarly and constructive testimony by the eminent witnesses who attended the Cambridge hearing. I learned much from them of the obstacles that lie in our pathway as we press toward this goal. 

There is still disbelief in many quarters that such a goal can be reached and indeed some of the obstacles seem insurmountable. 

The men and women who have pondered this problem and have made an effort to find a way to solve it have generally reached the following conclusions: 

1. It would be worse than foolhardy to consider partial disarmament. 

2. The only practical disarmament is universal disarmament enforceable under law-since it is the only kind that would afford the protection we seek. 

3. To insure disarmament we must have as complete inspection as it is possible to devise. 

4. To insure this kind of inspection we need law to control it and the power and strength to enforce the law.

Some of our political and business leaders in exploring this frontier have had the courage to say that our goal is attainable only under some form of world law-but unfortunately, they usually add, speaking categorically for the people, "We do not think the people are ready for it so we will have to try some other way." The other way is usually procedure which has never solved the problem and can only lead to another catastrophic failure. I do not believe this is either good politics, good business, or good sense. 

A PUBLIC RELATIONS JOB NEEDED 

It is my deep conviction that when the people understand what is needed to bring them freedom from the heavy burden of armaments and relief from the fears engendered by them, we will find that we are ready to take the necessary steps.

More is being spent to sell lipsticks today than to teach people a few simple facts of modern life. 

A public-relation job in the free world and as far behind the Iron Curtain as our broadcasts reach will soon reveal that the people are ready for practical and effective steps to achieve this high goal.

This committee is making a most valuable contribution and I commend you for holding these important hearings. 759

As one humble citizen who has long been dedicated to the task of eliminating war as a means of settling disputes between nations, I urge that you initiate and support a widespread program of education on this subject. It would bring a response that would surprise and hearten you.

President Eisenhower may have accomplished little with the Soviets at the summit meeting last year but he made a profound impression on the citizens of other countries and convinced them that he is a man of peace representing a peace-loving nation.

Human thought has recoiled from the materialistic modes and methods which have resulted in a bomb bringing total destruction to mankind and turns yearningly to a more spiritual concept of life. As thought becomes more enlightened, higher motives impel the acts of men to a course that will fulfill our hopes and aspirations for a free and peaceful way of life. 

The task that confronts us requires not only human devisement but the Christliness of our thinking and as we consider this grave and important work, I would urge that we take to heart Jesus' admonition in Mark's Gospel. (Mark 

11-24) "Therefore, I say unto you what things soever ye desire, when ye pray, believe that ye receive them, and ye shall have them."

STATEMENT BY CLARE MCLAUGHLIN, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

DISARMAMENT AND PUBLIC UNDERSTANDING 

I am a housewife with no claim to be heard except that of a citizen who has a deep feeling of the individual's responsibility for the destiny of our country and a faith that the ideals and principles of this country can provide leadership in the effort to achieve universal enforced disarmament. 

These hearings are concerned with factors vital to a system of universal, enforced disarmament, the avowed aim of our Government. Ultimately the decisive factor is the understanding and attitude of the people. For whether any plan proposed in these hearings or elsewhere is possible of achievement depends on the people's willingness to pay its price, accept its conditions. Mr. Thomas K. 

Finletter, former Secretary of Air, in his book, Power and Policy, states, “The most serious obstacle is the uncertainty in the minds of the leaders of government that the people of the United States would accept a system of enforced disarmament if it were ever put to them. This uncertainty is, I believe the basic reason why no such plan has ever been proposed by the United States Government."

A similar estimate of the attitude of the American people seems apparent in the statement made by Hon. John Foster Dulles before this committee in answer to a question by Senator Hickenlooper, "Of course, the theoretical ideal is to have in some such body as the United Nations a dependable force which would be greater than the force of any single member state. That would be extending to the society of nations the principle which is, broadly speaking, applied to maintain peace and order within a national society. However, that involves certain supernational aspects, and also the question of the veto power and its renunciation, so that I am afraid that is not a goal which we can regard as practically attainable within any foreseeble period."

STATEMENTS OF EMINENT AUTHORITIES ADVOCATING DISARMAMENT 

Both seem to testify to an assumption on the part of governmental leaders that the people are not ready to accept a disarmament system which would involve world law. On what facts is this assumption based? Is it true? Has there ever been an effort on the part of the Government to initiate a broad educational program on this issue, in which the people would be able to learn the facts, to understand the issues involved, to realize the great weight of authority behind this concept? May I read into the record a few of the many challenging statements which declare that the price of disarmament and peace in our world community, as in our local community, is law: 

President Dwight D. Eisenhower (from Crusade in Europe): 

"During the war it was demonstrated that international unity of purpose and execution could be attained without jeopardy to any nation's independence, if all were willing to pool a portion of their authority in a new single headquarter with power to enforce their decisions. In the formation of the new United Nations and of the Allied organization for the control of Germany this lesson had

#1 not yet been accepted. Its application would have meant some form of limited, federated world government, which, while conforming to the Western Allies' battlefront experience as providing the only sure way to success, was politically unacceptable to any of the new great nations concerned." 

President Dwight D. Eisenhower (from Silver Lecture on International Peace, 

Columbia University, March 23, 1950): "It is clear that international disarmament is essential to a stable, enduring peace. In a disarmed world-should it be attained-there must be an effective 

United Nations with a police power universally recognized and strong enough to earn universal respect.'

The Atomic Declaration of 1945 (President Truman, Prime Minister Atlee, Prime Minister King): 

“*** We are aware that the only complete protection for the civilized world from the destructive use of scientific knowledge lies in the prevention of war. 

No system of safeguards that can be desired will of itself provide an effective guaranty against the production of atomic weapons by a nation bent on aggression * * *. Faced with the terrible reality of the application of scientific destruction, every nation will realize more urgently than before the overwhelming need to maintain the rule of law and to banish the scourge of war from the earth ✶ ✶ ✶" 

Hon. John Foster Dulles (address before the American Bar Association, Boston, August 26, 1953): “One of the inadequacies of the United Nations Charter came out of disregard for the fact that world order, in the long run, depends, not on men, but upon law, law which embodies eternal principles of justice and morality." 

Chief Justice Earl Warren (Fortune magazine, November 1955): 

"It is not just the United States that needs law, it is the entire world. The world's chief need in these next decades will be peace and order; and of all human institutions, law has the best historical claim to satisfy this need." 

Gov. Adlai Stevenson (from the New York Times, October 25, 1952): "We will not lose faith in the United Nations. We see it as a living thing and we will work and pray for its full growth and development. We want it to become what it was intended to be a world society of nations under law, not merely law backed by force, but law backed by justice and popular consent. We believe the answer to world war can only be world law. This is our hope and our commitment."

Ambassador Benjamin V. Cohen, then Deputy United States Repesentative to the Disarmament Commission of the United Nations (address before the American Branch of the International Law Association, May 9, 1952): "Effective disarmament means universal disarmament ***. If we are to make real progress in the field of disarmament, we must approach the problem not as a means of regulating war, or the armaments used in war, but of rendering war impossible as a method of settling international disputes. * * * Large national armies and armaments are as inimical to international peace as large private armies and armaments have always been to domestic peace. "Practical men may say all such thinking in the international field is visionary and unreal, but I suppose practical men in the early days of the development of private law regarded the lawmakers who sought to outlaw private wars and private armies as visionaries and dreamers. It is worth remembering that the primary end of all primitive private law was to keep the peace. *** "In the long run, it is likely that justice will fare better in a world where neither nations nor individuals may take the law into their own hands and fight it out. 

There is much greater danger of the confusion of right and might in an armed world than in a disarmed world. *** "It is important that our people as well as the people of the Soviet Union understand what must be done if the world is to be relieved of the burdens and fears of armaments and the scourge of war. "It is true that virtually all efforts in the past to reduce national armaments have failed. But there is much reason to suspect that the failures in the past were caused by the unwillingness of men and nations to go far enough—by the lack of vision, rather than by visionary ideas. We cannot disarm unless others are willing to disarm. We cannot lay bare our secrets unless others are willing to reveal theirs. But if we wish to accept the gospel of peace under law in an open and disarmed world we must be sure that we ourselves understand and are willing to accept that gospel. We must be willing to espouse and accept a program of arms reduction to such a point, in such a thorough fashion, and with such foolproof safeguards that no nation is in a position to wage a successful war. We

must be willing to espouse and accept a truly open world in which every human being can live free from the fear that others are secretly plotting the destruction of his hearth and home. We must be willing to espouse and accept a world in which all nations not only agree to refrain from the threat or use of force except in defense of the law but recognize that uncontrolled armaments themselves constitute an unlawful threat to the peace. If civilization is to continue, no nation can have a sovereign right to wage war or to menace the world with its arms." 

The editors of Time magazine (Time, April 12, 1954): 

"The H-bomb's existence requires the United States to put much more strongly the case for international control of atomic weapons. Such control might impair unlimited national sovereignty as the world now knows it. It might imply a measure of world government. But the United States need not flinch at this prospect. Its own political history encourages the chance of a constitutional solution of a force so big that it calls for supranational control.” 

David Sarnoff (Fortune magazine, January 1955): 

"We know in our hearts that modern war can cause such overwhelming devastation of life and property as to become a species of suicide. The atom and the electron have made it almost as disastrous for the winner as for the loser. This decisive fact must, in the long run, cancel out war as an instrument of national policy. We cannot know when or what form the coming 'one world' will take, but world law enforced by world police seems inherent in the age of science and technology." 

Rt. Hon. Harold Macmillan, then British Minister of Defense (statement in House of Commons, March 2, 1955): 

"On the whole question of disarmament, our purpose is simple and our record is clear. Genuine disarmament must be based on two simple but vital principles. It must be comprehensive and it must provide a proper system of control. It must be comprehensive, by which I mean that it must include all weapons, new and old, conventional and unconventional. The control must provide effective international, or if we like supranational, authority invested with real power. Honorable members may say that this is elevating the United Nations, or whatever may be the authority into something like world government; be it so, it is none the worse for that. In the long run this is the only way out for mankind."

Henry R. Luce (address, November 17, 1954, aniversary dinner of New Republic magazine): "The great words of high politics are liberty, justice and law-freedom under law. These words will never be repealed as the basic stuff to human destiny. They must therefore be made to mean something in our age. If these words perish, even for a space, America will perish with them ***. "Who *** is trying to define for our age *** the universal relationship between human law and ideal justice? It is a deep and prickly subject. Easier to ignore it. But like the Hound of Heaven, this question will pursue youfor the fact is: There can be no peace without law. Nor freedom either. "Personally I wish that right now the United States would be putting herself in the forefront of the great worldwide concern for law and the rule of law.” 

Bernard M. Baruch (from article in New York Times, January 2, 1955) : "The concept of an international organization to enforce peace was not original with Wilson. For centuries men have proposed it. He was the first to translate the idea into a reality. 'What we seek,' he said, 'is the reign of law based on the consent of the governed and sustained by the organized opinion of mankind.' "He knew that without such a reign of law civilization itself might crash in ruins in another outburst of lawlessness * * *. The very same issue confronts us today in the question of the control of atomic energy, disarmament, and peace. 

There must be a guaranty. Declarations of intent and good will are not enough." 

John Cowles (commencement address, June 6, 1955, Hamline University, St. 

Paul, Minn.): "There is, I believe, a better course than a continuance at an accelerated rate of this arms race, with increasing tensions and the possibility at any time of a holocaust. The better course is universal disarmament under effective, continuous, unqualified international inspection and control. "Universal disarmament, not one-sided or partial, but complete, is the paramount need of the second half of the 20th century. The mutual fears and tensions produced by nuclear weapons and intercontinental missiles are such that

there cannot possibly be real peace until there has been universal disarmament, right down to the level of lightly armed and strictly limited forces for internal order only, with continuous and effective international inspection and control. "We will have to discard a lot of our outworn political ideas and prejudices before we will be willing to do those things that are essential if we are to have complete and enforcible world disarmament."

Rt. Hon. Selwyn Lloyd, British Foreign Secretary (from first speech as Foreign Secretary, December 27, 1955): "A world instrument endowed with the necessary authority is the only way to promote peace ***. There is nothing fundamentally inconsistent between the two concepts of national patriotism guided along the right lines, and the building up of a world instrument to establish the rule of law in the world, just as we in the British Isles established the rule of law in our own national affairs."

Hon. Robert N. Wilkin, United States district judge (Kiwanis magazine, July 

1951): "Within our time, modern inventions have so facilitated travel and communications that the whole world has become one neighborhood. The need for world law and a peaceable method of settling disputes now is absolute." 

Brig. Gen. Carlos P. Romulo (address before U. N. General Assembly, Novem ber 21, 1955): "The people look to the United Nations for control of armaments. They know that control must rest on much more than polite agreements or solemn declarations. All our yesterdays are littered with Locarnos. The people cannot be blamed for expecting us to abandon the habit of error. "The people look to us to define the basis of enforcible disarmament under law. And, in an even more fundamental sense, they look to us to deal with the situations that lead to war ***. "The people have a wisdom about these things, and they know that no armaments can be effective and enforcible unless there is a direct connection between the United Nations and the individual violator. If we fail to support this principle and give it standing and authority, then Nuremberg loses its status in world justice and becomes instead the long limb of lynch law." 

These and similar statements are perhaps known to this committee and our governmental leaders, but regrettably they have been made to an extremely limited group, and little effort has been made to bring them to the attention of the public. How many people have read any of these statements? 

How many people have read Mr. Dulles' testimony before this committee, or that of Senator 

Flanders? What effort has been made to let the people know that the effort to establish law in the world community is considered a reasonable and hopeful solution of the disarmament problem? What effort has been made to acquaint the people with the assumption that they would not support this concept, or that their action would make such disarmament proposals possible for our Government? In short, what impetus has the public been given to act along these lines?

Is it the purpose of the committee to seek suggestions for enforced disarmament which are considered to be acceptable to the public now, even though they fall far short of what is required according to the opinions quoted above? In which case I believe the committee is greatly underestimating the American people. Or is the committee seeking suggestions which are basic to the problem of enforced disarmament, regardless of how Utopian or politically unrealistic some might believe them to be? If proposals that actually provide for a rational system are presented, will an all out effort be made to bring the facts, however revolutionary, to the people, in a manner which will promote understanding and action?

Until now there has been a great gap between our leaders who recognize the need for law at the world level, but who despair of the people, and the people who would accept this concept, were it presented as a reasonable and realistic road to disarmament and peace, endorsed by respected world leaders. I urgently propose that this committee take whatever steps are within its power to close this gap, for the key to the disarmament problem is public understanding. Certainly no greater contribution could be made to the achievement of universal enforced disarmament.

STATEMENT BY RONALD C. MCLAUGHLIN, RESERVE OFFICER IN UNITED STATES AIR FORCE, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

DISARMAMENT AS A BYPRODUCT 

The noted anthropologist, Margaret Meade, has written, "War is not inherent in humanity. Warfare was an invention which accompanied the development of group solidarity. * * * We cannot expect that warfare, which is the most adequate invention to date for protecting one's own group against the purposeful depredations of others, will be given up because it is branded as ethically unacceptable. It will not be given up until we invent something better." If seems evident to me that the failure of all disarmament efforts in the past has been due not only to the futility of expecting disarmament agreements to be kept voluntarily, but also to the tragic ignoring of the need for an effective substitute for arms. The substitute which has been accepted on every level except the world level is law.

Throughout the ages there have been voices raised in increasing numbers, saying that there can be no peace without law, and the institutions of law which permit men to live together without resort to violence. Basically most of us agree with them-but the establishment of a legal world community seems so difficult and remote that we tend to postpone it, to concentrate on the symbols of man's discontent and fear, his weapons of self-defense. Here is something which almost everyone deplores. The wasting of man's substance in the means of destruction-the shame of relying on brute force instead of on moral values, by default actually accepting the philosophy that might makes rightthe degradation of resorting to mass murders of one's fellow men-from all this mankind yearns to be released. For this reason efforts toward disarmament are possible, and because of the H-bomb and the ICBM efforts toward disarmament are imperative. At the same time people need to understand that even a start on disarmament is futile without at least a start on the substitute. 

DISARMAMENT FOLLOWS ESTABLISHMENT OF ENFORCIBLE LAW 

In the early days of the Western United States frontier, men, good or bad, had no protection but their own guns. Disarmament here was not accomplished by any attempt to ban six-shooters, but came as the result of the establishment of lawful communities, wherein men could feel that their interests were protected by the community. Under these circumstances, although the possession of guns was not prohibited, they ceased to be a factor in man's relationship with his neighbors. Community law was substituted for violence; disarmament was a byproduct of the establishment of enforcible law. 

Disarmament in the world will become an absolute fact when we shall have institutionalized worldwide this principle which has brought peace to our local communities, enforcible law, by and for society, guaranteeing the security of men against the use of violence in the attempt to settle their grievances. Then armed might will wither away as a useless expense. But much as I advocate this goal, I realize that the problem of the arms race is urgent and cannot wait for the complete solution. It is imperative, however, that the goal to be reached be a determining factor in the present steps toward disarmament. Any steps toward disarmament will be futile unless accompanied by laying some of the foundation stones in the edifice of world law. 

DISINTERESTED THIRD PARTY JUDGMENT IN SETTLEMENT OF DISPUTES 

Among these looms large the principle of third party judgment-familiar to every man whether as the father of the family, the tribal chieftain, justice of the peace, referee, mediation board, arbitration board, or Supreme Court. Whether in rudimentary form or highly institutionalized, it is the respect for and acceptance of disinterested third party judgment which by and large his induced men to lay down their arms. Here, they came to realize, was a method of settling their conflicts much more in the interest of society, and more satisfactory to themselves in the long run, than the mustering of brute force. I am convinced that much of the world's tension revolves around interested parties trying to settle their own disputes, where little or no machinery or prestige exists for third party judgment.

Consider the Israeli-Arab dispute. The personal efforts of the Secretary General of the United Nations have secured certain pledges about the use of force, and have eased tensions to a degree. How much comfort can we take in this,

Ta lacking a satisfactory settlement of the basic dispute? And yet Mr. Hammarskjold says that a settlement should not be forced upon them from the outside, but that they should be left to work out the settlement themselves. I know of no one who would suggest that a baseball game be played without an umpire-leaving it up to the managers of the opposing teams to decide whether the pitch is a ball or a strike, or whether the runner is safe on third. In the Middle East, where the issues involve life and death and national survival, how can we possibly expect the Arabs and Israelis to pass concerted judgment in their own case? And yet of course Mr. Hammarskjold is quite right in insisting that a settlement cannot be forced from the outside. To be effective, third party settlement should be sought by the contending parties, through an agency, a referee or a tribunal which they have helped to establish, and in which they have mutual confidence. No such agency exists, except the International Court of Justice, which has no compulsory jurisdiction, and little prestige due to being continually ignored by the nations. 

The world has an uneasy conscience about its disregard of the International Court, and judicial proceedings in general. That conscience manifested itself in the Nuremberg and Tokyo trials when an attempt was made to impose justice, not merely the will of the victors. The attempt fell short, because it was an attempt to "impose," and because the vanquished had had no part in establishing the system under which they were being judged. The conscience of mankind, dimly sensing the dilemma, had paid lip service to the judicial concept; it has established the International Court as a function of the U. N., has constantly urged peaceful settlements of disputes, and has sent its U. N. mediators throughout the world to troubled spots. But still the nations have not been willing to include the principles of Nuremberg in the U. N. Charter. Still each nation insists it would be fine for others to abide by third party judgment, but each one retains full right of decision in its own case. Why should not the Arab-Israeli dispute be judged by the International Court? To the best of my knowledge this has not been urged by the great nations. Is this because each knows that under similar critical circumstances it would not submit to such judgment? 

Are we so afraid of the possible adverse decision of a world court that we are less afraid of the H-bomb, and prefer to take a chance on the latter? Individual man will say "no," that he prefers the legal process-it is high time for collective man to admit the necessity. Let the United States state unequivocally what factors, what safeguards would be necessary before we as a nation could accept compulsory jurisdiction of the International Court in all cases. At the same time let the United States show more faith in humanity, and a willingness to expand greatly the jurisdiction of the court, even under present conditions. Confidence in judicial proceedings can be built by use, growth is possible by use, and the United States has a great responsibility to foster such growth. 

ADVANCEMENT OF AUTHORITY OF INTERNATIONAL. COURT PREREQUISITE OF DISARMAMENT 

Advancement of disarmament, then, must be accompanied by advancement of the authority of the International Court and related judicial procedures. Plans for disarmament become more realistic if considered in a framework that concurrently provides for third party judgment. For two essential reasons I believe this program would be more effective than any direct frontal assault on armaments alone:

First: Although individual men living under law have given up arms, collective men, as nations have always relied on weapons, and are extremely suspicious of attempts to deprive them of those weapons. The term "disarmament" itself has become so synonymous with unilateral disarmament in the minds of many people, as to come into disrepute. Even the term "enforced disarmament," now the professed goal of our Government is viewed with suspicion by these people because the Government has not explained the term, and lacking such explanation it seems to connote a forceful taking away of our means of self-defense, with no protection in an uncertain and hostile environment. At the same time, in all walks of life except the international, men understand and accept third-party judgment. I am convinced it would be much easier for them to consider accepting such judgment on the world level than to consider losing their means of self-defense. Once weapons become unimportant there will be no wrench in giving them up.

Second: The feeling of desperation over the development of the ICBM would be largely mitigated. Many seriously believe that unless some effective means

of inspection and controlling weapons of mass destruction is found before the ICBM is perfected and in production, it will then be too late because the launching sites of these "ultimate" weapons would be beyond detection. The seriousness of this development cannot be overemphasized, but we should not be panicked into feeling that all is lost if effective controls are not established in the very short time that remains before the ICBM will be a fact. The approach that 

I am advocating, that I am urging our country to advocate, does not depend upon the state of perfection of weapons. It depends upon man's attitude toward the substitute for those weapons. If he feels there is no other recourse, the time will certainly come when he will use violence even at the risk of suicide. But if he is given some hope of legal recourse by the world community, he need not resort to those weapons, and they will sooner or later become meaningless. 

I am not competent to suggest details as to how this development of third party judgment might take place. There are many legal experts who can advise on that. What I am suggesting is that our country urge at every opportunity, in and out of the U. N. again and again, the principle of impartial, third party judgment, until it is made clear to the world that United States hopes for the future are based on judicial procedures. Under such circumstances, what we say about disarmament will assume real dimensions. 

When Ambassador Luis Padilla Nervo of Mexico was President of the Gen eral Assembly of the U. N., he made a speech in Minneapolis, in which expounding the possibilities of the U. N. he said that only in the last resort would nations go to war. We could not help contrasting that with our country, with any country, where, though violence may be attempted, the last resort is the courts. In the world community courts can provide the acceptable substitute for violence, the only civilized last resort.

STATEMENT BY HARLAN M. SMITH, MINNEAPOLIS, MINN. 

Consideration of the efforts to date to secure international agreement on disarmament should reveal two reasons which have received too little attention underlying the failure of the efforts. First, it is fairly clear that the nations involved are interested in disarmament proposals which are thought to strengthen their respective power positions. No agreement can be expected so long as each is seeking advantage over the other rather than a mutually beneficial arrangement. Second, a really adequate inspection scheme will in the end prove a stumbling block to us as well as to Russia in connection with any scheme of arms limitation or arms reduction which leaves nations sufficiently armed to carry on a modern war. Inspection would give away valuable information to a potential enemy which might be unwise if reliance is still placed upon war as a possible instrument of policy.

These two points lead me to a third. Our leadership is still not leading us or the world toward a scale of disarmament which could circumvent the above obstacles or which could be adequate safeguard against atomic war even if adopted. We are following old and long discredited patterns for the most part, and have not yet leaders with the vision, the boldness and the courage to urge serious efforts to develop a plan for complete, universal, and enforcible disarmament. Such notions are dismissed as impossible but the only obstacle lies in men's minds; this obstacle might prove not to be insuperable if our leaders would tell people the true gravity of the situation, the truth about halfway measures, and show them that only a more drastic solution will provide the world security. 

Acceptance might not come as slowly as some think if the United States stood ready to take such a big step whenever other nations would join us. 

STATEMENT BY JAMES M. YOUNGDALE, BENSON, MINN. 

The issue of world disarmament is the number 1 issue facing the people of the world. Arms races have always resulted in war; and a big war today would simply mean the suicide of the human race. 

Furthermore, all nations of the world could better use funds and resources for constructive purposes rather than for the arms race. Also, the very fact that so much time and energy goes into the arms race means that serious social and economic problems are being ignored.

Throughout history, the building of military machines has blotted out democracy and idealism in social outlook. Militarism breeds fear, suspicion, and

concentration camps in the various nations. Furthermore, each nation tends to develop groups of people whose jobs or profits depend on a continuation of the armament race, such groups often being called a war lobby. 

In our own Nation, we have certainly witnessed an erosion of democracy during the recent war and cold-war period saying nothing of our failure to extend democracy to our Negro citizens. We have a war lobby composed of military men, of business groups that thrive on bigger and bigger war spending and of some political leaders in both parties. We have six concentration camps ready for use in event of an emergency. Formerly, we heard slogans about "drafting profits when we draft men to risk their lives" and we once heard President Roosevelt call for a $25,000 salary limit in wartime; but these kinds of ideas are today tagged as subversive and dismissed from public discussion. 

HUMAN WELFARE INSTEAD OF ARMAMENTS 

In our own Nation, we may think we can afford a big war budget. However, we have problems that will call for vast spending during the next 25 years. We are rapidly facing a water shortage that will become more acute as our population increases. We need to adopt a vast slum-clearance program to provide a better environment for large numbers of families. We need a farm program that will give our dirt farmers parity of living standards with the rest of the population. As we adopt a shorter and shorter work week, we will need to make adult recreation a public responsibility in large part as we do education with a tremendous network of recreation centers, swimming pools, and libraries that will provide an alternative to taverns, pool halls, and the like for many adults. 

Our school-building program has lagged during the war and cold-war period and consequently much spending is needed in this area. A Nation as rich as ours should be able to provide a universal old-age pension program patterned after the Scandinavian system to replace our inadequate social-security program. 

These are the kind of issues we ourselves face during the next years of our lifetimes; however, any adequate solution of these problems is virtually impossible as long as a world armament race rages among the nations of the world.

Under pressure of war hysteria and war spending, other nations of the world have also suffered an erosion of democracy and have also failed to give attention to pressing domestic problems.

It is good to see that the nations are holding discussions on the question of world disarmament. After the recent London meetings, Harold Stassen reported that progress was made in narrowing the gap between Russia and ourselves. 

It is good to see that President Eisenhower has suggested that small nations can sometimes contribute toward world peace through a policy of neutrality. 

Let me suggest that the United States Senate support the President and the United Nations in efforts to achieve world disarmament and that the Senate ignore the pleas for the war lobby for continuing the arms race.
